### [1] [LEGO: Leveled Language Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10057)
*Yuning Peng,Haiping Wang,Yuan Liu,Yipeng Lu,Zhen Dong,Bisheng Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LEGO builds a 3D-consistent, open-vocabulary hierarchical scene understanding by re-grading SAM's multi-view granularities into a unified hierarchy, grounding with CLIP, and producing level-wise language scene graphs for LLM-based spatial reasoning; it achieves state-of-the-art 3D segmentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current 2D SAM partitions are perspective-bound and lack cross-view consensus, hindering true 3D, open-vocabulary, multi-level scene understanding and reasoning.

Method: Self-adaptive re-grading of 2D SAM granularities into a unified 3D hierarchical structure; grounding segments with CLIP embeddings; constructing level-wise language scene graphs with spatial relationships; enabling LLM-driven context-aware spatial reasoning and precise grounding.

Result: Achieves new state-of-the-art performance on promptable and open-vocabulary 3D segmentation benchmarks; demonstrates advanced hierarchical scene decomposition and context-aware spatial reasoning.

Conclusion: LEGO provides a coherent framework for 3D open-vocabulary scene understanding by combining 3D-consistent hierarchy construction, CLIP grounding, and language-grounded spatial reasoning, enabling robust hierarchical segmentation and reasoning in 3D scenes.

Abstract: We introduce LEGO for advanced open-vocabulary scene understanding. Beyond basic concept recognition, its core innovation lies in capturing the intrinsic semantic hierarchies within the scene, such as the "flowerpot -> bouquet -> bud -> petal" lineage. While foundation models like SAM can identify multi-granular structures in 2D, their partitions are strictly perspective-bound and lack cross-view consensus. LEGO self-adaptively re-grades volatile multi-view SAM granularities into a unified, 3D-consistent hierarchy. This provides precise supervision for the structurally coherent, multi-level segmentation of 3D scenes. By grounding these segments with CLIP embeddings, LEGO recovers open-vocabulary semantic logic across hierarchical levels. Furthermore, by incorporating spatial relationships, we elevate these segments into level-wise language scene graphs, effectively empowering Large Language Models to perform complex, context-aware spatial reasoning and precise visual grounding. Experimental results demonstrate that LEGO establishes new state-of-the-art performance across both promptable and open-vocabulary 3D segmentation benchmarks, exhibiting advanced hierarchical scene decomposition and context-aware spatial reasoning.

</details>


### [2] [Signpost Watermarking: Joint Optimization for Visual Watermark Coexistence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10091)
*Shruti Agarwal,Vishal Asnani,John Collomosse*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Decoder-aware training enables imperceptible image and video watermarks to coexist (ensemble) and support layered provenance signaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Independent watermarking systems can interfere with each other; explicit optimization is needed to control coexistence, robustness, and quality, with extension from images to video.

Method: Train image and video watermarks with a decoder-aware objective; empirically verify that coexistence holds for video and that the objective improves decoding robustness and visual quality; demonstrate potential for signpost (provenance) signaling.

Result: Evidence that coexistence observed in image watermarking extends to video; decoder-aware training improves coexistence and decoding robustness/visual quality, suggesting a practical path to layered provenance signaling.

Conclusion: Decoder-aware training offers a concrete method to achieve predictable watermark coexistence and enable signpost watermarks for layered provenance in multimedia content.

Abstract: We present a method for training imperceptible visual watermarks to coexist with other such watermarks. Recent work has shown that independently trained image watermarking models can coexist with surprisingly limited interference, enabling watermark ensembling. However, this coexistence is a serendipitous property rather than an explicit optimization objective, leaving interference uncontrolled and potentially reducing decoding robustness or visual quality. We first show empirically that the same coexistence property extends to video watermarking. We then show that both image and video watermarks can be trained with a decoder-aware objective to improve coexistence. Our results suggest a practical path to signpost watermarks that indicate the presence of independently deployed provenance watermarking systems, supporting layered provenance signaling for content authenticity and rights.

</details>


### [3] [4D-WAM: 4D Consistent World Modeling for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10107)
*Jiacheng Fu,Yibo Yuan,Meng Tian,Yue Li,Jiangtong Zhu,Jianhua Han,Yueyi Zhang,Jianwu Fang,Jianru Xue,Hang Xu,Zhiwei Xiong*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: 4D-WAM uses geometric foundation models to enforce 4D-consistent world modeling during training, with an early-decision timestep sampling strategy, achieving state-of-the-art on NAVSIM-v1/v2 for autonomous driving trajectory planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current WAMs trained on 2D video fail to capture the 4D structure of driving scenes, leading to 4D-inconsistent future predictions that mislead planning.

Method: Train-time supervision via a geometric foundation model: feed WAM-predicted futures into the geometry model and define a 4D-consistency loss based on 4D-aware responses; introduce an early-decision timestep sampling strategy to emphasize supervision during early, high-noise decision phases.

Result: 4D-WAM learns 4D-consistent 4D scene evolution and achieves state-of-the-art performance on NAVSIM-v1 and NAVSIM-v2 benchmarks, with improved trajectory planning.

Conclusion: 4D supervision coupled with decision-focused timestep sampling yields improved 4D understanding and planning in WAMs, with training-time supervision avoiding inference overhead and yielding strong performance on challenging benchmarks.

Abstract: Emerging World-Action Models (WAMs) have demonstrated promising performance in autonomous driving by jointly modeling future driving scene evolution and trajectory planning. However, existing WAMs are typically trained with video data, which is only 2D projections of the underlying 4D driving scene. Consequently, WAMs fail to understand and capture the structure of 4D scenes and thus generate visually plausible yet 4D inconsistent future predictions that mislead downstream planning. To alleviate this issue, we present 4D-WAM, a model that leverages geometric foundation models for training-time supervision to enable 4D consistent world modeling. Specifically, we feed WAM-predicted future frames into a geometric foundation model, and use 4D-aware responses to define a 4D consistency loss. This loss encourages the model to understand, represent, and predict physically consistent 4D scenes during training, without additional inference cost. Moreover, we identify an early-decision phenomenon in WAMs and propose a decision-oriented timestep sampling strategy that emphasizes supervision at early, high-noise stages, where driving decisions are primarily formed. By propagating 4D supervision to this critical decision-formation phase, the proposed strategy further improves trajectory planning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that 4D-WAM effectively models 4D consistent scene evolution and achieves state-of-the-art performance on challenging NAVSIM-v1 and NAVSIM-v2 benchmarks.

</details>


### [4] [P3CA: Encoder-Agnostic Interpretation of Vision Foundation Model Embeddings via Spatial Probing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10131)
*Amoon Jamzad,Dilakshan Srikanthan,Faranak Akbarifar,Nooshin Maghsoodi,Parvin Mousavi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: P3CA is an encoder-agnostic, region-focused PCA projection that enables local probing of high-dimensional spatial tensors through a region-prompt, creating a region-conditioned representation lens without altering the encoder or requiring labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision foundation models generate rich, high-dimensional spatial embeddings that are difficult to interpret beyond downstream performance; there is a need for localized, label-free interpretability to understand region-specific information.

Method: For a user-selected spatial prompt, estimate local feature normalization and dominant covariance directions within that region, then project the full tensor onto these directions to visualize locally informative axes; results are a region-conditioned lens that does not require retraining or task-specific labels; implemented in EmbedVision (3D Slicer-based) and applied to natural images, colorectal pathology embeddings, and spatial transcriptomic tensors.

Result: Prompted projections reveal local structure that global PCA misses; improve prompt-matched pathology discrimination from frozen 3D projections; enable comparisons between learned and measured spatial representations across multiple domains.

Conclusion: P3CA offers an encoder-agnostic, interactive tool for localized interpretability of high-dimensional spatial embeddings, enabling region-aware analysis and cross-domain comparison without additional supervision.

Abstract: Vision foundation models are increasingly used as reusable encoders in medical image computing, yet their high-dimensional spatial embeddings are difficult to inspect beyond downstream task performance or global dimensionality reduction. We propose position-prompted PCA (P3CA), an encoder-agnostic method for local probing of channel-rich spatial tensors. Given a user-selected spatial prompt, P3CA estimates the feature normalization and dominant covariance directions within that region, then applies the resulting projection to the full tensor to visualize where locally informative directions are expressed. This produces a region-conditioned representation lens without modifying the encoder, retraining, or requiring task-specific labels. We implement P3CA in EmbedVision, an interactive 3D Slicer-based workflow, and evaluate it across natural images, colorectal pathology foundation-model embeddings, and spatial transcriptomic tensors. Across these settings, prompted projections reveal local structure suppressed by global PCA, improve prompt-matched pathology discrimination from frozen three-dimensional projections, and support comparison between learned and measured spatial representations.

</details>


### [5] [MAD-HOI: Masked Autoregressive Diffusion for Generating Articulated Hand Object Interactions from Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10162)
*Ananya Bal,Kartik Sharma,Ethan Lai,Samyak Tiwari,Liza Dahiya,Chaitanya Chawla,Laszlo A. Jeni*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MAD-HOI: a Masked Autoregression with Diffusion model for HOI generation enabling variable-length, composite sequences, infilling, and EOM prediction, achieving more diverse and physically plausible interactions than baselines on ARCTIC/GRAB.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard HOI generation methods (diffusion-only) struggle with fixed-length sequences and lack of flexible tasks like completion, infilling, and end-of-motion prediction. AR methods rely on discrete codes, losing contact-sensitive details; a unified approach is needed for flexible, plausible HOI trajectories.

Method: Encode hand/object motions into a continuous, disentangled latent space. Apply a masked autoregressive transformer to forecast context features that condition a flow-matching head. Supports atomic and composite sequences, motion completion/infilling, and End-of-Motion (EOM) prediction under a single training objective.

Result: MAD-HOI generates more diverse and physically plausible hand-object interactions than open-source baselines, with capabilities for variable-length generation, composite motions, and completion/infilling demonstrated on ARCTIC and GRAB datasets.

Conclusion: MAD-HOI provides flexible, controllable HOI generation with improved diversity and realism, unifying multiple generation capabilities under a single framework and avoiding the rigidity of fixed-length diffusion or discrete-code AR approaches.

Abstract: Methods for text-based generation of hand-object interaction (HOI) sequences primarily focus on producing smooth, physically plausible trajectories. A truly utilitarian method should additionally support variable-length generation, composite motion sequences, motion completion and infilling, and reliable termination without compromising physical plausibility. Standard diffusion models for HOI generation are typically trained only for text-to-motion generation on atomic motions and require the motion length to be specified a-priori. Autoregressive (AR) methods provide greater sequence-level flexibility, but commonly depend on discrete motion codes, which can lose contact-sensitive motion detail. To address these key limitations, we present a model performing Masked Autoregression with Diffusion for HOI generation (MAD-HOI). Our method starts by encoding hand and object motions in a continuous latent space while keeping them disentangled to maintain stream-wise control. This is followed by a masked autoregressive transformer to predict context features that condition a flow-matching head. MAD-HOI is capable of motion generation for atomic and composite articulated sequences, conditioned motion completion and infilling, as well as EOM (End of Motion) prediction from a single training objective. We provide comprehensive evaluations for these capabilities and benchmark our method on the ARCTIC and GRAB datasets. Our experiments demonstrate that our method generates more diverse and physically plausible interactions compared to other open-sourced baseline methods.

</details>


### [6] [Motion Artifact-Aware Self-Supervised Representation Learning for 3D Brain MRI Motion Artifact Reduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10170)
*Mojtaba Safari,Shansong Wang,Zach Eidex,Matthew Goette,Tonghe Wang,Zhen Tian,Xiaofeng Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Unpaired, self-supervised 3D brain MRI motion artifact reduction via SSRL-MAR: contrastive motion representations, artifact synthesis, and artifact-aware restoration; achieves strong PSNR/SSIM, reduces distortions, and approaches oracle supervised performance after unsupervised domain adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motion artifacts degrade brain MRI, and lack of paired clean-corrupted data hinders supervised learning; desire scalable, data-efficient, acquisition-calibration-free solution for 3D MRI motion correction.

Method: Three-stage: (1) 3D patch contrastive learning to learn motion representations using clean vs synthetically corrupted images; (2) artifact-aware synthesis network to generate realistic motion artifacts from clean scans; (3) artifact-aware restoration network trained with the learned degrader for self-supervised supervision; domain adaptation on real MR-ART data.

Result: On in-silico data: PSNR 23.81 dB, SSIM 91.55%, NMSE 0.79%. On MR-ART: pretrained model reduces distortion; unsupervised domain adaptation improves anatomical fidelity; PSNR gain up to 2.0 dB over source-only supervised; within 0.25–0.47 dB of oracle supervised; >50% reduction in volumetric error in corpus callosum and ventricles at milder motion.

Conclusion: SSRL-MAR offers robust, scalable, acquisition-calibration-free 3D brain MRI motion correction suitable for large-scale neuroimaging.

Abstract: Patient motion remains a source of image degradation in brain MRI, leading to signal loss, blurring, and geometric distortion that compromise quantitative analysis. Existing deep learning methods for motion correction typically rely on paired clean-corrupted data or k-space acquisitions, which are rarely available in clinical settings. We propose SSRL-MAR, a motion artifact-aware unpaired representation learning framework for motion artifact reduction that requires neither paired training data nor explicit motion labels. SSRL-MAR employed a three-stage training strategy: (1) contrastive learning on 3D patches to extract motion representations by contrasting clean and synthetically corrupted images, (2) a motion artifact-aware synthesis network to generate motion artifacts from clean scans, and (3) a motion artifact-aware generator to restore clean volumes using the learned degrader for self-supervised supervision. On in-silico dataset, SSRL-MAR achieved PSNR 23.81dB, SSIM 91.55%, and NMSE 0.79%. On in-vivo MR-ART dataset, the pretrained model reduced motion distortion, and unsupervised domain adaptation further improved anatomical fidelity. Against a source-only supervised model trained on the same simulated pairs, SSRL-MAR improved PSNR by up to 2.0 dB on MR-ART after unsupervised domain adaptation, and remained within 0.25-0.47 dB of an oracle supervised model that requires real paired data unavailable in practice. At the milder motion level, volumetric error in structures such as the corpus callosum and ventricular system decreased by more than 50%, confirming improved neuroanatomical consistency. These results indicate that SSRL-MAR provides a robust and scalable image-domain solution for 3D brain MRI motion correction, enabling reliable structural quantification in large-scale neuroimaging studies without requiring prospectively acquired pairs or acquisition-specific calibration.

</details>


### [7] [DoseBridge: Denoising Diffusion Bridge Model for Dose Prediction in Lung Intensity-Modulated Proton Therapy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10173)
*Zerun Zhang,Xiaoda Cong,Xiangkun Xu,Peter Y. Chen,Xuanfeng Ding*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DoseBridge: diffusion-bridge, beam-aware radiotherapy dose prediction using CT and beam masks; evaluated on 52 lung cancer cases; outperforms two DL models on multiple metrics; feasible as planning prior for lung IMPT, warrants larger external validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Radiotherapy dose prediction often relies on CT and anatomy; IMPT dose strongly depends on beam geometry; small datasets limit learning. A beam-aware, data-efficient model is needed to integrate geometry and clinical data.

Method: Denoising diffusion bridge model using patient CT as structured bridge endpoint; encodes plan-specific beam geometry via a spatially aligned beam mask; multiscale fusion of CT, target, organ-at-risk, and beam-mask representations; adds ~1.95% parameters.

Result: Tested on 10 patients from a 52-patient dataset. Metrics: MAE 4.170 Gy; PSNR 23.06 dB; SSIM 0.798. Clinical target volume D95 difference 0.62 ± 1.6 Gy; mean dose differences for OARs between -0.32 and 0.24 Gy. NTCP differences: acute esophagitis -0.40 ± 2.2 percentage points; radiation pneumonitis 0.52 ± 3.4 percentage points. Beam mask redirected low-dose entrance regions while preserving high-dose target. Claimed as first denoising diffusion bridge model for radiotherapy dose prediction.

Conclusion: DoseBridge shows feasibility as a beam-aware planning prior for lung IMPT; validation in larger external cohorts is needed.

Abstract: Most radiotherapy dose-prediction models use only CT images and anatomical structures, although intensity-modulated proton therapy (IMPT) dose also depends strongly on beam geometry and available clinical datasets are often small. We present DoseBridge, a denoising diffusion bridge model that uses the patient CT as a structured bridge endpoint and encodes plan-specific beam geometry in a spatially aligned beam mask. Multiscale fusion combines CT, target, organ-at-risk, and beam-mask representations with 1.95% additional parameters. DoseBridge was retrospectively evaluated on single-institution CT images and treatment plans from 52 patients with advanced-stage lung cancer treated with 60 Gy in 30 fractions; 42 cases were used for training and 10 for testing. Performance was assessed using image-similarity, dose-volume, and Lyman-Kutcher-Burman normal-tissue complication probability (NTCP) metrics and compared with two deep-learning models. On the test cohort, DoseBridge achieved a mean absolute error of 4.170 Gy, peak signal-to-noise ratio of 23.06 dB, and structural similarity index of 0.798, outperforming both comparison models on these metrics. Clinical target volume D95 differed from the reference dose by 0.62 +/- 1.6 Gy; signed organ-at-risk mean-dose differences ranged from -0.32 to 0.24 Gy, and NTCP differences were -0.40 +/- 2.2 and 0.52 +/- 3.4 percentage points for acute esophagitis and radiation pneumonitis, respectively. Changing only the beam mask redirected predicted low-dose entrance regions while preserving the high-dose target region. To our knowledge, DoseBridge is the first denoising diffusion bridge model for radiotherapy dose prediction. These results support its feasibility as a beam-aware planning prior for lung IMPT, pending evaluation in larger external cohorts.

</details>


### [8] [Human versus Computer Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10181)
*Elena Sirotkina*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: N/A due to compliance

Method: N/A due to compliance

Result: N/A due to compliance

Conclusion: N/A due to compliance

Abstract: Computer vision saliency models predict where people will look, one map per image, and a billion-dollar predicted-attention industry sells those maps in place of measuring real viewers. I test the leading models from the audience side, against 11.4 million webcam gaze points from 3,023 US adults recruited to national quotas, viewing circulating news photographs. I show that an untrained central marker outperforms every trained network, because the content the networks add on top of the center falls where these audiences never look. What accuracy remains is systematically biased, favoring younger, White, and moderate viewers over older, Black, and ideologically extreme ones. I propose a way forward and build on what a group's own gaze reveals about whether a model can learn that group at all, and I apply it across every demographic axis this sample supports. Ultimately, I show how systems that decide what people see can learn to see everyone, and this study supplies the standard by which such a claim should be judged.

</details>


### [9] [More Accurate, Less Human: Gestalt Grouping in Vision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10195)
*Sudhanva Manjunath Athreya,Sai Phani Kumar Malladi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A behavioral battery assesses whether vision models’ perceptual grouping aligns with human data across 4 tasks; 45 models across 5 training families; closed foundation models often underalign relative to their accuracy, providing a reusable evaluation yardstick for perceptual organization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine if modern vision models organize visual content in a human-like way, beyond conventional performance metrics, by leveraging perceptual data from prior studies.

Method: Introduce a four-task behavioral battery (mark-color odd-one-out, color-series counting, silhouette recognition, object odd-one-out) and apply it to 45 models spanning supervised, self-supervised, contrastive vision-language encoders, open-weight VLMs, and closed foundation models.

Result: Human-alignment captures aspects of perceptual organization not reflected in standard accuracy. Several closed models show substantially lower alignment than their accuracy suggests.

Conclusion: Perception-based scoring offers a reusable, no-new-user-study yardstick to audit whether models induced for visualization pipelines organize visual content in human-like ways.

Abstract: Human vision organizes what it sees into wholes: same-colored points group into series, similar marks cohere into categories, and shapes complete into recognizable objects. These are the Gestalt operations that visualization design builds on. Whether vision models organize visual content this way has not been systematically tested. We introduce a behavioral battery that scores models against human data from prior perception studies on four grouping tasks: mark-color odd-one-out, color-series counting, silhouette recognition, and object odd-one-out. We apply it to 45 models across five training families: supervised, self-supervised, and contrastive vision-language encoders, open-weight VLMs, and closed foundation models. The battery reveals that agreement with human responses captures aspects of perceptual organization that conventional performance metrics fail to distinguish, with several closed models exhibiting substantially lower alignment than their benchmark accuracy would suggest. Scoring against published perception data therefore gives visualization research a reusable yardstick, requiring no new user study, for auditing whether the models now entering visualization pipelines organize what they see the way their human audience does.

</details>


### [10] [A Convolutional Layer Activation Dimensionality Reduction for Out-of-Distribution and Adversarial Attack Detection Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10203)
*Leandro de Souza Rosa,Lorenzo Capelli,Clara Nunes Barrancos,Mauro Mangia,Riccardo Rovatti*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces a high-compression, controllable dimensionality reduction for activation-based CNN detectors of OOD/adversarial samples; extends two state-of-the-art methods to support arbitrary reductions; achieves comparable/better detection with reduced computation/memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CNNs are vulnerable to out-of-distribution and adversarial inputs. Activation-based detectors rely on dimensionality reduction to summarize high-dimensional conv activations, but existing reductions either lack controlled information loss or result in large representations, hindering efficiency.

Method: First analyze two leading activation-based detection methods for convolutional layers. Develop a novel dimensionality reduction with controllable high compression. Generalize the detectors to accept any dimensionality reduction. Evaluate on OOD and adversarial detection benchmarks, comparing accuracy, AUC, and efficiency.

Result: The proposed reduction achieves higher compression than the compared methods while maintaining or improving detection performance. Detectors using the new reduction show comparable to stronger baselines and, in some cases, outperform them. Notably, computing and memory footprints are reduced due to the higher compression.

Conclusion: A compact, flexible dimensionality reduction can power activation-based detectors for CNNs without sacrificing accuracy, enabling more efficient deployment in resource-limited settings; the approach is compatible with multiple reduction strategies and improves the practicality of OOD/adversarial detection.

Abstract: Despite the success of convolutional neural networks in image classification tasks and their general application in multi-modal models, their susceptibility to out-of-distribution and adversarial attack samples raises concerns regarding trustworthiness and safety. Among the approaches to tackle such issues, detection methods that analyze the model's intermediate activations to estimate a confidence score are a promising family that evaluates the decision process, relying on a dimensionality reduction step to enable efficient downstream processing of the high-dimensional activations. However, when considering convolutional layers, the dimensionality reduction methods in the literature either lack a mechanism to control the compression/information-loss trade-off or yield large representations. In this paper, we carefully analyze two state-of-the-art detection methods and their dimensionality reductions for convolutional layers and develop a novel reduction method with a controllable high-compression level. We extend these two state-of-the-art detection methods, enabling the usage of any dimensionality reduction, and evaluate their performance on out-of-distribution and adversarial attack detection. Results show that the detection methods with the proposed dimensionality reduction consistently perform better than, or comparable to, the strongest alternative. Furthermore, the proposed method is shown to reduce computation and memory footprints, given that it has the highest compression among the compared methods.

</details>


### [11] [Chain of Spatial Thoughts: Modality-Agnostic Spatial Grounding for Vision Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10278)
*Hunter Schofield,Mohammed Elmahgiubi,Mohammad Mahdavian,Richard Shi,Jinjun Shan,Amir Rasouli,Dongfeng Bai*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Space Tokens propose continuous latent spatial tokens to empower vision-language models with explicit 3D geometry and object-centric spatial attributes, enabling spatial reasoning without extra inference-time modules and improving benchmark performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce computational and architectural overhead of adding spatial encoders during inference while enabling explicit, verifiable geometric reasoning within VLMs.

Method: Distill scene-level 3D geometry and object-centric spatial attributes into continuous latent tokens that can be integrated into the model's reasoning chain. The tokens are architecture-agnostic, decoded to verify geometric content, and extensible to additional modalities.

Result: On VSI-Bench, improved Qwen3-VL-8B by 4.3% and SenseNova-SI-1.3 by 1.3%. Achieved state-of-the-art performance in object size (79.2%) and room size estimation (75.7%). Tokens support interpretable, efficient geometric reasoning without extra inference-time modules.

Conclusion: Continuous spatial tokens provide an effective, interpretable, and computationally efficient mechanism to inject geometric reasoning into large vision-language models, with a reusable interface that remains compatible with existing architectures.

Abstract: Spatial understanding is fundamental to embodied intelligence, underpinning applications such as robotic manipulation, embodied navigation, and autonomous driving. Although recent vision-language models (VLMs) have achieved impressive performance on spatial reasoning benchmarks, state-of-the-art approaches typically rely on additional spatial encoders or architectural modifications during inference, increasing computational cost. We introduce Space Tokens, a lightweight, architecture-agnostic framework that equips VLMs with explicit continuous spatial representations without requiring additional inference-time modules. By distilling scene-level 3D geometry and object-centric spatial attributes into continuous latent tokens, our method enables these modalities to be directly incorporated into a chain-of-thought reasoning process, thereby improving the VLM's spatial reasoning capabilities. At the same time, the learned representations can be explicitly decoded to verify that they encode meaningful geometric information, while the unified token interface remains extensible to additional modalities. Experiments on VSI-Bench improve Qwen3-VL-8B by 4.3% and SenseNova-SI-1.3 by 1.3%, while achieving state-of-the-art performance on object size (79.2%) and room size estimation (75.7%). These results demonstrate that continuous spatial tokens provide an effective, interpretable, and computationally efficient mechanism for integrating geometric reasoning into large vision-language models.

</details>


### [12] [TRACE-GS: On-Policy Trajectory Distillation with Privileged Geometric Conditioning for Sparse-View 3DGS Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10286)
*Linlian Jiang,Yuchen Xi,Sadman Rakib Pinon,Ruigang Yang,Yang Wang,Xinxin Zuo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TRACE-GS introduces on-policy trajectory distillation with privileged geometric conditioning during training to adapt a diffusion prior for sparse-view 3DGS restoration; it aligns denoising with the student's rollout using training-time privileged information, improving generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion-based restoration trained on independently noised states does not cover inference states, leading to compounding errors in under-constrained sparse-view 3D geometry.

Method: A teacher conditioned on richer geometry from additional training views provides targets along the sparse-view student's rollout. Training-time privileged information (LUPI) guides denoising directions and cross-view responses; deployment uses only the sparse-view student.

Result: Consistent performance gains and strong generalization across datasets and sparse-view configurations; first to use on-policy supervision from privileged geometry for sparse-view 3DGS restoration.

Conclusion: On-policy privileged information during training improves diffusion-based sparse-view 3DGS restoration; TRACE-GS demonstrates effective LUPI-based trajectory distillation for this task.

Abstract: We present TRACE-GS, an on-policy trajectory distillation framework that leverages privileged geometric conditioning at training time, thereby adapting a diffusion prior to sparse-view 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) restoration. Rather than pursuing increasingly sophisticated restoration architectures, we identify a more fundamental limitation shared by existing diffusion-based approaches: supervision at independently noised states does not cover those reached during inference. In sparse-view 3DGS, under-constrained geometry biases denoising from the outset, and the resulting deviations compound along the rollout. TRACE-GS instead performs on-policy trajectory distillation: a teacher conditioned on richer geometry from additional training views supplies targets along the sparse-view student's own rollout, aligning denoising directions and cross-view responses at each visited state. This training-only geometry places TRACE-GS in the learning using privileged information (LUPI) setting. At deployment, only the sparse-view student is retained, and its restored renderings serve as pseudo-observations for 3DGS refinement. To the best of our knowledge, TRACE-GS is the first to derive on-policy supervision from privileged geometry for sparse-view 3DGS restoration, achieving consistent gains and strong generalization across datasets and sparse-view settings.

</details>


### [13] [SeFaR: Semantic Feature-aware Robustness Testing of Deep Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10289)
*Nusrat Jahan Mozumder,Divya Gopinath,Corina Pasareanu,Matthew Dwyer*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SeFaR is a semantic-feature-centric testing framework for vision models that evaluates robustness to semantics-preserving perturbations guided by high-level NL requirements. It uses a hierarchical concept model and diffusion/vision-language models to generate realistic variations, with a feedback-driven process to surface failure-inducing concepts and inputs; it identifies features that influence decisions beyond requirement satisfaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Perception models in safety-critical domains are susceptible to failures in rare, underrepresented scenarios. Current evaluation often misses semantics-level robustness and alignment with high-level requirements.

Method: Introduce a hierarchical concept model enabling structured feature-space exploration and domain knowledge via user-defined concepts. Leverage state-of-the-art diffusion and vision-language models to generate photorealistic, semantics-preserving perturbations. Implement a feedback-driven adaptive loop to extract interpretable failure-inducing semantic concepts and corresponding test inputs.

Result: Empirically shows that SeFaR can satisfy requirement preconditions while uncovering requirement-independent features that influence model decisions, enabling fault discovery and attribution to specific features.

Conclusion: SeFaR provides a systematic, semantics-aware testing framework for vision models, enabling identification of latent features affecting behavior and improving reliability in safety-critical perception tasks.

Abstract: Deep neural networks are increasingly deployed in safety-critical domains as perception modules, where failures are often caused due to rare and under-represented scenarios. This necessitates the need to evaluate the semantic robustness of perception models; conformance of behavior to high-level requirements over real-world perceptual variability. To address this, we propose SeFaR, a framework for systematic semantic-feature-centric testing of vision models. Given a natural-language requirement and a set of satisfying inputs, SeFaR evaluates robustness with respect to diverse realistic semantic variations that preserve requirement satisfaction. The approach employs a novel hierarchical concept model enabling structured exploration of the feature space and incorporation of domain knowledge via user-defined concepts. State-of-the-art diffusion and vision-language models are leveraged to generate photorealistic semantics-preserving perturbations and identification of previously unknown features impacting behavior. A feedback-driven adaptive process is adopted to generate interpretable failure-inducing semantic concepts along with corresponding test inputs. Evaluation on case studies demonstrates that the proposed framework effectively satisfies requirement preconditions while identifying requirement-independent features that influence model decisions, enabling it to both uncover faults and relate them to such features.

</details>


### [14] [MRIComp4Flow: Compression of 3D Brain MRI for Training Multi-Modal Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10291)
*Lisa K. Fischer,Mykhailo Riabets,Daniel Rueckert,Benedikt Wiestler,Anke Meyer-Baese,Sandeep Nagar*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: JPEG2000/near-lossless JPEG-LS at 20:1 preserves 3D MRI synthesis quality for a Wavelet Flow Matching model; PSNR/SSIM margins are within ~1 dB and 0.02, enabling scalable training without noticeable degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-scale multi-modal MRI caches impose storage and I/O costs that hinder training of 3D generative models. It is unclear whether lossy compression, known to preserve discriminative task performance, can also preserve the full data distribution needed for generative modeling.

Method: Compress each 3D brain MRI volume with JPEG2000 or near-lossless JPEG-LS (20:1). Train a Wavelet Flow Matching model conditioned on BraTS sequences (T1n, T1c, T2, T2f) on the compressed data. Evaluate on the validation set and compare to a model trained on uncompressed data using a paired equivalence test (TOST) for PSNR/SSIM.

Result: At 20:1 compression, synthesis quality is statistically equivalent to training on uncompressed data: mean PSNR 27.3 dB vs. 27.0 dB; mean SSIM 0.95 vs. 0.96 across modalities; ΔPSNR < 1 dB and ΔSSIM < 0.02; paired TOST significance p-value indicated by placeholder [[p]].

Conclusion: JPEG2000 compression is a practical step toward scalable 3D MRI generative modeling without degrading synthesis quality; supports use of standard codecs for compressing MRI data prior to generative modeling. Code available at the provided GitHub link.

Abstract: Large-scale multi-modal MRI datasets impose substantial storage and I/O costs, limiting the training of 3D generative models on commodity infrastructure. While lossy compression is known to preserve accuracy for discriminative segmentation networks, its effect on generative models, which must learn the full data distribution rather than a decision boundary, is unexplored. We study whether standard image codecs can effectively compress semantically rich brain tumor MRI while preserving the fidelity required to train and deploy a 3D MRI generative model. Each 3D volume is compressed with JPEG2000 or a near-lossless JPEG-LS pipeline. Next, a Wavelet Flow Matching model, conditioned on BraTS image sequences (T1n, T1c, T2, T2f), is trained on compressed data, and the resulting models are evaluated on the validation set. At a 20:1 compression ratio, synthesis quality is statistically equivalent to a model trained on uncompressed data within a pre-specified margin ($Δ$PSNR $<1$,dB, $Δ$SSIM $<0.02$; paired TOST $p=[[p]]$): mean PSNR is 27.3,dB vs. 27.0,dB and mean SSIM is 0.95 vs. 0.96 across modalities. Our results indicate that JPEG2000 compression is a practical step toward scalable 3D MRI generative modeling without degrading synthesis quality. The codebase is available at https://github.com/lisafis/MRIComp4Flow .

</details>


### [15] [Frozen Brain-MRI Foundation Models Are Site Fingerprints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10295)
*Saman Rahbar*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Frozen foundation-model embeddings for brain-MRI encode a strong site signal that is intrinsic and linearly decodable, undermining the assumption that they primarily reflect anatomy; site information can be removed post-hoc but with trade-offs, raising privacy/federated concerns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Audit what frozen brain-MRI FMs actually encode to understand risks, biases, and guide safe usage.

Method: Cross-cohort analysis (ABIDE-I/II) using three frozen 3D encoders (brain-pretrained, CT-pretrained, randomly initialized) across network depths; assess linear decodability of site and clinical/demographic variables; compare with raw images; residualize measured covariates; nonlinear probing; site removal via iterative null-space projection or ComBat; evaluate impact on dense segmentation; analyze entanglement between site and anatomy.

Result: Site is a large, intrinsic component; decodable ~0.9 balanced accuracy in deep layers; even random encoders yield ~0.9; site decodability ~0.95 directly from raw downsampled image without an encoder; fingerprint reflects low-level image statistics preserved by any encoder; residualizing covariates leaves decodability unchanged; nonlinear probe agrees with linear; site subspace removable post hoc (ComBat, null-space) but removal is not free for segmentation due to entanglement with anatomy; raises site attribution/privacy concerns for shared/federated embeddings; open audit toolkit recommended.

Conclusion: Auditing frozen brain-MRI foundation models for site-dependence is essential; site removal is possible but incurs costs for certain tasks; encourages site-audited deployment and release of audit toolkit.

Abstract: Frozen foundation-model (FM) embeddings are increasingly used as off-the-shelf brain-MRI representations, on the assumption that they capture anatomy. We audit what they actually encode and find that acquisition site is a large, intrinsic component of the representation. Across two independent cohorts (ABIDE-I, ABIDE-II), three frozen 3-D encoders (brain-pretrained, CT-pretrained, and randomly initialized), and every network depth, site is linearly decodable at roughly 0.9 balanced accuracy at deep layers, exceeding the decodability of every clinical or demographic variable (sex, age, autism diagnosis) at every layer. The effect is intrinsic rather than learned: a randomly initialized encoder is already a ~0.9 site classifier on both cohorts and across three architecture families (Swin, ViT, ResNet), and site is decodable at ~0.95 directly from the raw downsampled image with no encoder, so the fingerprint reflects low-level image statistics that any encoder preserves rather than a product of pretraining. Residualizing measured population covariates leaves site decodability essentially unchanged, indicating an acquisition- rather than population-driven effect. A nonlinear probe matches the linear one, so the fingerprint is fully linearly accessible. The site subspace is removable post hoc by iterative null-space projection or ComBat (site decodability 0.94 -> 0.07/0.00), and is a site-attribution concern for shared or federated embeddings; but for dense segmentation this removal is not free, because site and anatomy occupy an entangled linear subspace (a matched-rank random-direction projection is Dice-neutral, whereas removing the site subspace is destructive). We recommend site-audited use of frozen brain-MRI FMs and release an open audit toolkit.

</details>


### [16] [UniMod: Enhancing Multi-Modal Medical Diagnosis through Cross-Modality and Within-Modality Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10316)
*Zijian Gu,Weikai Lin,Shuang Zhou,Zihan Chen,Song Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: UniMod mitigates shortcut learning in multimodal medical diagnosis by enforcing modality-specific predictions, cross-modality alignment, and within-modality supervised contrastive alignment, achieving superior AUC on Harvard-Glaucoma (0.850) and CheXpert Plus (0.966) and extending to 5-class multi-label diagnosis without changing architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard multimodal training often relies on the easier modality (e.g., text cues) and ignores harder-to-learn visual patterns, leading to shortcut learning and suboptimal joint representations. There is a need to ensure both image and text modalities contribute meaningfully to diagnosis.

Method: UniMod requires each modality to predict the diagnosis alone, with image-only, text-only, and multi-modal supervision. It introduces cross-modality alignment for knowledge transfer and within-modality supervised contrastive alignment across patients with the same diagnosis.

Result: Empirical gains on two datasets: Harvard-Glaucoma: AUC 0.850, outperforming OGM-GE and Gradient Blending by 1.6–1.8%; CheXpert Plus: AUC 0.966, surpassing baselines by over 5%. Extends to 5-class multi-label diagnosis without architectural changes, increasing mean AUC by 0.097 over CGGM.

Conclusion: UniMod effectively mitigates shortcut learning in multimodal medical diagnosis, leveraging per-modality supervision, cross-modal transfer, and intra-modality contrastive alignment to achieve stronger diagnostic performance and extensibility to multi-label tasks without changing architecture.

Abstract: Multi-modal learning combining medical images and clinical text is promising for disease diagnosis. However, standard multi-modal training leads to shortcut learning: models exploit the easier modality (e.g., diagnostic cues in text) while neglecting harder-to-learn features (e.g., subtle visual patterns). We propose UniMod, a framework that mitigates shortcut learning by requiring each modality to predict the diagnosis on its own. It supervises image-only, text-only, and multi-modal classification simultaneously, so each modality must extract diagnostic features. We add cross-modality alignment for knowledge transfer and within-modality supervised contrastive alignment over same-diagnosis patients. On Harvard-Glaucoma, UniMod reaches 0.850 AUC, outperforming OGM-GE and Gradient Blending by 1.6-1.8%; on CheXpert Plus, it reaches 0.966 AUC, surpassing them by over 5%. UniMod also extends to 5-class multi-label diagnosis without architectural change, improving mean AUC by 0.097 over CGGM.

</details>


### [17] [From Detection to Understanding: TAR and TAR-Bench for Multi-Task Traffic Anomaly Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10317)
*Han Zhang,Yilin Zhao,Zaid Pervaiz Bhat,Zheng Tang,Varun Praveen,Vidya N. Murali,David C. Anastasiu,Tomasz Kornuta*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces TAR and TAR-Bench: a dataset and evaluation benchmark for training/evaluating video-language models on reasoning tasks beyond anomaly detection, featuring large-scale chain-of-thought annotations and multi-task evaluation across 10 tasks; reveals QA accuracy is not a reliable proxy for reasoning; demonstrates gains from multi-task fine-tuning and establishes AI City Challenge 2026 Track 3 baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between anomaly-detection-focused evaluation and genuine temporal and scene reasoning in video-language models. Current QA performance does not reliably reflect reasoning ability; need structured, multi-task, domain-specific data and evaluation.

Method: Construct TAR with 44,040 chain-of-thought training annotations over 3,670 CCTV videos from eight datasets across 10 tasks; create TAR-Bench with 960 human-curated test annotations for 80 held-out clips from 17 YouTube videos. Use MAVEN to convert multi-scale video evidence into structured event descriptions before generating Q&A pairs and reasoning traces. Evaluate 11 vision-language models; perform multi-task fine-tuning on TAR across 10 tasks.

Result: Multi-task fine-tuning on TAR yields a 21.4-point aggregate improvement over zero-shot baselines for the full 10-task model. TAR-Bench results show that strong QA accuracy does not reliably predict temporal or scene reasoning ability. TAR and TAR-Bench serve as official training/evaluation data for AI City Challenge 2026 Track 3.

Conclusion: TAR and TAR-Bench provide a robust resource for training and evaluating video-language reasoning beyond simple QA. They reveal limitations of QA-based metrics as proxies for reasoning and demonstrate the efficacy of multi-task learning across diverse tasks. The datasets establish a domain-specific benchmark, with broad potential for future research in reasoning-rich video-language models.

Abstract: We present TAR (Traffic Anomaly Reasoning) and TAR-Bench datasets, resources for training and evaluating video-language models beyond anomaly detection. TAR contains 44,040 chain-of-thought training annotations across 10 tasks for 3,670 CCTV videos ($\sim$26 hours) from eight public datasets. Its evaluation component, TAR-Bench, contains 960 human-curated test annotations for 80 held-out clips trimmed from 17 public YouTube videos. TAR's training annotations are produced with MAVEN, which consolidates multi-scale video evidence into structured event descriptions before generating question-answer pairs and reasoning traces. On TAR-Bench, eleven vision-language models reveal that strong question-answering accuracy does not reliably predict temporal or scene reasoning ability. Multi-task fine-tuning on TAR yields consistent gains, with the full 10-task model improving aggregate score by 21.4 points over its zero-shot baseline. TAR and TAR-Bench provide the official training and in-domain evaluation data for AI City Challenge 2026 Track 3. The dataset is available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/nvidia/PhysicalAI-Traffic-Anomaly-Reasoning

</details>


### [18] [ENCORE: Efficient Noise Context-Aware Representation for Low-Dose CT Denoising](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10343)
*Minwoo Yu,N. Robert Bennett,Jongduk Baek,Adam S. Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ENCORE introduces a context-aware CT denoising framework that models realistic non-Gaussian, non-stationary noise, uses local noise context and a FlyingConv module to adapt filters per region, and enables zero-shot conditional denoising via noise-context manipulation, achieving better denoising and efficiency with an open-source implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CT scans exhibit non-stationary, spatially correlated noise that deviates from Gaussian assumptions used in many denoisers; capturing noise statistics and anatomical context can improve denoising performance.

Method: 1) Reformulate noise synthesis using a realistic noise distribution beyond Gaussian. 2) Extract local noise power and correlation contexts to guide denoising. 3) Introduce FlyingConv that adaptively changes convolution weights per image region. 4) Integrate noise-context maps for conditional denoising, enabling inference-time manipulation for zero-shot control.

Result: Reported substantial gains in denoising quality and computational efficiency. Demonstrated dynamic, zero-shot control over texture via noise-context intensity. Code is available at the provided GitHub URL.

Conclusion: ENCORE effectively leverages CT noise characteristics and anatomical features to improve denoising, with a flexible framework enabling conditional control and offering a publicly accessible implementation.

Abstract: While deep learning-based denoising has become widely adopted in low-dose CT, conventional models use generic architectures designed for natural images, failing to account for non-stationary and spatially correlated CT noise characteristics. To address this, we propose an Efficient Noise COntext-aware REpresentation (ENCORE) framework that explicitly leverages CT noise characteristics and anatomical features. First, we reformulate the noise synthesis procedure based on a realistic noise distribution beyond the conventional Gaussian approximation, establishing a rigorous foundation for training pair generation. Next, we extract local noise power and correlation contexts to guide the denoising process. To fully leverage the potential of noise context, we propose a FlyingConv module, which adaptively changes convolution weights for each local image region. Notably, our approach demonstrates substantial gains in both denoising quality and computational efficiency. Furthermore, manipulating the intensity of the noise context maps at inference time enables zero-shot conditional denoising, allowing for dynamic control over the output image texture. The entire pipeline is available at https://github.com/minwoo-yu/ENCORE.git

</details>


### [19] [CasDeblurGS: Cascaded 2D-to-3D Multi-View Consistency for 3D Gaussian Splatting from Two Blurry Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10345)
*Haeyun Choi,Minhyuk Jang,I-Gil Kim*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CasDeblurGS presents a cascaded framework to reconstruct coherent 3D scenes from only two motion-blurred images with known intrinsics, without input-view poses or per-scene optimization, by progressively building locally reliable 2D guidance and a pose-free 3D Gaussian representation for dense global guidance, enabling improved novel-view synthesis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address practical capture constraints of severe view sparsity and motion blur without input poses, auxiliary sharp images, or per-scene optimization, aiming for high-quality free-viewpoint rendering from minimal data.

Method: Stage 1: occlusion-aware 2D correspondence filtering to derive locally reliable guidance. Stage 2: aggregate restorations into a provisional pose-free 3D Gaussian representation; input-view re-renderings provide dense global guidance for final restoration. The framework uses only two blurred images with known intrinsics and avoids per-scene test-time optimization.

Result: Consistent PSNR gains on Deblur-NeRF scenes: +1.19 dB (real-world) and +2.11 dB (synthetic). Ablations, cross-view correspondence visualization, and camera reprojection analyses show improvements in rendering quality and multi-view geometric consistency.

Conclusion: CasDeblurGS effectively leverages a cascaded, pose-free 3D representation to recover reliable cross-view information from extreme blur and view sparsity, enabling higher-quality free-viewpoint synthesis without input poses or per-scene optimization.

Abstract: Free-viewpoint 3D scene media is increasingly important for immersive applications, yet practical capture often suffers from severe view sparsity and motion blur. Although neural rendering has advanced sparse-view synthesis, existing blur-aware methods typically require substantial multi-view redundancy, accurate camera poses, or costly per-scene optimization. We address a stringent yet practical setting: reconstructing a coherent 3D scene from only two motion-blurred images with known intrinsics, without input-view poses, auxiliary sharp images, or per-scene test-time optimization. To this end, we propose CasDeblurGS, a cascaded framework that progressively recovers reliable cross-view information from local 2D correspondences to global 3D guidance. Stage 1 constructs locally reliable guidance through occlusion-aware correspondence filtering, while Stage 2 aggregates the intermediate restorations into a provisional pose-free 3D Gaussian representation whose input-view re-renders provide dense global guidance for final restoration. The resulting views enable a more coherent 3D representation and higher-quality novel-view synthesis. Experiments on real-world and synthetic Deblur-NeRF scenes show consistent gains over strong baselines, improving PSNR by 1.19 dB and 2.11 dB, respectively. Progressive ablations, cross-view correspondence visualization, and camera reprojection analysis further demonstrate improvements in both rendering quality and multi-view geometric consistency.

</details>


### [20] [Towards Unified Dynamic Face Landmark Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10346)
*Sebastian Regalado,Varshanth R. Rao,Ruowei Jiang,Parham Aarabi,Igor Gilitschenski*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A unified, query-driven face landmark detector that uses Face Part-Anchored Landmark Positions (FPALPs) to unify N-point datasets and predict any number of landmarks with a single model and runtime-loaded queries; achieves competitive results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current FLD approaches require training separate models per dataset and output a fixed N; this limits scalability and cross-dataset transfer.

Method: Propose FPALP representation; each landmark is a progression [0,1] along a face part contour; use FPALP-based queries refined via cross-modality decoder; at inference, load the desired landmark queries to predict coordinates; train on multiple N-point datasets with a single model.

Result: Experiments on multiple benchmarks show the approach remains competitive with, and sometimes surpasses, state-of-the-art methods, while enabling flexible or multiple landmark outputs.

Conclusion: Unified Dynamic FLD offers dataset-agnostic landmark representation and scalable, flexible landmark prediction without dataset-specific retraining.

Abstract: Although advancements in face landmark detection (FLD) methods continue to push performance boundaries, they overlook two major functional limitations: (1) different network parameters need to be trained independently for each ``$N$-point'' benchmark dataset, and (2) a model trained on an ``$N$-point'' dataset reliably outputs only the $N$ landmarks. In our work, we first conceptualize Face Part-Anchored Landmark Positions (FPALPs), wherein each landmark is treated as a progression value between zero (start) and one (end) along a face part's contour. Every landmark can be expressed in the FPALP format, irrespective of its source dataset, hence unlocking the ability to unify all ``$N$-point'' datasets into a single dataset. Secondly, we represent each landmark with an FPALP-based query, refine it progressively with a cross-modality decoder, and predict its coordinates based on the final representation. Our approach, called Unified Dynamic FLD, embodies these two design choices and streamlines the landmark detection pipeline by enabling (1) a single model to learn on any number of ``$N$-point'' datasets, and (2) yield any number of specific landmark predictions by loading the designated landmark queries at runtime. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmark datasets show that our method delivers these benefits while remaining competitive with, and in several cases outperforming existing state-of-the-art methods.

</details>


### [21] [FormStruct-Bench:A Hierarchical and Diagnostic Benchmark for Table-Form Document Structure Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10396)
*Lujie Ban,Jiangtao Zhu,Yuanheng Yu,Jiasheng Shi,Chenhao Ma*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FormStruct-Bench provides hierarchical, diagnostic evaluation for table-form document structure recognition, enabling tracing performance to specific structural failure modes instead of only holistic accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks offer coarse, end-to-end scores and miss where structural failures occur. A multilevel, auditable benchmark is needed to diagnose structure-recovery errors in table-form documents.

Method: Create FormStruct-Bench with 70 reusable templates expanded to 7,000 verified instances via a Director–Artist–Verifier pipeline; 1,100 template-disjoint test instances with human review. Evaluate using five primary metrics and three structure-specific diagnostics across page, schema, and component levels, plus slices by difficulty, structural constraints, and visual degradation. Assess 14 API-hosted/local systems and two SFT variants.

Result: Best document-level score: 83.85%. Best fine-grained structural score: below 18%. Demonstrates a substantial gap between reading content and recovering the document hierarchy and regional organization.

Conclusion: FormStruct-Bench enables diagnosis of structure-recognition performance at multiple granularities and guides the development of more structure-aware models for reliable table-form understanding.

Abstract: Transforming table-form documents into machine-processable records requires recovering not only their visible content but also the multilevel structure that organizes it. However, existing benchmarks evaluate either holistic document outputs or conventional table grids, and their aggregate scores provide little insight into where structural failures occur. We introduce FormStruct-Bench, a hierarchical and diagnostic benchmark that evaluates table-form document structure recognition at both the document level and progressively finer component levels, allowing aggregate performance to be traced back to specific structural failure modes. To construct auditable ground truth at scale, we annotate 70 reusable templates and expand them into 7,000 verified instances through a provenance-preserving Director--Artist--Verifier pipeline; all 1,100 instances in the template-disjoint test set additionally receive human review. Our evaluation protocol uses five primary metrics and three structure-specific diagnostics across page, schema, and component levels, together with slices over difficulty, structural constraints, and visual degradation. Across 14 API-hosted and locally deployable systems plus two SFT variants, the best document-level score reaches 83.85%, whereas the best reported fine-grained structural score remains below 18%. These results reveal a pronounced gap between reading document content and recovering the hierarchy and regional organization required for reliable table-form understanding.

</details>


### [22] [A second-order theory of texture for depth from focus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10411)
*Sreekar Ranganathan,Ioannis Gkioulekas*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A wave-optics theory explains textured appearance on optically rough surfaces via second-order speckle texture, enabling passive depth-from-focus on textureless scenes; applying a narrowband spectral filter enhances the texture contrast under ambient light, yielding substantial gains in depth reconstruction, supported by theory, simulations, and real-world experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the limitation of textureless scenes in passive depth-from-focus by leveraging subjetive speckle texture arising from microgeometry, and show that spectral filtering can amplify this cue under natural lighting.

Method: Develop a wave-optics framework to analyze second-order texture from surface microgeometry, quantify its properties, and demonstrate that narrowband filtering increases texture contrast; validate through theoretical derivations, simulations, and real-world experiments.

Result: Narrowband spectral filtering under natural lighting enhances the second-order texture contrast, leading to dramatic improvements in passive depth reconstruction for scenes previously deemed textureless; results backed by theory, simulations, and experiments.

Conclusion: Subjective speckle-based texture on rough surfaces can be exploited for passive depth sensing, and simple spectral filtering is an effective method to boost depth-from-focus performance in textureless scenes.

Abstract: We present a theory of textured appearance of optically rough surfaces based on wave optics, emphasizing the role of texture for passive depth from focus. Our theory shows that even surfaces that traditional computer vision would consider textureless can produce textured appearance, due to subjective speckle from surface microgeometry. We analyze the properties of this second-order texture, and show that we can enhance its contrast under natural ambient lighting by simply using a narrowband spectral filter. Doing so results in dramatic improvements in passive depth reconstruction of seemingly textureless scenes, as we demonstrate through extensive theory, simulations, and real-world experiments.

</details>


### [23] [DriveVLA-M0: Failure-Aware Memory Augmentation for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10413)
*Zebin Xing,Yupeng Zheng,Qiang Chen,Linbo Wang,Yichen Zhang,Pengxuan Yang,Junli Wang,Deheng Qian,Xiaoqing Ye,Junyu Han,Yifeng Pan,Qichao Zhang,Dongbin Zhao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DriveVLA-M0 introduces retrieval-augmented VLA with failure-aware latent memory and a LoRA-based test-time training mechanism to inject retrieved failure cases, achieving strong NAVSIM benchmarks with low latency and scalable memory expansion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address persistent failure cases and distribution shifts in Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models for end-to-end autonomous driving by leveraging past failures through structured memory and retrieval.

Method: Construct a latent memory pool of failure cases with scene representations and expert trajectories; implement a dedicated Retrieve Model that decouples static road structure from dynamic agent interactions for structurally grounded retrieval; perform lightweight, decoupled LoRA-based test-time training to inject retrieved cases without backbone modification.

Result: Outperform previous methods on NAVSIMv1/v2: 94.1 PDMS on Navtest and 47.0 EPDMS on Navhard; 26.44 ms TTT backward latency; memory expansion yields training-free performance gains; code available at GitHub.

Conclusion: DriveVLA-M0 demonstrates effective, scalable retrieval-augmented VLA with failure-aware memory and test-time adaptation, achieving strong results with minimal latency and enabling memory-driven performance gains.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have recently emerged as a promising paradigm for end-to-end autonomous driving by enabling unified reasoning across perception, language, and planning. However, existing approaches lack mechanisms to exploit past failures or adapt to distribution shifts, causing the model to persistently underperform on similar scenarios where it has previously failed. In this paper, we propose DriveVLA-M0, a retrieval-augmented VLA with failure-aware latent memory. We construct a latent memory pool that stores failure cases along with their structure scene representations and expert trajectory labels, and design a dedicated Retrieve Model that decouples static road structure and dynamic agent interactions to enable structurally grounded retrieval. At inference time, retrieved cases are injected into the model via a lightweight decoupled LoRA-based test-time training (TTT) mechanism, allowing targeted and scenario-specific correction without modifying the backbone. Extensive experiments on NAVSIMv1 and NAVSIMv2 benchmark demonstrate that our approach consistently outperforms prior methods, achieving 94.1 PDMS on Navtest and 47.0 EPDMS on Navhard with only 26.44 ms TTT backward latency overhead. Furthermore, we show that DriveVLA-M0 scales effectively with additional memory, enabling training-free performance gains through memory expansion. The code is available at https://github.com/ZebinX/DriveVLA-M0.

</details>


### [24] [GeoSeg-OV: Bridging Geospatial Gaps with Structural Guidance for Open-Vocabulary Remote Sensing Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10426)
*Ruizhong Liu,Tingzhang Luo,Zaiyan Zhang,Jundong Chen,Hongruixuan Chen,Shaoguang Huang,Hongyan Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GeoSeg-OV decouples auxiliary vision foundation model (VFM) features from visual-text matching and repurposes them as structural guidance for open-vocabulary remote sensing segmentation. It builds an orientation-robust cost volume from multi-rotation CLIP features, uses a frozen VFM for multi-scale structure features, and introduces Structure-Guided Aggregation (SGA) and Cost-Aware Decoding (CAD) to drive coherent spatial propagation and text-conditioned reasoning. It achieves state-of-the-art on HRLC and shows strong zero-shot generalization across domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geospatial data exhibit domain shifts due to heterogeneous regions, resolutions, and acquisition platforms, which degrade visual-text alignment and cross-dataset generalization in open-vocabulary segmentation. Existing methods fuse VFM features with text embeddings, but this can introduce inconsistent signals and underutilize structure-sensitive cues.

Method: 1) Construct an orientation-robust cost volume from multi-rotation CLIP features. 2) Freeze a vision foundation model to extract multi-scale, structure-sensitive features in parallel. 3) Propose Structure-Guided Aggregation (SGA) to combine cost tokens, CLIP guidance, and VFM-derived structural biases for spatial propagation and text-conditioned class reasoning. 4) Propose Cost-Aware Decoding (CAD) to adaptively refine and fuse multi-scale semantic and structural guidance based on current decoder context.

Result: On the global High-Resolution Land Cover (HRLC) benchmark spanning seven datasets across six continents, GeoSeg-OV outperforms the state-of-the-art by +2.5 and +2.7 average mIoU under two training settings. A large-scale zero-shot case study demonstrates strong cross-domain and cross-category generalization without target-domain annotations or retraining.

Conclusion: GeoSeg-OV demonstrates that decoupling auxiliary VFM features from direct visual-text matching and repurposing them as structural guidance yields coherent spatial propagation and robust open-vocabulary segmentation across diverse geospatial datasets, with strong cross-domain generalization.

Abstract: Open-vocabulary remote sensing segmentation has recently emerged as a promising paradigm that enables pixel-level recognition of arbitrary categories specified by natural language, including classes unseen during training. However, geospatial domain shifts caused by heterogeneous regions, spatial resolutions, and acquisition platforms weaken visual-text matching and limit cross-dataset generalization. Recent attempts have begun to incorporate auxiliary vision foundation models (VFMs), typically coupling their features with text embeddings as additional matching evidence. However, this strategy may introduce inconsistent matching signals while leaving the structure-sensitive representations of VFMs insufficiently exploited. We therefore propose GeoSeg-OV, which decouples auxiliary VFM features from visual-text matching and repurposes them as structural guidance for cost aggregation and decoding. GeoSeg-OV constructs an orientation-robust cost volume from multi-rotation CLIP features, while a frozen VFM extracts multi-scale structure-sensitive features in parallel. We propose Structure-Guided Aggregation (SGA), which integrates cost tokens and CLIP semantic guidance with VFM-derived pairwise structural biases for coherent spatial propagation, followed by text-conditioned class-wise reasoning. We further introduce Cost-Aware Decoding (CAD) to adaptively refine and fuse multi-scale semantic and structural guidance based on the current decoder context. On the global High-Resolution Land Cover (HRLC) benchmark spanning seven datasets across six continents, GeoSeg-OV outperforms the state-of-the-art by +2.5 and +2.7 average mIoU under two training settings. A large-scale zero-shot case study further demonstrates its generalization across geographic domains and category systems without target-domain annotations or retraining.

</details>


### [25] [Lesion-Aware Adaptive Fourier Neural Operator for CT-to-PSMA PET Synthesis in Prostate Cancer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10429)
*Rashmi Bhaskara,Waleed M. Almutairi,Matthew Gopaulchan,Maram Musaad Alqurashi,Francis Asamoah,Alex Ocana,Clinton D. Bahler,Oluwaseyi M. Oderinde*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes LAFNO, a lesion-aware adaptive Fourier Neural Operator for CT-to-PSMA-PET synthesis that uses two CT-derived proxy channels to inject lesion-level information, improving lesion activity fidelity while preserving whole-volume quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Global loss objectives (L1/MSE) underweight tumor lesions in whole-body PSMA-PET reconstruction; radiomics-guided conditioning is effective but slow due to feature extraction from delineated lesions. There is a need for efficient lesion-aware conditioning that enhances clinically relevant lesion fidelity.

Method: Introduce LAFNO, which replaces radiomics conditioning with two CT-derived proxy channels (contrast proxy for local density variation and disorder proxy for local texture heterogeneity) injected into the model bottleneck of a Fourier Neural Operator architecture. The model performs whole-volume reconstruction while incorporating lesion-level supervision: total lesion activity (TLA), tumor-core contrast, and peritumoral supervision. Evaluated against four baselines on the TCIA PSMA-PET-CT-Lesions dataset.

Result: LAFNO achieves competitive whole-volume image quality (SSIM 0.960 for 18F-PSMA and 0.938 for 68Ga-PSMA) and substantially reduces per-patient TLA error (by 48.3% for 18F and 64.0% for 68Ga). It also yields the highest tumor-core radiomics reproducibility across feature classes for both tracers; peritumoral reproducibility remains tracer-dependent.

Conclusion: Lesion-aware conditioning via CT-derived proxies enables accurate lesion activity representation within a unified reconstruction framework, achieving strong tumor-core fidelity and competitive whole-volume quality, though biological fidelity in synthetic PSMA-PET remains challenging, particularly for peritumoral regions.

Abstract: Deep learning models that synthesize PET from CT or MRI can reduce patient dose and scanner demand, but are typically optimized with global losses such as L1 or mean squared error (MSE) that treat all voxels similarly. In whole-body PSMA-PET, tumor voxels occupy only a small fraction of the volume, yet carry the clinically relevant activity signal; as a result, models can achieve high structural similarity index measure (SSIM) and peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) while still underestimating lesion activity or failing to preserve tumor-specific structure. Radiomics provides biologically meaningful descriptors of tumor intensity and texture, but direct radiomics conditioning is time-consuming because it requires feature extraction from delineated lesion regions. We propose LAFNO, a Lesion-Aware Adaptive Fourier Neural Operator for CT-to-PSMA-PET synthesis that replaces high-dimensional radiomics conditioning with two efficient CT-derived proxy channels. Motivated by radiomics analysis of PSMA-avid tumor core and peritumoral regions, LAFNO uses a contrast proxy for local density variation and a disorder proxy for local texture heterogeneity, both injected into the model bottleneck. LAFNO combines whole-volume reconstruction with lesion-level total lesion activity (TLA), tumor-core contrast, and peritumoral supervision. We evaluated LAFNO against four baseline architectures on the TCIA PSMA-PET-CT-Lesions dataset. LAFNO remained competitive on whole-volume image quality, achieving SSIM of 0.960 and 0.938 for 18F- and 68Ga-PSMA, respectively, while reducing per-patient TLA error to 48.3% and 64.0% for 18F- and 68Ga-PSMA, respectively, and achieving the highest tumor-core radiomics reproducibility across all feature classes for both tracers. Peritumoral reproducibility remained tracer-dependent, indicating that biological fidelity in synthetic PSMA-PET remains challenging.

</details>


### [26] [DynaPPI: A Large-scale Dynamic Protein Dataset for AI-driven Advances in Protein Interactomics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10435)
*Jiabao Wei,Zilong Geng,Yuze Wang,Jianjun Li,Ning Ding,Bowen Zhou,Bing Zhang,Zhiyuan Ma*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DynaPPI is a dynamic MD-based protein complex formation dataset enabling diffusion models to learn assembly dynamics and predict unknown complexes, bridging static structures and temporal interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the gap between static snapshots/single-entity trajectories and the dynamic process of multi-chain complex formation; provides a resource to enable AI-driven structural biology and protein interactomics.

Method: Construct MD trajectories of protein dissociation to bound complex; train diffusion models on these dynamic trajectories to learn binding pathways and generate novel complex structures; position the dataset as a resource to enable dynamic prediction of unknown complexes.

Result: Claims that diffusion models can explicitly learn dynamic binding trajectories from the dataset and accurately predict structures of unknown complexes, leveraging diverse generative properties; positions DynaPPI as a pivotal resource.

Conclusion: DynaPPI bridges static structural biology and the temporal nature of molecular interactions, catalyzing AI-driven structural biology and protein interactomics.

Abstract: Diffusion models have been widely explored in protein backbone generation due to their powerful generation capabilities.However, in today's AI-driven biological research, predicting the structure of unknown multi-chain protein aggregates (called "complexes" in biology) remains an unsolved challenge.This is because existing static or dynamic protein datasets focus solely on static snapshots or single-entity trajectories, neglecting the dynamic process of multiple monomers forming complexes.To alleviate this dilemma, we present DynaPPI, a dynamic protein dataset comprising molecular dynamics (MD) trajectories of protein complex formation from dissociated chains to the bound state, as a pivotal resource to bridge the gap between static structural biology and the inherently temporal nature of dynamic molecular interactions.Benefiting from this dataset, diffusion models can explicitly learn the dynamic binding trajectories of known complexes and accurately predict the structures of unknown complexes based on their diverse generative properties, thereby further catalyzing AI-driven structural biology and protein interactomics.

</details>


### [27] [MammoMix: Leveraging Mixture of Experts for Robust Mammogram Breast Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10437)
*Dinh Tan Nguyen,Hoang Quan Dang,Chen Zhang,Sai Ho Ling*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A Mixture-of-Experts framework (MammoMix) for robust cross-domain lesion detection in mammography, with domain-specific experts, adaptive gating, and a calibration module (MoCAE); evaluated on CSAW, DDSM, and DMID, showing improved average precision and reliability over baselines, especially under high variability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
