As an application, we consider a cooperative classification task in the context of semantic communication, under model and data heterogeneity. Our results show that Sheaf-FRL outperforms baseline approaches in terms of local and post-communication classification accuracy across different levels of local distribution shift and exhibits greater robustness to latent-space dimensionality compression.

</details>


### [153] [DOCSCHISEL: Adaptive Tool Documentation Optimization Framework for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10037)
*You Lu,Kun Zhang,Bihuan Chen,Xin Peng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adaptive, task-dependent optimization of tool documentation (DocsChisel) significantly boosts LLM agent success by tailoring documentation fields; outperforms baselines EasyTool and DRAFT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tool documentation is a key grounding resource for LLM agents; existing work treats docs as fixed and lacks systematic analysis of how information fields affect performance across tasks and backbones; a large-scale empirical study is needed to identify heterogeneity and task-dependent effects.

Method: Conduct a large-scale empirical study of tool documentation; develop DocsChisel that analyzes failed execution traces to identify documentation gaps, then iteratively modify per-tool information fields (add/remove/refine) to improve agent performance.

Result: DocsChisel improves LLM agent task success rate by 95.89% over original tool documentation and by 75.15% on average over baselines EasyTool and DRAFT, with limited optimization time and token overhead.

Conclusion: Adaptive, per-domain tool documentation optimization is effective; fixed documentation generalizes poorly across settings; DocsChisel demonstrates the value of adaptive doc design and suggests future work on generalization and efficiency.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly rely on external tools to accomplish complex real-world tasks, making tool documentation a critical grounding resource for LLM agents. Existing studies mainly focus on improving the tool-use capabilities of LLM agents, while largely treating tool documentation as a fixed input. Although several recent works attempt to optimize tool documentation through rewriting or compression, little is known about how the information contained in tool documentation affects agent performance across different settings.
  To bridge this gap, we conduct a large-scale empirical study on tool documentation for LLM agents. Our study reveals substantial heterogeneity in the information fields provided by existing tool documentation. Moreover, the effectiveness of different information fields is highly dependent on the task domain, LLM backbone, and agent paradigm, indicating that no fixed tool documentation can consistently generalize across diverse agent settings.
  Motivated by these findings, we propose DocsChisel, an adaptive tool documentation optimization framework for LLM agents. DocsChisel analyzes failed execution traces of a target LLM agent to identify documentation-related issues, and iteratively optimizes tool documentation by adding, removing, and refining information fields for each tool. We evaluate DocsChisel against two state-of-the-art baselines, i.e., EasyTool and DRAFT. Experimental results show that DocsChisel improves the task success rate of LLM agents by 95.89% over the original tool documentation and by 75.15%, on average, over existing baselines, while incurring limited optimization time and token overhead

</details>


### [154] [FlowScout: From Execution Feedback to Reliable Tool-Using Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10039)
*Shuo Hao,You Lu,Bihuan Chen,Xin Peng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlowScout is an execution-guided framework that generates tool-integrated agentic workflows from historical task-solving records by mining a tool coordination skeleton and refining it with Monte Carlo tree search, achieving improved tool invocation correctness and execution quality compared to PM4Py, ReAct, and AFlow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual construction of agentic workflows is error-prone and data-efficient integration of real tool executions is lacking in existing automatic generation approaches; leveraging historical task-solving records can yield stable, usable workflows with real tool interactions.

Method: Represent workflows as directed graphs with LLM nodes and tool-calling nodes; mine a common tool coordination skeleton from historical records to initialize a workflow; refine topology via Monte Carlo tree search guided by execution feedback from actual tool invocations.

Result: FlowScout outperforms three baselines (PM4Py, ReAct, AFlow) across four domains, achieving at least 92.69% higher tool invocation correctness and 17.66% higher execution quality, with reduced performance variation across runs.

Conclusion: An execution-guided, tool-integrated workflow synthesis approach can produce more reliable and effective agentic workflows than existing baselines by grounding generation in execution feedback and historical coordination patterns.

Abstract: Agentic workflows have become an important abstraction for building reliable LLM-based automation systems by organizing large language models (LLMs), tools, and control logic into explicit execution structures. However, constructing high-quality agentic workflows remains largely manual and requires substantial domain expertise. Recent studies have explored automatic agentic workflow generation from historical task-solving records, but they mainly produce LLM-centric workflows, where real tool executions are abstracted and simulated by LLM nodes, limiting the usability and stability of generated workflows. To address these limitations, we propose FlowScout, an execution-guided framework for generating tool-integrated agentic workflows from historical task-solving records. Specifically, FlowScout represents an agentic workflow as a directed graph composed of LLM nodes, tool-calling nodes, and dependency edges. It first mines a common tool coordination skeleton from historical records to construct an initial workflow, and then refines the workflow topology through Monte Carlo tree search guided by execution feedback. We evaluate FlowScout on four representative task domains and compare it with three baselines, i.e., PM4Py, ReAct and AFlow. Experimental results show that agentic workflows generated by FlowScout improve tool invocation correctness by at least 92.69% and execution quality by at least 17.66% over the baselines, while achieving lower performance variation across repeated runs.

</details>


### [155] [UserToolBench: A User-Profile-Hidden Benchmark for Personalized Decision Making in Tool-Use LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10042)
*Xuexiong Yin,Zechuan Chen,Yongsen Zheng,Yuxiang Zhang,Jingyuan Yang,Bin Wang,Yubin Wang,Keze Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A benchmark, UserToolBench, evaluates personalized decision making in tool-use LLMs by testing latent user-preference inference, need for clarifications, and user-aligned tool-call trajectories under incomplete information. Built from privacy-sanitized traces with 10 personas, 36 tool sets, 1,065 turns, and 170 tools; examines lack-of-information, single-tool, and multi-tool settings. Strong tool-use LLMs still struggle with personalization, especially multi-tool coordination, missing-constraint inference, and long-horizon consistency, suggesting personalization evaluation should test correct user-aligned decisions rather than surface-level user-specific outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of personalized LLM behavior mostly focus on stylistic imitation or generic tool use, not on whether models make decisions that align with the user's true preferences under partial information. There is a need for benchmarks that probe inference of latent preferences, anticipation of information gaps, and long-horizon planning.

Method: Construct a benchmark from privacy-sanitized real interaction traces with 10 persona profiles, 36 tool ecosystems, 1,065 turns, and 170 tools. Task types cover lack-of-information, single-tool, and multi-tool scenarios. Evaluation centers on the model’s ability to infer user preferences from history, decide when to ask clarifications, and generate user-aligned tool-call trajectories over long horizons.

Result: Experiments show current strong tool-use LLMs struggle with personalized delegation. Key bottlenecks include multi-tool coordination, missing-constraint inference, and maintaining long-horizon behavioral consistency.

Conclusion: Personalization evaluation should assess whether models make correct decisions for the users they represent, not merely outputs that sound user-specific; advancing personalization requires benchmarks that stress long-horizon planning and constraint-aware tool orchestration.

Abstract: Tool-use LLMs are increasingly asked to act on users' behalf, but existing benchmarks usually focus on profile recall, style imitation, generic tool use, or response-level personalization. We introduce UserToolBench , a benchmark for personalized decision making in tool-use LLMs. UserToolBench tests whether a model can infer latent user preferences from interaction history, recognize when clarification is needed, and produce user-aligned tool-call trajectories under incomplete information. The benchmark is built from privacy-sanitized real interaction traces and combines structured persona profiles, public API-style tool ecosystems, and long-horizon multi-turn trajectories. It includes 10 user profiles, 36 tool sets, 1,065 turns, 170 unique tools, and evaluation-focused task types covering lack-of-information, single-tool, and multi-tool settings. Experiments with strong tool-use LLMs show that current models still have difficulty with personalized delegation. Multi-tool coordination, missing-constraint inference, and long-horizon behavioral consistency remain major bottlenecks. These results suggest that personalization evaluation should move beyond asking whether outputs sound user-specific and instead ask whether LLMs make correct decisions for the users they represent.

</details>


### [156] [Finding the Signal in the Spam: Jointly Learning Rewards and Worker Reliability from Pairwise Comparisons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10045)
*Kaustubh Shivshankar Shejole,Tanish Agarwal,Arpit Agarwal,Avishek Ghosh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Jointly learns item rewards and worker reliability in pairwise comparison settings using a Boltzmann-rational model; EM with Polya-Gamma latent variables makes logistic likelihood tractable; reduces to matrix sensing with convergence guarantees; robust to spammers; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Crowdworker unreliability in collecting pairwise comparisons; need to model competency to improve reward estimation and resist spam/adversaries.

Method: Use Boltzmann-rational model (extension of Bradley-Terry-Luce) incorporating worker competencies. Develop EM algorithm; integrate Polya-Gamma augmentation to convert logistic likelihood to Gaussian, enabling tractable E-step and a simplified Q function; reduction to a matrix sensing formulation.

Result: Theoretical convergence guarantees for the algorithm; empirical validation on real and synthetic data; algorithm robust to spammers and adversarial workers; outperforms baselines; practical effectiveness; code and data available.

Conclusion: Demonstrates viability of joint learning in crowdsourced reward learning; provides a scalable, robust method; contributes to understanding of how to model worker reliability in pairwise comparison settings; potential applicability in crowdsourcing and LLM fine-tuning.

Abstract: The problem of learning from pairwise comparisons has been widely studied across many domains such as recommendation systems, social choice, and more recently, fine-tuning large language models. In this problem, the goal is to learn item rewards based on pairwise comparisons between them. In many scenarios, these comparisons are elicited from crowdworkers using platforms such as Amazon Mechanical Turk, Scale AI, etc. However, crowdworkers are often unreliable due to limited domain knowledge or revenue-maximizing (spamming) behavior. In this work, our goal is to understand whether worker reliability (competency) can be learned jointly with item rewards. To this end, we adopt the Boltzmann-rational model for pairwise comparisons, which extends the Bradley-Terry-Luce model by incorporating worker competencies. We derive an EM-based algorithm for learning under this model by introducing Polya-Gamma latent variables to transform the logistic likelihood into a conditionally Gaussian form, enabling tractable optimization and leading to a simplified $Q$ function in the E-step of the algorithm. This technique allows us to reduce our formulation to a matrix sensing problem, using which we establish theoretical convergence guarantees for our algorithm. We conduct extensive experiments on real-world and synthetic datasets. These experiments demonstrate the advantages of using our algorithm over several baselines and confirm its strong robustness to both spammers and adversarial workers, highlighting its practical effectiveness in realistic crowdsourcing and reward learning settings. The code and data is publicly available at https://github.com/KaustubhShejole/BoRa_EM.

</details>


### [157] [Detecting Soft Skills in ML Engineering Roles CVs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10046)
*Aidin Azamnouri,Nouran Ayad,Justus Bogner,Stefan Wagner*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Candidate-side data reveals soft skills are predominantly described narratively rather than as keywords, with strong support for most hypothesized role signatures and disclosure patterns across ML-related roles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the gap between demand-side claims about soft skills and how candidates actually articulate them, and to move beyond keyword-based CV mining by validating narrative extraction against ground truth while testing falsifiable hypotheses.

Method: Use a balanced corpus of 300 curated CVs across ML engineer, data scientist, and software engineer roles. Develop an LLM-based pipeline to extract explicitly listed and implicitly narrated soft skills, validated against human annotations. Translate demand-side literature into 13 falsifiable hypotheses about role signatures, seniority progression, and disclosure style, and test with effect sizes under family-wise error control.

Result: Eleven hypotheses supported, one partially, one refuted. Narratives account for roughly 3x more disclosure of soft skills than keywords. Leadership, coordination, and mentoring are most narrated (88–96%). Seniority triples the odds of articulating leadership; software engineers articulate leadership at about half the rate of peers. Technical candidates do disclose soft skills, but keyword-based screening misses them.

Conclusion: Candidate-side evidence largely corroborates demand-side claims but also reveals systematic underestimation of soft skills when relying on keywords alone. Narrative disclosure patterns differ by role and seniority, with implications for CV screening practices and how organizations interpret soft-skill signals.

Abstract: Soft skills shape collaboration among ML engineers, data scientists, and software engineers building ML-enabled systems, yet what we know about them comes almost entirely from the demand side. Job advertisements, surveys, and hiring manager interviews capture what employers ask for. How candidates themselves articulate these competencies has not been studied, and existing CV-mining work is both keyword-based, so it cannot see skills conveyed through narrative, and descriptive, reporting frequency rankings without testing whether group differences exceed sampling variation. We close both gaps. Using a balanced corpus of 300 curated CVs spanning the three roles, we extract explicitly listed and implicitly narrated soft skills with an LLM-based pipeline validated against a human-annotated ground truth, a distinction that existing extractors were not designed to make. We then convert the demand-side literature's claims into 13 falsifiable hypotheses about role signatures, seniority progression, and disclosure style, and test them with effect sizes under family-wise error control, so that candidate-side data can corroborate or contradict the demand-side account rather than merely illustrate it. Eleven hypotheses are supported, one partially, and one refuted. Candidates disclose soft skills through narrative rather than keyword lists by roughly three to one, and most so for the competencies employers value most: leadership, coordination, and mentoring (88-96% narrative). Seniority nearly triples the odds of articulating leadership. That competency, assumed universal in prior work, is articulated by software engineers at half the rate of their peers. Technical candidates do articulate soft skills, but a keyword-based screening systematically misses them.

</details>


### [158] [Physics-Informed Machine Learning in Prognostics and Health Management: A Systematic Literature Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10047)
*Christopher Braun,Julian Raible,Marco F. Huber*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Physics-informed ML improves prognostics and health management (PHM) predictions across 212 studies using a four-class taxonomy (observational, inductive, learning, hybrid). Benefits over baselines are tangible but evidence for overcoming core ML limitations (generalization, causality, interpretability) is uneven. Studies are biased toward batteries and bearings and are often problem-specific. Future work should focus on transferable design patterns, benchmarks for integration strategies, and uncertainty-aware, lightweight models for online deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PHM requires reliable, safe, and efficient operation; purely data-driven ML often struggles with generalization, causal inference, and interpretability. Physics-informed ML (PIML) integrates prior physical knowledge to mitigate these issues and enhance PHM performance.

Method: Systematic literature review of 212 PHM studies. Proposes a four-class scheme (observational bias, inductive bias, learning bias, hybrid) and categorizes studies by PHM task to assess how PIML is used and its reported benefits.

Result: Across four classes, PIML approaches show improved predictive performance over conventional baselines across diverse assets. The literature is heavily skewed toward lithium-ion batteries and bearings and tends toward problem-specific solutions. While PIML provides tangible benefits, claims that it resolves core limitations (generalization, causal inference, interpretability) lack robust supporting evidence.

Conclusion: Physics-informed approaches already offer tangible benefits in PHM; future work should develop transferable design patterns, benchmark integration strategies, and uncertainty-aware, lightweight models suitable for online deployment in real-world settings.

Abstract: In modern industry, keeping complex systems reliable, safe, and efficient hinges on Prognostics and Health Management (PHM). Machine Learning (ML) has largely driven advancements in diagnostics and prognostics, yet purely data-driven models face inherent limitations, such as poor generalization, an inability to infer causal relationships, and a lack of interpretability. Physics-Informed Machine Learning (PIML) helps mitigate these limitations by incorporating prior physical knowledge directly into the ML pipeline, thereby fostering growing interest in its application to PHM. This work investigates how PIML is being leveraged in the context of PHM through a systematic literature review of 212 studies. The review introduces a four-class classification scheme, consisting of observational bias, inductive bias, learning bias, and hybrid approaches, and further categorizes studies by PHM task. Across all four classes, the reviewed studies consistently demonstrate improved predictive performance over conventional baselines across a broad range of assets, although the literature is heavily skewed toward lithium-ion batteries and bearings, and dominated by problem-specific solutions. Overall, the review indicates that physics-informed approaches already provide tangible benefits, whereas claims of improvements concerning some of the aforementioned limitations lack sufficient supporting evidence. Future research should prioritize transferable design patterns, benchmarks comparing integration strategies, and uncertainty-aware models that are lightweight and robust enough for online deployment in real-world settings.

</details>


### [159] [Observational Policy Ranking for SMB Financial Guidance from Multi-Action Accounting Logs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10050)
*Shrutendra Harsola,Vignesh Subrahmaniam,Vikas Raturi,Kamalika Das,Xiang Gao,Kratika Gupta,Ruocheng Guo,Padmaja Jonnalagedda,Ananya Pramod,Sricharan Kumar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Covariate-Adjusted Residual Policy Learning (CAR-PL), an action-wise R-learner for observational policy ranking from multi-action ledger logs; compares against baselines and finds competitive performance with objective-specific KPI leaders and more diverse action selections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SMBs need timely, targeted financial guidance, but historical data are observational and co-varying; there is a need to rank multi-action policy recommendations for a key KPI from ledger-derived features while accounting for confounding and limited policy support.

Method: An action-wise R-learner called CAR-PL operating on multi-hot action logs; regularizes by observational support, uses covariate adjustment to handle confounding, and compares against uplift T-Learner, conservative CVM, a zero-shot LLM, and non-personalized references on held-out, company-disjoint folds under a shared scoring rule.

Result: CAR-PL achieves a Gross Profit point estimate of 0.084 (highest among evaluated methods); the uplift T-Learner achieves 0.085 for Revenue (highest); the contextual value model achieves 0.062 for Quick Ratio; CAR-PL and T-Learner are not statistically separated on growth KPIs in matched company-clustered comparisons; CAR-PL selects 33–34 categories and yields more dispersed action choices than competitors; outcome-model-only scoring preserves KPI leaders; replacing the all-zero treatment with the most common training co-action pattern does not materially alter rankings.

Conclusion: The study supports objective-specific, data-driven ranking of SMB financial guidance using multi-action accounting logs, with CAR-PL offering robust performance and more diverse action recommendations, while KPI leadership can depend on the chosen objective and may not be significantly different from alternative methods in some settings.

Abstract: Small and medium-sized businesses need timely financial guidance, yet historical accounting logs record self-selected and often co-occurring business changes rather than randomized recommendations. We formulate this setting as observational policy ranking: from pre-decision financial information, a policy selects one of 34 ledger-derived business-change categories for a target financial KPI. Using 85,078 company-month observations from 7,505 firms, we introduce Covariate-Adjusted Residual Policy Learning (CAR-PL), an action-wise R-learner that operates directly on multi-hot logs and regularizes selection by observational support. We compare CAR-PL with an uplift T-Learner, a conservative contextual value model, a zero-shot LLM, and non-personalized references on company-disjoint held-out firms under a shared model-assisted scoring rule. CAR-PL has the highest Gross Profit point estimate (0.084), the T-Learner has the highest Revenue point estimate (0.085), and the contextual value model has the highest Quick Ratio point estimate (0.062). CAR-PL and the T-Learner are not statistically separated on either growth KPI in matched company-clustered comparisons, while CAR-PL selects 33-34 categories and produces less concentrated selections across the catalog. Outcome-model-only scoring retains the same KPI-level point-estimate leader or top pair, and category rankings remain similar when the all-zero treatment reference is replaced by the most common training co-action pattern. These findings support objective-specific ranking of SMB financial guidance from multi-action accounting logs.

</details>


### [160] [ChronoSSM: Training for Temporally Aware Representations in Autoregressive State Space Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10120)
*Adrien Schoen,Nachiketa Ratnakar Patil,Arjun Bhagoji,Francesco Bronzino*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Jointly train an autoregressive ChronoSSM to model events and timestamps, yielding temporally richer representations without harming content generation, outperforming a two-stage timing approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the limitation of treating timing as auxiliary and improve temporal reasoning and event chronology reconstruction in data with explicit timing, by integrating temporal supervision into the backbone.

Method: An autoregressive State Space Model (ChronoSSM) with a shared backbone trained with combined token (event) and temporal generation objectives; compare joint training to a two-stage regime across four domains with varying timestamp supervision.

Result: Joint training makes inter-arrival information more recoverable from frozen representations and does not degrade content-generation quality overall, across diverse supervision settings.

Conclusion: Temporal supervision via joint training yields more temporally informative representations while preserving autoregressive event modeling performance, supporting integrated timing modeling over two-stage approaches.

Abstract: Modern sequence models, from Transformers to State Space Models, have enabled powerful generative modeling across diverse domains, yet they are typically trained to predict what happens while treating when it happens as a secondary concern. In data-mining settings where events are associated with explicit timing information, this separation can limit temporal reasoning, anomaly detection, and faithful reconstruction of event chronology. A common strategy is to treat timing as an auxiliary signal, training a separate timing model using representations learned solely for event prediction. However, this two-stage approach implicitly assumes that representations optimized for event prediction already contain sufficient temporal structure.
  We introduce ChronoSSM, an autoregressive State Space Model (SSM) that jointly models events and timestamps with a shared backbone trained using combined token and temporal generation objectives. We compare the joint regime, where temporal supervision updates the backbone, with the two-stage regime, where timing is learned only using the frozen event representations. Across four domains spanning dense and partial timestamp supervision, joint training consistently makes inter-arrival information more recoverable from frozen representations without any systematic degradation in content-generation quality overall. Our results show that temporal supervision can produce more temporally informative representations without materially degrading autoregressive event modeling.

</details>


### [161] [Procedural Fairness Failures in RLHF from Preference Averaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10126)
*M P V S Gopinadh,Karthik Kamuju,Kummari Avinash,John Joshua,Srinivasa Raju Rudraraju*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Procedural fairness in RLHF is violated by averaging heterogeneous user preferences; PA-RLHF preserves distinct preference signals by separating optimization by preference mode, improving alignment and reducing fairness gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heterogeneous human preferences can bias reward models toward majority signals, under-representing minority preferences, leading to unfair alignment outcomes.

Method: Introduce Preference-Aware RLHF (PA-RLHF) that separates optimization across distinct preference modes at the reward-learning stage, rather than aggregating them into a single reward model.

Result: In a controlled setting, PA-RLHF increases overall alignment accuracy from 46.9% to 67.9% and reduces the best-vs-worst aligned-group fairness gap from 15.9 to 9.6 percentage points.

Conclusion: Procedural fairness failures in alignment can stem from the structural design of reward learning; PA-RLHF demonstrates that preserving distinct preference signals yields better alignment and fairness, with implications for LLMs and agentic systems.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) aggregates heterogeneous preferences into a single reward model, assuming preference homogeneity. When preferences are heterogeneous, this aggregation induces a procedural fairness failure where majority preference groups dominate reward learning while minority preferences are systematically under-represented. This work defines procedural fairness in alignment as preserving distinct preference signals during reward modeling and shows that standard RLHF violates this via preference averaging. Preference-Aware RLHF (PA-RLHF) is introduced, separating optimization across preference modes at the reward learning stage. In a controlled setting, PA-RLHF improves overall alignment accuracy from 46.9% to 67.9% and reduces the fairness gap between best and worst aligned groups from 15.9 to 9.6 percentage points. These results show that procedural fairness failures in alignment can arise from structural design choices in reward learning, even in controlled, noise-free settings, with direct implications for large language models and agentic systems, where biased reward models can compound inequities across sequential decisions.

</details>


### [162] [SeFoRA: Sketch-Aggregated Federated Low-Rank Adaptation with Heterogeneous Client Ranks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10144)
*Yue Xia,Tayyebeh Jahani-Nezhad,Mayank Bakshi,Rawad Bitar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes SeFoRA and SeFoRA-Ho, sketch-aggregated federated LoRA for parameter-efficient fine-tuning; resolves dimension mismatch across clients with varying LoRA ranks; enables subspace aggregation; proves O(1/T) convergence in rank-homogeneous case; demonstrates empirical gains on RoBERTa-Large/GLUE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated PEFT with LoRA faces bilinear mismatch and dimension-incompatibility across clients with different ranks, hindering aggregation; need efficient aggregation in a subspace and convergence guarantees.

Method: Introduce sketch-aggregated aggregation: clients send linear sketches of their updates; federator aggregates in compressed space; rank-homogeneous variant allows direct adapter aggregation; provide convergence results; experiments.

Result: Convergence to a neighborhood of first-order stationary point at rate O(1/T) for rank-homogeneous; empirical improvements over state-of-the-art on RoBERTa-Large fine-tuning on GLUE.

Conclusion: SeFoRA framework resolves key challenges of federated LoRA by aggregating in a shared sketch space, enabling scalable, efficient PEFT in federated settings, with theoretical and empirical validation.

Abstract: We consider federated parameter efficient fine-tuning of large neural networks with low-rank adaptation (LoRA,~Hu et al.\ 2022). Combining LoRA with federated PEFT introduces challenges absent from either setting alone: clients may use different LoRA ranks, making their factor matrices dimension-incompatible, and factor-wise averaging suffers from a bilinear mismatch. We propose SeFoRA, a sketch-aggregated federated LoRA algorithm in which each client transmits a linear sketch of its local updates, enabling direct aggregation at the federator. As a result, SeFoRA alleviates the bilinear mismatch, and allows for aggregation in a small subspace of the full model. We introduce a rank-homogeneous version called SeFoRA-Ho which allows for direct adapter aggregation in this setting. We prove convergence to a neighborhood of the first-order stationary point at rate $\cO(1/T)$ for the rank-homogeneous setting. Numerical experiments on fine-tuning RoBERTa-Large on GLUE datasets show how our algorithms outperform the state-of-the-art.

</details>


### [163] [The Evaluation Protocol Determines the Result: An Independent Reproduction of LeWorldModel on TwoRoom](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10145)
*Joyjeet Singh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Reproducibility study of LeWorldModel exposes fragility to evaluation conventions and protocol; long-horizon planning not explained by one-step prediction; normalization and undocumented conventions drastically shift results; calls for robust, transparent reporting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess robustness and reproducibility of reported gains in latent world models and highlight biases from evaluation setups and hyperparameter conventions.

Method: Independent reimplementation using ~25 rented compute units; baseline replication on a laptop CPU; evaluation across goal offsets/budgets; analysis of undocumented conventions (frameskip, action-encoder width, ImageNet normalization, action z-scoring); compare with authors’ released weights.

Result: Partial replication: 94.0% vs 84.0% reported by authors; representation metric (Pearson r) nearly matched; four conventions crucial for achieving results; released configurations alone fail to converge; evaluation protocol differs between appendix and repository, causing large fluctuations (e.g., 84.0% vs 14.0%); exactly changing goal construction shifts a checkpoint from 84.0% to 8.0%; one-step prediction accuracy poorly predicts long-horizon success; batch normalization inflated validation loss by up to 300x, masking flat training loss.

Conclusion: Reproducibility is fragile and highly sensitive to undocumented conventions and evaluation protocol details; emphasize transparent reporting of data processing, hyperparameters, and evaluation metrics; long-horizon planning cannot be inferred from short-horizon predictors in this setup; calls for standardized, fully disclosed evaluation pipelines.

Abstract: LeWorldModel trains a latent world model with a prediction loss and a single anti-collapse regulariser, and reports approximately 87% of goals reached on TwoRoom, its simplest diagnostic environment. We reproduce that result by independent reimplementation on roughly $25 of rented compute, with all evaluation on one laptop CPU.
  We reach 94.0% at the repository's evaluation goal offset, against 84.0% for the authors' own released checkpoint measured under our protocol on identical episodes, and we reproduce the reported representation result directly (position probe Pearson r = 0.9988 against a reported 0.996). Reaching that point required four conventions that determine the outcome and appear in no released configuration file: dense action gathering across a frameskip block, a programmatically-set action-encoder width, ImageNet pixel normalisation, and action z-scoring. A reproducer following the released configurations alone obtains a model whose predictor cannot converge.
  The evaluation protocol is itself contested by the released material. The paper's appendix and the repository's configuration specify different goal offsets and step budgets; on the authors' own weights these yield 14.0% and 84.0%, and only the configuration's values reproduce the reported figure. On fifty identical episodes, changing nothing but how the goal is constructed moves that checkpoint from 84.0% to 8.0%.
  Two findings generalise. One-step prediction accuracy does not predict long-horizon planning success: across three checkpoints spanning a sevenfold range in prediction error, including the authors' own, it orders short-horizon success monotonically and fails to order long-horizon success at all. And a batch normalisation layer inflated our reported validation loss by up to a factor of 300, concealing a training loss that was flat throughout.

</details>


### [164] [REATS: LLM Reasoning-based Ensemble Learning for Adaptive Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10149)
*Xu Zhang,Chang Xu,Hui Sun,Nan Ma,Zijian Zhang,Peng Wang,Wei Wang,Li Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLM-driven ensemble routing for time-series forecasting that uses structured hybrid textual-numerical inputs and chain-of-thought to generate interpretable, sample-adaptive weights; introduces fixed-cost token representations, retrieved priors, multi-row weight guidance with a percentage-table, and a reciprocal reward-based GRPO fine-tuning, achieving strong performance and generalization on eight benchmarks with explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time-series forecasting suffers from model diversity; no single model dominates across samples. Ensemble methods help but current approaches rely on fixed rules or opaque models. The work harnesses LLM reasoning to make interpretable, sample-adaptive weighting decisions.

Method: Introduce REATS: an LLM-based ensemble router that processes textual temporal-pattern descriptions and numerical features to produce weights. Build a hybrid textual-numerical representation with fixed token cost; incorporate retrieved similar-sample priors; implement a diverse multi-row weight supervision scheme using a token-efficient percentage-table; apply a two-stage fine-tuning framework (SFT + GRPO) with a reciprocal reward mapping to convert MSE gaps into bounded signals and enhance near-oracle sensitivity.

Result: Empirical evaluation on eight benchmarks shows REATS outperforms competitive ensemble baselines, provides natural language explanations, and demonstrates strong transfer learning and out-of-domain generalization to unseen models.

Conclusion: LLM-based, interpretable, sample-adaptive ensembling is effective for real-world time-series forecasting and generalizes to unseen models, with robust training design to mitigate LLM-related issues.

Abstract: Due to the diversity of real-world time series, no single forecasting model consistently dominates across all samples. Ensemble learning addresses this by combining complementary model strengths, yet existing methods rely on fixed rules or black-box models based solely on numerical inputs, failing to leverage LLM reasoning for interpretable weighting decisions. We propose REATS, which leverages LLM reasoning capabilities as an intelligent ensemble router that jointly processes textual temporal pattern descriptions and numerical features to produce interpretable, sample-adaptive ensemble weights through chain-of-thought reasoning. To enable effective LLM-based ensembling, we study its key design choices and propose: (i) a structured input pipeline that transforms raw time series into hybrid textual--numerical representations with fixed token cost, enabling rule-based chain-of-thought construction without API dependency, augmented with retrieved similar-sample priors; (ii) a diverse multi-row weight supervision scheme coupled with a token-efficient percentage-table format that reduces numerical complexity and mitigates LLM hallucinations; and (iii) a two-stage fine-tuning framework combining SFT with GRPO, where a reciprocal reward mapping transforms the continuous unbounded MSE gap into bounded signals with amplified near-oracle sensitivity, addressing the uniform sensitivity and outlier-dominated advantage compression inherent in naive reward designs for regression-based GRPO. Experiments on eight benchmarks demonstrate that REATS outperforms competitive ensemble baselines while providing natural language explanations and demonstrating strong transfer learning and out-of-domain generalization to unseen candidate models.

</details>


### [165] [Intrinsic Structure: Spectral Identifiability for Mechanistic Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10172)
*Ashim Dhor,Pin-Yu Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces an identifiability framework for mechanistic interpretability via Koopman lifting of the neural forward pass, proving that a spectrum of a finite linear realisation is recoverable from calibration samples (rate M^{-1/2} up to permutation) and serves as a model-intrinsic fingerprint rather than a legible decomposition; supports this with lower bounds, heavy-tailed variants, and a dissociation theorem; validated on multiple LLMs with spectrum convergence and a measurable exponent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mechanistic interpretability currently yields circuits that may be artifacts of the method rather than true model properties; variability across seeds/architectures undermines reliability. The work seeks an identifiability guarantee and a coordinate-free, model-intrinsic primitive for interpretability by casting the forward pass as a dynamical system and applying Koopman theory.

Method: Treat the forward pass as a controlled dynamical system with depth as time; lift to a finite linear realisation via the Koopman operator; define a spectrum that is invariant to coordinate choices; establish identifiability: the spectrum is recoverable from M calibration samples at rate M^{-1/2} up to permutation; provide a matching minimax lower bound; introduce a median-of-means variant for heavy-tailed activations; prove a dissociation theorem: in non-normal realisations, activation-variance directions and depth-information directions cannot coincide; contrast the identifiable spectrum with legible decompositions.

Result: Empirical validation on GPT-2 small, Gemma-2-2B, and Qwen3-8B-Base shows the spectrum converges across models; the Qwen3-8B-Base exponent is 0.506 ± 0.031; shortfalls align along a curve each cell’s sample threshold; Koopman modes outperform random directions but underperform PCA for indirect-object identification, with a depth-distance gap shrinking ~4.1× as predicted; the spectrum acts as an identifiable, model-intrinsic fingerprint with an explicit error bar, not a legible decomposition.

Conclusion: The identifiable spectrum is a model-intrinsic fingerprint distinct from the legible decomposition; the framework provides a formal identifiability guarantee for a mechanistic-interpretability primitive and a reproducible, coordinate-free diagnostic across models.

Abstract: Mechanistic interpretability explains models by identifying circuits inside them, but has no way to tell whether a circuit is a property of the model or an artifact of the method that found it. Sparse autoencoders illustrate the problem: different seeds and widths recover materially different features from the same activations, and no theory says whether that variability is incidental or structural. We put dictionary learning for interpretability on an identifiability footing. Treating the forward pass as a controlled dynamical system with depth as time and lifting it with the Koopman operator yields a finite linear realisation whose \emph{spectrum} is a coordinate-free property of the model. We prove the spectrum is recoverable from $M$ calibration samples at rate $M^{-1/2}$ up to permutation - to our knowledge the first identifiability theorem for a mechanistic-interpretability primitive, with a matching minimax lower bound, a median-of-means variant for heavy-tailed activations, and a dissociation theorem: whenever the realisation is non-normal, the directions carrying activation variance and the directions carrying information across depth cannot coincide. The identifiable object and the legible object are not the same object. On GPT-2 small, Gemma-2-2B and Qwen3-8B-Base the spectrum converges everywhere and attains the predicted exponent on Qwen3-8B-Base ($0.506 \pm 0.031$); shortfalls collapse onto one curve against each cell's sample threshold. Koopman modes beat random directions but lose to principal components on indirect-object identification, with the gap decaying $4.1\times$ in depth-distance, as the theorem predicts. The Koopman spectrum is an identifiable, model-intrinsic fingerprint with a stated error bar, not a legible decomposition.

</details>


### [166] [From Prediction to Incrementality: Causal Optimization for Large-Scale Targeting and Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10182)
*Changshuai Wei,John Bencina,Phuc Nguyen,Andre Assuncao Silva T Ribeiro,Benjamin Zelditch*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A decision-centric causal-optimization framework that unifies a Transformer-based causal model, a Bayesian neural-bandit for uncertainty-aware exploration, and an LP-based allocator to maximize long-term value under business constraints, with sequential-context and multi-outcome support; demonstrated a +7.2% lift in LTV in production.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predictive scores for targeting often misallocate resources toward users who would act anyway, reducing incremental impact. A framework that optimizes causal effects under global constraints can improve incremental lift and align with business objectives.

Method: 1) Causal neural network with Transformer backbone for individual treatment-effect estimation; 2) Bayesian neural-bandit layer for uncertainty-aware exploration; 3) Dual-based large-scale linear-programming layer for constrained allocation; 4) Transformer encoder and outcome embeddings enabling sequential-context and multi-outcome, attribute-conditioned scoring; evaluated offline on public bandit data, ablations, and an online LinkedIn Feed A/B test; distilled production lessons on data construction and cost/delivery control.

Result: End-to-end treatment policy achieved a statistically significant +7.20% lift in the primary long-term-value metric in production; offline simulations and online experiments validate the framework’s effectiveness; insights provided for causal training data construction and deployment constraints.

Conclusion: Production-scale causal optimization under business constraints is feasible using a unified architecture that integrates causal inference, uncertainty-aware exploration, and constrained allocation, with practical guidelines for deployment.

Abstract: Large-scale targeting and recommendation systems are typically built around predictive scores fed into heuristic or local allocation. When the business goal is incremental impact, as in marketing campaigns, incentives, and notifications, this paradigm systematically misallocates resources toward users who would have acted anyway. We present a decision-centric framework that instead optimizes causal effects under global constraints, aligning three components under a single objective: a causal neural network with a Transformer backbone for individual treatment-effect estimation, a Bayesian neural-bandit layer for uncertainty-aware exploration, and a dual-based large-scale linear-programming layer for constrained allocation. The framework also supports sequential context and multi-outcome, attribute-conditioned scoring through a Transformer encoder and outcome embeddings. We evaluate it with offline simulations on a public bandit dataset, targeted architectural ablations, and an online A/B test on LinkedIn Feed marketing traffic. We also distill production lessons on causal training-data construction and cost and delivery control, which were critical to successful deployment. The end-to-end treatment policy delivered a statistically significant $+7.20\%$ lift in the primary long-term-value metric, demonstrating the feasibility of production-scale causal optimization under business constraints.

</details>


### [167] [ELMER: Evolutionary Language Model that Explores and Refines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10196)
*Matthew Siper,Ahmed Khalifa,Julian Togelius*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An Evolutionary Language Model uses a fine-tuned Qwen3-8B to navigate executable program space via natural-language mutations, improving behavioral search efficiency under variable mutation strength.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional mutation-based evolution often decouples mutation size from behavioral impact; natural-language prompts offer a steerable, execution-grounded representation to explore executable space.

Method: Fine-tune a Qwen3-8B model with conditional inputs for mutation strength (low/med/high) using Direct Preference Optimization (oDPO). The system performs 252 fixed-budget evolutionary searches, enabling tasks: (1) conditional semantic mutation in natural language, (2) natural language to a domain-specific language (GPTL) compilation, and (3) GPTL to natural language translation.

Result: oDPO improves behavioral calibration and finite-budget search efficiency. Natural-language representations yield the highest held-out fitness. The conditioning variable (mutation strength) systematically changes semantic edit composition; language mutations preserve more parent fitness at small-to-moderate behavioral displacement.

Conclusion: Language can serve as a steerable, execution-grounded search representation over executable program space.

Abstract: Program evolution can measure whether a mutation helped, but it rarely controls how far the mutation moves in behavior space. Syntactic edit size is an unreliable proxy: a small code change can alter nearly every action, while a larger rewrite can preserve the same execution trace. We introduce an Evolutionary Language Model that searches over natural-language policy descriptions and compiles typed programs for execution. A fully fine-tuned Qwen3-8B model learns three task-conditioned operations: conditional semantic mutation, natural language to domain-specific language (GPTL) compilation, and GPTL to natural language translation. The model is fine-tuned with conditional input on the mutation strength (low, medium, high) using Direct Preference Optimization (oDPO). Across 252 fixed-budget evolutionary searches, oDPO improves both behavioral calibration and finite-budget search efficiency. Natural-language attains the highest observed held-out fitness. Our analysis shows that the condition input (mutation strength) systematically changes semantic edit composition and that language mutations preserve more parent fitness at matched small-to-moderate behavioral displacement. These results show that language can serve as a steerable, execution-grounded search representation over executable program space.

</details>


### [168] [Boundary-Seeking Policy Gradient for Safe Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10204)
*Chenhua Fan,Jiahui Zhu,Yuhang Zhang,Honghao Wei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BSPG is a first-order policy gradient method for safe RL in constrained MDPs that exploits the active constraint boundary. It decomposes the update into a tangential reward-improving component and a signed normal residual toward the boundary, yielding a Lagrangian-like form without dual variables. The method achieves convergence of the constraint residual to zero, moves along the boundary to maximize reward, and satisfies KKT conditions under exact gradients. Experiments on Safety-Gym navigation show higher reward and tighter boundary tracking than baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In constrained Markov Decision Processes (CMDPs), optimal policies lie on the active constraint boundary when constraints are binding. Standard gradient methods fail to exploit this geometry, often converging to interior feasible points and underutilizing the boundary information.

Method: Introduce Boundary-Seeking Policy Gradient (BSPG), a first-order update combining a tangential component that increases reward while preserving the constraint to first order with a signed normal component driven by the constraint residual that nudges the policy toward the active boundary. The update has an algebraic Lagrangian form with an induced coefficient and no learned dual variable.

Result: With exact gradients and regularity conditions, the constraint residual converges to zero from either side at O(1/√T); the tangential direction is a reward-ascent on the boundary; any convergent sequence is stationary on the active constraint set and satisfies KKT if the limit is a local feasible maximizer. Empirically, BSPG achieves higher rewards and tracks the boundary more tightly than baselines on a Safety-Gymnasium navigation task.

Conclusion: BSPG complements existing feasibility-only guarantees by characterizing boundary behavior and optimality on the active set, providing a practical method that leverages the boundary structure to improve reward while respecting safety constraints.

Abstract: Safe reinforcement learning maximizes reward subject to safety constraints. For Constrained Markov Decision Processes, the linear-programming view over occupancy measures implies that whenever the constraint is active at optimality, the optimal policy lies exactly on the constraint boundary, yet standard gradient-based methods do not exploit this structure and often settle in the feasible interior. We introduce Boundary-Seeking Policy Gradient (BSPG), a first-order method whose update combines a tangential component that improves reward while preserving cost to first order with a signed, residual-driven normal component that regulates the policy toward the active boundary from either side; the combined direction admits an algebraic Lagrangian form with an induced coefficient and no learned dual variable. Under exact gradients and stated regularity conditions, the constraint residual converges to zero from either side with a finite-horizon $O(1/\sqrt{T})$ bound, the tangential component is a reward-ascent direction on the boundary, and any convergent parameter sequence is stationary on the active constraint set, satisfying the KKT conditions when the limit is also a local maximizer over the feasible set. This complements existing analyses, which certify feasibility but do not characterize the constraint value at convergence. On a standard Safety-Gymnasium navigation task, BSPG attains higher reward while tracking the boundary more tightly than the compared baselines.

</details>


### [169] [A matched-integrator evaluation of Hamiltonian neural networks on pendulum and Kepler dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10235)
*Lenick Kemunto Nyabuto,Yae Ulrich Gaba,Birahim Tewe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HNNs provide superior long-horizon predictions and physical consistency for conservative dynamics, outperforming a matched feedforward baseline in nonlinear pendulum and Kepler two-body problems under matched data and integrator protocols; energy drift, trajectory error, and angular momentum drift are substantially reduced, with bounded energy drift and lower seed variability; symplectic guarantees may not apply for non-separable H, but benefits persist.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether encoding the conservative structure via a learned Hamiltonian improves long-horizon prediction and physical consistency, under a controlled protocol that isolates the effect of Hamiltonian parameterization.

Method: Train an HNN and a parameter-matched feedforward baseline on RK4-generated trajectories using the same central-difference derivative targets and optimization, then perform inference with RK4. Repeat across five random seeds. Assess energy drift, trajectory MSE, and boundedness on a nonlinear pendulum; perform an explicit Störmer–Verlet rollout; extend protocol to the Kepler two-body problem and compare drift in trajectories, energy, and angular momentum.

Result: On the nonlinear pendulum, HNN reduces mean energy drift by 42x and mean trajectory MSE by 15.8x at T=100, with energy drift bounded and less seed-variant than baseline; benefits amplify in more nonlinear phase-space regions. Störmer–Verlet rollout is used despite non-separable H not guaranteeing standard symplecticity. On the Kepler two-body problem, HNN again exhibits lower drift in trajectory, energy, and angular momentum than the baseline.

Conclusion: Hamiltonian parameterization yields substantially improved long-horizon fidelity and physical consistency for conservative dynamical systems, even when the Hamiltonian is not restricted to a separable form; the controlled protocol demonstrates robustness across different problems, though standard symplectic guarantees may not strictly apply under non-separable Hamiltonians.

Abstract: Hamiltonian Neural Networks (HNNs) parameterize conservative dynamics through a learned scalar Hamiltonian, providing an architectural prior that is absent from generic vector-field neural networks. We evaluate this prior under a controlled protocol in which an HNN and a parameter-matched feedforward baseline are trained on the same RK4-generated trajectories, use the same central-difference derivative targets and optimization settings, and are integrated at inference with the same RK4 scheme. Results are reported over five independent training seeds.
  On the nonlinear pendulum, the HNN reduces mean energy drift by 42-fold and mean trajectory MSE by 15.8-fold at T = 100, approximately 16 pendulum periods. Its energy drift also remains bounded and exhibits substantially lower seed-to-seed variability than the standard-network baseline. An energy-stratified analysis shows that the difference becomes more pronounced as trajectories explore more nonlinear regions of phase space.
  As an additional diagnostic, we examine an explicit Störmer--Verlet-style rollout of the learned HNN. Because the learned Hamiltonian is not constrained to the separable form H(q,p) = T(p) + V(q), the standard symplecticity guarantee of velocity Verlet does not directly apply.
  We further apply the same matched-integrator protocol to the three-dimensional Kepler two-body problem. The HNN again exhibits lower trajectory, energy, and angular-momentum drift than the parameter-matched baseline. These experiments provide a controlled study of how Hamiltonian parameterization affects long-horizon prediction and physical consistency across two conservative dynamical systems.

</details>


### [170] [STCAD: Scalable Trajectory Clustering and Anomaly Detection on Terabyte-Scale AIS Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10249)
*Bertram Hage,Alexander Schiøtz,Felix Thomsen,Christian Rand,Peder Heiselberg*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A scalable unsupervised framework for clustering terabyte-scale maritime AIS trajectories using a BERT-based encoder and CURE hierarchical clustering, plus intrinsic anomaly detection, yielding interpretable trajectory groups without predefined cluster counts and distinguishing nominal from anomalous behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Handle massive, unlabeled AIS archives with variable-length trajectories, enabling scalable, interpretable clustering and anomaly detection without supervision.

Method: Encode variable-length trajectories with a custom BERT-based model trained by masked token modeling; cluster with CURE hierarchical clustering; detect anomalies via reconstruction loss and clustering noise assignment in an entirely unsupervised manner.

Result: Applied to national-scale AIS data consisting of billions of messages over a year; achieves stable trajectory clusters and clear separation between nominal and anomalous vessel behavior.

Conclusion: Demonstrates a scalable, interpretable unsupervised framework for maritime trajectory analysis that combines representation learning with hierarchical clustering and intrinsic anomaly detection; suitable for large-scale surveillance and traffic pattern discovery.

Abstract: We present a scalable framework for unsupervised clustering of maritime trajectories derived from terabyte-scale Automatic Identification System (AIS) archives. Variable-length trajectories are encoded with a custom BERT-based model trained via masked token modeling and clustered using CURE hierarchical clustering, producing physically interpretable trajectory groups without requiring a predefined number of clusters. An intrinsic unsupervised anomaly detection method based on reconstruction loss and clustering noise assignment identifies irregular navigation patterns. The framework is demonstrated on a national-scale AIS dataset comprising billions of messages spanning one year, yielding stable trajectory clusters and a clear separation between nominal and anomalous vessel behavior.

</details>


### [171] [CRHT: A Continuous Regression Hybrid Transformer for Vessel Trajectory Prediction with Online Cluster Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10256)
*Alexander Schiøtz,Bertram Hage,Christian Rand,Felix Thomsen,Peder Heiselberg*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes the Continuous Regression Hybrid Transformer (CRHT) for vessel trajectory prediction using AIS, with online K-means sampling to combat geographic data imbalance; a hybrid 1D-CNN and multi-head attention architecture yielding state-of-the-art 1-hour forecasts; highlights trade-off between short-term accuracy and long-horizon stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address geographic bias and realism in vessel trajectory forecasting; mitigate data imbalance and rare maneuvers in AIS-based prediction.

Method: CRHT architecture: 1D convolutions for local kinematics; multi-head self-attention for global temporal context; online K-means cluster sampling to ensure diverse exposure to maneuvers during training.

Result: Superior short-term performance; lowest error at 1-hour horizon; discrete models more stable long-horizon; CRHT provides balance for real-time maritime surveillance.

Conclusion: CRHT improves short-term accuracy and maneuver tracking; suitable for real-time monitoring; indicates a trade-off between short-term precision and long-horizon stability.

Abstract: Accurate vessel trajectory prediction is critical for maritime safety and anomaly detection, yet existing models often struggle with geographic bias and navigational realism. We propose the Continuous Regression Hybrid Transformer (CRHT), a deep learning framework designed to forecast vessel motion using Automatic Identification System (AIS) data. To mitigate spatial data imbalance, we introduce an online K-means cluster sampling strategy that ensures diverse exposure to rare maneuvers during training. Our hybrid architecture integrates 1D convolutional layers for local kinematic feature extraction with a multi-head attention mechanism for global temporal context. CRHT demonstrates superior performance in short-term forecasting, achieving the lowest errors at the 1-hour horizon. The results demonstrate that while discrete models provide high navigational stability over long horizons, CRHT offers an optimal balance of precision and maneuver tracking for real-time maritime surveillance.

</details>


### [172] [Toward Human Rights Benchmarking for LLMs: A Pilot Methodology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10268)
*Savannah Thais,Wm. Matthew Kennedy,Abhigyan Acherjee,Matilda Wysocki,Malcolm Langford,Caitlin Kraft Buchman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HumRightsBench is the first expert-validated, scenario-based benchmark to evaluate LLM reasoning in international human rights law, using an IRAP adaptation (IRAC→IRAP) and expert-annotated real-world scenarios; results show substantial variation in model accuracy (0.339–0.577 overall, task ranges 0.025–0.774), indicating the benchmark’s potential for advancing AI evaluation in a critical legal domain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is no robust, scalable benchmark to assess how well LLMs reason about human rights law; a validated, scenario-based benchmark is needed to test reasoning grounded in the obligation structure of international human rights law.

Method: Develop HumRightsBench, adapt the IRAC framework to IRAP for human rights reasoning, create a pilot series of authentic, expert-annotated scenarios covering diverse human rights issues, and evaluate LLMs on these tasks; include structured evaluation heuristics.

Result: Model accuracy varies across tasks; overall range 0.339–0.577; task-specific ranges 0.025–0.774, indicating non-uniform capability across tasks and highlighting the benchmark’s sensitivity to different reasoning demands.

Conclusion: HumRightsBench provides a robust, scalable methodology for evaluating human rights reasoning in LLMs and represents a meaningful step forward for AI evaluation in a critical legal domain.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly mediate legal determinations over what human rights are realized, and how. Yet, no evaluation benchmark exists to assess whether they can reason correctly about human rights law. To this end, we report our efforts to develop a robust and scalable methodology for creating HumRightsBench: the first expert-validated, scenario-based benchmark for evaluating reasoning grounded in the obligation structure of international human rights law. We adapt the IRAC framework for legal reasoning to better suit the unique reasoning patterns of human rights work (substituting P, "proposing remedies," for C, "legal conclusion," yielding IRAP) to structure our evaluation heuristics. We also produce a pilot series of authentic scenarios designed to implicate the many dimensions of real-world human rights issues and annotated by human rights lawyers and professionals across the world. Ultimately, we find that model accuracy scores range considerably across legal reasoning tasks (overall model performance ranges from 0.339 to 0.577, task min-max ranges from 0.025 to 0.774), which strongly implies that HumRightsBench is a capable instrument for advancing this emerging subfield of AI evaluations science at a critical moment in its evolution.

</details>


### [173] [Power law graph attention: exact generalization of scaled dot-product attention, empirical collapse at inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10288)
*Burc Gokden*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a power-law graph attention (PLGA) that replaces SDPA with a learned bilinear operator G_LM built from a positive tensor A_LM, with extensive theorems, conjectures, and measurements. Claims include exact SDPA recovery at G_LM=I, invariance properties, and a three-stage mechanism, plus self-organized criticality framing. Reproducibility and empirical validation are mixed, relying on released checkpoints and Lean 4 proofs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To generalize fixed dot-product attention by learning a data-generated bilinear operator from power-law structures, enabling invariant and possibly more expressive attention while aiming for theoretical guarantees and verifiable proofs.

Method: Define G_LM from A_LM via elementwise power laws; enforce Perron-Frobenius positivity; replace SDPA with PLGA; incorporate NOTEARS-like DAG regularizer; derive a commutant criterion for relative-position preservation; prove an inference-collapse theorem; perform measurements of invariance and perturbation bounds; outline a conditional three-stage mechanism; implement blockwise training/score under a global Gram; release a checkpoint for validation; machine-check selected proofs in Lean 4.

Result: Unconditional: SDPA is exactly recovered when G_LM = I; A_LM and A_P are strictly positive with PF-structure; posits DAG regularizer obstructs exact acyclicity; nonresonance enables commutant criterion for operator preservation; inference-collapse theorem (input invariance collapses inference to generalized SDPA with a constant operator); measured invariance with relative fluctuations ~1e-6; perturbation bounds exist but do not certify cache; proxy misses decoding margin; conditional mechanism (rotary twirl, concentration, row-map contraction) measured on a released checkpoint; blockwise training and scoring yield identical answers and match TruthfulQA mass metric within 5e-5 per item; self-organized criticality discussed as phenomenology; claims flagged as conjectures or theorem/measurement; proof cores machine-checked in Lean 4.

Conclusion: The work presents a coherent but heavy theoretical framework with mixed empirical support. Many claims hinge on untested assumptions and externally released data; reproducibility depends on access to checkpoints and Lean-verified proofs. The results are falsifiable; further independent proofs, ablation studies, and standard benchmark validations are needed to establish practical merit.

Abstract: The Large Language Model from Power Law Decoder Representations (PLDR-LLM) and its attention, Power Law Graph Attention (PLGA), replace the fixed bilinear form of scaled dot-product attention (SDPA) with a learned, input-generated bilinear operator $G_{LM}$, built from a positive tensor $A_{LM}$ by elementwise power laws. The architecture is fully specified, verified against pinned reference releases; claims are labeled theorem, conditional theorem, measurement, or conjecture. Unconditionally: PLGA contains SDPA exactly at $G_{LM}=I$; $A_{LM}$ and $A_P$ are strictly entrywise positive, with Perron-Frobenius structure on $A_{LM}$; the DAG regularizer has the NOTEARS walk-counting form and positivity obstructs exact acyclicity; and, under nonresonance (satisfied by standard rotary frequencies), a commutant criterion identifies which operators preserve relative-position dependence. An inference-collapse theorem: exact input invariance of deductive outputs collapses inference to generalized SDPA with a constant operator. Measured invariance: relative fluctuations of $10^{-6}$ and below; perturbation bounds quantify but do not certify cached inference; the assembled proxy misses the decoding margin. A conditional three-stage mechanism (rotary twirl, concentration, row-map contraction) is measured on a released checkpoint. Blockwise training and scoring under the global Gram are stated with explicit target exposure; on tested samples, block and sequential scoring select identical answers and agree on the published TruthfulQA probability-mass metric within $5\times 10^{-5}$ per item. Self-organized criticality enters as a phenomenological framework with an intrinsic order parameter; open claims become falsifiable conjectures. Selected proof cores are machine-checked in Lean 4.

</details>


### [174] [MERA: Model Evolution and Routing with Skill Adaptation for Agentic Systems at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10333)
*Yuhang Yao,Zeyu Wang,Wanyi Chen,Tongyun Yang,Yuhang Han,Jie Xiao,Chengke Bao,Tianyi Zhao,Lynn Ai,Eric Yang,Tianyu Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MERA uses multi-cycle self-improvement to elevate small LLMs: replay failed invocations to create teacher demonstrations, distill procedures into a SkillBook, fine-tune a LoRA student, and employ verifier-backed routing. This yields large performance gains on code/evaluation tasks at lower cost, showing that adaptation can enlarge small-model capabilities rather than relying solely on routing to bigger models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the limitation of routing-based approaches that keep small models fixed while offloading difficulty to larger models; the goal is to enhance the small model itself through iterative self-guided learning with verifier-backed evaluation.

Method: In each adaptation cycle: replay failed student invocations to obtain teacher demonstrations; distill recurring procedures into an iteratively updated SkillBook; fine-tune a student LoRA adapter via supervised learning (and optional GRPO). Deployment uses a cost-calibrated router with verifier-backed fallback; candidate SkillBook/adapter/router admitted only if joint replay preserves task quality.

Result: Four-cycle adaptation raises Qwen2.5-Coder-1.5B pass on HumanEval+MBPP from 28.7% to 49.7%; with verifier-backed fallback deployed, 88.3% pass at 60.8% of always-Luna cost. On TAU-2, Qwen3.5-2B improves from 14/35 to 18/35 and matches an unadapted 4B. Demonstrates that verifier-backed multi-cycle adaptation can increase small-model capability rather than only routing around a fixed student.

Conclusion: Verifier-backed multi-cycle adaptation is an effective approach to raise small-model capabilities, delivering substantial performance gains with cost savings, and shifting the focus from routing alone to capability improvement of the student model.

Abstract: LLM agents execute heterogeneous sequences of model calls within a single task: some invocations require careful reasoning, while others are structured steps such as formatting or tool-argument construction. Prior routing methods exploit this asymmetry by assigning easy invocations to a cheaper small model and difficult ones to a large model. Such policies reduce inference cost, but they leave the small model's capability unchanged, so attainable savings remain bounded by the work the student can already solve. MERA instead improves the small model itself, using a single model invocation as the unit of adaptation. In each cycle, MERA replays failed student invocations to obtain execution-verified teacher demonstrations, distills recurring procedures into an iteratively updated SkillBook, and fine-tunes a student LoRA adapter via supervised learning and optional GRPO. Routing serves as supporting machinery for deployment: the improved student is served behind a cost-calibrated router with verifier-backed fallback, and a candidate SkillBook, adapter, or router is admitted only when joint replay preserves task quality. Empirically, four-cycle adaptation raises Qwen2.5-Coder-1.5B from 28.7% to 49.7% pass on held-out HumanEval+MBPP. Under verifier-backed fallback, the deployed policy retains 88.3% pass at 60.8% of always-Luna cost. On TAU-2, a fine-tuned Qwen3.5-2B improves from 14/35 to 18/35 and matches an unadapted 4B model. These results indicate that verifier-backed multi-cycle adaptation can increase small-model capability, rather than only routing around a fixed student.

</details>


### [175] [Accelerated Learning of High Dimensional Functions with a Tensor-Featured Training Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10351)
*Karl Pierce,Yuehaw Khoo,Haizhao Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A method to accelerate optimization of high-dimensional functions using contextual features in a neural network, combining fast rank-1 features with tensor-network decomposed features, and employing randomized tensor decomposition to dramatically reduce storage, enabling training in moderate dimensionalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-dimensional optimization is computationally expensive and storage-intensive; standard DNN-based approaches struggle to scale when the input feature space or function approximation requires large or structured representations.

Method: Introduce contextual features into the first layer of a DNN. Optimize DNN parameters via gradient descent with a fixed input-feature basis; periodically allow the feature layer to update; categorize features as fast rank-1 matrix-free evaluations or tensor features requiring TN decomposition. Distill pretrained DNNs into TNs via discretize-and-decompose. Use randomized tensor decomposition to handle high-dimensional discretized functions, reducing storage by orders of magnitude. Validate by training models in 5–40 dimensions.

Result: Randomized tensor decomposition reduces storage cost of decomposed high-dimensional functions by at least eight orders of magnitude; enables efficient training for models in the 5–40 dimension range.

Conclusion: Hybrid feature representations—fast rank-1 features and TN-decomposed tensor features—together with randomized TN decomposition offer scalable acceleration for high-dimensional optimization, making practical training feasible in moderate dimensional spaces and suggesting potential for further scaling.

Abstract: In this work we present a method to accelerate the optimization of learning high dimensional functions using deep neural network (DNN). This optimization procedure introduces contextual features into the first layer of a DNN. The parameters of DNN are optimized via standard gradient descent while keeping the input-feature basis fixed. After optimization of the DNN parameters, the feature layer is provided a chance to update and change before DNN optimization resumes. The feature layer has two types of functions: those that can be evaluated quickly in a matrix-free way on the domain (i.e. rank-1 features) and more complex features that must first be decomposed using tensor network (TN) decomposition strategies (tensor features). In particular, we study the effect of adding features which distill pretrained DNN into TNs using a discretize and decompose strategy. To efficiently decompose high-dimensional functions constructed from discretized DNN, we leverage a randomized tensor decomposition strategy. Using randomization, we are able to reduce the storage cost of decomposing high dimensional functions by at least 8 orders of magnitude. Using this approach, we are able to efficiently train models between 5 and 40 dimensions.

</details>


### [176] [Efficient Reinforcement Learning for Long-Horizon Tool-Use Agentic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10357)
*Zelei Cheng,Amritansh Mishra,Sambit Sahu,William Campbell*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces SINKFLEX-RL, a modular RL framework for long-horizon, dual-control tool-use tasks that combines environment wrappers, VERL dataflow, group-relative policy optimization without a separate value model, and a sink-aware FlexAttention path to maintain sink scaling under causal/sliding-window masks; demonstrates memory efficiency and performance gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon tool-using agents must reason over goals, domain policies, tool calls, simulator state, and delayed rewards. RL is suitable but faces long contexts, while model-specific attention with masks and normalization adds complexity and memory costs.

Method: A modular system: Gymnasium-compatible environment wrapper; VERL-style rollout dataflow; group-relative policy optimization without a separate value model; sink-aware FlexAttention preserving sink scaling under causal and sliding-window masks.

Result: Validation reward (mean@1) rises from 0.25 early to 0.44 later in Tau2Bench; training-score and trajectory-reward proxies trend upward. In memory benchmarks, optimized attention reduces peak VRAM from 28.06 GB to 22.52 GB at 4096 tokens (19.7% reduction). 8192-token run uses 25.53 GB; baseline runs out of memory.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that integrating environment interfaces, RL dataflow, and attention-kernel design enables memory-feasible long-horizon agent training.

Abstract: Long-horizon tool-using agents must reason over user goals, domain policies, tool calls, simulator state, and delayed verifiable rewards. Reinforcement learning (RL) is a natural fit for this setting, but multi-turn on-policy rollouts create long contexts, while model-specific attention layers may require custom masks and learned sink normalization. We present SINKFLEX-RL, a modular training system for RL in dual-control tool-use environments. The system combines a Gymnasium-compatible environment wrapper, a VERL-style rollout dataflow, group-relative policy optimization without a separate value model, and a sink-aware FlexAttention path designed to preserve model-specific sink scaling under causal and sliding-window masks.
