In a preliminary Tau2Bench retail run, validation reward (mean@1) rises from 0.25 early in training to $0.44$ later in the observed training window, while training-score and trajectory-reward proxies also trend upward. In a fixed-configuration memory benchmark, the optimized attention path reduces peak VRAM from 28.06GB to 22.52GB at 4096 tokens, a $19.7\%$ reduction, and runs the measured 8192-token configuration using $25.53$~GB where the eager baseline runs out of memory. These results illustrate the value of integrating environment interfaces, RL dataflow, and attention-kernel design for memory-feasible long-horizon agent training.

</details>


### [177] [Dreamer-SAC: Off-Policy Learning in Latent World Models for Sample-Efficient Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10386)
*Jiazhuo Li,Linjiang Cao,Qi Liu,Xi Xiong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Dreamer-SAC combines a recurrent latent-state space world model with an off-policy SAC trained in latent space, using a mix of real data and short-horizon imagined rollouts with n-step targets and multi-objective supervision to achieve strong sample-efficient RL for autonomous driving, outperforming DreamerV3, SAC, and PPO with fewer real interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve sample efficiency in reinforcement learning for autonomous driving by mitigating model bias from world-model-based planning while leveraging off-policy learning.

Method: A recurrent state-space world model is trained in latent space alongside an off-policy Soft Actor-Critic algorithm. The training uses both real interactions and short-horizon generated trajectories, employs n-step target estimation, and applies multi-objective supervision to balance driving efficiency and safety.

Result: The approach consistently outperforms representative RL baselines (DreamerV3, SAC, PPO) in autonomous driving scenarios, achieving better performance with substantially fewer real-environment interactions. An inverted-U relation is observed between rollout horizon and policy performance, with short-horizon latent rollouts providing the best trade-off. n-step targets outperform one-step TD targets for value learning.

Conclusion: Dreamer-SAC improves sample efficiency and policy performance by combining latent-space world models with short-horizon, n-step, off-policy learning and multi-objective supervision; short horizons mitigate model bias while still providing informative training signals, suggesting practical guidance for model-based RL in driving tasks.

Abstract: Sample-efficient reinforcement learning for autonomous driving is often limited by the trade-off between data efficiency and model bias. While world models reduce the reliance on costly environment interactions, policy optimization over learned dynamics remains sensitive to prediction errors. This paper proposes the Dreamer-SAC framework, which integrates a recurrent state-space world model with an off-policy soft actor-critic algorithm trained directly in latent space. The framework uses a combination of real interactions and short-horizon generated trajectories with n-step target estimation and multi-objective supervision. Evaluated in autonomous driving scenarios with objectives encompassing driving efficiency and safety, the proposed framework consistently outperforms representative reinforcement learning baselines, including DreamerV3, SAC, and PPO, while achieving improved performance with substantially fewer real environment interactions. Experiments reveal an inverted-U relationship between rollout horizon and policy performance, where short-horizon latent rollouts achieve the best trade-off between additional training signals and accumulated model bias. Furthermore, n-step target estimation demonstrates more effectiveness over one-step temporal-difference targets in exploiting predicted experience for value learning.

</details>


### [178] [Invertible Logits Transformation for Accuracy-Preserving Post-Hoc Uncertainty Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10372)
*Lening Zhao,Qipeng Zhan,Li Shen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: InvLT: an invertible, shared-scalar-MLP calibration applied to logits that preserves argmax, scales to many classes, and outperforms baselines on standard calibration metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-hoc calibration aims to align predicted confidences with empirical accuracy. Existing methods either lack expressivity (temperature scaling), require parameters growing with the number of classes, or can alter the predicted class or score ranking. A scalable, monotone, and prediction-preserving calibrator is needed for large label spaces.

Method: Apply a learned scalar MLP f: R -> R element-wise to the pre-softmax logits, with f shared across all class logits to keep parameter count independent of C. Enforce monotonicity (and thus argmax preservation) softly via a paired inverse network rather than numerical integration, avoiding the overhead of traditional monotone calibrators.

Result: Empirical evaluation across standard image classification benchmarks and architectures shows InvLT consistently outperforms a wide range of post-hoc calibration baselines on calibration metrics, while preserving the original predictions.

Conclusion: InvLT provides a simple, scalable, and effective post-hoc calibration method that maintains ranking and argmax, with improved calibration performance and constant parameter count regardless of the number of classes.

Abstract: Post-hoc calibration aligns a classifier's predicted confidences with its empirical accuracy without retraining. An ideal calibrator should correct nonlinear miscalibration, scale gracefully to large label spaces, and preserve the original predictions; existing methods typically violate at least one of these properties---temperature scaling lacks expressivity, more flexible parametric alternatives introduce parameters that grow with the number of classes $C$, and other expressive methods do not preserve the rank ordering of class scores and may alter the predicted class. We propose \textbf{Invertible Logits Transformation (InvLT)}, which applies a learned scalar MLP $f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}$ element-wise to the pre-softmax logits. Sharing $f$ across all logit dimensions makes the parameter count independent of $C$. Monotonicity of $f$---and hence preservation of the argmax prediction---is softly encouraged via a paired inverse network rather than enforced through the numerical integration required by prior monotone calibrators; this avoids their computational overhead while empirically preserving the original classification accuracy in every setting we evaluate. Across standard image classification benchmarks and a range of architectures, InvLT consistently outperforms a broad set of post-hoc baselines on standard calibration metrics.

</details>


### [179] [Fisher8: Stabilizing Neural Heteroscedastic Regression via Output-Layer Fisher Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10374)
*Sumedh Vemuganti,Nickvash Kani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Fisher8 is an output-layer gradient correction using Fisher geometry to stabilize training for joint mean and uncertainty prediction, reducing reliance on data-dependent hyperparameters and improving likelihood–error tradeoffs and calibrated uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training neural networks to jointly predict mean and uncertainty from noisy data can be unstable due to misalignment between gradient steps and the loss landscape geometry.

Method: Derive Fisher8, an output-layer gradient correction that reorients and rescales updates using Fisher information geometry rather than Euclidean geometry. It introduces no data-dependent hyperparameters beyond learning rate and uses an approximate KL trust radius between successive predictive distributions.

Result: Across multidimensional regression and representation-learning tasks, Fisher8 achieves superior likelihood–error tradeoffs, calibrated uncertainty estimates, and rich uncertainty-aware feature spaces; prior stabilizers converge on overlapping components of this geometric correction.

Conclusion: Fisher8 provides a geometry-based stabilization that aligns updates with local curvature, improving stability and uncertainty estimation with minimal hyperparameter overhead and subsuming prior stabilizers as special cases.

Abstract: Training neural networks to jointly predict mean and uncertainty estimates from noisy observations can be unstable, prompting a series of independent stabilization efforts. We argue that these interventions highlight a common underlying issue where gradient steps are poorly aligned with the geometry of the loss landscape. To better align updates with local curvature, we derive Fisher8, an output-layer gradient correction that reorients and rescales updates using Fisher geometry rather than Euclidean geometry. Unlike past stabilizers, Fisher8 introduces no data-dependent hyperparameters beyond learning rate and admits an approximate KL trust radius between successive predictive distributions. We show that prior stabilizers converge on overlapping components of this geometric correction. Across multidimensional regression and representation-learning tasks, Fisher8 obtains superior likelihood--error tradeoffs, predicts calibrated uncertainty estimates, and learns rich uncertainty-aware feature spaces.

</details>


### [180] [Generator-Guided Inverse Sampling for Lévy-Driven Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10384)
*Tianfu Qi,Jun Wang,Jun Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Structured reverse sampling for Lévy-driven models: decompose into diffusion, small jump, and large jump components; use a neural network to amortize large-jump rate with analytic jump amplitudes; provide a tractable sampler for isotropic linear symmetric α-stable Lévy SDEs; apply to OFDM-SISO channel estimation under mixed Gaussian/impulsive noise with robust performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonlocal, infinite-activity jump dynamics in Lévy-driven generators render reverse processes challenging to characterize by scores alone. A principled, tractable reverse mechanism is needed for effective sampling and inference.

Method: Analyze forward and reverse generators; show the reversed jump term is a state-dependent Markov jump process governed by a nonlocal density ratio. Propose a structured reverse sampler that splits dynamics into diffusion, small jumps, and large jumps. Develop a computationally tractable sampler for isotropic linear Lévy SDEs with symmetric α-stable jumps. Use a neural network only to amortize the rate of large jumps; jump amplitudes drawn from analytically derived conditional distributions. Add efficiency techniques to avoid high-dimensional integration. Extend to observation-guided sampling. Apply to OFDM-SISO channel estimation under mixed Gaussian and impulsive noise.

Result: A tractable sampling framework for Lévy-driven generative models, with a decomposed reverse dynamics and a neural-network-assisted large-jump rate, yielding improved interpretability and computational efficiency. Demonstrated on OFDM-SISO channel estimation under mixed noise, showing robust performance and favorable complexity–performance tradeoffs.

Conclusion: The reversed jump component forms a state-dependent Markov jump process characterized by a nonlocal density ratio. A structured sampler balancing diffusion, small-jump, and large-jump dynamics enables interpretable control over jump activity while maintaining tractable sampling. The approach is effective for isotropic linear Lévy SDEs with symmetric α-stable components and is applicable to real-world problems with impulsive noise.

Abstract: This paper studies inverse sampling for Lévy-driven generative models from the perspective of Markov generators. Unlike conventional diffusion models, Lévy-driven dynamics involve infinite jump activities, which makes their reverse process nonlocal and difficult to characterize using score information alone. We address this challenge by analyzing the forward and reversed generators. It is derived that the reversed jump component generally becomes a state-dependent Markov jump process governed by a nonlocal density ratio. This observation motivates a structured reverse sampler that decomposes the dynamics into diffusion, small jump, and large jump components. Based on this characterization, we develop a computationally tractable sampler for a class of isotropic linear Lévy SDEs with symmetric $α$-stable jump components. For the jump component, the neural network is used only to amortize the rate of large jump activities, while jump amplitudes are generated from analytically derived conditional distributions, which improves interpretability and controllability. Efficient implementation techniques are further introduced under this setting to avoid expensive high-dimensional integration and sampling. The sampler is further adapted to approximate observation-guided sampling and applied to OFDM-SISO channel estimation under mixed Gaussian and impulsive noise. Simulations show robust estimation performance with a favorable tradeoff between complexity and performance.

</details>


### [181] [Share First, Route What Remains: A Unified Framework for Token-Adaptive MoE Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10392)
*Gongli Zhang,Zhulin Liu,C. L. Philip Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified, token-adaptive Mixture-of-Experts (UniF-MoE) framework integrates sharing and routing decisions by decomposing expert computations into aligned blocks, enabling a shared content representation and residual routing that reduces computation while improving accuracy on standard benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MoE systems usually decide sharing of computation and routing independently, ignoring that extracting reusable computation changes what remains and the needed expert capacity. The paper investigates the dependency between sharing and routing by decomposing sparse upcycling into key-value channels to understand how co-activation, coverage, and residual demand interact.

Method: Each expert is partitioned into aligned blocks. A shared-demand score selects how many blocks are shared and weights the routing pathway; key prototypes determine the shared content; complementary demand computes the residual expert count via cumulative routing mass. A Gram regularizer enforces separation and normalization of router embeddings to promote diverse routing directions and reduce overlap. The approach yields a unified framework, UniF-MoE, for token-adaptive MoE computation.

Result: Empirical evaluation on DomainBed and GLUE shows that UniF-MoE improves predictive performance relative to representative static and dynamic MoEs while reducing activated computation, inference latency, and memory usage.

Conclusion: Sharing-first, then routing what remains, yields better efficiency-accuracy trade-offs in MoE systems. UniF-MoE offers a practical, unified approach with code available for replication.

Abstract: Mixture-of-experts (MoE) models have recently moved beyond routing a fixed number of complete experts. Shared-expert designs preserve reusable knowledge, fine-grained methods vary computation within experts, and dynamic routers adapt the number of active experts. Yet these decisions are usually made independently, overlooking a basic dependency: extracting reusable computation changes both what remains and how much expert capacity the remainder needs. We study this dependency by decomposing sparsely upcycled feed-forward experts into key-value channels. Co-activated experts align at a subset of value positions; removing these positions changes expert preference; and greater shared coverage is associated with lower residual expert demand. These observations lead to one principle: share first, then route what remains. We instantiate it in UniF-MoE, a unified framework for token-adaptive MoE computation. Each expert is partitioned into aligned blocks. A shared-demand score sets the shared block count and pathway weight, key prototypes select the shared content, and the complementary demand determines the residual expert count through cumulative routing mass. A Gram regularizer separates and normalizes router embeddings, promoting diverse routing directions, sparse expert overlap, and a simple routing geometry. Experiments on DomainBed and GLUE show that this unified design improves predictive performance over representative static and dynamic MoEs while reducing activated computation, inference latency, and memory. Code is available at https://github.com/existence0420/UniF-MoE.

</details>


### [182] [ELVAE: Evidential Learning-Based Variational Autoencoder for Uncertainty-Aware Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10398)
*Ge Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ELVAE introduces an evidential VAE where each latent dimension is governed by an input-dependent normal-inverse-gamma posterior, yielding explicit latent-location uncertainty for uncertainty-aware generation and stress-testing; with an exact ELBO and full-hierarchy regularization, it demonstrates that anchor reliability and perturbation-induced failure can be separated in MNIST generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Differentiate uncertainty about latent location from conventional data variability and enable controllable, uncertainty-aware generation by using an evidential latent model.

Method: Propose an evidential learning-based VAE (ELVAE) where latent coordinates follow an input-dependent normal-inverse-gamma posterior. Train with an exact evidence lower bound (ELBO) and enforce regularization of the full latent hierarchy to identify uncertainty decomposition. Use the latent-location uncertainty during generation.

Result: In MNIST with a frozen external classifier, latent-location uncertainty stratifies the semantic reliability of generated digits; analysis shows a dominant effect from how reliably an anchor can be re-generated and a secondary effect from uncertainty-scaled perturbations, with variability across seeds and within-class uncertainty ranking.

Conclusion: Latent-location uncertainty is a practical control variable for uncertainty-aware generation, enabling separation of anchor reliability from perturbation effects and supporting stress-testing and reliability-aware synthesis.

Abstract: Variational autoencoders generate samples from probabilistic latent representations but do not distinguish uncertainty about the latent location from variability around it. We formulate ELVAE, an evidential learning-based VAE in which each latent coordinate is governed by an input-dependent normal-inverse-gamma posterior. This hierarchy yields an explicit latent-location uncertainty that can be used during generation, not merely reported after inference: low-uncertainty anchors support more reliable synthetic samples, while high-uncertainty anchors can be deliberately exploited for stress testing. The objective is an exact evidence lower bound, and we show that direct regularization of the full hierarchy is required, since the marginalized latent law alone cannot identify the uncertainty decomposition. In an MNIST generation pilot with a frozen external classifier, this uncertainty clearly stratified the semantic reliability of generated digits. A zero-displacement control revealed that most of the effect reflects how reliably an anchor can be re-generated, while a smaller but distinct component is attributable to uncertainty-scaled perturbation itself. The effect holds only under within-class uncertainty ranking, and its magnitude varies across seeds. These findings support the learned latent-location uncertainty as a practical control variable for uncertainty-aware generation, separating anchor reliability from perturbation-induced failure.

</details>


### [183] [Do Judges Behave Like Algorithms?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10400)
*Riya Manchanda,Eric Chen,Chloe Zhu,Cynthia Rudin,Brandon Garrett,Songman Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Judges in Harris County misdemeanor bail hearings largely follow simple, algorithm-like rules, but notable differences between judges cause inconsistent and potentially unequal outcomes; some cases suggest individualized reasoning beyond available court data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate whether judges’ decisions align with predictable, rule-based processes and how much decision-making relies on variables that can be captured in court data, with implications for AI deployment in the judiciary and standards-based decision-making.

Method: Per-judge machine learning models trained on available court data; variable-importance metrics to identify influential factors; analysis of outcomes for similar cases to assess intra- and inter-judge consistency; evaluation of whether small, interpretable formulas capture decisions.

Result: Overall, judges’ decisions resemble algorithmic rules that can be expressed with small, interpretable formulas. However, substantial between-judge variation exists, leading to inconsistent and potentially unequal treatment of similar defendants.

Conclusion: Where algorithms fail to explain decisions, those cases warrant closer scrutiny to determine whether individualized standards should guide outcomes. The approach can help identify where transparency and standardization may improve fairness, while acknowledging data limitations and jurisdiction-specific factors.

Abstract: What if judges already behave like algorithms? As artificial intelligence and algorithms are deployed in many settings, including the judicial system, many have debated whether judges should be allowed to rely on them. Instead, we ask whether judges follow predictable, algorithmic-like rules already. If judges already follow consistent, formula-like rules based on discrete and static factors such as criminal history, age, and charge type, then judicial behavior may be improved. However, if judges rely on individualized information that cannot be identified through court data, then standards-based decision-making may be more challenging to understand or improve. This work explores these questions by studying judicial decision-making in misdemeanor bail hearings in Harris County, Texas. Using available court data, we investigate whether magistrate judges follow what resembles an algorithm; whether they consider the same variables in their decision-making; and whether they are consistent with themselves and with each other. To do this, we train machine learning models for each judge, measure variable importance metrics to determine important variables for each judge's decision-making, and analyze outcomes of similar cases for judges. Our results reveal that these judges generally behave algorithmically: their decisions can be captured by small, interpretable formulas. However, in some cases, judges differ substantially, leading to surprising inconsistency and unequal treatment across similar defendants. Identifying cases where algorithms do not explain judicial decision-making can improve the justice system by focusing attention on decisions where individualized standards, rather than rules, better explains outcomes.

</details>


### [184] [TideRL: Boosting Agentic RL Goodput with Readiness-Aware Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10402)
*Yanyu Ren,Xizheng Wang,Xiao Liu,Bowen Lv,Hanchen Zhang,Shudan Zhang,Hanyu Lai,Shuai Wang,Li Chen,Dan Li,Jie Tang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TideRL is a readiness-aware elastic RL system for large language models that uses Continuous Task Batching, Resource-Aware Ref-Actor Pipelining, and Elastic Resource Scaling to improve RL training goodput while maintaining task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-turn agentic RL workloads, external environments cause variable task times and wasted GPU occupancy; improving training throughput (goodput) is crucial.

Method: CTB preserves rollout state; RA^2P selects between decoupled streaming and colocated aggregation from ready backlog and arrival interval; ERS moves ranks between rollout and training using readiness signals.

Result: Across text-only and multi-modal workloads, TideRL achieves up to 5.6x improvement in RL training goodput over synchronous baselines and >33% over asynchronous baselines, with similar task performance; KV cache hit rate up 1.58x; per-step training time down up to 44.3%; total waiting time down up to 77.6%.

Conclusion: TideRL effectively boosts RL training throughput for large-model agentic tasks with readiness-aware elasticity and task-state preservation, reducing environment-induced overhead without sacrificing performance.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models is moving toward multi-turn agentic workloads, where rollout tasks repeatedly pause for external environments, resume with growing contexts, and finish at highly variable times. In this setting, RL training goodput, measured by training throughput, matters more than raw GPU occupancy: GPU waiting and repeated prefill recomputation are pure overhead. We present TideRL, a readiness-aware elastic RL system with Continuous Task Batching, Resource-Aware Ref-Actor Pipelining, and Elastic Resource Scaling. CTB preserves useful rollout state, $\textrm{RA}^2\textrm{P}$ selects between decoupled streaming and colocated aggregation from the ready backlog and arrival interval, and ERS moves ranks between rollout and training using the same readiness signals. Across text-only and multi-modal agentic workloads, TideRL improves RL training goodput by up to 5.6$\times$ over synchronous baselines and over 33% over asynchronous baselines, while reaching similar task performance. It also improves KV cache hit rate by 1.58$\times$, reduces per-step training time by up to 44.3%, and cuts total waiting time by up to 77.6%.

</details>


### [185] [Actionable Hallucination Detection: Translating Latent Uncertainty into Agentic Critique](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10430)
*Sanidhya Vijayvargiya,Rahul Lokesh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A lightweight latent critic LoRA intercedes during generation to amplify grounding signals and produce localized feedback, enabling real-time detection and correction of hallucinations with low latency, outperforming external detectors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often follow user instructions even when ungrounded, producing hallucinations; existing detectors either fail to localize these hallucinations or incur high inference latency. There is a need for real-time, localized, low-latency correction mechanisms that can be integrated with generation.

Method: Introduce Latent Critic: a rank-constrained LoRA that runs concurrently with a frozen base LLM to actively reshape the transformer's residual stream. It amplifies latent grounding signals and translates them into localized, natural-language feedback within the same sequence. The approach uses activation patching and layer-wise probing to show rank-invariant, linearly separable uncertainty geometry that transfers better than base representations. Validation uses tool-calling as a granular hallucination instantiation across Qwen and Llama-based models. Demonstrates real-time detection and closed-loop guardrail in ReAct with negligible latency.

Result: Achieves 0.966 AUROC and >80% localization accuracy (e.g., for ungrounded dates). Outperforms equivalent-scale fine-tuned external detectors, semantic-entropy baselines, and passive internal probes in isolating hallucinations. Enables safe interception before execution in a closed-loop ReAct setup, improving self-correction.

Conclusion: The Latent Critic provides an efficient, model-friendly mechanism for real-time, localized detection and mitigation of hallucinations in LLM-based agents, reducing reliance on costly post-hoc detectors and secondary inference loops.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) deployed as AI agents frequently exhibit user specification-grounding failures, executing hallucinated, undesired actions to force a resolution rather than expressing uncertainty. Existing detection methods fail to provide actionable, real-time correction as they either do not localize the hallucinations, or incur prohibitive inference latency. We introduce the Latent Critic, a lightweight low-rank adapter (LoRA) that operates concurrently with a frozen base LLM's generation to actively restructure the transformer's residual stream---amplifying latent grounding signals and translating them into localized, natural language feedback within a single sequence. By refining the base model's native uncertainty signals, this manipulation of the latent space enables reliable, granular detection without the overhead of secondary inference loops. Mechanistic analysis via activation patching and layer-wise probing shows that this rank-invariant behavior restructures pre-existing uncertainty geometry into a linearly separable representation that transfers more reliably than base model representations alone. Using tool-calling as an instantiation of granular hallucinations, we validate the detection and downstream improvements enabled by the Latent Critic architecture across Qwen and Llama-based models. Demonstrating superior real-time efficacy, our approach significantly outperforms equivalent-scale fine-tuned external detectors, semantic entropy baselines, and passive internal probes in isolating hallucinations, achieving 0.966 AUROC and >80% accuracy in localization (e.g., ungrounded: date). When deployed in a closed-loop ReAct environment, the Critic acts as a negligible latency guardrail, intercepting hallucinations before execution to prevent undesired actions while simultaneously leveraging this specific localized feedback to enable efficient agent self-correction.

</details>


### [186] [Do Time-Series Forecasters Use the Right History: Recoverability, Recovery, and Functional Use of Temporal Delays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10433)
*Qipeng Qian,Yuntao Qian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Correct delay reporting does not guarantee use of the correct history; a model can have near-oracle forecast risk while using a wrong lag, and recoverability of delays is distinct from forecast performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To diagnose identifiability and behavior in delay-structured time-series predictors by separating intrinsic ambiguity from model error; quantify recoverability of true delays and whether forecasts reflect that history.

Method: Derive input-conditioned recoverability measures; prove theoretical decoupling between delay reporting reliability and forecast risk; finite-sample point-delay experiments with matched masking tests; compare N-HiTS and TCN; explore routing through reported history and enforcing fixed-report alignment with a hard one-hot control.

Result: Delay reports can become arbitrarily reliable while forecast risk approaches the oracle despite using the wrong lag; in finite samples, among forecasts with correct delay reporting and within 10% of oracle risk, the reported history is functionally unused in 55.4% (N-HiTS) and 92.7% (TCN); routing through reported history removes off-report bypass paths; a hard one-hot control achieves exact fixed-report alignment.

Conclusion: A good forecast, even with a correct delay report, does not guarantee that the model used the right history; evaluating forecast performance without checking actual history usage can obscure the true model behavior.

Abstract: Forecast accuracy does not tell us which past inputs produced a prediction. We separate three questions for time-series models with known delay structure: can the true delay be recovered from the observed data, does the model report it, and does the forecast actually use the same history? We first derive input-conditioned recoverability measures that separate intrinsic ambiguity from model error. We then prove that a delay report can become arbitrarily reliable while forecast risk approaches the oracle even though the predictor still uses the wrong lag. This failure also appears in finite samples on the point-delay task: among forecasts with a correct delay report and normalized excess risk within 10\% of the oracle, the reported history is functionally unused under our matched masking test in 55.4\% of N-HiTS cases and 92.7\% of TCN cases. Finally, we show that routing the prediction through the reported history removes off-report bypass paths; a hard one-hot control achieves exact fixed-report alignment. The main conclusion is simple: a good forecast, even with a correct delay report, does not show that the model used the right history.

</details>


### [187] [Detecting an Effect Is Not Learning to Act on It: A Reward-SNR Floor for LLM Acquisition Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10441)
*Ying Yuan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Distinguish average value of an auxiliary, costly signal from learnability of per-instance acquisition; establish a reward-SNR floor that governs when per-instance routing is possible; show that offline estimable gains are limited and regime gating matters; propose Structured Hypothesis Embeddings (SHE) and demonstrate both calibration and limited effect across datasets; per-instance learned acquisition fails while regime-level design is the realizable unit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Acquiring per-example auxiliary signals (e.g., LLM reasoning, slow or costly measurements) is common, but learning when to use such signals per instance often fails even when the signal is genuinely informative. A mean-effect detectability does not imply per-instance learnability. A reward-SNR floor may cap when a learned acquisition policy is possible.

Method: Theoretical derivation of a reward-SNR detectability threshold rho*(N) ≈ 2.8 / sqrt(N). Empirical validation across per-impression, cluster, regime, and uplift-tree granularities. Introduction of Structured Hypothesis Embeddings (SHE): freeze an LLM to transform user history into ranked, confidence-scored, evidence-grounded intent hypotheses, fused into a recommender. Evaluation on MIND, REES46, and Amazon-Beauty, comparing to a baseline ordered GRU and conducting a noise placebo control to test for true learnability. Analysis of estimability offline shows learned acquisition collapses at all granularities; the realizable unit is a design-time regime gate rather than a per-instance policy.

Result: SHE is faithful and calibratable but its value is backbone- and regime-conditional; it yields significant gains over an ordered GRU on some settings (+0.0114, 95% CI [+0.0030, +0.0209]), yet a global redundancy gap is indistinguishable from zero. All three datasets sit below the inferred reward-SNR floor, so learned per-instance acquisition policies collapse. The authors conclude that the deployable gain arises from regime-level design, not per-instance routing. Code and a one-command reproduction are released.

Conclusion: The key takeaway is that detecting a mean effect is not sufficient for learnability of per-instance acquisition; a reward-SNR detectability floor restricts when per-instance routing is feasible. The practical unit of deployment is a regime-gate design, not a per-instance policy, and SHE provides a calibratable, evidence-grounded representation that is useful within such regime constraints.

Abstract: Many pipelines can pay a per-example cost to acquire an auxiliary, model-derived observation -- an LLM's structured reasoning, a slow oracle, an expensive measurement -- and then must decide when the acquired signal is worth using. Our thesis is a distinction that is easy to miss: detecting that such a signal helps on average is not the same as learning to act on it per instance, and a reward-SNR floor governs when the second is even possible. Even when the signal is faithful and an in-sample oracle picking the top-b examples by realized reward shows a sizable apparent gain, no deployable policy can learn when to acquire it: across per-impression, cluster, regime, and uplift-tree granularities, learned routing never beats random, and a matched-moment noise placebo reproduces >=100% of the oracle's apparent gain -- the apparent "learnable structure" is order statistics of noise. We explain this with one distinction, detecting a mean effect vs. learning a per-instance acquisition policy, and a reward-SNR detectability floor: routing is estimable offline only if the reward SNR rho clears rho*(N) ~= 2.8/sqrt(N), with a positive control confirming a true low-SNR limit rather than a broken pipeline. As a concrete instantiation we introduce Structured Hypothesis Embeddings (SHE): a frozen LLM turns a user history into ranked, confidence-scored, evidence-grounded intent hypotheses, fused into a recommender. On three public datasets (MIND, REES46, Amazon-Beauty), SHE is faithful and calibratable, yet its value is backbone- and regime-conditional (significant over an ordered GRU, +0.0114, 95% CI [+0.0030, +0.0209], but a global redundancy gap indistinguishable from zero), and learned acquisition collapses at every granularity because all three datasets sit below the floor. The realizable unit is a design-time regime gate, not a per-instance policy. We release code and a one-command reproduction.

</details>


### [188] [Critic-Free Pretraining for Efficient Online Reinforcement Learning Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10473)
*Daoyi Li,Yixian Zhang,Chao Yu,Wenbo Ding,Yu Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CFP proposes rejecting offline critic pretraining in O2O RL; initialize a fresh critic during online fine-tuning to avoid inherited biases, improving adaptability and exploration across algorithms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offline critics can become misaligned as data distribution shifts; this hurts policy improvement and exploration; need a paradigm that decouples offline critic from online updates.

Method: Critic-Free Pretraining (CFP): drop offline critic training; use a freshly initialized critic that learns online alongside the policy; compatible with mainstream O2O algorithms; no inherited bias estimates.

Result: CFP matches or improves over conventional O2O methods on various tasks, with especially strong gains on challenging tasks.

Conclusion: A simple yet effective paradigm to stabilize and enhance O2O RL by removing offline critic pretraining, enabling better online adaptation.

Abstract: Offline-to-online (O2O) reinforcement learning aims to leverage policies pretrained on static datasets while improving them through online interaction. However, directly reusing an offline-trained critic can hinder online fine-tuning: as the policy and data distribution change rapidly, value estimates inherited from offline training may become misaligned with the online environment, leading to inaccurate policy improvement and inefficient exploration. To address this problem, we introduce \textbf{C}ritic-\textbf{F}ree \textbf{P}retraining: an efficient paradigm that completely abandons the approach of offline critic training, allowing a freshly initialized critic to adapt without inheriting biased estimates. CFP is compatible with various mainstream O2O algorithms and consistently matches or improves upon conventional O2O algorithms across a diverse set of tasks, with particularly pronounced gains on several challenging tasks.

</details>


### [189] [Exploration-Driven Personalized Federated Reinforcement Learning via Intrinsic Motivation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10499)
*Md Rafid Islam,Rafsan Jany,Zahid Hasan,Ratun Rahman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Exploration-Driven Personalized Federated Reinforcement Learning via Intrinsic Motivation (EDPFRL-IM) that uses curiosity-driven intrinsic rewards (RND) at each client, with privacy-preserving server design, leading to improved exploration, personalization, and sample efficiency in sparse/delayed reward environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome overreliance on extrinsic rewards in PFRL and to enhance exploration under non-stationary, sparse-reward settings while preserving client privacy.

Method: Each client augments extrinsic reward with intrinsic RND signal; server distributes global exploration priors and collects only minimal novelty summaries instead of raw experiences or gradients, enabling coordinated exploration without data leakage.

Result: Empirical results show superior policy personalization and sample efficiency compared to average PFRL benchmarks, especially in delayed/sparse reward tasks.

Conclusion: EDPFRL-IM demonstrates that explicit intrinsic motivation and privacy-preserving coordination can improve exploration and learning in federated RL without sharing sensitive client data.

Abstract: Personalized Federated Reinforcement Learning (PFRL) takes a decentralized approach to storing and accessing information based on past experiences while keeping each client's data private during the learning of each client's policy. Many current methods for PFRL rely heavily on exploiting existing reinforcement learning reward signals to derive an optimal policy for each client, thereby neglecting exploration in non-stationary or sparse-reward environments. In this work, we introduce a new exploration-driven framework, Exploration-Driven Personalized Federated Reinforcement Learning via Intrinsic Motivation (EDPFRL-IM), that leverages an inherent curiosity-driven exploration at each client to promote local exploration and protect client privacy. Furthermore, to facilitate policy discovery via exploration in previously unexplored state spaces, clients add an intrinsic random network distillation (RND) signal to their extrinsic reward. Additionally, the server does not have access to clients' raw experiences or local gradient estimates; instead, the server sends global exploration priors and collects minimal novelty summaries from each client to enable both diverse and coordinated exploration among clients. Experiments in benchmark environments show that our framework outperforms average PFRL benchmarks in policy personalization and sample efficiency, primarily in delayed and sparse reward systems. Overall, EDPFRL-IM enables the integration of a flexible exploratory learning structure into federated reinforcement learning systems while preserving client privacy.

</details>


### [190] [Coordinating the Unknown Lipschitz Constant in Multiplayer Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10526)
*Ricardo Parada,Chenzhang Zhao,William Chang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Cooperative multi-agent bandits in continuous Lipschitz action spaces with unknown Lipschitz constant are studied under three information structures. The authors design algorithms that estimate the Lipschitz constant, discretize the joint action space, and apply a discrete cooperative bandit method, aiming for agreement on discretization without communication after learning starts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address decentralized applications where multiple agents must optimally explore a continuous action space with Lipschitz structure, but lack centralized knowledge of the Lipschitz constant and may have limited information sharing.

Method: Estimate the unknown Lipschitz constant, construct a discretization of the joint action space, and apply a cooperative bandit algorithm on the induced discrete problem. Analyze three information structures: (A) unobserved actions with common rewards, (B) observed actions with independent rewards, and (C) unobserved actions with independent rewards. Ensure agents converge to the same discretization without communication, using the data they obtain. For structures B and A, agreement is achieved for free; for C, agreement is achieved via a dithering quantization of the estimate without incurring leading-order regret.

Result: Regret guarantees are established for all three structures. In cases with common rewards or observed actions, the agents naturally converge to a common discretization. In the absence of shared information (case C), agreement is still attainable through a carefully designed dithered quantization of the Lipschitz estimate, without affecting the leading-order regret.

Conclusion: Discretization-consensus-based cooperative bandits are feasible under practical information structures, with the major hurdle of agreement on discretization solvable without communication or at negligible cost in regret, showcasing a robust approach to cooperative learning in continuous action spaces.

Abstract: Motivated by decentralized applications, we study cooperative multi-agent bandits in continuous (Lipschitz) action spaces when the Lipschitz constant is unknown. We consider three information structures: (A)~unobserved actions with common rewards, (B)~observed actions with independent rewards, and (C)~unobserved actions with independent rewards. In each case we design and analyze an algorithm that estimates the Lipschitz constant, chooses a discretization of the joint action space, and applies a cooperative bandit method to the induced discrete problem. Players never communicate once learning starts, so the central difficulty is that they must reach the \emph{same} discretization from their own data. We prove regret guarantees showing that common rewards and observable actions each supply this agreement for free, and that in their absence agreement can still be bought, through a dithered quantization of the estimate, at no cost in the leading order of the regret.

</details>


### [191] [Robust Multi-Agent Bandits with Heavy-Tailed Rewards and Information Asymmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10529)
*Daphne Feng,Ricardo Parada,Lily Jiang,Sophia Yi,William Chang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: We analyze multi-agent MAB with heavy-tailed rewards under three information-asymmetry regimes, proposing robust decentralized algorithms that achieve regret close to centralized heavy-tailed rates; experiments with Pareto rewards validate theory and reveal trade-offs among synchronization, coordination, and exploration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world bandit applications often feature heavy-tailed rewards and decentralized, information-asymmetric interactions. Extending heavy-tailed bandit theory to multi-agent, distributed settings is important for practical performance guarantees.

Method: For each information-asymmetry regime, design robust decentralized algorithms capable of handling heavy-tailed reward distributions (likely via robust mean estimators such as median-of-means/Catoni-type M-estimators) and restricted communication. Derive regret bounds that match centralized heavy-tailed rates up to constants and conduct experiments under Pareto-distributed rewards to validate theory and explore trade-offs.

Result: The paper provides regret guarantees that nearly match centralized heavy-tailed rates in all three regimes and confirms these findings with experiments on Pareto-distributed rewards, illustrating the impact of synchronization, coordination, and exploration on performance across regimes.

Conclusion: Robust decentralized algorithms for heavy-tailed multi-armed bandits can achieve near-optimal regret across diverse information-asymmetry settings, with regime-specific coordination and synchronization considerations shaping exploration efficiency.

Abstract: The multi-armed bandit problem is a central framework in sequential decision-making, extensively studied under sub-Gaussian reward assumptions. However, real-world applications often involve heavy-tailed reward distributions and decentralized, information-asymmetric interactions. We study multi-agent multi-armed bandits with heavy-tailed rewards under three information-asymmetry regimes: unobserved actions with common rewards, observed actions with independent rewards, and unobserved actions with independent rewards. We develop robust decentralized algorithms for each setting and derive regret guarantees that nearly match centralized heavy-tailed rates. Experiments on a Pareto-distributed reward environment validate our theoretical findings and illustrate the trade-offs between synchronization, coordination, and exploration across the three regimes.

</details>


### [192] [Retrieval-Corrected Conformal Prediction for Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10553)
*Sangjin Jin,Kangmin Kim,Junhyeong Lee,Yongjae Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A retrieval-augmented conformal prediction method (RCCP) for time-series intervals that uses similar past residuals as local evidence and corrects calibration via a scalar conformal adjustment, achieving-target coverage with improved Winkler scores and low overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard conformal prediction for time series suffers from inefficiency due to temporal dependence and nonstationarity; local calibration methods exist but may dilute evidence relevant to the current forecast. A retrieval-based approach can provide targeted evidence from past residuals, enabling better local calibration.

Method: Retrieve past one-sided residuals similar to the current forecast, construct an asymmetric interval from these retrieved residuals, and calibrate the resulting retrieval error with a scalar conformal correction to achieve coverage. Provide a coverage-gap bound based on the stability of the normalized retrieval error distribution.

Result: RCCP attains the target coverage in standard benchmarks across backbone forecasters, achieves the lowest Winkler scores with fewer severe misses, and exhibits low calibration and inference overhead.

Conclusion: Retrieval-corrected calibration is an effective and scalable approach for uncertainty quantification in time series forecasting; RCCP provides robust, coverage-guaranteed intervals with practical computational efficiency; code is available at the provided repository.

Abstract: Conformal prediction (CP) provides distribution-free prediction intervals for fixed forecasters, but its standard calibration procedure is often inefficient for time series data, where forecast errors are temporally dependent and change across time and operating conditions. Recent time series CP methods improve local calibration using recent, weighted, or localized residuals. Yet local calibration can remain indirect, since broad residual weighting or additional adaptation procedures may dilute the evidence most relevant to the current prediction. This motivates a simple retrieval and correction strategy that selects similar past residuals as local evidence and then corrects the coverage error left by retrieval. In this paper, we propose Retrieval--Corrected Conformal Prediction (RCCP), a retrieval-augmented calibration method for time series prediction intervals. RCCP builds an asymmetric interval from retrieved one-sided residuals and calibrates its normalized retrieval error with a scalar conformal correction. Thus, retrieval provides local residual evidence, while conformal correction determines the final scale needed for coverage. We provide a coverage-gap bound based on the stability of the normalized retrieval error distribution. Across standard benchmarks and backbone forecasters, RCCP attains the target coverage in every setting and achieves the lowest Winkler scores, with fewer severe misses. RCCP also achieves low calibration and inference overhead, showing that retrieval-corrected calibration is an effective and scalable approach to uncertainty quantification in time series forecasting. Code is available at https://github.com/jinsaaang/rccp.

</details>


### [193] [MARCO: Click-Intent Decomposition for Calibrated Ads Conversion Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10562)
*Shiwen Shen,Xiru Huang,Liang Luo,Jianbo Sun,He Lyu,Zihang Fu,Ivonne Xu,Zhizhuo Li,Zhengyu Zhang,Pei-Ju Sung,Yunmiao Wang,Zixuan Wang,Zhengli Zhao,Qiang Jin,Mike Jermann,Mingda Li,Yang Xiao,Bhavana Challa,Brooke Bian,Yang Li,Ashish Chamoli,Bibek Bhusal,Danning Di,Yuan Jin,Meet Raval,Zhiwen Chen,Boyao Sun,Shuguang Wang,Yunlong He,Yantao Yao,Sagar Chordia,Wenlin Chen,Santanu Kolay,Qin Huang,Ellie Wen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Decompose clicks by user intent to train per-intent CVR heads, then compose at serving; improves calibration and conversions with scalable attribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard CVR models treat all clicks equally, ignoring the free intent signal from click types, causing bias that is masked by aggregate calibration.

Method: Use logged click type as label to train per-intent CVR heads on homogeneous populations; at serving time, compose per-intent CVR estimates under the predicted intent distribution; provide theoretical guarantees (no population risk increase, headroom under squared loss, non-negativity under deployed loss) and a routing-efficiency dial; model multi-impression/multi-click attribution as bias-variance tradeoff akin to RL return, advocating last-impression/first-click attribution under constraints with three end-to-end consistency conditions.

Result: Offline and online validation show per-intent calibration approaches ~100%; conversions per click up by +2.80%; cumulative topline improvement of +0.98%.

Conclusion: Decomposition yields finite-capacity estimation/calibration gains without changing the population-optimal score; MARCO enables scalable multi-intent ad ranking with improved per-intent calibration and guided attribution.

Abstract: Not all clicks are equal. Industrial ads ranking decouples conversion probability into click-through rate (CTR) and post-click conversion rate (CVR), yet treats every click as the same event. In reality, users provide a free, self-generated signal of intent through their physical UI interactions. Different click types on the same ad exhibit a 4-fold difference in actual conversion rates. By conflating these signals, the standard CVR model under-predicts high-intent clicks and over-predicts low-intent ones, which is a bias masked by near-perfect aggregate calibration. We propose MARCO (Multi-intent Ads Ranking Composition Optimization), a framework that resolves this bias by decomposing each click by intent. Using the logged click type as a free behavioral label, MARCO trains per-intent CVR heads on homogeneous populations, and at serving time composes their per-intent CVR estimates under a predicted distribution over intents. Theoretically, we prove that decomposition never raises population risk, give the exact headroom under squared loss and non-negativity under the deployed loss, and show through a routing-efficiency dial how much of it reaches serving. Because the population-optimal score is unchanged, any gain is a finite-capacity estimation and calibration effect that we validated both offline and online. For deployment at scale, we further cast multi-impression, multi-click attribution as credit assignment with a bias-variance tradeoff analogous to RL return estimation, showing last-impression, first-click attribution is the low-bias, low-variance, deterministic choice under production constraints, and derive three consistency conditions enforced end-to-end at scale. Deployed at binary intent granularity, MARCO corrects per-intent calibration to approximately 100%, lifts conversions per click by +2.80%, and drives +0.98% cumulative improvement in topline metrics.

</details>


### [194] [BREAD: Baseline-Referenced Explanations for Anomaly Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10587)
*Jiaqi Qiu,Rob Goedhart,Jannis Kurtz,Inez M. Zwetsloot*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a scalable, baseline-referenced, model-agnostic diagnosis method for AI-based prospective anomaly detection, with mathematical guarantees of higher faithfulness than LIME under mean-shift anomalies; validated via simulations and a real-world case.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for faithful, scalable feature attribution for AI-driven anomaly detection in high-dimensional nonlinear settings; existing XAI methods struggle with noise attribution and scalability.

Method: Introduce a baseline-referenced diagnosis approach that uses both the anomalous observation and normal baseline data; provide mathematical guarantees of faithfulness compared to LIME under mean-shift anomalies; design likely algorithmic steps and comparative evaluation.

Result: Demonstrates higher faithfulness in detecting anomaly-causing features vs LIME; simulation studies and a real-world case study show improved diagnosis accuracy for AI-based SPM.

Conclusion: The proposed method improves diagnosis fidelity for AI-based prospective anomaly detection, is scalable, and robust to feature noise; offers theoretical guarantees and empirical validation.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI)-based prospective anomaly detection methods are increasingly deployed in high-dimensional and nonlinear settings. Among these approaches, AI-based statistical process monitoring (SPM) is widely used, providing a structured framework for prospective monitoring. Once an anomaly is detected, a diagnosis method is needed to identify the features driving the flagged observation away from normal behaviour. Traditional SPM diagnosis methods are typically designed for specific detection models and cannot be directly applied to AI-based methods. Model-agnostic explainable AI (XAI) offers a general framework for feature relevance explanation. However, existing methods suffer from scalability limitations or assign relevance to noise features, reducing diagnosis accuracy. We propose a scalable, baseline-referenced diagnosis method that uses both the anomalous observation and normal baseline information. We provide mathematical guarantees that under a mean-shift anomaly setting, the proposed method achieves higher faithfulness in detecting the features causing the anomaly compared to LIME. Simulation studies and a real-world case study validate the effectiveness of the proposed method and show that it generates more faithful and accurate diagnosis results for AI-based prospective anomaly detection methods.

</details>


### [195] [$β$-VAEs as Effective Theories: Tolerance-Dependent Dimension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10599)
*Johannes Hirn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Nonlinear VAEs retain the spectral-cutoff intuition: low-utility coordinates are collapsed, but nonlinear interactions shift/broaden collapse thresholds. The ordinal utility ranking across resolved coordinates is largely preserved, so the spectral cutoff still acts as a utility cutoff; deeper networks concentrate utility in the top coordinates at the expense of the tail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether the β-VAE spectral-cutoff and PCA-based collapse observed in linear Gaussian VAEs extend to fully connected nonlinear VAEs trained on real-world data (WorldClim).

Method: Compare a linear Gaussian VAE (PCA-driven) with fully connected nonlinear VAEs trained on WorldClim. Analyze collapse onsets, utility thresholds, and coordinate rankings; construct effective-dimension curves; examine how depth affects the distribution of utility (head-tail tradeoff).

Result: Nonlinear interactions shift and broaden collapse onsets; thresholds no longer align exactly with reconstruction utilities; however, the relative ordering of utilities across resolved ranks remains preserved, so the spectral cutoff continues to function as a utility cutoff. Increasing depth concentrates utility into the first few coordinates but degrades tail fidelity. Effective-dimension curves reveal a head–tail tradeoff. 

Conclusion: The effective-description logic of spectral cutoff generalizes to nonlinear VAEs on real data: utility-based dimensional control persists with nonlinearities, though thresholds broaden and depth introduces a head-tail tradeoff that tightens top-coordinate utility at the expense of the tail.

Abstract: In a $β$-VAE, increasing the regularization strength acts as a spectral cutoff by collapsing low-utility latent coordinates.
  In the linear Gaussian VAE, the collapse order matches the ranking of reconstruction utilities exactly, because both are set by the PCA spectrum. We ask which parts of this picture survive in fully connected nonlinear VAEs trained on WorldClim.
  We find that nonlinear interactions shift and broaden collapse onsets, so thresholds no longer coincide exactly with utilities. However, the common ordering is preserved over the resolved ranks, so the spectral cutoff still acts as a utility cutoff and the effective-description logic carries through.
  The resulting effective-dimension curves reveal a head--tail tradeoff: increasing depth concentrates utility into the first few coordinates but worsens tail fidelity.

</details>


### [196] [Compute-Optimal Is Not Cluster-Optimal: Systems-Aware Scaling for Sparse Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10605)
*Soumajyoti Sarkar,Yuxin Tang,Sheng Zha*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MOSAIC unifies architecture and systems design for large-scale pretraining by coupling a predictive scaling law with a calibrated performance model to optimize MoE sparsity and layout under compute and hardware constraints, demonstrating the need for joint co-design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the mismatch between scaling-law driven architecture selection and hardware-aware systems optimization in frontier language-model pretraining, particularly for sparse mixtures of experts (MoE) where sparsity affects loss and systems metrics.

Method: Introduce MOSAIC, a joint architecture–systems optimization framework that couples a scalable model of loss with a calibrated performance model (Model FLOPs Utilization, MFU, communication, memory, parallel layout). Instantiate for sparse MoE, fit a scaling law including sparsity as a dimension, sweep active parameters from 1.04e8 to 2.7e9 with total sizes up to 79B, and evaluate MFU, memory, and routing/layout under system constraints to identify optimal sparsity.

Result: Without considering systems constraints (an efficiency-agnostic compute budget), the loss decreases monotonically with increased sparsity and the optimum moves to the upper boundary of available data. When system constraints are incorporated via MOSAIC, an optimal sparsity emerges dictated by hardware and cluster constraints, highlighting that the best frontier is achieved through joint architecture and systems optimization rather than isolated scaling.

Conclusion: A unified architecture–systems co-design approach is essential for frontier language-model training, as demonstrated by the MoE-focused MOSAIC framework.

Abstract: In large-scale pretraining, the algorithm, architecture, and systems decisions are conventionally made in disconnected stages. A scaling law stage selects an architecture and training recipe, optimizing loss under compute constraints, and a separate systems stage then optimizes the implementation for hardware efficiency. In this work, we develop MOSAIC, which formulates model architecture and systems co-design as an optimization problem. MOSAIC couples a predictive scaling law with a calibrated performance model that estimates Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU), communication cost, memory footprint, and the best parallel layout. We instantiate the framework for sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language models, where expert count, routing sparsity, and other MoE layer dimensions affect both the loss and systems efficiency. We fit a scaling law on sparse MoE models trained on text data, whose scaling dimensions include the sparsity factor, which is the fraction of model parameters inactive per token in a forward pass. The scaling law sweeps in our work span active parameters from $104$ million to $2.7$ billion and total model sizes reaching $79$ billion parameters. We show that, within the calibrated sparsity range, an efficiency-agnostic model-FLOPs budget admits no interior optimal sparsity. The fitted loss decreases monotonically with sparser models and the compute optimum lies at the upper boundary of the data support. An optimal sparsity in MoE models instead emerges under the cluster's systems constraints, as captured by MOSAIC. Our results argue for a shift towards unified architecture and systems co-design for frontier language model training.

</details>


### [197] [Pair-Centric Graph Rewiring for Over-Squashing via Optimal Transport-Guided Communication Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10619)
*Yan Wang,Chuan-Xian Ren*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PairAlign introduces a pair-centric rewiring framework that targets over-squashing by modeling demand–support shortage and using optimal transport for budget allocation; it improves MPNN performance across backbones on standard benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MPNNs struggle to propagate task-relevant signals distributed across distant graph regions, leading to over-squashing. Existing rewiring focuses on edge-level or global connectivity and under budgets; there is a need for pairwise, budget-aware guidance to identify the most impactful communications to augment.

Method: PairAlign computes a pair-level shortage as the ratio of original-graph structural demand to current-graph finite-hop propagation support. It leverages this shortage to select edge insertions that alleviate over-squashing, acknowledging that new edges can both enable useful walks and dilute existing mass. It further introduces an Optimal Transport-guided rewiring scheme to coordinate the limited edge budget with shortage targets, achieving better coverage than greedy approaches.

Result: Empirical evaluation on standard graph benchmarks shows that PairAlign improves performance across various message-passing backbones, validating the effectiveness of pair-level repair for alleviating over-squashing.

Conclusion: PairAlign demonstrates that pair-level repair—guided by demand–support shortage and coordinated by OT-based budget allocation—is a useful and effective route to mitigate over-squashing in GNNs.

Abstract: Message-passing neural networks (MPNNs) often struggle when task-relevant information is distributed across distant regions of a graph, since local propagation must compress remote signals through limited structural interfaces. Graph rewiring provides a structural response to over-squashing. Most existing methods rely on edge-level bottleneck scores or graph-level connectivity surrogates. With a limited rewiring budget, the key question is which pairwise communications most need structural support. This paper proposes PairAlign, a pair-centric graph rewiring framework that makes this question explicit through demand-support shortage. Specifically, PairAlign combines original-graph structural demand with current-graph finite-hop propagation support; their ratio highlights interactions whose communication demand is poorly supported by topology, and our theory shows that this score provides a computable proxy for the corresponding Jacobian-based shortage with a pair-level interpretation of over-squashing. Our theory reveals a two-sided effect of edge insertion: a new edge can create useful walks and simultaneously dilute existing normalized transition mass. Guided by this observation, PairAlign optimizes shortage to favor edge additions that alleviate over-squashing. Beyond selecting useful additions, PairAlign further introduces an Optimal Transport-guided rewiring mechanism to coordinate the finite edge budget for pair-level structural compatibility and shortage-target coverage. It formulates communication alignment between the candidate edge budget and the shortage targets, and the theory shows that this allocation covers shortage targets more broadly and effectively than a greedy-local assignment. Experiments on standard graph benchmarks show PairAlign's improvement across message-passing backbones, validating pair-level repair as an effective route for alleviating over-squashing.

</details>


### [198] [ProbGuard: Calibrated Safety Risk Estimation from LLM Output Distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10621)
*Xinzhe Huang,Biwu Yao,Kedong Xiu,Mengnan Zhao,Di Wang,Puning Zhao,Tianhang Zheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProbGuard, a probabilistic, architecture-agnostic guardrail for LLM safety, uses early output distributions and Monte-Carlo sampling to estimate unsafe continuation probability, achieving superior calibration and robust jailbreak resistance with early stopping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety assessment is inherently uncertain, especially in early generation; discrete classification loses rich probabilistic information in the LLM output distribution; a distribution-aware, architecture-agnostic guardrail can enable earlier, calibrated safety risk estimation and stopping of unsafe outputs.

Method: Take the LLM's generated prefix distribution. Model the unsafe probability of continuation; estimate this risk via Monte-Carlo sampling of continuation dynamics. Post-train/calibrate on distributional signals to produce calibrated safety risk. Enable early stopping of unsafe ongoing outputs. Architecture-agnostic.

Result: ProbGuard achieves best calibration across nine model–dataset settings, reducing average Brier score by 79.6% and ECE by 71.9% versus the best baseline; limits jailbreak attack success to at most 1% across six attacks after observing the first ten decoding steps.

Conclusion: ProbGuard demonstrates that leveraging distributional signals with probabilistic risk estimation yields better-calibrated, architecture-agnostic guardrails, enabling earlier detection and improved robustness against jailbreak attempts.

Abstract: Recent research on Large Language Model (LLM) safety has widely adopted guardrails to identify unsafe LLM outputs. Existing guardrails typically formulate safety assessment as a deterministic classification task, mapping a discrete token sequence to a discrete safety label. However, this paradigm has two limitations: First, safety assessment is inherently an uncertain problem, particularly during the early generation state. Second, relying solely on discrete token sequences discards the rich probabilistic information embedded in the LLM output distribution. To address these limitations, we propose the first completely probabilistic architecture-agnostic guardrail \textsc{ProbGuard} to leverage the LLM early output distributional signals for estimating and calibrating the safety probability, thereby enabling early stopping of unsafe ongoing outputs. Specifically, given an LLM's generated prefix distribution, we formulate the safety risk as the unsafe probability of its continued generation dynamics and estimate this risk by Monte-Carlo sampling. Through post-training on the distributional signals and calibrated safety risk, \textsc{ProbGuard} achieves the best calibration performance across all nine model--dataset combination settings, reducing the average Brier score and ECE by 79.6\% and 71.9\%, respectively, over the best baseline. \textsc{ProbGuard} further limits the attack success rate to at most 1\% across six representative jailbreak attacks after observing the LLM early output distributions from only the first ten decoding steps.

</details>


### [199] [IADD-TR: Intervention-Aware Dynamics Decoupling with Targeted Regularization for Model-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10634)
*Zefeng Liang,Jie Qiao,Ruichu Cai,Weilin Chen,Zhifeng Hao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified framework (IADD-TR) decouples dynamics and improves policy learning in model-based RL by addressing policy-induced data bias; it uses a zero-action anchor for dynamics factorization and a targeted regularization that yields doubly robust policy gradients, achieving competitive performance and better sample efficiency on MuJoCo tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Policy-induced data bias in model-based RL leads to entanglement between actions and environmental evolution and biased counterfactual value estimates. There is a need to decouple dynamics from policy data and to stabilize policy improvements under imperfect models.

Method: Introduce Intervention-Aware Dynamics Decoupling (IADD) to factor transitions into an action-intervention stage and an action-free natural evolution stage with a zero-action anchor. Establish identifiability up to invertible within-block transformations. Develop Targeted Regularization (TR) derived from the efficient influence function of a replay-state policy-gradient, augmenting the critic with action-density-scaled residuals and optimizing a targeted loss to achieve doubly robust policy-gradient estimation when either critic or replay action density is well-specified.

Result: Empirical validation on five MuJoCo tasks shows competitive returns and improved sample efficiency, demonstrating effective handling of data bias and robust policy improvement through the proposed framework.

Conclusion: IADD-TR provides a principled, practical approach to reduce policy-induced data bias in model-based RL by decoupling dynamics and applying targeted regularization, achieving strong empirical performance and offering a path toward doubly robust policy optimization.

Abstract: Model-based reinforcement learning (MBRL), which learns environment dynamics to generate synthetic experience, is a promising approach to sample-efficient decision making. Numerous methods have been developed to improve dynamics prediction and policy optimization for MBRL through uncertainty estimation, model regularization, and conservative value learning. However, these methods typically treat the transition model and critic as monolithic predictors, overlooking the policy-induced data bias. Consequently, action can become entangled with environmental evolution, while uneven action coverage may distort the counterfactual value estimates used for policy improvement. To address this, we propose IADD-TR, a unified framework combining Intervention-Aware Dynamics Decoupling (IADD) and Targeted Regularization (TR). IADD factorizes transitions into an action-intervention stage and an action-free natural evolution stage, using a zero-action anchor to resolve the non-uniqueness of this two-stage factorization for robust generalization. Its latent and state-aligned components are identifiable up to an invertible within-block transformation and pointwise, respectively. For policy learning, we derive TR from the efficient influence function of a replay-state policy-gradient functional. TR augments the critic with an action-density-scaled residual correction and optimizes a targeted loss, yielding doubly robust policy-gradient estimation when either the critic or the replay action density is consistently specified. Extensive experiments on five MuJoCo tasks show that IADD-TR achieves competitive returns with improved sample efficiency.

</details>


### [200] [Optimize Cheap, Deploy Strong: Cost-Aware Cross-Tier Transfer for Evolutionary Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10694)
*Tal Oved,Roi Pony,Oshri Naparstek,Udi barzelay*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Decouples LLM roles across tiers to reduce cost in evolutionary prompt optimization, using cheap-tier answering with a strong model for reflection/variation and cross-tier transfer; provides cost-controlled conditions for when this substitution works; achieves 5.6-14x, up to 25-54x cost reductions across multiple tasks and models while maintaining or improving performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High cost of fitness evaluation in evolutionary optimization of LLM prompts and agentic programs, driven by running a candidate LLM on large validation sets.

Method: Partition the LLM's roles into three tiers: cheap-tier for high-volume answering, a strong model reserved for reflection/variation operators, and cross-tier transfer to deploy evolved prompts on stronger targets. Provide a cost-controlled characterization of when cheap-tier search substitutes for target-tier search and where it fails, evaluated across multiple tasks and model families.

Result: The proposed approach achieves prompts that match or exceed same-tier optimization while placing over 96% of search tokens on the cheapest tier, with 5.6-14x lower search cost (and 25-54x in cases with long chain-of-thought fitness calls) across HotpotQA, IFBench, LiveBench-Math, HoVer, and eleven models from four families.

Conclusion: Cheap-tier, decoupled-role search can substitute for target-tier search to substantial cost savings in evolutionary LLM prompt optimization, with caveats on scenarios requiring heavy reasoning that may limit cross-tier transfer effectiveness.

Abstract: Evolutionary optimization of LLM prompts and agentic programs (e.g., GEPA) is dominated by fitness evaluation: scoring each candidate runs an answering LLM over a validation set, so the evaluator's price tier dictates total search cost. We restructure that search by decoupling the three roles an LLM plays, running the high-volume answering role on the cheapest tier, reserving a strong model for the rare reflection/variation operator, then exploiting upward cross-tier transfer to deploy the cheaply evolved prompt on a stronger target. We contribute a cost-controlled characterization of when cheap-tier search substitutes for target-tier search, and where it fails. Across four tasks (HotpotQA, IFBench, LiveBench-Math, HoVer) and eleven models in four model families, the resulting prompt matches or exceeds same-tier optimization while placing over 96% of search tokens on the cheapest tier, at 5.6-14x lower search cost, rising to 25-54x where reasoning tiers emit long chains of thought on every fitness call.

</details>


### [201] [ProTAGAD: A Foundation Model for TAG Anomaly Detection with Decoupled Topological and Textual Prototypes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10699)
*Ziyan Wang,Liwen Wu,Cheng Xie,Song Gao,Zhenli He,Xin Jin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A decoupled dual-prototype foundation model for text-attributed graphs (TAGs) to improve anomaly detection by separately modeling structural normality and semantic consistency, addressing the blurred-anomaly-boundary (BAB) issue and enhancing cross-domain robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: TAG anomaly detection blends topology and text, but holistic, coupled aggregation propagates noise across modalities, causing a blurred decision boundary and poor cross-domain generalization; a decoupled approach can isolate anomaly cues and improve robustness.

Method: Introduce dual prototype banks for topology and text to independently capture structural normality and semantic consistency. Employ decoupled aggregation that prevents cross-modal interference, inspired by foundation-model scalability and cross-domain generalization. Validate on 14 diverse benchmarks with ablation studies.

Result: Consistent state-of-the-art performance in cross-domain settings. Ablations confirm the presence of BAB in conventional coupled detectors and demonstrate mitigation via decoupled prototypes across datasets.

Conclusion: Decoupled topological and textual prototypes effectively isolate anomaly cues in TAGs, mitigating BAB and improving cross-domain generalization for TAG anomaly detection.
