Abstract: Text-Attributed Graphs (TAGs), endowed with abundant textual content along with topological structures, have emerged as a versatile backbone for real-world anomaly detection spanning large language model security, social network moderation, and cyber threat identification. Unlike conventional Graph Anomaly Detection (GAD), which relies primarily on structural irregularities, TAG anomaly detection must jointly leverage both topological patterns and fine-grained textual semantics to capture nuanced anomalous behaviors. The current GNN-based anomaly detectors adopt holistic message-passing schemes that indiscriminately fuse structural proximity and textual semantics during propagation, leading to deep cross-modality coupling. This entanglement acts as a noise amplifier, obscuring subtle anomalous signals and directly giving rise to the Blurred-Anomaly-Boundary (BAB) issue by rendering normal-anomalous decision boundaries poorly separable. This challenge is further amplified for graph foundation models that require robust cross-domain generalization. To bridge this gap, we introduce a novel foundation model for TAG anomaly detection featuring decoupled topological and textual prototypes. Our framework constructs dual prototype banks to independently model structural normality and semantic consistency, effectively isolating anomaly cues that are otherwise diluted during coupled aggregation. Extensive experiments across 14 diverse benchmark datasets demonstrate that our method consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance in cross-domain settings. Notably, the ablation studies further corroborate the prevalence of the BAB issue in conventional coupled TAG anomaly detectors, and show that our decoupled prototype design effectively mitigates this challenge.

</details>


### [202] [Your LLM, Your Style: Behavioral Mode Axes for LLM Behavioral Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10703)
*Haoze Liu,Run Liu,Haiying Xu,Jiahui Han,Siyuan Fang,Siyu Yan,Huiqi Deng,Guanchu Wang,Na Zou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a situated behavioral-data framework to study and control LLM behavioral personality, showing stable model-specific profiles and controllable behavioral modes via Behavioral Mode Axes (BMAs).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcomes reliance on self-report personality surveys for LLMs by grounding analysis in concrete, interactive behavior and validating against psychometric facets.

Method: Construct 3,200 contrastive behavioral scenarios spanning 20 behavioral patterns and four prompt registers, anchored to BFI-2, DOSPERT, and HEXACO; analyze LLM behavior across first-person decisions, advice, and task execution; derive Activation-space directions (BMAs) from contrastive traces; compare response-derived vs thought-derived BMAs.

Result: LLMs exhibit stable, model-specific behavioral profiles with register-dependent shifts; BMAs enable controlled manipulation of behavioral styles; thought-derived BMAs better capture underlying mechanisms and offer cleaner control than response-derived BMAs.

Conclusion: LLM personality-like tendencies are better understood as measurable, controllable behavioral modes grounded in interaction context; code and data available for replication.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly act in interactive settings where their behavioral styles affect user experience, safety, and downstream decision making. Existing LLM personality studies largely rely on self-report questionnaires administered in first-person settings, making the resulting profiles sensitive to surface elicitation choices and poorly grounded in concrete model behavior. In this work, we introduce a situated behavioral-data (B-data) framework for studying and controlling LLM behavioral personality. We construct 3,200 contrastive behavioral scenarios spanning 20 behavioral patterns and four prompt registers, grounded in validated psychometric facets such as BFI-2, DOSPERT, and HEXACO. Using this framework, we find that LLMs exhibit stable and model-specific behavioral profiles, while also revealing register-dependent shifts across first-person decisions, advice-giving, and task execution. We then show that these behavioral patterns can be controlled through Behavioral Mode Axes (BMAs), activation-space directions derived from contrastive behavioral traces. Compared with response-derived BMAs, which are more prone to trait drift, thought-derived BMAs more faithfully capture the intended behavioral mechanism and provide cleaner control over situated behavioral styles. Our results suggest that LLM personality-like tendencies are better understood not as abstract self-report traits, but as measurable and controllable behavioral modes grounded in concrete interaction contexts. Our code and data are available at https://github.com/lhz191/LLM-Behavioral-Personality.

</details>


### [203] [SQuaT: Self-Supervised Knowledge Distillation via Student-Aware Quantized Teacher Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10709)
*HyeonJun Lee,Hyeonsik Jo,Jinwoo Chung,Jangho Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SQuaT introduces label-free QAT with KD by quantizing the teacher's features using the student's quantization parameters, thereby eliminating the irreducible distortion bound due to range mismatch and improving distillation, especially at very low bitwidths (1–2 bits).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In quantization-aware training with knowledge distillation, a range mismatch between teacher and quantized student creates an irrecoverable residual in the distillation loss when labels are unavailable. A label-free KD framework that removes this bound is desirable for robust low-bit quantization.

Method: During distillation, quantize teacher features with the student’s quantization parameters, ensuring matched ranges between teacher and student representations. The approach yields a theoretical guarantee to remove the lower bound on distillation loss and is designed to be architecture-agnostic. The code is released.

Result: Empirical evaluation across diverse settings shows SQuaT consistently outperforms strong baselines, with larger gains in extreme low-bit regimes (1–2 bit). The method demonstrates robustness across model designs and doesn’t rely on specific architectural assumptions.

Conclusion: SQuaT provides a principled, broadly applicable solution to label-free QAT with KD by aligning quantization ranges via student-parameterized quantization of teacher features, delivering notable improvements in low-bit quantization without requiring labels.

Abstract: Quantization-Aware Training (QAT) enables the deployment of quantized models with minimal accuracy degradation. However, in practical scenarios, training labels are often unavailable due to privacy, copyright, or cost constraints. Knowledge Distillation (KD) is a common approach to address this challenge, but we observe that prior work combining QAT with KD suffers from a fundamental limitation: during distillation, the range mismatch between the teacher and the quantized student model induces an unattainable residual, resulting in an irreducible lower bound on the distillation loss. Motivated by this observation, we propose SQuaT (Student-Aware Quantized Teacher Features), a label-free QAT framework with KD that theoretically eliminates this lower bound by applying the student's quantization parameters to quantize the teacher's features during distillation. Through comprehensive experiments across diverse settings, we demonstrate that SQuaT consistently outperforms strong baselines, with particularly pronounced gains in extreme low-bit (e.g., 1- and 2-bit) settings. Furthermore, extensive evaluations across various model design choices show that our approach does not rely on specific architectural assumptions, making it broadly applicable across diverse architectures and quantization settings. The source code is available at https://github.com/lcdbsa522/SQuaT.

</details>


### [204] [Long-Time Trajectory Approximation via SA-NODEs: Model Predictive and Floquet Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10738)
*Ziqian Li,Nikolaos M. Matzakos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Two reset-based training strategies for SA-NODEs enable long-horizon guarantees: a model-predictive horizon partition with data-driven restarts yields uniform bounds (horizon-linear resource use under regular reachability); and a Floquet strategy for autonomous targets with a stable limit cycle achieves linear error growth via contraction of the return map. The time-periodic architecture offers a uniform orbital guarantee with model-measured hypotheses, plus an obstruction showing incompatibility of small one-step error with contraction in the exact periodic case. Four benchmarks corroborate the predicted error laws and hypotheses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the deterioration of long-horizon error bounds when approximating dynamical systems with SA-NODEs trained on the full horizon; develop guarantees and practical training procedures that preserve accuracy over long time spans.

Method: 1) Model predictive strategy: partition the horizon into windows, restart each window from observed data; enforce a tolerance on every window; yields a composite model with uniform-in-time error bound and horizon-linear parameter budget for targets with a bounded, uniformly regular reachable tube. 2) Floquet strategy: for autonomous targets with a stable limit cycle, use no deployment data; certify contraction of the learned return map to confine error to linear growth with the number of elapsed periods. 3) Time-periodic architecture: derive a uniform-in-time orbital guarantee based on the trained model's hypotheses; prove an obstruction showing that exact periodic learned fields cannot simultaneously have small one-period error and a contracting stroboscopic map. 4) Numerical experiments on four benchmarks to validate error laws and verify all guarantees.

Result: The two strategies provide provable long-horizon error control in SA-NODEs: horizon-linear budgets under reachability assumptions (MPC) and linear-in-period growth via return-map contraction (Floquet); the time-periodic case yields orbital bounds but exhibits a fundamental trade-off in the exact periodic setting; empirical results across four benchmarks support the theoretical guarantees and the required hypotheses.

Conclusion: Reset-based training strategies extend reliable long-horizon approximations with SA-NODEs: MPC-based horizon partitioning and data restarts deliver uniform bounds under reachability conditions, while Floquet-based contraction yields linear-in-period error growth for limit-cycle targets; orbital guarantees are possible for time-periodic architectures with model-driven hypotheses, though exact periodicity imposes a prohibitive trade-off. Empirical validation across multiple benchmarks corroborates the theoretical error laws and conditions.

Abstract: We study the approximation of dynamical systems by semi-autonomous neural ordinary differential equations (SA-NODEs) over long time horizons. For a single network trained on the whole horizon, the available error bound deteriorates double exponentially in the horizon length. We develop two training strategies that avoid this barrier, each built on a reset of the state. The model predictive strategy partitions the horizon adaptively and restarts every window from observed data: when training meets a prescribed tolerance on every window, the composite model meets it uniformly in time, with a parameter budget linear in the horizon for targets with a bounded, uniformly regular reachable tube. The Floquet strategy addresses autonomous targets with a stable limit cycle and uses no data at deployment: a certified contraction of the learned return map confines the error to linear growth in the number of elapsed periods. For the time-periodic architecture we deploy, the scalar certificate degenerates; we prove instead a uniform-in-time orbital guarantee whose hypotheses are measured on the trained model, and an obstruction showing that, for an exactly periodic learned field, small one-period error and a contracting stroboscopic map cannot hold at once. Numerical experiments on four benchmarks confirm the predicted error laws and measure the hypotheses of every guarantee.

</details>


### [205] [Path Integral Value Matching for Linear Quadratic Stochastic Optimal Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10777)
*Bangyan Liao,Chenglei Yu,Yuchen Yang,Chuanrui Wang,Zhisheng Song,Peidong Liu,Tailin Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A value-based Path Integral method (PI-VM) for stochastic optimal control that recasts PIC into a temporal recursion via truncation and marginalization, enabling off-policy TD learning with Girsanov reweighting; achieves SOTA-like accuracy with much higher efficiency in low dimensions and reduces mode collapse in high dimensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Policy-based LQ-SOC methods are computationally expensive and unstable due to reliance on full-trajectory simulations; a value-based, recursion-friendly formulation could improve efficiency and stability.

Method: Derive a temporal recursive form of the value function by truncating and marginalizing the path integral. Propose Path Integral Value Matching (PI-VM) using temporal-difference learning to approximate the recursion, and integrate the Girsanov theorem with experience replay to enable off-policy training.

Result: PI-VM matches state-of-the-art precision with an order-of-magnitude efficiency gain in low-dimensional settings, and mitigates mode collapse in high-dimensional scenarios.

Conclusion: PI-VM provides a scalable, efficient, and stable value-based alternative to policy-based SOC methods by marrying path-integral control with TD learning and Girsanov reweighting.

Abstract: Linear Quadratic Stochastic Optimal Control (LQ-SOC) establishes a fundamental framework for steering noisy dynamical systems and has recently gained renewed interest in the machine learning community. However, current state-of-the-art policy-based methods suffer from prohibitive computational costs and instability due to their heavy reliance on full-trajectory simulation. To overcome these limitations, we propose a paradigm shift toward a value-based approach by revisiting Path Integral Control (PIC). Although standard PIC suffers from the same high-variance bottleneck as policy-based methods, we discover that by truncating and marginalizing the original path integral formulation, we can derive a temporal recursive form of the value function. Building upon this theoretical foundation, we propose the Path Integral Value Matching (PI-VM) algorithm. Specifically, we employ temporal-difference learning to approximate the recursive value dynamics, and further integrate the Girsanov theorem with experience replay to enable off-policy training. We benchmark PI-VM against SOTA policy-based methods across various SOC benchmarks and sampling tasks. Empirical results demonstrate that PI-VM matches SOTA precision with an order-of-magnitude efficiency gain in low-dimensional settings, while effectively mitigating mode collapse in high-dimensional scenarios. Consequently, PI-VM offers a scalable solution for solving complex SOC problems.

</details>


### [206] [MoE Proxy Models for Low-Cost Failure Reproduction and Diagnosis in LLM RL Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10823)
*Yikai Wang,Chuansai Zhou,Yuhang Zhou,Weiqiang Wu,Cong Wu,Yue Deng,Ben Feng,Mingming Zhu,Beirong Zhou,Zhibin Wang,Sheng Zhong,Chen Tian,Wangze Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proxy-models built via structure-preserving, clustering-based expert pruning offer low-cost surrogates for reproducing faults in RL post-training of LLMs on Huawei Ascend, drastically reducing hardware cost while preserving training dynamics and fault responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL post-training of LLMs is computationally intensive and brittle due to framework adaptation, numerical precision, and operator issues; reproducing failures on large models is expensive and time-consuming, hindering debugging and diagnosis.

Method: Construct proxy models by structure-preserving pruning of experts using clustering to select representative experts, preserving backbone architecture, routing mechanism, and basic task capabilities; the proxies act as surrogates for fault reproduction and auxiliary diagnosis.

Result: They reduce accelerator requirements by 50%–87.5% and achieve up to 33.3x reduction in per-step NPU-hour cost, while preserving major training dynamics and reproducing fault responses consistent with the original models.

Conclusion: Proxy models serve as low-cost surrogates for fault reproduction, targeted validation, and auxiliary diagnosis in RL post-training.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training of large language models (LLMs) is computationally intensive and involves complex system pipelines with substantial debugging overhead. In practice, factors such as framework adaptation, numerical precision, and operator implementation can cause failures, including gradient overflow and loss divergence. Reproducing such failures directly on large models requires considerable time and computational resources. This paper systematically analyzes failures encountered during large-scale RL training on the Huawei Ascend platform, summarizes representative failure types, and identifies three model-side factors relevant to fault reproduction. Based on these factors, we propose a proxy-model construction method for low-cost fault investigation and auxiliary diagnosis. It employs structure-preserving, clustering-based expert pruning to select representative experts while retaining the model's backbone architecture, routing mechanism, and basic task capabilities. Our experimental results show that the proxy models reduce accelerator requirements by 50%-87.5% and achieve up to a 33.3x reduction in per-step NPU-hour cost, while preserving major training dynamics and reproducing fault responses consistent with the original models. Overall, the proxy models can serve as low-cost surrogates for fault reproduction, targeted validation, and auxiliary diagnosis in RL post-training.

</details>


### [207] [TACTICL: Task-Aware Compression of Tabular ICL Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10837)
*Mykhailo Koshil,Matthias Feurer,Katharina Eggensperger*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TACTICL compresses tabular foundation models by pruning layers and inserting task-specific adapters, achieving substantial layer reduction with minimal performance loss while preserving in-context capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce inference cost and memory for tabular foundation models without sacrificing in-context adaptability and robustness to data shifts.

Method: Joint depth-wise pruning of transformer layers and insertion/training of lightweight adapters on downstream tasks, enabling task-aware, structured compression that blends in-context learning with in-weight learning.

Result: On 47 benchmark tabular datasets, up to 85% of layers can be substituted with adapters, with no substantial drop in downstream performance; robustness to data shifts is maintained; code released.

Conclusion: TACTICL demonstrates a robust, practical approach to exploiting depth redundancy in tabular foundation models by combining task-specific adaptation with structured compression, enabling efficient yet adaptable models.

Abstract: The strong performance of foundation models for tabular tasks comes at substantial inference costs. Distilling models into task-specific architectures reduces model size and computational demands but also sacrifices in-context adaptability. Here we introduce TACTICL, an automated task-aware compression framework for tabular in-context learning models that jointly prunes transformer layers and replaces them with lightweight adapters trained on downstream tasks, thus blending in-context with in-weight learning. We study TACTICL on 47 benchmark datasets and show that we can substitute up to 85% of layers without substantial performance drop on a given downstream task. We further show that TACTICL maintains robustness to data shifts, leaving its in-context ability intact. Overall, TACTICL provides a robust framework for exploiting the depth-wise redundancy of tabular foundation models by combining task-specific adaptation and structured compression. We provide the code at: https://github.com/Hebog/tfm_compression

</details>


### [208] [Diffract: Spectral View of LLM Domain Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10850)
*Nikita Borodin,Maria Krylova,Artem Zabolotnyi,Dmitry Aspisov,Egor Shikov,Nikita Tyuplyaev,Oleg Travkin,Roman Alferov,Dmitry Vinichenko*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CPT adapts LLMs to domains with minimal spectral distortion; adaptation is in singular vectors rather than singular values; domain-specific head heterogeneity enables a head-importance criterion allowing significant pruning; selective rewinding improves performance; domain connectivity via interpolations yields smooth cross-domain quality; release of Diffract toolkit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate the mechanisms by which continual pre-training (CPT) adapts general-purpose LLMs to specialized domains and identify efficient, scalable strategies for targeted adaptation and cross-domain transfer.

Method: Apply singular value decomposition to weight matrices to study spectral changes; analyze attention-head projection matrices to quantify head heterogeneity and derive a head-importance criterion; implement selective rewinding of low-importance heads; assess domain connectivity via linear interpolation between CPT checkpoints; release an open-source toolkit (Diffract) for scalable spectral analysis of billion-parameter models.

Result: Singular value spectra remain largely invariant; adaptation is driven by changes in singular vectors. Domain-dependent head heterogeneity is observed, enabling a head-importance criterion in which up to 60% of head updates can be removed without noticeable quality loss. Selective rewinding of low-importance heads improves benchmark accuracy by up to 4% over the fully trained baseline. Domain connectivity shows smooth quality interpolation through linear interpolation between CPT checkpoints with no notable degradation in either domain.

Conclusion: Continual pre-training can robustly adapt to diverse domains with minimal spectral distortion; targeted head adaptations drive domain-specific performance, and selective rollback of low-importance heads is beneficial. Linear interpolation across CPT checkpoints supports predictable cross-domain quality, and Diffract provides scalable spectral analysis for large models.

Abstract: We study continual pre-training (CPT) as a mechanism for adapting general-purpose large language models to specialized domains: mathematics, instruction, code, and natural text. Using singular value decomposition of weight matrices, we find that CPT leaves singular value spectra largely invariant, with adaptation driven mainly by changes in singular vectors. An analysis of attention-head projection matrices reveals strong, domain-dependent head heterogeneity, which we exploit to define a head importance criterion: up to 60% of head updates can be removed without measurable quality loss. Selectively rewinding low-importance heads to their pre-trained state improves benchmark accuracy by up to 4% versus the fully trained baseline. Finally, we identify domain connectivity - linear interpolation between CPT checkpoints yields smooth domain-quality interpolation without notable degradation on either domain - and release Diffract, an open-source toolkit for scalable spectral analysis of billion-parameter models.

</details>


### [209] [FiGuRO: Intrinsic Dimension Estimation for Multi-Modal Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10857)
*Viktoria Schuster,Sana Tonekaboni,Caroline Uhler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FiGuRO proposes Fidelity-Guided Rank Optimization to estimate intrinsic dimension (ID) for uni- and multi-modal data by learning low-rank projections via truncated SVD with an adaptive dimension algorithm; disentanglement of shared/private emerges without auxiliary losses and improves robustness over existing ID estimators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Intrinsic dimension under model capacity constraints is crucial for compact, interpretable representations, particularly in multi-modal settings where separating shared and private information is difficult; existing methods are static, uni-modal, or indirectly capture shared ID.

Method: Truncated SVD-based low-rank projections; an adaptive algorithm that decides when to increase/decrease dimension and in which latent space; objective couples projection ranks with capacity constraints; disentanglement emerges as a byproduct; applicable to post-hoc disentanglement of uni-modal pretrained models.

Result: Outperforms existing ID estimation techniques; robust to hyperparameter changes; captures distinct ID scales and subspace ratios; successfully decomposes shared/private information across simulations and real-world data; enables efficient post-hoc disentanglement for multi-modal representations.

Conclusion: FiGuRO provides a practical, robust framework for ID estimation and multi-modal disentanglement under capacity constraints, with broad applicability to representation learning and model efficiency.

Abstract: Determining the complexity, or Intrinsic Dimension (ID), of data is fundamental to efficient and interpretable representation learning. This is particularly challenging in multi-modal settings when trying to learn disentangled representations for shared and private information. Existing techniques leave a critical gap: they are often static, uni-modal, or in the case of contrastive methods, adapt only to the shared ID implicitly. We introduce Fidelity-Guided Rank Optimization (FiGuRO), a framework for approximating the ID of uni- and multi-modal data under constraints of model capacity and hyperparameters. FiGuRO learns the dimensions of low-rank projections using truncated singular value decomposition and an algorithm that determines when to reduce or increase dimension and in which latent space. Disentanglement of shared and private information arises as an emergent property of this optimization, eliminating the need for complex auxiliary loss functions. We demonstrate that FiGuRO outperforms existing ID estimation techniques and is more robust to hyperparameter changes. Across simulations and real-world data, FiGuRO captures distinct ID scales and varying subspace ratios, and decomposes shared and private information successfully. Furthermore, we show that FiGuRO can be applied to modern uni-modal pretrained models, enabling efficient, post-hoc disentanglement of multi-modal representations.

</details>


### [210] [Can Bayesian Optimization Efficiently Find a Strong Single Expert in Neural Thickets?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10867)
*Nigel Bastian Cendra,Abdelhamid Ezzerg,Fernando Julio Cendra,Jeremias Knoblauch,Jakob Zeitler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Bayesian optimization in a random linear embedding enables efficient gradient-free post-training on LLMs, matching or surpassing baselines with 5x fewer evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the high cost of gradient-free post-training for large language models by exploiting low-dimensional subspace structure to enable efficient search for a strong single expert.

Method: Apply Bayesian optimization with a Gaussian-process surrogate in a random linear embedding of weight space (no backpropagation). Evaluate candidates under a modest budget to identify a deployable single expert for Qwen2.5-Instruct models (0.5B–3B) on reasoning tasks.

Result: With five times fewer candidate evaluations, Bayesian optimization matches or exceeds RandOpt across multiple reasoning benchmarks on Qwen2.5-Instruct models, yielding stronger deployable single experts and lower evaluation cost than prior gradient-free methods.

Conclusion: Surrogate-guided search in a random subspace effectively reduces the evaluation cost of gradient-free post-training while producing superior single experts, indicating that useful updates lie in low-dimensional weight subspaces and that BO can guide efficient discovery.

Abstract: Gradient-free post-training has emerged as a compelling alternative to gradient-based optimization for large language models (LLMs), but existing approaches remain costly. We ask whether structured search can identify a strong single expert under a modest evaluation budget. Motivated by evidence that useful weight updates lie in low-dimensional subspaces, we apply Bayesian optimization within a random linear embedding of weight space. Our method requires no backpropagation and uses a Gaussian process surrogate to guide candidate evaluations efficiently. Across several reasoning benchmarks with Qwen2.5-Instruct models from 0.5B to 3B parameters, Bayesian optimization using five times less candidate evaluations matches or exceeds RandOpt. These results show that surrogate-guided search can substantially reduce the evaluation cost of gradient-free post-training while producing stronger deployable single experts.

</details>


### [211] [Optimistic Rates for Multiclass PAC Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10869)
*Xiaoyu Li,Andi Han,Jiaojiao Jiang,Junbin Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proves an optimistic excess-risk rate for multiclass learning, showing the optimal rate at fixed oracle risk L* is tildeTheta(sqrt(L* dN / n) + dDS / n), uniformly in alphabet size. It provides matching upper and lower bounds, independent of L* and confidence, and extends to list learning. The upper bound uses a cover-menu-compression framework plus a new comparator-facing relative compression theorem; the lower bound uses a pair-Assouad construction and fiber arguments around Natarajan vs DS separation. A toolkit generalizes to list learning (r-tuples) with the same rate structure, removing the r-factor in the lower bound for realizable lists. The work closes the gap between endpoints (realizable and agnostic) and introduces new compression-based tools for multiclass optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a known gap in worst-case multiclass excess risk between the realizable and agnostic regimes. The authors seek an optimistic rate whose fluctuations scale with the oracle risk L*, to obtain a tighter, rate-aware bound for multiclass settings characterized by Natarajan and DS dimensions.

Method: Combine the cover-menu-compression architecture of CEH+26 with Pab26 realizable rates. Introduce a comparator-facing relative compression theorem: a size-k compression rule that empirically dominates a comparator h yields a population risk bound L(h) + O(sqrt{L(h)Γ} + Γ) with Γ = (k log n + log(1/δ))/n, without requiring stability. Use this to transfer sharp binary comparison principles to multiclass. Prove a matching lower bound via a pair-Assouad construction calibrated to L* and a fiber argument on pseudo-cubes arising from the Natarajan-versus-DS separation (BCD+22). Extend the construction to list learning by considering the best r-tuple of hypotheses and applying the same two engines.

Result: An optimal optimistic excess-risk rate is established: for fixed L*, the optimal excess risk is tildeTheta(sqrt(L* dN / n) + dDS / n), uniform in alphabet size. This rate is achieved by a learner without knowledge of L* or confidence, combining the upper-bound machinery with the new relative compression tool. A matching lower bound demonstrates the necessity of both terms, and the framework extends to list learning (r-tuples), yielding the same rate-form without a factor r in the realizable list lower bound.

Conclusion: The authors close the gap between endpoint rates in multiclass learning by deriving an optimistic, L*-dependent rate that matches upper and lower bounds and extends to list learning. They introduce a new relative compression tool and a comparator-based approach that generalizes binary sharp theory to multiclass settings, and show the rate holds uniformly in alphabet size. This unifies the dN and dDS dimensions in the optimistic regime and strengthens the case for compression-based strategies in multiclass learning.

Abstract: Worst-case multiclass bounds do not become smaller when the best classifier is already nearly correct: what is missing is an optimistic rate, a guarantee whose fluctuation scales with the oracle risk itself. For a class of Natarajan dimension $d_N$ and Daniely-Shalev-Shwartz dimension $d_{DS}$, the optimal excess risk is known at the two endpoints ($d_{DS}/n$ realizable, $\sqrt{d_N/n}+d_{DS}/n$ agnostic [HMZ24, CEH+26, Pab26]) and open in between. We close the gap: at every fixed oracle risk $L^\star$, the optimal excess risk is $\widetildeΘ(\sqrt{L^\star d_N/n}+d_{DS}/n)$, uniformly in the alphabet size, attained by a learner that knows neither $L^\star$ nor the confidence level. The upper bound composes the cover-menu-compression architecture of [CEH+26], at the realizable rate of [Pab26], with a new comparator-facing relative compression theorem: a size-$k$ compression rule that empirically dominates a comparator $h$ has population risk at most $L(h)+O(\sqrt{L(h)Γ}+Γ)$ with $Γ=(k\log n+\log(1/δ))/n$, without stability; this transfers the comparison principle of the sharp binary theory [MQZ26] while discarding its Boolean-cube geometry, which does not lift to multiclass labels. The lower bound forces both terms using one class and one distribution at every fixed $L^\star$, by a pair-Assouad scheme calibrated to $L^\star$ and a fiber argument on the pseudo-cubes underlying the Natarajan-versus-DS separation of [BCD+22]. Both theorems extend to list learning: against the best $r$-tuple of hypotheses, the same architecture and the same two engines yield an optimistic rate and a lower bound of the same shape, forcing the fluctuation term that [Pab26] expected to be necessary against list comparators, and removing the factor $r$ from the known realizable list lower bound.

</details>


### [212] [Benchmarking Time Series Generation Methods for Privacy-Preserving Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10891)
*Luis Amorim,Vitor Cerqueira,Moises Santos,Paulo J. Azevedo,Carlos Soares*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Benchmark shows no synthetic method fully substitutes original training data for time-series forecasting under Train on Synthetic, Test on Real (TSTR); noise-based anonymization offers strongest privacy but poor forecasting; simple transformation-based generators beat deep generative models here; Grasynda-P achieves a Pareto-optimal balance with competitive forecasting and stronger privacy separation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need to understand the trade-offs between data utility and privacy when replacing original time-series data with synthetic data. The study addresses a gap by evaluating how well synthetic generation methods perform under a full data substitution scenario (TSTR) and by quantifying privacy risk, across diverse datasets. It also contributes a privacy-motivated generator (Grasynda-P) to explore improved privacy-utility trade-offs.

Method: A benchmark across seven datasets comparing multiple synthetic generation methods and noise-based anonymization baselines under the Train on Synthetic, Test on Real (TSTR) protocol. Assess forecasting performance (utility) and distance-based privacy risk (privacy) to characterize the trade-off. Introduce Grasynda-P, an extension of the graph-based generator Grasynda that incorporates matrix ensembling and kernel density estimation to enhance privacy.

Result: Findings: (1) No generation method fully substitutes original training data. (2) Noise-based anonymization yields the strongest privacy protection but the worst forecasting performance. (3) Simple transformation-based generators outperform deep generative models for forecasting in this setting. (4) Grasynda-P lies on the Pareto frontier, achieving competitive forecasting with stronger privacy separation than other generators.

Conclusion: The benchmark provides a reference point for evaluating and developing privacy-aware synthetic time-series generation methods. Grasynda-P demonstrates a promising balance of forecasting utility and privacy protection, highlighting the need for further exploration of privacy-utility trade-offs and improved synthetic methods for privacy-sensitive forecasting.

Abstract: Time series forecasting in privacy-sensitive domains often requires training models on released data rather than original observations. Synthetic time series generation has been developed primarily for data augmentation, where generated series supplement the original training set. How well these methods perform when fully replacing the original data - and how much privacy risk the released series carry - remains underexplored. We address this gap through a benchmark evaluating synthetic generation methods and noise-based anonymization baselines under a Train on Synthetic, Test on Real (TSTR) protocol. We jointly assess forecasting performance and distance-based empirical privacy risk across seven datasets, characterizing the trade-off between these objectives. We also introduce Grasynda-P, a privacy-motivated extension of the graph-based generator Grasynda, incorporating matrix ensembling and kernel density estimation. Our results show that: (1) no generation method fully substitutes for original training data; (2) noise-based anonymization yields the strongest privacy but the worst forecasting performance; (3) simple transformation-based generators outperform deep generative models for forecasting in this setting; and (4) Grasynda-P lies on the Pareto frontier, achieving competitive forecasting with stronger privacy separation than other generators. This benchmark establishes a reference point for evaluating and developing new privacy-aware synthetic time series generation methods.

</details>


### [213] [Partially Observable Learning for Multi-Platform Dispatch Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10897)
*Fengming Yao,Man Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: POLO is a partially observable, multi-agent RL framework for multi-platform dispatch in which each platform-grid is an independent agent learning from local observations. It uses an attention-based policy representation to fuse inter-courier information and a counterfactual reward shaping mechanism to mitigate non-stationarity from joint actions. A high-fidelity simulator validates the approach, showing improved platform revenue and courier travel efficiency across varying numbers of platforms and scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In real-world multi-platform instant delivery, couriers often serve multiple platforms and each platform only observes its own orders and couriers due to privacy constraints, leading to partial observability. Existing RL dispatch methods assume full observability and mandatory acceptance, causing degraded performance in practice. A framework that handles partial observability and non-stationarity is needed.

Method: Model each platform-grid pair as an independent agent learning dispatch policies from platform-local observations. Introduce an attention-based policy representation to selectively aggregate inter-courier information. Implement counterfactual reward shaping to reduce non-stationarity from joint actions across grids. Validate with a high-fidelity simulator across varying numbers of platforms and scales.

Result: POLO consistently outperforms strong baselines in platform revenue and courier travel efficiency, demonstrating robustness and effectiveness under realistic multi-platform settings.

Conclusion: POLO provides an effective solution for partial observability in multi-platform dispatch, with the attention-based representation and counterfactual rewards improving learning stability and scalability, as demonstrated by simulation experiments.

Abstract: Instant delivery platforms have become a critical component of urban logistics, increasingly relying on crowdsourced couriers to fulfill highly dynamic orders. In real-world systems, couriers are not exclusive to a single platform and may concurrently serve multiple platforms, while each platform can only observe its own orders and couriers' interactions due to privacy and operational constraints. This results in a multi-platform dispatch environment with inherent partial observability. However, most existing works on dispatch optimization assume full courier observability and mandatory assignment acceptance, causing substantial performance degradation when deployed in realistic multi-platform settings. In this paper, we propose POLO, a partially observable multi-agent reinforcement learning framework for dispatching optimization in multi-platform instant delivery systems. POLO firstly models each platform-grid pair as an independent agent that learns dispatch policies solely from platform-local observations, aligning the learning process with real-world privacy and operational constraints. To support effective decision-making under incomplete and heterogeneous courier information, POLO introduces a novel attention-based policy representation that selectively aggregates inter-courier information. Moreover, we design a counterfactual reward shaping mechanism to mitigate the non-stationarity induced by joint actions across grids, leading to more stable and scalable learning. We develop a high-fidelity simulator to evaluate dispatch performance under varying numbers of platforms and system scales. Extensive experiments demonstrate that POLO consistently outperforms strong baselines in terms of platform revenue and courier travel efficiency, highlighting its robustness and effectiveness in realistic multi-platform settings.

</details>


### [214] [ReOrder-OPD:Reliability-Aware Prompt Ordering for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10905)
*Ximo Zhu,Ruiqi Liu,Rong Wang,Ping Wu,Xiang Zheng,Wenzhuo Xu,Xubin Yao,Zhiyuan Yan,Bo Li,Jun Gao,Xiaolei Lv*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes ReOrder-OPD: prompt-level ordering to improve on-policy distillation by aligning prompts with higher predicted continuation reliability, estimated via a ROUGE-5 F1 proxy; shows gains across math and code settings with FiRe-OPD/ExOPD.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-policy distillation suffers from unreliable teacher supervision at the trajectory level. The paper seeks a principled way to select and order prompts to maximize training value by exploiting prompt-level continuation reliability (R).

Method: Define prompt-level reliability R as the probability the teacher reaches a correct answer from a student prefix. Estimate R using the maximum ROUGE-5 F1 between independent student rollouts and verifier-correct same-prompt teacher trajectories. Bin prompts by this proxy, then ReOrder-OPD sorts prompts by proxy and draws on-policy training trajectories for vanilla OPD.

Result: Oracle experiments show high-R prompts yield larger OPD gains; descending-R training outperforms random and ascending orders on a fixed prompt pool. Across ten equal-frequency bins, mean R rises monotonically, indicating the proxy separates reliability levels. ReOrder-OPD improves aggregate performance across Qwen3 and Gemma4 mathematics and Qwen3 code settings; gains appear in all FiRe-OPD and ExOPD configurations.

Conclusion: Prompt-level reliability estimates via a ROUGE-5 F1 proxy can effectively sequence prompts to enhance on-policy distillation, and prompt ordering complements within-trajectory supervision, yielding robust gains across diverse tasks and settings.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) applies token-level teacher supervision to student-generated trajectories, but this supervision is not always reliable. Existing methods use local confidence or teacher-student agreement to weight, filter, or truncate the sampled trajectory. These signals do not directly determine whether the teacher can continue a student prefix to a correct answer, and trajectory-level interventions can conflate one rollout's unreliability with low expected training value of its prompt. We define prompt-level teacher continuation reliability $R$ as the teacher's probability of reaching a correct answer from a student prefix, averaged over prefixes and trajectories induced by the current student. Oracle experiments show that high-$R$ prompts yield larger OPD gains and that descending-$R$ training outperforms random and ascending orders on a fixed prompt pool. Because estimating $R$ requires many teacher continuations, we use the maximum ROUGE-5 F1 between one independent student rollout and verifier-correct same-prompt teacher trajectories. Across ten equal-frequency bins of this actual score, mean $R$ rises monotonically, showing that the proxy separates coarse reliability levels. ReOrder-OPD sorts prompts by the proxy, then draws independent on-policy training trajectories for vanilla OPD. It improves every matched aggregate comparison across Qwen3 and Gemma4 mathematics settings and Qwen3 code settings. Gains in all six FiRe-OPD and ExOPD settings show that prompt ordering complements within-trajectory supervision.

</details>


### [215] [Physics-informed Diffusion Generative Model for Time-Series Data Synthesis in Dynamic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10941)
*Haiteng Wang,Yunfei Zhu,Tao Wang,Yikang Li,Jiabao Dong,Xiaoge Zhang,Lei Ren*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A stepwise physics-embedded diffusion model (PhysDGM) generates physically consistent industrial time-series data to augment scarce real data; creates a large synthetic dataset and improves multiple downstream prognostics tasks with substantially less real data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address data scarcity and high collection cost in harsh industrial environments; require trajectories that respect physical laws for reliable AI in prognostics and health management.

Method: A diffusion-based generative model with physics embedded at each reverse diffusion step, ensuring trajectory-level physical consistency. Trained on 34 datasets across turbofan engines, aero-engines, batteries, and chemical processes, producing 4.4 million synthetic samples (20x scale-up). Used to augment real data for downstream tasks.

Result: Enhances downstream task performance when using synthetic data: remaining useful life +48%, health indicator +15%, state-of-health +22%, fault diagnosis +20%. Requires 10–20x less real training data than competing approaches. Demonstrates potential for early-stage fault identification with AI-synthesized data.

Conclusion: PhysDGM establishes a solid foundation for physically consistent industrial time-series generation, enabling physics-guided AI in data-scarce environments and broadening applications to industrial machinery and chemical reaction dynamics.

Abstract: Industrial time-series signals, such as turbine temperature and rotational speed in aero-engines, are essential for monitoring the health and operational status of complex dynamical systems. However, collecting such data is often limited by harsh environments (e.g., high temperature and high pressure) and the high cost of experimental testing. To address this challenge, we introduce PhysDGM, a stepwise physics-embedded diffusion generative model for synthesizing time-series data that are consistent with the underlying physical laws of dynamical systems. PhysDGM embeds physical laws directly into each reverse diffusion step of the generative process, ensuring trajectory-level physical consistency, rather than enforcing constraints only at the final output. A large-scale AI-synthetic dataset (4.4 million samples, 20x scale-up) constructed by PhysDGM demonstrates strong fidelity across 34 datasets spanning turbofan engines, aero-engines, batteries, and chemical processes. After incorporating the synthetic data, the downstream task performance substantially surpassed that using real data alone by 48% for remaining useful life prediction, 15% for health indicator estimation, 22% for state-of-health assessment, and 20% for fault diagnosis. Moreover, it requires 10-20x less training data than existing approaches, substantially reducing the high cost of data collection in dynamical systems. We further demonstrate PhysDGM's potential in identifying early-stage faults in aero-engines by incorporating AI-synthesized data. In summary, PhysDGM provides a solid foundation for generating physically consistent industrial time-series, paving the way for expanding physics-guided AI into diverse data-scarce environments, including both industrial machinery and complex chemical reaction dynamics.

</details>


### [216] [GARLIC: Graph Attention-based Relational Learning of Multivariate Time Series in Intensive Care](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10969)
*Ruirui Wang,Yanke Li,Manuel Günther,Diego Paez-Granados*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GARLIC is a graph-attention neural network for irregular ICU time series that imputes missing data, models inter-sensor dependencies via time-lagged graphs, and provides end-to-end explainability, achieving state-of-the-art predictive performance on critical-care benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical ICU time series are irregular and incomplete, requiring models that are both accurate and interpretable for real-time decision support.

Method: A three-component architecture: (1) a learnable exponential-decay encoder for imputing missing values; (2) time-lagged summary graphs to capture inter-sensor dependencies; (3) cross-dimensional sequential attention to fuse global patterns with local sequences. All components and edges are learned end-to-end. An alternating decoupled optimization scheme balances the auxiliary reconstruction objective with primary classification. Extensive ablations and feature-removal analyses confirm module contributions and attribution fidelity. Real-time case studies demonstrate actionable risk warnings with interpretable explanations. The method reportedly generalizes beyond ICU data to other time-series tasks.

Result: GARLIC achieves new state-of-the-art performance on ICU benchmarks PhysioNet 2012 & 2019 and MIMIC-III, with significant AUROC and AUPRC improvements over strong baselines at comparable computational cost. Ablations show each module contributes; attribution tests exhibit monotonic degradation when removing top features; real-time demonstrations offer actionable and transparent risk explanations. The approach also shows superior imputation and classification on non-ICU time-series datasets, indicating broad applicability.

Conclusion: GARLIC represents a significant advance in accurate, explainable DL for irregularly sampled ICU time series, with end-to-end learnable explanations and robust performance gains. The modular design's contribution is validated through ablations, and its generalizability suggests applicability to wider time-series tasks.

Abstract: Healthcare data, such as Intensive Care Unit (ICU) records, comprise heterogeneous multivariate time series sampled at irregular intervals with pervasive missingness. However, clinical applications demand predictive models that are both accurate and interpretable. We present our Graph Attention-based Relational Learning for Intensive Care (GARLIC) model, a novel neural network architecture that imputes missing data through a learnable exponential-decay encoder, captures inter-sensor dependencies via time-lagged summary graphs, and fuses global patterns with cross-dimensional sequential attention. All attention weights and graph edges are learned end-to-end to serve as built-in observation-, signal-, and edge-level explanations. To reconcile auxiliary reconstruction and primary classification objectives, we developed an alternating decoupled optimization scheme that stabilizes training. On three ICU benchmarks (PhysioNet 2012 & 2019, MIMIC-III), GARLIC sets the new state of the art in outcome prediction, significantly improving AUROC and AUPRC over best-performing baselines at comparable computational cost. Ablation studies confirm the contribution of each module, and feature-removal trials validate the fidelity of importance attribution through a monotonic performance drop (full > top 50% > random 50% > bottom 50%). Real-time case studies demonstrate actionable risk warnings with transparent explanations, marking a significant advance toward accurate, explainable deep learning for irregularly sampled ICU time series data. Moreover, we demonstrated \proposed{}'s superiority in data imputation and classification on various time-series datasets beyond the ICU domain, showing its generalizability and applicability to broader tasks.

</details>


### [217] [DEFT: Data-Efficient Frequency-domain Top-k Sampling via Inverse Discrete Fourier Transform for Spatiotemporal Dynamical Systems Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11019)
*Hengbo Xiao,Jiale Liu,Jiahao Song,Guannan He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DEFT uses frequency-domain sampling by identifying dominant Fourier modes, varying their amplitudes/phases, and reconstructing data via inverse DFT to train PDE operators. It also provides a generalization bound and a principled criterion for selecting K, showing strong data-efficiency and transferability across PDE domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce data requirements and improve generalization of data-driven PDE surrogates while avoiding expensive physics-based solvers by exploiting spectral structure.

Method: Extract dominant Fourier components, perturb amplitudes/phases within physically plausible ranges, synthesize training data with inverse DFT, and derive a generalization bound plus a criterion for selecting K.

Result: Outperforms traditional data-driven methods when spectra are dominated by few modes; reduces data needs by ~40% on diffusion–sorption and Burgers PDEs with <2% loss in accuracy; achieves R^2 > 0.99 on battery-degradation PDEs; frequency-domain features transfer to other chemistries with ~20% fine-tuning; demonstrates effective data-sampling for operator learning across domains.

Conclusion: DEFT is a principled, effective data-sampling framework for PDE operator learning with theoretical guarantees and cross-domain transferability.

Abstract: Modeling spatiotemporal dynamical systems governed by partial differential equations (PDEs) poses two major challenges: it either requires expensive physics-based simulators that entail iterative numerical solving at high computational cost, or it depends on abundant training data, yet purely data-driven models often generalize poorly to downstream dynamic operating conditions. We propose DEFT, a frequency-domain data sampling method that identifies the dominant Fourier modes of a physical system and systematically varies the corresponding amplitudes and phases to generate physically consistent training data via the inverse discrete Fourier transform. In addition, we derive a generalization bound of this method. We note that it also provides a theoretically principled criterion for selecting $K$. We evaluate the proposed method through three sets of experiments, each targeting a distinct aspect of its utility. First, we validate the framework on canonical PDEs solving demonstrating that it outperforms traditional methods when the system is dominated by a few prominent frequency components. Second, we employ DEFT as a data-value filter on the diffusion--sorption and Burgers equations of PDEBench, showing that it reduces data requirements by $40\%$ while sacrificing less than $2\%$ in predictive accuracy. Third, to evaluate DEFT for more challenging and practically relevant problems, we validate it in the battery degradation PDE system, achieving consistently high predictive accuracy across various test datasets with $R^2$ values exceeding $0.99$. Moreover, the learned frequency-domain features transfer to other battery chemistries with only $20\%$ of the fine-tuning data. These results demonstrate that DEFT is an effective data-sampling method for efficient operator learning.

</details>


### [218] [Derivative Computation in PINNs: Automatic Differentiation, Finite Differences and Beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11020)
*Maciej J. Mikulski,Tadeusz Uhl*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Finite-difference (FD) derivatives in PINNs can match automatic differentiation (AD) accuracy while improving speed and reducing memory; a stochastic FD variant can outperform AD on certain problems; autograd can be incorrect for per-sample derivatives with inter-sample dependencies, where FD forward-only estimates are a practical alternative.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce computational cost and memory footprint of derivative evaluations in Physics-Informed Neural Networks, especially for large batch sizes and architectures with inter-sample dependencies (e.g., BatchNorm, self-attention). Evaluate whether FD can serve as a viable replacement for AD in PINNs and under what conditions.

Method: Three benchmark PDEs are used to compare FD and AD derivative computations. Step-size calibration is performed to align FD accuracy with AD. A stochastic variant of FD is introduced. The study also analyzes architectures with inter-sample dependencies to assess per-sample derivative correctness; per-sample autograd is shown to be incorrect or infeasible at PINN-scale, while FD offers a forward-only approximation.

Result: FD, with appropriately calibrated step size, matches AD accuracy across all tested problems and executes faster across the full batch-size range while using substantially less GPU memory. A stochastic FD variant outperforms AD on a stationary problem. For architectures with inter-sample dependencies, standard autograd can be silently incorrect; the correct per-sample alternative is computationally infeasible at PINN-scale, whereas FD provides a forward-only approximation that is empirically an order of magnitude closer to the true per-sample derivative.

Conclusion: FD is a practical and competitive alternative to AD for PINNs, offering speed and memory benefits and favorable accuracy under calibration. It is particularly advantageous for architectures with inter-sample dependencies where per-sample derivatives are important but expensive to compute with autograd; a stochastic FD variant can further improve performance on certain problems. Future work should explore broader PDE benchmarks, robust step-size strategies, and integration with common PINN training pipelines.

Abstract: We systematically investigate finite-difference (FD) derivative computation in Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) as an alternative to automatic differentiation (AD). On three benchmark PDEs we show that, with a properly calibrated step size, FD matches AD in accuracy on every problem while running faster across the full tested batch-size range and using substantially less GPU memory, and that a stochastic variant we propose outperforms AD on a stationary problem. We further show that for neural architectures with inter-sample dependencies (e.g. BatchNorm, self-attention) the standard PyTorch autograd idiom is silently incorrect; the correct per-sample alternative is computationally infeasible at PINN-relevant batch sizes, while FD provides a forward-only approximation that is empirically an order of magnitude closer to the true per-sample derivative.

</details>


### [219] [Mapping and Measuring the Behavioral Evolution of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11027)
*Dong Qiao,Chris Ding,Jicong Fan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A comprehensive, label-free analysis of model behavior across 32 models using three complementary, sentence- and token-level dissimilarities to study static organization and temporal drift, showing cross-family convergence and that newer reasoning models have compact response clouds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Go beyond benchmark leaderboards to understand inter-model relationships and how model behavior evolves over generations, addressing questions of alignment, drift, and predictability across model families.

Method: Embed each model's responses to 10,000 prompts; compute three dissimilarities per prompt: aligned mean per-prompt distance (pseudometric on responses), PCA-based prompt-wise disagreement, and an alignment-free Gromov–Wasserstein discrepancy of internal response geometries; analyze on a release-date axis via behavioral maps, clustering, drift, convergence, and dispersion; cross-check with token-level Maximum Mean Discrepancy; reformulate in a measure-theoretic framework; derive a sufficient condition tying behavioral similarity to training signals; verify robustness to re-encoding with three alternative encoders while preserving rank geometry.

Result: Found coherent clustering by model family, with gpt-2 as a global outlier; cross-family distances decrease over time; recent reasoning-oriented models exhibit more compact response clouds; token-level MMD aligns with sentence-level distances (Spearman ρ≈0.98); re-encoding preserves qualitative findings; accession of a measure-theoretic framing; proposed training-side explanation via a sufficient condition.

Conclusion: The pipeline offers a label-free, architecture-agnostic framework for analyzing model behavior beyond scores, revealing evolution and relationships among models. It highlights converging behavior across families and identifies persistent outliers. The approach is robust to representation changes and provides a theoretical bridge to training dynamics, suggesting practical avenues for model auditing and development.

Abstract: Benchmark leaderboards summarize how well a language model performs, but not how its behavior relates to that of other models or changes across generations. We characterize the output behavior of 32 models from six families using their responses to a shared bank of 10{,}000 prompts. After embedding each response, we construct three complementary sentence-level dissimilarities: an aligned mean per-prompt distance, which is a pseudometric on observed model responses; a PCA-compressed summary of prompt-wise disagreement; and an alignment-free Gromov--Wasserstein discrepancy between models' internal response geometries. We use these constructions to study static organization and temporal change on a release-date axis through behavioral maps, family-wise drift, hierarchical clustering, cross-family convergence, and response-cloud dispersion. Across the three constructions, model families form coherent clusters, with \texttt{gpt-2} as a global outlier; cross-family distances decrease over time; and several recent reasoning-oriented models have comparatively compact response clouds. A token-level cross-check based on per-prompt Maximum Mean Discrepancy closely agrees with the sentence-level mean distance (Spearman $ρ=0.98$) and recovers the same qualitative findings. We organize these comparisons through a measure-theoretic lens making their alignment and invariance assumptions explicit. We also establish an architecture-agnostic sufficient condition linking behavioral similarity to inference-prompt coverage, small excess population log-loss, and similar effective target distributions---a possible training-side account rather than an empirical explanation of the observed trends. Our pipeline is label-free, and re-encoding every response with three further encoders---down to one $73\times$ smaller---preserves the rank geometry, the outliers, and the sign of the time trend.

</details>


### [220] [ReRound: Reconstructive Rounding to Resolve Midpoint Ambiguity in Calibration-Free LLM Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11045)
*He-Yen Hsieh,H. T. Kung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReRound uses diffusion-guided reconstructions to resolve midpoint rounding ambiguity during post-training quantization of low-bit weights. It blends diffusion-based reconstructions within a tolerance region with RTN, generates multiple candidates, and selects the one that best preserves the original weight spectrum based on leading singular values. It improves 3-4 bit accuracy on small LLMs, is calibration-free offline, and may generalize beyond LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Midpoint ambiguity in standard round-to-nearest (RTN) quantization near interval centers causes accuracy loss for low-bit weights. A calibration-free, offline approach is desired to improve post-training quantization for small LLMs and other models.

Method: From a pretrained LLM, train a conditional diffusion model to reconstruct continuous low-bit weights. Use these reconstructions as a guidance signal for weights near interval midpoints. Define a tolerance region around midpoints where diffusion reconstructions are used; otherwise use RTN. Sweep the tolerance to produce multiple candidate weight matrices and select the one whose leading singular values most closely match those of the original full-precision weights.

Result: Performs better than standard RTN for 3-bit and 4-bit quantization on small LLMs across calibration-free baselines; competitive with calibration-dependent methods; operates offline with no additional inference overhead.

Conclusion: ReRound introduces a novel, offline, diffusion-guided post-training quantization strategy that reduces midpoint rounding ambiguity, yielding improved low-bit accuracy and potential applicability beyond LLMs.

Abstract: ReRound (Reconstructive Rounding) is a post-training quantization method that addresses the midpoint ambiguity inherent in standard round-to-nearest (RTN) schemes when quantizing weights near the centers of quantization intervals.
  Starting from a pretrained LLM, ReRound trains a conditional diffusion model to produce continuous reconstructions of low-bit weights for the LLM. These reconstructed weights act as a guidance signal to disambiguate the rounding direction of weights located close to interval midpoints.
  To integrate this reconstruction-guided rounding with conventional RTN, ReRound introduces a tolerance metric measuring how far the quantized weight (not the final quantized integer) is away from the midpoint: quantized weights within a tolerance region around midpoints are quantized using diffusion-based reconstructions, whereas weights closer to quantization boundaries are quantized with RTN. By sweeping the tolerance parameter, ReRound generates multiple candidate quantized integer weight matrices and selects the de-quantized weight matrix candidate whose leading singular values most closely match those of the original full-precision weights. This selected candidate determines the tolerance parameter ReRound uses.
  ReRound is particularly effective for smaller LLMs. Across a range of such models, it consistently outperforms standard RTN for 3-bit and 4-bit weight quantization. ReRound achieves superior accuracy compared to an extensive set of calibration-free methods, remains competitive with calibration-dependent approaches, and operates entirely offline, introducing no additional overhead during low-bit inference.
  The ReRound strategy represents a new approach for low-bit quantization. The method applies to AI models beyond LLMs. This paper focuses on its applications to small LLMs.

</details>


### [221] [Efficient Hypergradient Descent for Inverse Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11052)
*Nikita Sevriukov,Anna Barabanova,Uliana Gagarina,Karina Ivanova,Sofiia Kasaeva,Ilya Levin,Marina Sheshukova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a scalable Fisher-based hypergradient for bilevel IRL using a streaming spectral sketch to approximate inverse Fisher-vector products; achieves competitive policy performance with reduced curvature storage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: IRL via bilevel optimization yields difficult hypergradient computations due to inverse-Hessian-vector products; scalable, information-geometric approaches can reduce computational burden.

Method: Show inner Hessian proportional to the Fisher information at the optimum; derive a structured Fisher-based hypergradient akin to Natural Hypergradient Descent; replace the inverse-Fisher-vector product with a streaming spectral sketch to avoid explicit Fisher matrix; evaluate against a first-order stochastic bilevel baseline on discrete and continuous control tasks.

Result: Policy performance and reward-ranking quality are competitive; Fisher sketching reduces curvature-storage complexity and can improve computational efficiency relative to explicit Fisher solvers.

Conclusion: A scalable, information-geometric bilevel IRL framework: leverage Fisher structure and spectral sketching to enable efficient hypergradient computation without constructing large matrices.

Abstract: Inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) aims to recover a reward function under which the resulting policy reproduces the behavior observed in expert demonstrations. A natural approach is to formulate IRL as a bilevel optimization problem, in which the inner level corresponds to policy optimization under the learned reward and the outer level measures the discrepancy between the induced policy and expert data. However, this formulation is computationally challenging in practice because the outer update requires a hypergradient involving an inverse-Hessian-vector product for the inner objective. We address this challenge by showing that, at the inner optimum, the Hessian of the inner objective is proportional to the Fisher information matrix of the policy, yielding a structured Fisher-based hypergradient closely related to Natural Hypergradient Descent. To address the resulting scalability bottleneck associated with large Fisher matrices, we approximate the required inverse-Fisher-vector product using a streaming spectral sketch, avoiding explicit construction of the Fisher matrix. We evaluate our approach against a first-order stochastic bilevel baseline across discrete- and continuous-control environments. The results demonstrate competitive policy performance and strong reward-ranking quality, while Fisher sketching reduces curvature-storage complexity and can improve computational efficiency relative to an explicit Fisher solver.

</details>


### [222] [Uncertainty-Aware Deep Learning for Genomics Applications: Insights from an Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11054)
*Sepideh Saran,Mahsa Ghanbari,Uwe Ohler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Bayesian neural networks provide more reliable uncertainty estimates in genomics—especially under class imbalance and distribution shift—with higher computational cost; uncertainty can improve high-quality prediction selection in protein–RNA interactions, supported by a cross-method empirical analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uncertainty quantification (UQ) in deep learning for genomics is underexplored. A systematic evaluation of major UQ methods is needed to understand their reliability given genomic data characteristics and modalities.

Method: Empirical evaluation comparing Deep Ensembles, Bayesian Neural Networks, and Monte Carlo dropout across two genomic application areas and modalities: sequence-to-activity models and single-cell expression analysis; assesses calibration, reliability, and utility of UQ for prediction selection; derives practical guidelines.

Result: Bayesian Neural Networks better capture uncertainty due to strong class imbalance and out-of-distribution data in genomics, despite higher computational cost; uncertainty scores can be used to select high-quality predictions in protein–RNA interactions; Deep Ensembles and MC-Dropout are comparatively less effective in these aspects.

Conclusion: Provides guidelines on when and how to apply UQ methods in genomics, highlighting the strengths and limitations of each approach and demonstrating the practical utility of uncertainty estimates for improving prediction reliability.

Abstract: Deep learning models have emerged as the standard computational tool for a wide range of applications in genomics. Yet, uncertainty quantification (UQ) -- and more specifically, the reliability of different uncertainty estimates in this domain -- has received little systematic attention. This work presents an empirical analysis of UQ in deep learning models, focusing on genomics applications. In a series of experiments, we contrast Deep Ensembles, Bayesian Neural Networks, and Monte Carlo-dropout methods. We assess their ability to quantify uncertainty in different scenarios, accounting for common dataset characteristics in two genomic application areas and modalities: sequence-to-activity models, and single-cell expression analysis. Our systematic comparison framework provides guidelines for the applicability and reliability of UQ methods in genomics, highlighting their strengths and limitations in different scenarios. We show that Bayesian Neural Networks are better at capturing uncertainty caused by strong class imbalance and out-of-distribution data in genomics, despite their computational disadvantages. Moreover, we show how uncertainty scores can be used to select high-quality predictions in protein-RNA interactions.

</details>


### [223] [Batch Size or Negatives? A Selection Rule for Memory-Constrained Recommender Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11061)
*Artyom Sabitov,Daniil Volkov,Alexey Zaytsev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Under a fixed memory budget B = nk for sampled-softmax in recommender training, fastest convergence occurs with n ≈ B and k ≈ 1; maximize batch size rather than negatives; validated on synthetic data and four real benchmarks including MovieLens-20M; code is provided.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Determine how to allocate memory between batch size and negative samples in sampled-softmax training for large-scale neural recommender systems to optimize convergence and final performance under a fixed memory budget.

Method: Theoretical analysis under standard smoothness and gradient-variance assumptions to derive convergence behavior as a function of n (batch size) and k (negatives) with fixed B = nk; identify optimal allocation n ≈ B, k ≈ 1. Empirical validation on controlled synthetic data and four real sequential recommender benchmarks (including MovieLens-20M), comparing memory-efficient configurations and reporting convergence speed and final performance.

Result: The analysis indicates that, under the fixed memory constraint, the fastest convergence is achieved when the batch size is maximized and the number of negative samples is minimized, i.e., n ~ B and k ~ 1. This yields faster convergence and better final recommendation quality compared to imbalanced allocations within the same memory budget.

Conclusion: The study provides both theoretical and empirical guidance for configuring memory usage during training of recommender systems. Practitioners should favor larger batches with very few negatives under a fixed memory budget. The authors also release code and reproducibility materials.

Abstract: Large-scale neural recommender systems are typically trained with a softmax cross-entropy objective over the full item vocabulary. For a typical large number of possible items $K$, the final classification layer dominates memory, requiring $O(nK)$ logits and gradients to materialize for a batch of $n$ examples. Sampled softmax reduces this cost by restricting the objective to only $k \ll K$ candidate negative items, resulting in an $O(nk)$ memory. However, for a fixed budget $B = n k$, it remains unclear whether one should prioritize larger batches or the inclusion of more negative items.
  We address this question by analyzing sampled-softmax training under a fixed memory constraint. Under standard smoothness and variance assumptions, our theoretical evidence suggests that the fastest convergence arises from an $ n \sim B, k \sim 1$ allocation. So, an actionable rule is to include as many objects as possible given computational constraints.
  Our theory is supported by controlled synthetic and synthetic and four real sequential recommendation benchmarks, including MovieLens-20M. The suggested configuration achieve faster convergence and better final recommendation quality than imbalanced alternatives within the same memory constraint. These findings provide a theoretical and empirical foundation for configuring memory during the training of recommender systems. Code, reproducibility materials, and all scripts for generating figures are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/LimitedMemoryRule-BBFB

</details>


### [224] [Cross-View Feature Matching: Survey, Benchmarking, and Foundation-Model Perspectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11093)
*Songlin Du,Xiaoyong Lu,Zeyu Wu,Xiaobo Lu,Guobao Xiao,Bin Fan,Jiayi Ma,Takeshi Ikenaga*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A survey synthesizing cross-view feature matching with a unified taxonomy, benchmarking, and references to vision foundation models (VFMs).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address fragmentation in problem formulations, architectures, training paradigms, and evaluation protocols, and to provide a coherent framework for analysis and fair comparisons.

Method: Proposes a structured taxonomy (feature extraction, single-type and multi-type matchers, VFM-based methods, training strategies, robust estimation), and a unified experimental benchmarking under consistent protocols; analyzes advances and distills design principles.

Result: A consolidated review with a coherent framework, unified benchmarking, and guidance on current trends and future directions in cross-view feature matching, emphasizing efficiency, robustness, and cross-domain generalization.

Conclusion: Offers a comprehensive reference for evolution, current landscape, and future development of cross-view feature matching in the era of vision foundation models.

Abstract: Cross-view feature matching aims to establish reliable correspondences across images with large viewpoint variations. Over the past decade, the field has evolved from task-specific models toward increasingly unified and generalizable correspondence models, with recent progress further driven by the emergence of vision foundation models (VFMs). Despite these advances, existing studies remain highly diverse in their problem formulations, model architectures, training paradigms, and evaluation protocols, making it difficult to obtain a unified understanding of the field. In this survey, we present a unified review of cross-view feature matching. We first introduce a structured taxonomy covering feature extraction, single-type feature matcher, multi-type feature matcher, VFMs based methods, training strategy and robust estimation, providing a coherent framework for analysis and comparison. We further examine recent advances, distilling key design principles and highlighting the shift toward unified and generalizable correspondence models. We also provide a unified experimental benchmarking of representative state-of-the-art methods under consistent protocols, enabling fair and comprehensive performance comparisons.
