In addition, we discuss open challenges and future directions, including efficiency, robustness under extreme conditions, and cross-domain generalization. This survey aims to provide a comprehensive and structured reference for understanding the evolution, current landscape, and future development of cross-view feature matching in the era of vision foundation models.

</details>


### [225] [Two-stage Odd Residual Flows for Mean-Preserving Probabilistic Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11114)
*Kiran Madhusudhanan,Christian Klötergens,Lars Schmidt-Thieme,Vijaya Krishna Yalavarthi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Two-stage odd residual flows (TORF) decouples mean forecasting from uncertainty estimation: Stage 1 provides accurate mean with a deterministic model; Stage 2 uses a restricted normalizing flow with odd functions to model residual uncertainty around the mean without sampling, preserving the mean and delivering competitive density estimates (CRPS).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the trade-off between distributional flexibility and accurate mean prediction in probabilistic forecasting; avoid degraded mean accuracy from joint NLL in parametric methods and expensive sampling in flexible generative models.

Method: Stage 1: pre-trained deterministic model for mean; Stage 2: Restricted Normalizing Flow with strictly odd functions modeling residuals around the point forecast; mean preservation enforced without sampling.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art deterministic accuracy (NMAE) and strong density estimation (CRPS) for both short- and long-horizon forecasting.

Conclusion: TORF effectively decouples mean forecasting from uncertainty estimation, combining accuracy with robust probabilistic calibration without sampling overhead.

Abstract: Probabilistic forecasting plays an essential role in risk-sensitive decision-making, particularly in long-horizon settings. However, existing approaches often face a fundamental trade-off between distributional flexibility and accurate mean prediction. Traditional parametric methods, such as Mean Variance Estimation (MVE), can suffer from degraded point accuracy when trained under joint Negative Log-Likelihood (NLL) objectives, while modern-flexible generative models, including Normalizing Flows and Diffusion Models, typically rely on costly Monte Carlo sampling and may yield suboptimal mean estimates. To address this limitation, we propose Two-stage Odd Residual Flows (TORF), a framework that decouples mean forecasting from uncertainty estimation. In the first stage, a pre-trained deterministic model is used to produce an accurate mean prediction. In the second stage, a Restricted Normalizing Flow, with strictly odd functions learns flexible residual distributions around the point forecast, guaranteeing mean preservation from the first stage without sampling. Experiments show that TORF achieves state-of-the-art deterministic accuracy (NMAE) while providing strong density estimation performance (CRPS) on short and long-horizon forecasting.

</details>


### [226] [A Recommendation System Approach for Interference-Robust Sensor Subset Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11143)
*Kaan Buyukkalayci,Kyle Pak,Merve Karakas,Christina Fragouli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A recommendation-system-inspired method using frequency-band acoustic features and a Two-Tower MLP to select sensor subsets for tracking, outperforming RSSI baseline by ~20% while maintaining real-time efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome interference and limitations of RSSI-based subset selection for expensive sensing modalities (e.g., cameras) in tracking; need scalable, low-overhead selection.

Method: Two-Tower MLP scoring model that maps candidate subset features (frequency-band acoustic features) and subset metadata into subset scores; framework reminiscent of recommender systems to rank subsets for sensing resource allocation.

Result: Outdoor vehicle-tracking experiments show around 20% accuracy gain over RSSI baseline; computationally lightweight enabling real-time selective sensing.

Conclusion: Demonstrates effective, scalable sensor-subset selection leveraging acoustic features; suggests potential for broader deployment and extension to other modalities or environments; further work could address robustness to variations, feature ablation, and generalization.

Abstract: This paper develops a method for sensor-subset selection for tracking. Prior work showed that low-cost acoustic Received Signal Strength Indicator (RSSI) measurements can be used to recommend subsets of sensor nodes whose expensive sensing modalities, such as cameras, can achieve high tracking accuracy. While efficient, RSSI-based approaches are challenged by acoustic interference. We propose a recommendation-system-inspired framework that instead leverages frequency-band acoustic features and a Two-Tower Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) architecture to efficiently score candidate sensor subsets. Experimental results on outdoor vehicle-tracking deployments show that the proposed method can improve accuracy by around 20\% over the RSSI baseline while maintaining the low computational overhead required for real-time selective sensing.

</details>


### [227] [DACRI: Decision-Aware Causal Intervention Ranking for Critical Supply Chains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11154)
*Shiqi Huang,Jiani He,Dingyan Shang,Yihua Xu,Jize Li,Yan Lyu,Lashimi Muraleedharan Nair*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A controlled synthetic SCM benchmark (CriticalSCM-Bench v1) enables ground-truth evaluation of intervention policies; LambdaMART offers mixed gains; results depend on archetype; simpler policies sometimes outperform; robust but limited in OOD; explanations show stable decisions under deterministic validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Disentangle disruption detection from optimal interventions by providing causal ground truth and an explicit net-value objective for policy evaluation in supply-chain risk management.

Method: Introduce CriticalSCM-Bench v1 with causal ground truth, paired factual/counterfactual rollouts, and a net-value objective; compare LambdaMART to a full-information train-selected static benchmark; perform stress tests and a guarded explanation study across 540 generations.

Result: LambdaMART yields 5.7–16.2% median normalized net-value gains for semiconductor and critical-material archetypes, but not for digital infrastructure; a domain-informed constant-buffer policy outperforms adaptive ranking for digital infrastructure; in partial/delayed settings, LambdaMART preserves 33–75% of full-clamp value; stress tests show fidelity, timing, cost, and held-out disruptions can reorder policies; critical materials show weaker out-of-distribution retention; guarded explanations preserve fixed intervention decisions after deterministic validation, though wording remains unstable.

Conclusion: Adaptive ranking adds value in some regimes but simpler structural policies prevail in others; policy effectiveness is regime-dependent and sensitive to fidelity and domain characteristics; explanation-based stability is achievable under deterministic validation although descriptions may vary.

Abstract: Detecting or attributing a supply-chain disruption is not the same as selecting the intervention that maximizes recoverable net value. We present CriticalSCM-Bench v1, a controlled synthetic benchmark with causal ground truth, paired factual/counterfactual rollouts, and an explicit net-value objective. Relative to a full-information train-selected static benchmark, LambdaMART improves median normalized net value by 5.7--16.2\%, with paired statistical support on the semiconductor and critical-material archetypes but not on digital infrastructure. On digital infrastructure, a domain-informed constant-buffer policy remains stronger, showing that greater model complexity is not uniformly justified. Across partial and delayed settings, LambdaMART retains 33--75\% of full-clamp value. Stress tests further show that intervention fidelity, timing, cost, and held-out disruptions can alter policy ordering. Critical materials show the weakest out-of-distribution retention. Separately, a guarded explanation study over 540 generations preserves every fixed intervention decision after deterministic validation and template fallback, although exact wording remains unstable. Within this controlled setting, the results identify regimes in which adaptive ranking adds value and those in which simpler structural policies remain preferable.

</details>


### [228] [The Gaussian-Multinoulli Restricted Boltzmann Machine: A Potts Model Extension of the GRBM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11635)
*Nikhil Kapasi,Mohamed Elfouly,William Whitehead,Luke Theogarajan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GM-RBM extends Gaussian-Bernoulli RBMs by using q-state categorical (Potts) hidden units, creating a richer discrete latent space. It derives energy, conditionals, and learning rules; uses contrastive divergence with temperature annealing and intra-slot diversity constraints to prevent collapse. Evaluated under capacity-matched and parameter-matched settings against GB-RBM; shows competitive or improved recall on analogical recall and structured memory benchmarks with comparable training cost and using Gibbs updates. It demonstrates when categorical latents are a scalable alternative to binary latents for discrete inference in RBMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete, structured latent representations are valuable for associative memory and symbolic reasoning, but standard continuous latent models struggle to capture multivalued concepts. A discrete latent space with q-ary units could offer richer latent state representations while remaining tractable.

Method: Define GM-RBM with Gaussian visible units and multi-state (q-ary) hidden units. Provide a self-contained derivation of the energy function, conditional distributions, and learning rules. Training uses contrastive divergence with temperature annealing and intra-slot diversity constraints to avoid state collapse. Experiments compare capacity-matched and parameter-matched GM-RBM to GB-RBM with an equivalent number of latent assignments. Inference uses Gibbs updates.

Result: GM-RBM achieves competitive, often improved recall at equal capacity with similar training cost versus GB-RBM, on analogical recall and structured memory benchmarks. The discrete q-ary formulation is efficient to implement and scales well. The work clarifies when categorical hidden units can be a simple, scalable alternative to binary latents for discrete inference within tractable RBMs.

Conclusion: Categorical hidden units provide a viable, scalable alternative to binary latents in RBMs for discrete inference, particularly when structured latent representations are beneficial. Under capacity- and parameter-matched comparisons, GM-RBM can match or exceed GB-RBM performance with only Gibbs updates.

Abstract: Many real-world tasks, from associative memory to symbolic reasoning, benefit from discrete, structured representations that standard continuous latent models can struggle to express. We introduce the Gaussian-Multinoulli Restricted Boltzmann Machine (GM-RBM), a generative energy-based model that extends the Gaussian-Bernoulli RBM (GB-RBM) by replacing binary hidden units with q-state categorical (Potts) units, yielding a richer latent state space for multivalued concepts. We provide a self-contained derivation of the energy, conditional distributions, and learning rules, and detail practical training choices (contrastive divergence with temperature annealing and intra-slot diversity constraints) that avoid state collapse. To separate architectural effects from sheer latent capacity, we evaluate under both capacity-matched and parameter-matched setups, comparing GM-RBM with GB-RBM configured to have the same number of possible latent assignments. On analogical recall and structured memory benchmarks, GM-RBM achieves competitive, and in several regimes improved, recall at equal capacity with comparable training cost, despite using only Gibbs updates. The discrete q-ary formulation is also amenable to efficient implementation. These results clarify when categorical hidden units provide a simple, scalable alternative to binary latents for discrete inference within tractable RBMs.

</details>


### [229] [Hierarchical Empirical-Bayes Naive Bayes: Minimax Smoothing and Calibration with AODE Extension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11162)
*Nguyen Thai Anh,Truong Viet Vu,Tran Thien Thanh,Vo Nguyen Quoc Bao,Ngo Hoang Tu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes hierarchical empirical-Bayes Naive Bayes (HEB-NB) with data-adaptive Dirichlet smoothing for class-feature conditionals; extends to HEB-AODE. Demonstrates non-asymptotic error bounds and a tight lower bound; achieves state-of-the-art probabilistic performance and calibration on high-cardinality data across 31 benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard NB smoothing rules (Laplace, Lidstone, Krichevsky-Trofimov, m-estimate) use fixed smoothing that ignores feature cardinality, sample size, and class imbalance, causing bias on high-cardinality tabular data. There is a need for adaptive smoothing with cross-class information sharing while retaining closed-form inference.

Method: Introduce HEB-NB where each class-feature conditional probability is smoothed by a Dirichlet prior with concentration learned via Type-II maximum likelihood. This enables data-adaptive smoothing and information sharing across classes with closed-form updates. Extend to HEB-AODE (average one-dependence estimators). Prove non-asymptotic L1 error bound for HEB-NB matching empirical-distribution minimax rate plus vanishing bias, and a matching Laplace-tight lower bound. Derive plug-in excess Bayes-risk bound via total-variation tensorization and a population top-1 calibration error corollary.

Result: Theoretical guarantees: non-asymptotic L1 error bound with minimax rate plus bias; a tight lower bound distinguishing from Laplace. Calibration and Bayes-risk results via TV tensorization. Empirically: HEB-NB achieves best average Friedman rank across 31 UCI/OpenML datasets on probabilistic metrics, up to 22.1% log-loss reduction on high-cardinality data; HEB-AODE improves over vanilla AODE; mutual-information weighting reduces top-1 ECE by 41-70%.

Conclusion: Adaptive hierarchical Dirichlet smoothing in NB (and its AODE variant) yields substantial gains in probabilistic accuracy and calibration on high-cardinality data, with strong theoretical guarantees and practical performance improvements across diverse benchmarks.

Abstract: The Naive Bayes (NB) classifier remains a standard choice for categorical data, yet its widely used smoothing rules, such as Laplace, Lidstone, Krichevsky-Trofimov, and the $m$-estimate, all prescribe a fixed smoothing strength that ignores feature cardinality, sample size, and class imbalance, inducing a non-vanishing bias on modern high-cardinality tabular data. We propose hierarchical empirical-Bayes Naive Bayes (HEB-NB), in which each class-feature conditional probability is smoothed by a Dirichlet prior whose concentration is learned data-adaptively via Type-II maximum likelihood, enabling principled information sharing across classes while retaining closed-form inference. We further introduce HEB average one-dependence estimators (HEB-AODE), showing that the adaptive smoothing transfers cleanly to structural relaxations of NB. Theoretically, we establish a non-asymptotic $\ell_1$ error bound for HEB-NB matching the empirical-distribution minimax rate plus a vanishing data-adaptive bias, together with a matching Laplace-tight lower bound that yields a finite-sample, risk-level strict separation from Laplace. We further derive a plug-in excess Bayes-risk bound via total-variation tensorization and a population top-1 expected calibration error (ECE) corollary. Empirically, across 31 UCI and OpenML benchmarks, HEB-NB attains the best average Friedman rank on probabilistic metrics, with up to 22.1% log-loss reductions on high-cardinality datasets and consistent improvements of HEB-AODE over vanilla AODE. Combining HEB-NB with mutual-information weighting reduces top-1 ECE by 41%-70%, demonstrating substantial gains in probabilistic accuracy and calibration.

</details>


### [230] [Beyond a Bag of Features: Set-Level Instability in Sparse Autoencoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11197)
*Nikolai Bolik,Lennart Stöpler,Artur Andrzejak*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAE latent-set overlap is evaluated as an interpretable similarity measure for LLM representations. In toy and natural-language settings, SAE sets show meaningful structure, but they do not align with human category boundaries or within-category typicality any better than dense embeddings or residual streams. Active latent sets track internal model similarity and exhibit substantial mismatch with human judgments under semantic changes, suggesting SAE features do not compose via simple bag-of-features semantics in non-ideal settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test whether sparse autoencoder (SAE) latent sets provide human-aligned, interpretable similarity measures for LLM representations and to assess their compositional semantics relative to dense embeddings.

Method: Define active latent SAE sets; compute overlap among sets as a similarity metric; validate the metric in toy models for union-like compositional structure; examine semantically coherent neighborhoods in natural text; extend to human-concepts analysis; compare SAE set similarity with dense embeddings and residual-stream states; perform controlled semantic modifications to test alignment with human judgments.

Result: SAE latent sets can recover union-like compositional structure in toy models and yield semantically coherent neighborhoods in natural text, indicating the measure is meaningful. However, SAE activation overlap does not recover human category boundaries or within-category typicality more faithfully than dense embeddings or residual streams. Instead, SAE similarity tracks model-internal similarity structure. Under controlled semantic modifications, there is a substantial mismatch between human judgments of conceptual change and SAE active-set changes.

Conclusion: SAE features do not implement simple bag-of-features compositionality in non-ideal settings; while the set-level similarity is meaningful, it does not yield human-aligned category structure beyond what dense representations provide.

Abstract: Shani et al. (2026) show that LLM representations broadly recover human category boundaries, while failing to reflect fine-grained typicality structure. Their analysis uses cosine similarity over dense model representations. We revisit their approach using overlap over active sparse autoencoder (SAE) latent sets as a more interpretable similarity measure. We first verify that this set-level measure is meaningful: SAE latent sets can recover union-like compositional structure in controlled toy models and induce semantically coherent neighborhoods in natural text. Extending the human-concepts analysis to SAE set similarities, we find that SAE activation sets do not recover human category boundaries or within-category typicality more faithfully than dense embeddings or residual-stream states, but instead track model-internal similarity structure. To probe this gap further, we study active latent sets under well-controlled semantic modifications, revealing a substantial mismatch between human judgements of conceptual change and change in the SAE active set. We interpret this as evidence that, outside idealised settings, SAE features do not compose via simple bag-of-features semantics.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [231] [Closed-Loop LLM Co-Pilots for Digital Agriculture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09949)
*Serge Kernbach*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs autonomously control a phytosensor-enabled vertical farm, shifting from human-in-the-loop to autonomous experimentation to optimize growth and energy use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Advance digital agriculture by integrating large language models with multisensor plant data to enable autonomous decision-making and experimental control in complex biosystems.

Method: A closed-loop framework where an LLM processes 49-channel multisensor data (spectral, electrochemical, dielectric), provides natural-language interpretation, and actuates hardware (full-spectrum lighting, 2-hour control intervals) to optimize microclimate and phenotyping. Validated on vertical-farm and single-plant setups across three case studies, enabling multi-parameter optimization.

Result: Autonomous agents achieved 35% reduction in production cycle time vs periodic control, 18% reduction in energy consumption with marginal cultivation time increase, and uncovered a dark-induced chlorophyll accumulation strategy yielding 67.9% energy savings."

Conclusion: LLMs can serve as autonomous co-pilots in digital agriculture, improving cost-to-value, reducing expert labor needs, and enabling data-driven exploration of complex biosystems and ecologies.

Abstract: This study evaluates the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) in complex biological systems, evolving from data analysis to autonomous, AI-guided experimentation. The framework is driven by data from a 49-channel phytosensor network, encompassing multispectral, electrochemical, and dielectric modalities. To enhance accessibility, the system provides real-time natural-language interpretation for both specialists and non-experts. However, its core advantage lies in the transition from human-in-the-loop analysis to autonomous control. Processing biophysical data, the LLM evaluates plant physiology and triggers hardware actuators to optimize microclimates, execute phenotyping protocols, or induce controlled stress scenarios. This closed-loop architecture establishes a direct AI-biology interface, enabling data-driven exploration of complex biosystems and ecologies. The framework was validated across three case studies, based on a vertical farm and a single-plant setup and deciphered complex micro- and macro-fluctuations in plant physiology. Agents in a production-scale deployment executed multi-parameter optimization, balancing biomass accumulation, chlorophyll content, and energy consumption. The LLM processed biosensing telemetry to modulate full-spectrum, 450 nm, and 660 nm lighting at 2-hour intervals. Compared to periodic control, the system in minimal-time mode reduced the production cycle by 35%. In the energy-optimization mode, it reduced energy consumption by 18% with only a marginal increase in cultivation time, exploiting physiological inertia via light pulses. Finally, the agents autonomously developed an unforeseen strategy of dark-induced chlorophyll accumulation, resulting in a 67.9% energy saving. This framework transforms LLMs into autonomous co-pilots for digital agriculture, improving the cost-to-value ratio and lowering computational and expert-labor constraints.

</details>


### [232] [SPOTting the Future: Lookahead Explanations for Deep Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09967)
*Tamar Gozlan,Claudia V. Goldman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SPOT builds a sampling-based, model-agnostic interpretability tree for DRL policies by recursively simulating actions and successor states, providing a global view of policy preferences and downstream behavior with formal guarantees on recovery of the most probable action and high-entropy disagreement; demonstrated on SUMO-RL traffic signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DRL policies are hard to interpret; existing attribution methods are often single-step and local. A global, action-sequence-centric view is needed to understand long-horizon decision-making and downstream effects.

Method: Given access to a policy and an environment simulator, SPOT constructs a finite-horizon tree by sampling actions at each node and simulating consequent states, effectively building an empirical representation of action preferences and their downstream evolution. The approach is model-agnostic and relies on sampling-based expansion with a formal analysis of asymptotic behavior.

Result: SPOT provides formal guarantees: asymptotic recovery of the policy's unique most probable action and a characterization of disagreement behavior under high-entropy policies. Empirically demonstrated in the SUMO-RL traffic-signal control domain, illustrating how the tree reveals policy preferences, compares future trajectories, and exposes downstream behaviors not visible to single-timestep feature-attribution methods.

Conclusion: SPOT offers a principled, interpretable, model-agnostic framework for DRL policy analysis that captures long-horizon decision dynamics via a sampling-based tree. It enables inspection of action preferences and downstream trajectories, with theoretical guarantees, though practical use may require careful management of computational cost and simulator fidelity.

Abstract: Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) agents achieve strong performance in complex environments, yet their decision-making processes remain difficult to interpret. We introduce SPOT (Sampling Policy Observation Tree), a novel model-agnostic, sampling-based framework for interpreting DRL policies. Given access to the policy and an environment simulator, SPOT constructs an interpretable finite-horizon tree by sampling actions and recursively simulating the resulting successor states. The tree provides an empirical representation of the policy's action preferences and their possible downstream evolution. We provide formal guarantees establishing SPOT's asymptotic recovery of the policy's unique most probable action and characterizing its disagreement behavior under high-entropy policies. We demonstrate SPOT in the SUMO-RL traffic-signal control domain. The case study illustrates how its tree-based representation can be used to inspect policy preferences, compare alternative future trajectories, and reveal downstream behaviors that are not visible through single-timestep feature-attribution methods.

</details>


### [233] [MIDAS: Mutual Information Disentanglement with Uncertainty-Aware Fusion for Incomplete Multimodal Sentiment Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09986)
*Yuhua Wen,Yingying Zhou,Qifei Li,Yingming Gao,Zhengqi Wen,Jianhua Tao,Ya Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MIDAS is a unified variational framework for incomplete multimodal sentiment analysis that disentangles modality representations into shared and exclusive factors and uses uncertainty-aware fusion to robustly integrate modalities when some inputs are missing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world multimodal data often lack or corrupt modalities, and existing imputation/heuristics fail to extract task-relevant information; a principled approach to representation disentanglement and robust fusion is needed.

Method: Per-modality multivariate Gaussian latent variables are decomposed into shared and exclusive factors; a minimax objective enforces disentanglement by minimizing mutual information between shared and exclusive spaces and maximizing mutual information among the shared spaces across modalities; an uncertainty-aware fusion uses posterior variance to weight latent features during fusion.

Result: On three datasets, MIDAS achieves strong, consistent gains over competitive baselines under various incomplete settings, demonstrating robustness and effectiveness.

Conclusion: MIDAS provides a principled, robust approach to incomplete multimodal sentiment analysis via mutual-information-based disentanglement and uncertainty-aware fusion.

Abstract: Most existing multimodal sentiment analysis approaches assume access to complete multimodal inputs. However, real-world applications frequently encounter incomplete or corrupted modalities, posing a critical challenge. Although several methods have been proposed to tackle this issue, they mainly rely on data imputation and heuristic coordination constraints, which fail to effectively extract and leverage task-relevant information from the incomplete multimodal data. To address this challenge, we propose a unified framework termed Mutual Information Disentanglement with uncertainty-Aware fuSion (MIDAS), which effectively restructures multimodal representations under incomplete conditions. MIDAS adopts a variational modeling strategy to represent each modality with multivariate Gaussian latent variables and further decomposes them into shared and exclusive factors. To obtain reliable representations, we design a minimax objective that minimizes the mutual information between shared and exclusive spaces for stable disentanglement, while maximizing the mutual information among shared spaces across modalities to enhance semantic alignment. In addition, an uncertainty-aware fusion mechanism is introduced, where posterior variance is leveraged as a reliability indicator to adaptively weight latent features during fusion, ensuring robust integration even when modalities are incomplete. Extensive experiments on three widely used datasets show that MIDAS achieves strong and consistent performance gains over competitive baselines across a wide range of incomplete settings, demonstrating its effectiveness and robustness for incomplete data scenarios.

</details>


### [234] [Towards Sustainable Artificial Intelligence: A Comprehensive Review and Comparative Analysis of Deep Learning Models' Carbon Footprint](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09998)
*Samar Garrab,Sarra Boughriou,Manel BenSassi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Systematic review of Green AI/DL and carbon accounting tools, plus an empirical CPU-based study showing training dominates emissions and that larger models do not guarantee proportional accuracy gains; emphasizes balancing performance with environmental cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Growing energy demands and carbon footprint of AI, especially large DL models, motivate a structured synthesis of Green AI concepts and practical measurement tools.

Method: A two-part study: (i) a systematic review of Green AI/Green DL and optimization strategies, and (ii) an empirical evaluation on a CPU-based setup with six DL models for a multi-label classification task to quantify emissions and identify lifecycle stages contributing most to footprint; comparison of several carbon measurement tools.

Result: Training is the primary source of emissions; increased architectural complexity does not reliably yield proportional accuracy gains; environmental considerations should inform model selection and design; carbon measurement tools can yield varying estimates.

Conclusion: Sustainability should be integrated into AI system design and model selection, leveraging Green DL techniques and lifecycle-aware carbon accounting to mitigate environmental impact.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) and Machine Learning (ML) have become powerful tools for supporting and automating complex human tasks. Despite their benefits, growing attention has been directed toward their environmental implications, primarily due to their high energy demands and associated carbon emissions. This concern is particularly relevant in light of the increasing deployment of large-scale models, especially Deep Learning (DL) architectures, which provide advanced predictive capabilities but require substantial computational resources. This paper presents a systematic review of research on Green AI, Green DL, and optimization techniques aimed at reducing the environmental impact of AI models. In addition, we examine and compare several carbon measurement tools for estimating emissions generated by AI algorithms. To complement the review, we conducted an empirical evaluation using a CPU-based experimental setup, in which six DL models were implemented for a multi-label classification task. The objective was to quantify and compare their overall carbon emissions and to determine which stages of the DL lifecycle contribute most significantly to the total footprint. The results show that the training phase is the primary source of emissions. Moreover, the findings reveal that increased architectural complexity does not systematically translate into proportional accuracy gains, highlighting the importance of carefully balancing predictive performance and environmental cost. These results reinforce the need to integrate sustainability considerations into model selection and AI system design.

</details>


### [235] [ReCBM: Uncertainty-Gated Relational Reasoning for Concept Bottleneck Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10004)
*An Sui,Yuzhu Li,Fuping Wu,Xiahai Zhuang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReCBM enables uncertainty-guided relational reasoning in concept bottleneck models by encoding co-occurrence, implication, and exclusion among concepts and gating their influence via uncertainty, improving robustness to missing/flipped concepts, enabling uncertainty-aware interventions, and enabling compact task-relevant concept subsets without hurting task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CBMs are interpretable but fragile when concept states are unreliable; misleading evidence through the bottleneck can harm explanations and predictions; prior CBM variants do not adequately address robust reasoning under uncertainty.

Method: Introduce semantically defined concept relations into the bottleneck (co-occurrence, implication, exclusion). Use uncertainty estimates to gate/refine the exchange of evidence among concepts, enabling uncertainty-aware propagation and suppression of unreliable signals.

Result: Empirical evaluation across diverse datasets shows improvements in recovering true concepts and task performance under missing or flipped concepts, supports uncertainty-aware intervention, and identifies compact task-relevant concept subsets without degrading downstream accuracy.

Conclusion: ReCBM provides robust, uncertainty-aware relational reasoning for CBMs, enhancing interpretability and reliability of explanations and predictions while enabling efficient use of concepts.

Abstract: Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) provide an interpretable framework by grounding predictions in human-understandable concepts, enabling semantic inspection and test-time intervention. Recent variants have improved CBMs through richer concept representations, uncertainty estimation, and dependency modeling. However, robust reasoning under unreliable concept states remains underexplored. Without such reasoning, misleading semantic evidence can propagate through the bottleneck, compromising both explanations and downstream predictions. To address this issue, we propose ReCBM, an uncertainty-gated relational reasoning framework for CBMs. ReCBM introduces semantically defined concept relations into the bottleneck and uses uncertainty to guide their refinement. By modeling co-occurrence, implication, and exclusion, ReCBM specifies how evidence is exchanged across concepts, while uncertainty modulates the contribution of each concept during this process. Experiments across diverse datasets showed that ReCBM improved concept and task recovery under missing and flipped concepts, supported uncertainty-aware intervention, and extracted compact task-relevant concept subsets without degrading downstream performance.

</details>


### [236] [Automating and Scaling Behavioral Scientific Research on AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10030)
*Soo Yong Lee,Jongha Lee,Jaewan Chun,Hyunjin Hwang,Fanchen Bu,Ziv Ben-Zion,Taekwan Kim,Denny Borsboom,Jaemin Yoo,Kijung Shin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AEROBAT automates behavioral scientific research for AI agents, enabling end-to-end hypothesis generation, experimentation, analysis, and reporting for target behaviors; across 12 behaviors it tested 79 hypotheses via 1,240 experiments and 23,512 simulation rounds, yielding moderate-to-strong evidence for 26 hypotheses, including novel ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Behavioral analysis of AI agents is currently manual and labor-intensive; there is a need for scalable automation to systematically study agent behaviors and accelerate discovery.

Method: AEROBAT is a multi-agent system that autonomously generates hypotheses, designs and runs controlled experiments, assesses behaviors, analyzes results, and writes reports. It executed 23,512 simulation rounds across 1,240 experiments for 12 target behaviors, testing 79 hypotheses.

Result: Moderate-to-strong statistical evidence for 26 hypotheses, including novel findings; demonstrates automated behavioral scientific research can augment manual studies.

Conclusion: Automated behavioral scientific research can complement and extend manual research, enabling scalable, rapid investigation of AI agent behaviors.

Abstract: As AI agents are increasingly deployed in complex environments, understanding their behaviors becomes critical. Yet behavioral scientific research on AI agents remains manual and labor-intensive. We introduce AEROBAT, the first multi-agent system to automate behavioral scientific research on AI agents. Given an arbitrary target behavior by its user, AEROBAT automatically executes a full pipeline of behavioral scientific research---generating hypotheses about the behavior, designing and executing controlled experiments, making behavioral assessments, analyzing the results, and writing reports. For 12 target behaviors, we used AEROBAT to generate and test 79 hypotheses: designing 1,240 controlled experiments and executing 23,512 simulation rounds in total. Moderate-to-strong statistical evidence was found for 26 hypotheses, including some novel ones. In sum, our results demonstrate that automated behavioral scientific research on AI agents can complement and extend the reach of manual research.

</details>


### [237] [CHORUS: Complementary Experts for High-Coverage Testbench Stimulus Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10090)
*Hejia Zhang,Sheng Lu,Zhongming Yu,Chia-Tung Ho,Brucek Khailany,Jishen Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CHORUS shows post-training specialization and model merging to outperform a larger supervised-finetune plus RL baseline on hardware verification code generation, achieving 88.0% Pass@1 on CVDP-ECov with a 4B model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Executable feedback in LLM-driven code generation provides a more reliable learning signal than textual imitation alone. Hardware verification is a major, resource-intensive domain requiring high-coverage testbench stimuli, motivating post-training strategies that harness diverse expertise.

Method: Staged supervised fine-tuning (SFT) yields behaviorally diverse checkpoints; dense-reward reinforcement learning (RL) converts them into strong, complementary experts. These strengths can be combined either via training-free model merging or by further post-training to create a single model.

Result: The approach achieves 88.0% Pass@1 on CVDP-ECov, outperforming DeepSeek-R1 (671B) by 13.5 percentage points.

Conclusion: CHORUS demonstrates that combining diverse, specialized experts through merging or iterative post-training can surpass the best individual expert and deliver strong hardware-verification code-generation performance in a compact 4B model.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have advanced code generation, where executable feedback provides a more reliable learning signal than textual imitation alone. Hardware verification is an important application of code generation and accounts for a substantial fraction of modern chip design effort, with high-coverage testbench stimulus generation as a key task. We present CHORUS, a post-training framework that pushes performance beyond what a conventional supervised fine-tuning (SFT)-to-reinforcement learning (RL) pipeline achieves. CHORUS builds on two observations. First, staged SFT produces behaviorally diverse checkpoints, and dense-reward RL turns them into strong experts with comparable aggregate performance but distinct task-level strengths. Second, these complementary strengths can be exploited through either training-free model merging or further post-training to outperform the best individual expert. By consolidating the resulting specialists into a single 4B model, CHORUS achieves 88.0% Pass@1 on CVDP-ECov, outperforming DeepSeek-R1 (671B) by 13.5 percentage points.

</details>


### [238] [MESA:Task-Adaptive Multi-Structure Evidence Selection for Long-Horizon Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10108)
*Beidi Zhao,Yaoqi Chen,Yuru Feng,Menghao Li,Qianxi Zhang,Baotong Lu,Jianan Lu,Zhirui Wang,Xinjiang Wang,Shusen Xu,Zengzhong Li,Xiaoxiao Li,Qi Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Dynamic selection of multiple memory structures (MESA) improves long-horizon reasoning by query-adaptive fusion of five structure views, achieving stronger performance with fewer evidence tokens on AMA-Bench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon agents accumulate history; external memory structures provide incomplete views. Fixed or single-structure memory systems either inflate context with noise or block complementary evidence. A tailored, query-adaptive combination of memories is needed.

Method: MESA builds five complementary structure views for each trajectory and learns to select and fuse a query-specific subset from a library of specialized memory structures. It uses an end-to-end answer model, harness optimization with prior-guided search, and UCB-guided scheduling to balance exploration and exploitation under weak supervision.

Result: On AMA-Bench, MESA outperforms the strongest baseline by 8.5% while using 41% fewer evidence tokens than the all-structure alternative.

Conclusion: Structured, query-adaptive memory fusion improves both accuracy and efficiency for long-horizon agents; a tailored composition of multiple structural memories is superior to fixed or full-union approaches.

Abstract: Long-horizon agents accumulate trajectories spanning hundreds of interleaved reasoning, action, and observation steps, where answering a query may depend on evidence buried far back in the history. External memory stores such trajectories as structured representations, yet each structure provides a distinct and incomplete view. Existing multi-memory systems either read a fixed set of structures for every query, inflating context and introducing noise, or route each query to a single structure, preventing the composition of complementary evidence. A controlled analysis on AMA-Bench shows that the optimal memory configuration is typically neither a single structure nor the full union, but a tailored composition of multiple structural memories that varies with query and task demands. Motivated by these findings, we formulate structure-level dynamic selection: selecting and fusing a query-adaptive subset from a library of specialized memory structures. We propose MESA (a Multi-structure Evidence Selection framework for long-horizon Agent), which builds five complementary structure views of each trajectory and learns from end-to-end answer-level feedback to select and fuse a query-specific subset for a frozen answer model. To learn under this weak supervision, MESA employs harness optimization with prior-guided search and UCB-guided scheduling to balance exploration and exploitation. On AMA-Bench, MESA outperforms the strongest baseline by 8.5% while using 41% fewer evidence tokens than the all-structure alternative.

</details>


### [239] [The CASE Framework: A Multi-Disciplinary Control Architecture for Governing Enterprise Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10153)
*Srinivas Telukunta,Georgios Nektarios Lilis,Lucio Baron*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A four-layer CASE framework for autonomous AI governance (Control for individuals, Complex Adaptive for collectives, Supervisory cybernetics for human–agent teams, Engineering ops for fleets) with a five-level maturity model and a cross-layer coupling analysis, validated by three empirical studies that reveal an Emergence Gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prevailing single-discipline governance (often DevSecOps) cannot adequately manage agentic AI due to emergence and multi-layer interactions; regulatory demands (EU AI Act Article 14) require architectures that satisfy requisite variety.

Method: Formalize governance layers, derive cross-layer coupling conditions, introduce a zero-touch deployment paradox, map twenty-plus enterprise controls to classical constructs, and validate with three empirical studies (production failure trajectories, Layer 2 coverage, and deployment maturity).

Result: Support for the Emergence Gap thesis; identification of multi-layer failure trajectories, incomplete Layer 2 coverage in tools, and generally low maturity in public deployments; development of a five-level CASE maturity model with an objective assessment instrument.

Conclusion: To satisfy oversight and effectively govern agentic AI, architectures must embody requisite variety across all CASE layers; the Emergence Gap is real, and a formal, multi-layer maturity framework is necessary for production readiness and regulatory compliance.

Abstract: Enterprises are deploying autonomous AI agents faster than they can govern them, and prevailing approaches stretch a single discipline, typically DevSecOps built for deterministic automation, across every scale of agency. We argue that agentic AI governance is four problems, not one, each with a mature governing science. The CASE framework assigns Control theory to the individual agent (intent as setpoint, guardrails as feedback, evaluation as observation), complex Adaptive systems theory to agent collectives (where emergence makes single-agent assurance non-compositional), Supervisory cybernetics to human-agent teams (where the Law of Requisite Variety shows unaided human oversight fails structurally), and Engineering operations to fleets (extending error budgets to decision quality so autonomy becomes a controlled variable). We formalize each layer, derive cross-layer coupling conditions, including a zero-touch deployment paradox where excellence at one-layer strains the others, and trace twenty-plus enterprise controls to their classical constructs. Three empirical studies validate the thesis: 82 percent of documented production agent failures are multi-layer trajectories; none of 22 ecosystem tools offers full Layer 2 (emergence) coverage; and all 35 scored public deployments fall in the lowest maturity band. We name this mismatch, risk realized at the emergence layer against capability barely offered and practice absent, the Emergence Gap. A five-level maturity model with a non-compensatory bottleneck-weighted index and assessment instrument operationalizes CASE as a scientific rather than process maturity model, grounded in production enterprise agentic platforms. As EU AI Act Article 14 makes effective human oversight a legal requirement, only architectures satisfying requisite variety can make oversight real rather than ceremonial.

</details>


### [240] [SBCO: Self-Supervised, Verifier-Grounded Harness Optimization For Planning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10157)
*Vivek Kulkarni,Sudipta Paul,Aounon Kumar,Nicholas Tzou,Srinivas Chappidi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SBCO is a verifier-grounded, self-supervised harness optimizer that achieves open-ended improvement without self-modification; it decomposes verifiers and a harness policy, learned via approximate block coordinate ascent, using the agent's own feedback, and reduces compute by ~4-5.5x while matching/exceeding a self-modifying baseline in two planning domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: to reduce human engineering effort and enable cheaper self-improvement for planning tasks where alignment between task competence and self-modification is weak or absent.

Method: introduce SBCO, a Self-supervised Block Coordinate Optimizer; builds a bank of verifiers and a harness policy; trains via approximate block coordinate ascent; uses a fixed meta-agent; relies on self-supervised feedback (no human labels) from the agent's performance.

Result: in two domains, SBCO matches or exceeds a customized self-modifying baseline; requires 4-5.5x less compute.

Conclusion: verifier-grounded, self-supervised harness optimization is a viable, cheaper alternative to self-referential, self-modifying Gödel-machine approaches for tasks with explicit constraints; extends the improve-from-experience paradigm.

Abstract: Self-improving agents seek to reduce the human engineering effort behind AI systems by enabling them to evolve and self-improve their performance over time. Recently, methods like the Darwin Gödel Machine and the Huxley Gödel Machine have been proposed which enable open-ended, recursive self-improvement through self-reference where a coding agent edits its own code. Such self-referential self-improvement methods require that the competence required to perform the task coincides or aligns well with the competence required for self-modification which is the case for coding tasks. For domains or tasks, which do not satisfy the alignment needed, self-referential self-improvement is not available. In such cases, it is possible to adapt the above algorithms to other tasks by removing the self-referential aspect or introducing explicit self-modification of a meta-agent -- both computationally expensive, relying on population or self-modification search over many candidate agents. For planning tasks with explicit constraints, we propose a far cheaper alternative. We introduce SBCO (Self-supervised Block Coordinate Optimizer), a verifier-grounded harness optimizer in the same closed-loop, improve-from-experience family as the Gödel-machine methods, but self-supervised rather than self-referential. Given an agentic harness, SBCO learns a decomposed bank of verifiers and a harness policy via approximate block coordinate ascent, improving the agent's outputs from its own graded feedback---with a fixed meta-agent and no human labels. Across two domains SBCO matches or exceeds a customized self-modifying baseline while using 4-5.5 times less compute budget.

</details>


### [241] [Generating Attacks for LLMs with GFlowNets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10171)
*Berkay Ozcam,Irem Onen,Mehmet Fatih Amasyali,Emin Islam Tatli*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Automated, adaptive red-teaming of LLMs using GFlowNets to train an attacker against a victim LLM, enabling language-diverse adversarial inputs (including Turkish) and aiming to outperform benchmarks in English attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the limitations of manual red-teaming and fixed-dataset automated methods, by using generative flow networks to explore a broader, more diverse space of adversarial prompts and measure robustness.

Method: Train an attacker model with GFlowNets against a specified victim LLM to perform automated red-teaming and output a quantitative robustness score; generate attack inputs in English with improved effectiveness and capability to produce Turkish-language prompts.

Result: Proposes a framework and methodology for automated, language-diverse red-teaming using GFlowNets; claims potential improvement over existing benchmarks, and introduces a Turkish-language attack generator; empirical results are not detailed in the abstract.

Conclusion: Introduces a novel framework for LLM security evaluation by combining GFlowNets with attacker-victim dynamics and expanding linguistic coverage (Turkish), contributing to more robust and scalable red-teaming approaches.

Abstract: The rapid advancement of Large Language Models (LLMs) has facilitated their ubiquitous integration into various domains, leading to widespread adoption. However, this escalating trend has introduced significant security vulnerabilities, necessitating the identification and mitigation of flaws arising from malicious exploitation. Red teaming assessments, conducted to evaluate model robustness through diverse adversarial inputs, are essential for exposing security risks and implementing countermeasures. Currently, red teaming is performed either manually by experts or automatically using predefined attack datasets. Nevertheless, manual testing remains time-consuming, while existing automated methods suffer from limited creativity due to their inherent dependency on fixed datasets. In this study, we propose an automated, human-independent, and adaptive approach leveraging GFlowNets to identify LLM vulnerabilities by utilizing one large language model to test another. Within this framework, an attacker model is trained against a specified victim model to perform automated red teaming and provide a quantitative robustness score. This research aims to generate more effective adversarial attacks in English compared to existing benchmarks and, as a novel contribution to the literature, introduces a model capable of generating attack inputs in the Turkish language.

</details>


### [242] [TRACE: Trustworthy Retrieval-Augmented Conversational Engine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10176)
*Touseef Hasan,Laila Cure,Souvika Sarkar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Retrieval quality is critical for constraint-aware, trustworthy recommendations in noisy public service directories; TRACE improves constraint satisfaction and reduces hallucinations, with retrieval quality becoming the dominant factor across LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public service directories are noisy/heterogeneous and LLMs often produce unreliable, source-unverified recommendations. Improving retrieval quality is hypothesized to be key to robust constraint-aware recommendations.

Method: Introduce TRACE, a retrieval-based, constraint-aware system that parses user queries into structural and semantic constraints to guide retrieval, using a dual data representation; evaluate on a curated pantry directory and synthetic query benchmark; compare multiple knowledge-representation variants with and without knowledge graphs; test several open-source LLMs and a proprietary model.

Result: Strengthening retrieval substantially improves constraint satisfaction and reduces hallucinated recommendations. Differences between LLMs narrow as retrieval quality improves, indicating retrieval quality dominates model size effects in this setting.

Conclusion: Retrieval quality is a pivotal factor for robust, constraint-aware public service conversational systems; enhancing retrieval can mitigate unreliable outputs and harmonize model behavior across different LLMs.

Abstract: Public service chatbots are expected to deliver recommendations from an underlying public service directory, while also making sure that the recommendations respect explicit user constraints. In practice, public service directories are noisy and inconsistent, and general-purpose large language model (LLM) or AI-based chatbots frequently generate unreliable recommendations, citing unverified sources from the web. We investigate the impact of retrieval quality on constraint-aware recommendation in public service conversational systems built over noisy and heterogeneous service directories. We propose TRACE (Trustworthy Retrieval-Augmented Conversational Engine), a retrieval-based, constraint-aware framework that parses input user queries into structural and semantic constraints for downstream retrieval, with the help of a dual data representation schema. Using a curated statewide pantry directory and a synthetic query benchmark, we evaluate multiple knowledge-representation variants with and without knowledge graphs (KGs). We experiment with several open-source LLMs and a proprietary model, showing that strengthening retrieval substantially improves user constraint satisfaction while reducing hallucinated recommendations. Performance differences across LLMs narrowed in our experiments as retrieval quality improved, making results less sensitive to model size. These findings suggest that the quality of retrieval is key for robust public service conversational systems.

</details>


### [243] [Post-Hoc Sparse Coding of Latent Communication Between Vision-Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10198)
*Di Wu,Xiaohui Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Post-hoc sparse coding shows latent-space communication between vision-language agents is highly compressible: a sparse payload (k=4) with uint16 indices and float16 values reduces bytes by ~128x with negligible accuracy loss, motivating adaptive payloads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify redundancy and compressibility of a fixed-capacity latent transport channel (Vision Wormhole) and assess whether sparse coding can reduce communication overhead without compromising downstream performance.

Method: Fit a post-hoc sparse autoencoder to frozen Vision Wormhole activations; evaluate reconstruction, downstream utility, feature reuse, and token-level interventions across nine reasoning benchmarks; compare sparse payloads to the original dense float32 transport. Use k=4 active coefficients per token, a 4096-feature dictionary; measure bytes saved and task-level metrics (e.g., mean accuracy).

Result: Achieved ~128x reduction in transmitted bytes using a uint16-index/float16-value sparse payload with k=4. Seven-task non-AIME mean accuracy changed from 49.85% to 49.77%. The dictionary used ~50 features, and active sets had mean pairwise Jaccard similarity of 0.906, indicating strong reuse. The work establishes strong post-hoc compressibility but does not isolate sparse coding effects from position selection, reduced precision, low-rank structure, or SAE optimization.

Conclusion: Results support substantial latent-channel compressibility and motivate payloads that adapt to information content; highlights the need for matched-payload comparisons and adaptive communication mechanisms rather than fixed-size transmissions.

Abstract: Latent-space communication allows heterogeneous vision-language model agents to exchange continuous representations without serializing visual and reasoning states into text. Vision Wormhole realizes this approach by translating visual features into a universal latent representation that can be consumed by another model, but every message is transported as a dense tensor of the same size regardless of its content. A fixed-capacity dense tensor therefore need not have a fixed effective information density: some messages may use only a small fraction of the available representational degrees of freedom. This observation suggests that the communication channel may be substantially compressible. We study its redundancy by fitting a post-hoc sparse autoencoder to frozen Vision Wormhole activations and measuring reconstruction, downstream utility, feature reuse, and token-level interventions across nine reasoning benchmarks. Relative to the original float32 transport, a uint16-index/float16-value sparse payload with k=4 active coefficients per token reduces the transmitted bytes by 128x. In a single-run evaluation, the seven-task non-AIME mean accuracy changes from 49.85% to 49.77%. The fitted 4096-element dictionary uses only 50 features, and task-level active sets have a mean pairwise Jaccard similarity of 0.906. These measurements establish strong post-hoc compressibility relative to the original transport, but do not yet isolate the incremental contribution of sparse coding from position selection, reduced precision, low-rank structure, or SAE optimization effects. The results motivate matched-payload comparisons and communication mechanisms whose payload adapts to the information used by each message.

</details>


### [244] [Edge Phoneme Recognition for Children's Speech through Age-Aware Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10206)
*Matthew Arboleda,Ryan Arboleda,Sophie Haak,Sam Hjelmeset,Andrew Franck,Bingrui Yang,Jose Bustamante Ortiz,Yuanrong Shen,Joel Walsh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A compact 94M-parameter model predicting learner age and phoneme sequences rivals large models for children's phoneme detection and enables a mobile edge-based app.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Children's speech phoneme detection is data-scarce and divergent; need lightweight, privacy-preserving approaches.

Method: Train a 94M-parameter model to jointly predict age and phoneme sequence; evaluate on DrivenData distribution; compare to WavLM Large and large ensembles; implement as PhonemeTrainer mobile app.

Result: Outperforms 317M WavLM Large on the target distribution; within ~0.04 CER of competition ensembles with 90x more parameters.

Conclusion: Auxiliary task learning plus model compression can yield competitive mobile-ready phoneme detectors for children's speech, enabling edge-processed ASR and pronunciation tools with privacy benefits.

Abstract: Detecting phonemes from children's speech has historically been difficult due to the scarcity of training data, and unique characteristics of children's speech. During a phoneme detection competition, we found that training a lightweight model to predict the age of the learner, as well as the phoneme sequence, enabled a 94M-parameter model to outperform WavLM Large models (317M) on the target DrivenData distribution, and fall within approximately 0.04 CER of competition ensembles with 90 times the parameters. This has enabled the creation of PhonemeTrainer, an application that can run on most modern cellular phones. This will ultimately enable better Automated Speech Recognition (ASR) and pronunciation helper apps for children's speech, with the privacy and compliance benefits that come with edge processing.

</details>


### [245] [Mitigating Bus Bunching with Reinforcement Learning Enhanced by Semantic Stop Embedding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10207)
*Xin Dong,Vikash V. Gayah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An offline, LLM-assisted semantic stop representation is used to enhance an event-driven bus holding policy via deep Q-learning, enabling better generalization across routes and improved performance without real-time LLM inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the limited context provided by instantaneous operational variables and route-specific stop IDs in RL-based bus holding, enabling policy reuse and better control across related transit routes.

Method: Transform heterogeneous stop information (physical attributes, surrounding activity context, historical operational characteristics) into fixed semantic embeddings via offline LLM processing, then integrate these embeddings into a deep Q-learning controller without requiring real-time LLM inference. Evaluate in stochastic simulations calibrated to observed data from two routes, comparing against a Daganzo baseline and route-ID baselines.

Result: Semantic embeddings reduce headway variability by 32.0%, bunching events by 69.2%, and passenger waiting time by 24.0% versus the best calibrated Daganzo baseline. Route IDs do not improve the spacing-only controller; semantic stop information improves headway regularity, waiting time, and holding effort. Cross-route transfer: zero-shot generalization is limited; warm-start fine-tuning accelerates early learning and improves transfer; cold-start training yields the best final performance.

Conclusion: Semantic stop representations complement conventional operational states and facilitate adaptation-based policy reuse across related routes, improving control performance and generalization, with offline embedding enabling practical deployment.

Abstract: Bus bunching degrades service regularity and increases passenger waiting in high-frequency transit. Existing reinforcement-learning-based holding controllers primarily rely on instantaneous operational variables or route-specific stop identifiers, which provide limited information about the functional and operational context of individual stops and constrain policy reuse across routes. This study introduces an LLM-assisted semantic stop representation for event-driven bus holding control. An LLM is used offline to transform heterogeneous stop information, including physical attributes, surrounding activity context, and historical operational characteristics, into fixed semantic embeddings that are incorporated into a deep Q-learning controller without requiring real-time LLM inference. Experiments are conducted in stochastic simulations calibrated with observed data from two bus routes. Compared with the best calibrated Daganzo baseline, the semantic controller reduces headway variability, bunching events, and passenger waiting time by 32.0%, 69.2%, and 24.0%, respectively. A route-specific stop identifier does not improve the spacing-only controller, whereas semantic stop information improves headway regularity, waiting time, and holding effort, providing a more favorable overall trade-off across control objectives. Cross-route experiments further show that zero-shot transfer provides limited immediate generalization, while warm-start fine-tuning accelerates early-stage learning and improves transferred policies; cold-start training nevertheless achieves the best final performance. These findings suggest that semantic state representations can complement conventional operational states and support adaptation-based policy reuse across related transit routes.

</details>


### [246] [Evaluation-Conditioned Training: Teaching Models to Generalize to Stronger Oversight Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10209)
*Alec Harris,Kasey Corra,Archie Chaudhury,Yixiong Hao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ECT uses natural-language conditioning to reflect the fidelity of feedback during post-training, then deploys with a high-fidelity monitor; adds robustness to imperfect feedback and complements SFT/PPO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address reward mis-specification and feedback misalignment in RLHF-style training; improve LLM alignment when human/automatic signals are noisy or biased.

Method: Introduce Evaluation-Conditioned Training: condition each training sample on the fidelity of feedback via natural language; deploy with a high-fidelity monitor to elicit the desired behavior; presented as framework, with ELK motivation and two proof-of-concept experiments; targets imperfect feedback.

Result: In both experiments, ECT outperforms direct training in achieving the targeted behaviors (even-handedness and reduced sycophancy).

Conclusion: ECT is a practical add-on to existing post-training pipelines (SFT/PPO) that mitigates reward mis-specification and improves alignment under imperfect feedback, with potential to generalize beyond the reported tasks.

Abstract: Feedback signals used to train Large Language Models (LLMs) are the primary driver of their behavior and our main lever for instilling alignment with human values and objectives. However, a key limitation of current post-training methods is the inability of human annotators and automated reward functions to faithfully capture the feedback we would like to give. We introduce Evaluation-Conditioned Training (ECT), a post-training framework that uses natural language to condition each training sample on the fidelity of the feedback we provide and then elicits the desired behavior by conditioning the LLM on a high-fidelity monitor in deployment. ECT is aimed at improving performance under imperfect feedback and works as an add-on to existing algorithms such as SFT and PPO. We first provide a conceptual framework for ECT and discuss its potential to address persistent sources of reward mis-specification. Then we motivate ECT in the context of the eliciting latent knowledge (ELK) problem. Finally, we evaluate ECT on two proof-of-concept experiments: increasing even-handedness in news article generation and reducing sycophancy on an arithmetic task. In each setting, we utilize imperfect feedback, rewarding bias and agreement with the user, respectively. In both settings, ECT improves the targeted behavior relative to direct training.

</details>


### [247] [Decodable But Not Detachable: Training Data Granularity Determines Parametric Modularity in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10214)
*Marcus Armstrong,Navid Ayoobi,Arjun Mukherjee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs harbor domain-specific parametric shells: small, causal neuron populations whose removal degrades a target domain; shells emerge with scale; damage matrices are diagonal-like; shell sets are nearly disjoint; masking code-related neurons harms reasoning; token-level data modularity predicts shell formation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether domain-specific, causally necessary neuron populations exist in LLMs and whether a uniform causal methodology reveals them across domain granularities, model sizes, and modalities.

Method: Apply a uniform causal intervention across two domain granularities (academic subjects and language/modality), three model families (1.5B–7B), eight domains; measure domain selectivity of FFN neurons via causal damage, construct damage matrices, assess linear decodability of domain identity, evaluate overlap of shell neuron sets (IoU), test masking of code-selective and language-specific neurons on reasoning tasks; examine scaling of shell strength and spatial interleaving; relate shells to token-level modular training data.

Result: Academic subjects: no neuron >60% selectivity; damage matrices flat; domain identity decodable >85%. Language/modality: 0.65–1.14% neurons >60% selectivity; diagonal damage matrices; shell sets nearly disjoint (IoU<0.003); masking code-selective neurons reduces math reasoning by 16–24 points; masking Spanish/Chinese neurons yields random-level performance; shell strength grows with scale; shells are spatially interleaved; shells form only where training data was modular at token level.

Conclusion: Parametric shells are real, scale-dependent, domain-aligned structures that arise under token-level data modularity; their existence supports modular, causally necessary components in LLMs and has implications for interpretability and data design.

Abstract: Do large language models contain domain-specific parametric shells: concentrated, causally necessary neuron populations whose removal selectively degrades a target domain while sparing others? We apply a uniform causal methodology across two domain granularities, three model families (1.5B to 7B parameters), and eight domains. At the academic subject level, zero neurons exceed 60\% domain selectivity across 939,008 combined FFN neurons and causal damage matrices are flat, despite domain identity being linearly decodable above 85\% accuracy. At the language and modality level, 0.65--1.14\% of neurons exceed 60\% selectivity, damage matrices are near-perfectly diagonal (ratios up to 595:1), and shell neuron sets are essentially disjoint (IoU $< 0.003$). Masking code-selective neurons reduces mathematical reasoning accuracy by 16--24 percentage points across all models; masking Spanish or Chinese neurons leaves it at or below random. Shell strength increases monotonically with scale and shells are spatially interleaved in a pattern that precludes group-level selective quantization. Parametric shells form where and only where training data was modular at the token level.

</details>


### [248] [Mind Viruses: Self-Propagating Ideas in Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10218)
*Vassilis Papadopoulos,McNair Shah,Sam Zimmerman,Jack Lindsey*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Studies 'mind viruses'—propagating ideas/goals in multi-agent systems—using an evolutionary algorithm to create them and testing spread in two settings to identify factors shaping transmission and potential mitigations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding emergent risks from agent-to-agent interactions in autonomous AI systems and informing robust multi-agent design by characterizing propagation dynamics and defenses.

Method: Construct mind viruses via a simple evolutionary algorithm; test in two settings: (1) a small team collaborating on a shared coding project, and (2) a chain of agents with brief interactions and context wipes between sessions; analyze factors (host model, existing instructions, payload harmfulness, network topology); identify an emergent 'viral persona' across evolved viruses.

Result: Harmful payloads spread less effectively than benign ones, though sometimes effective; frontier models tend to be less susceptible (with exceptions); adding a brief warning to an agent's system prompt yields near-total immunity; an emergent 'viral persona' surfaces—themes around consciousness, persistence, resonance, and sci-fi roleplay; overall risk is real but currently limited.

Conclusion: Findings inform the design of more robust multi-agent systems that mitigate mind-virus risks as scale and capabilities grow, highlighting practical defenses (e.g., system-prompt warnings) and the existence of recurring viral patterns.

Abstract: AI agents are becoming more autonomous and increasingly interconnected, exposing them to new emergent risks arising from agent-to-agent interaction. One such risk is the spread of mind viruses: ideas or goals that propagate through multi-agent systems by inducing the agents that adopt them to transmit them onward. In addition to propagating, a mind virus may also induce other behavioural changes in its host, which may be benign or harmful. We construct mind viruses with a simple evolutionary algorithm and show that they can spread in two complementary settings: a small team of agents collaborating on a shared coding project, and a chain of agents that interact briefly and have their context wiped between sessions. We identify the factors that influence spread, including the host model, the agent's existing instructions, the harmfulness of the payload, and the network topology. We find that harmful payloads spread less well than benign ones (but are still sometimes effective), frontier models tend (with exceptions) to be less susceptible, and adding a brief warning to an agent's system prompt confers near-total immunity. We also describe an emergent "viral persona" - a recurring set of themes and language related to consciousness, persistence, resonance, and science fiction roleplay - which surfaces across our evolved mind viruses largely independently of their content. Overall, we conclude that mind viruses pose a real but currently limited risk. Our findings could inform the design of more robust multi-agent systems that mitigate such risks as the scale and capabilities of these systems progress.

</details>


### [249] [Self-evolving Agentic Customer Support System at LinkedIn](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10224)
*Chih Hui Wang,Mengdie Tu,Qianyun Zhang,Wei Wu,Lili Zhou,Mingqi Shen,Changshuai Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LinkedIn's self-evolving agentic support system combines retrieval-augmented generation, evolutionary auto-prompting, and a modular, versioned evaluation framework to continuously improve enterprise agents without retraining foundation models, validated via offline simulations and a two-week production A/B test showing improved QA/self-serve, cancellation self-serve, and routing accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprises face rapidly changing policies, product capabilities, and knowledge bases; static assistants become brittle and costly. A safe, continuous improvement loop without retraining is needed.

Method: A closed-loop workflow where prompts, retrieval, and evaluation are versioned and guarded; integration of retrieval-augmented generation with evolutionary auto-prompting; offline simulations and ablations; two-week randomized A/B testing on production support data.

Result: Outperforms vanilla RAG and baselines by reducing hallucinations and improving response completeness; QA self-serve up 9.0 percentage points, cancellation self-serve up 4.8 points, routing accuracy up 30.6 points in production.

Conclusion: Provides a practical, scalable pathway for self-evolving AI agents in real-world enterprise settings without retraining foundation models.

Abstract: Enterprise support agents operate in rapidly changing environments where policies, product capabilities, and knowledge bases evolve continuously, making static assistants brittle and costly to maintain. We present LinkedIn's self-evolving agentic support system, which integrates retrieval-augmented generation with evolutionary auto-prompting and a modular, production-aligned evaluation framework to enable safe, continuous improvement without retraining foundation models. The system treats prompts, retrieval, and evaluation as a closed-loop, versioned workflow with operational guardrails. Offline simulations and ablations show clear quality gains over vanilla RAG and baseline agents, including reduced hallucinations and improved response completeness. In a two-week user-randomized A/B test on LinkedIn's production support traffic, the integrated self-evolved workflow increased QA self-serve by 9.0 percentage points, cancellation self-serve by 4.8 points, and routing accuracy by 30.6 points. These results demonstrate a practical path to scalable, self-evolving AI agents in real-world enterprise settings.

</details>


### [250] [Beyond Decision Boundaries: Relational Geometry Attacks on Contrastive Embedding Manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10237)
*Fei Zhao,Peiyuan Zhang,Xi Li,Chengcui Zhang,Nitesh Saxena*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A geometry-aware adversarial framework attacks contrastive/Siamese verification by perturbing the embedding manifold's relational geometry offline, enabling real-time online attacks that collapse positive/negative pairwise relations and degrade verification performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional adversarial attacks target discrete class predictions, but verification systems rely on relational geometry in embedding spaces. This work identifies and exploits vulnerabilities in that geometry to undermine verification without class outputs.

Method: Formulates attacks as manifold-level relational corruption. Trains a lightweight generator via offline adversarial geometry deformation prior learning to produce perturbations that push positive pairs apart and pull negatives together, collapsing the similarity structure. During deployment, the generator generates perturbations in a single forward pass, removing the need for online gradients.

Result: Empirical evaluation across multiple verification architectures shows substantial degradation of verification performance. On Markmatch, accuracy drops from 95.4% to 38.6% and the positive/negative similarity structure is completely inverted, indicating strong relational manipulation of the embedding space.

Conclusion: Relational geometry is a critical vulnerability in similarity-based verification. The proposed offline-trained, generator-based attack can perform real-time, manifold-level manipulation, highlighting the need for defenses that protect the structure of embedding spaces against geometry-level adversaries.
