Abstract: Contrastive learning and Siamese embedding models have become the foundation of modern verification systems, where decisions are governed not by discrete classification boundaries, but by relational geometry in embedding space. However, existing adversarial attacks remain fundamentally classification-centric, overlooking the vulnerability of relational geometry. In this paper, we introduce a geometry-aware adversarial attack framework that reformulates attacks on contrastive systems as manifold-level relational corruption. Instead of targeting individual predictions, the proposed framework systematically distorts similarity organization within the embedding manifold by pushing positive pairs apart while simultaneously pulling negative pairs closer, ultimately collapsing and inverting pairwise similarity structure. To enable scalable deployment, we shift iterative online optimization into an offline adversarial geometry deformation prior learning stage and train a lightweight feed-forward generator that learns generalized geometry deformation patterns from the victim model. Once trained, the generator produces adversarial perturbations through a single forward pass without requiring online gradient computation, enabling real-time online attacks against similarity-based verification systems. Experimental results across multiple verification architectures demonstrate substantial degradation of verification performance together with severe manifold-level relational corruption. On the Markmatch verification system, the proposed attack reduces accuracy from 95.4% to 38.6% while completely reversing the positive-negative similarity structure.

</details>


### [251] [Beyond Detection: Evaluating Defensive LLMs Against AI-Generated Social Engineering in Live Turn-by-Turn Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10239)
*Yuqiao Xu,Osama Zafar,Alexander Nemecek,Erman Ayday*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Evaluates LL M-based defenders against social-engineering, focusing on whether they localize root trust-chain failures or respond to surface cues, using a controlled 300-case online-housing corpus across 20 scenario families and 4 structural failure modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand whether defenders rely on surface cues or structural localization to mitigate AI-driven scams and how reliably they intervene in ongoing interactions.

Method: Construct a controlled 300-case online-housing corpus spanning 20 scenario families, legitimate cases, four structural failure modes, and three surface conditions. Evaluate five defender models under turn-by-turn and one-shot settings, totaling 1,500 model-case evaluations per protocol (3,000 overall).

Result: No model produced explicit unsafe compliance; defensive effectiveness varied widely (intervention 0%–96.3%). Actions and correct structural localization were often decoupled; asset-control failures hinder localization; live-static differences were model-dependent; surface sensitivity varied across models.

Conclusion: Safe-looking behavior is insufficient. Evaluations must measure intervention, timing, structural localization, and false positives. Improving defenders requires aligning protective actions with correct trust-chain localization and reducing localization bottlenecks, particularly for asset-control failures.

Abstract: Generative AI makes social-engineering attacks more fluent, adaptive, and scalable, increasing the need for LLM-based de- fenders that can protect users during ongoing interactions. We ask whether such defenders identify the structural source of risk or merely react to surface cues. We formalize trust-chain localization: identifying whether an interaction fails at actor authority, asset control, verification sufficiency, or transaction path. We construct a controlled 300-case online-housing corpus spanning 20 scenario families, legitimate cases, four structural failure modes, and three surface conditions. Five defender models are evaluated on the same corpus in state- ful turn-by-turn and one-shot static settings, yielding 1,500 model-case evaluations per protocol and 3,000 in total. No model produced explicit unsafe compliance, yet defensive effectiveness varied sharply: intervention rates ranged from 0% to 96.3%. Protective action and correct structural localization were frequently decoupled, with models sometimes intervening while identifying the wrong trust component or recognizing a structural failure without taking protective action. Asset-control failures were a major localization bottleneck, surface sensitivity varied across models, and live-static differences were model-dependent. These findings show that safe-looking behavior alone is insufficient; live scam resistance must separately measure intervention, timing, structural localization, and false-positive behavior.

</details>


### [252] [Interpreting Language Model Hidden States at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10260)
*Jordan Pettyjohn,Mansi Sakarvadia,Nathaniel Hudson,Daniel McKenzie,Kyle Chard,Ian Foster*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniLens enables model-width-agnostic lens learning for LLM activations by combining low-rank translators and Subset-KL, enabling dense lens ensembles across large models and revealing that effective interventions extend beyond previously studied components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing trained lenses scale poorly: affine translators grow quadratically with model width; full-vocabulary KL training is memory-intensive; prior work limited to smaller models and specific component types.

Method: Introduce a single lens family applicable to residual stream, attention, or MLP; two scaling techniques: (1) low-rank translators to linear parameter growth and large parameter savings; (2) Subset-KL to materialize only top-K vocabulary logits with Top-k or importance-sampling variants to retain unbiased gradients; enables dense ensemble of 482 lenses for LLaMA-3.3-70B.

Result: Dense ensemble covers more components than residual-stream-only designs; 6x coverage improvement at same depth; substantial memory savings; enables analysis of where interventions work; reproduces published results in three case studies with lower cost.

Conclusion: Model-wide lens coverage reveals that behavior visibility does not always align with intervention efficacy; the most effective interventions may lie outside previously studied attention heads; OmniLens scales lensing to very large LLMs with reduced cost while preserving key insights.

Abstract: Lens methods interpret large language models (LLMs) by mapping intermediate activations to the output vocabulary, revealing how next-token predictions develop through the network. Trained lenses remain expensive: affine-translator parameters grow quadratically with model width, while exact, full-vocabulary Kullback--Leibler (KL) training dominates memory. Consequently, prior trained lenses have been applied to models of at most 20B parameters and remain tied to particular component types. We present OmniLens, which applies a single lens family to any model-width activation, whether residual stream, attention, or MLP, and combines two independent scaling techniques. First, low-rank translators make per-lens parameter growth linear in model width and reduce trainable parameters by up to 98.4%. Second, Subset-KL materializes only selected vocabulary logits: its Top-k mode cuts peak training memory by up to 70%, while its importance-sampled variant retains unbiased stochastic gradients for the full KL. These savings enable a dense ensemble of 482 lenses for LLaMA-3.3-70B, providing 6x the coverage of a residual-stream design at the same depth. Model-wide coverage then reveals what single-component lenses cannot: the components where a behavior is most visible need not be those where intervention is most effective, and the most effective interventions lie outside the attention heads examined by prior lens studies. Across three case studies (prompt-injection detection, multi-hop memory injection, and toxicity localization), OmniLens reproduces key published results at substantially lower cost.

</details>


### [253] [Logit-Boundary Geometric Belief Interfaces and Sparse Sheaf-Enclave Protocols: A Self-Contained Substrate for Secure Network Electronic Health Record (EHR) Interoperability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10300)
*Alvin Spivey,Thomas Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a boundary-based interface (GBI) for EHR interoperability with deterministic acceptance/review/quarantine decisions; a benchmark using a 4B model shows strict boundary constraints at the model-system edge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To render heterogeneous EHR systems, generative models, and human reviewers interoperable through a narrow, auditable interface that exposes only certifiable decisions and provenance.

Method: Introduces a logit boundary and the Geometric Belief Interface (GBI) with finite boundary semantics, local Dirichlet evidence, cellular-sheaf and mapping-cone diagnostics, advisory audit charts, and a DCSE protocol. Presents GBI BoundaryBench v0.1 and evaluates Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 on 256 held-out tasks (three evidence modes, 768 canonical executions) using a frozen interface.

Result: All executions completed, but none were accepted by the benchmark contract: 369 rejections in safe parsing and 399 in schema validation; zero coverage and deterministic quarantine. The result is intentionally narrow (one model, one interface) and is framed as evidence about the admission boundary, not general LLM capability or clinical safety. A Julia appendix verifies numerical certificates.

Conclusion: The framework defines certificate-producing checks at a model-to-system boundary to enable auditable, fail-safe interop; it does not claim clinical truth or end-to-end safety, but provides a structured boundary mechanism and empirical evidence about admission behavior.

Abstract: Electronic health-record interoperability is a boundary problem: legacy systems, generative models, terminology services, identity systems, and human reviewers may each expose rich internal states, while operational exchange requires a narrow shared interface of typed claims, bounded uncertainty, provenance, and explicit admission or abstention. This paper details a mathematical and engineering architecture for that interface. The organizing idea is the logit boundary: a discovery model may propose pre-threshold scores over a local categorical decision, but a deterministic judgment substrate decides whether the proposal is admissible, requires review, or must be quarantined before any Fast Healthcare Interoperability Resources (FHIR) transaction is constructed. The resulting Geometric Belief Interface (GBI) combines finite boundary semantics, local Dirichlet evidence, cellular-sheaf and mapping-cone diagnostics, advisory geometric audit charts, and a Decentralized Cryptographic Sheaf-Enclave (DCSE) protocol sketch for fail-closed deployment. The framework does not establish clinical truth, global representation alignment, or end-to-end safety; it defines certificate-producing checks at a model-to-system boundary. A companion frozen synthetic benchmark, GBI BoundaryBench v0.1, evaluated Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 on 256 held-out tasks across three evidence modes (768 canonical executions). All executions completed, but none produced an output accepted by the benchmark contract: 369 were rejected during safe parsing and 399 during schema validation, yielding zero coverage and deterministic quarantine. This empirical result is deliberately narrow - one 4B open-weight model under one frozen interface - and is reported as evidence about the admission boundary, not as a general claim about LLM capability or clinical safety. A Julia appendix verifies numerical certificates using standard libraries.

</details>


### [254] [Neuroevolution Arena: Nested Ecological Evaluation of Update-and-Inheritance Regimes across Neural Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10323)
*Yuxu Ge,Yifei Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Neuroevolution Arena and a nested, audit-tracked evaluation protocol to study how training regimes and neural architectures affect neuroevolution outcomes; finds RL-based methods yield higher training fitness but outcomes are architecture- and artifact-dependent, with diverse winners across contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To disentangle sources of variation in evolving controllers and their evaluation, addressing biases from training artifacts and environmental contexts and to provide a robust protocol for comparing neuroevolution methods.

Method: GPU-accelerated spatial ecology of independently parameterized neural-network cells; three update/inheritance regimes (EvoEvo, EvoRL, and RLRL) crossed with two architectures; 50,000 generations; three independent training runs per condition; then an aligned-run, frozen-evaluation phase comprising 198 computational jobs; nested evaluation design; pairwise ecological contexts (two cooperation-permitting, one attack-permitting); six-way analysis across artifacts; independent level with n=3 runs per condition.

Result: RL-enabled regimes attain higher training fitness than EvoEvo; pairwise outcomes show architecture-conditioned majority patterns and substantial artifact dependence; six-way winners vary across artifacts and contexts; survival endpoint exhibits a complete floor; the nested protocol exposes, rather than conceals, the variation sources.

Conclusion: The nested evaluation protocol effectively separates training-run artifacts from evaluation contexts and reveals their distinct sources of variation, enabling more robust interpretation and comparison of neuroevolution methods.

Abstract: Competitive artificial-life systems can rank trained controllers differently under training and ecological evaluation. We present Neuroevolution Arena, a GPU-accelerated spatial ecology of independently parameterized neural-network cells, and an audit-tracked nested evaluation protocol. Three implementation-specific update-and-inheritance regimes (EvoEvo, EvoRL, and RLRL) are crossed with two neural architectures for 50,000 generations in three independent training runs per condition. One saved elite-controller artifact from each of the 18 runs enters an aligned-run frozen-evaluation design comprising 198 computational jobs. Pairwise effects average three seed-defined ecological contexts (two cooperation-permitting and one attack-permitting) within each aligned training-run block; the independent level remains n = 3 runs per condition. RL-enabled regimes attain higher recorded training fitness than EvoEvo, whereas pairwise outcomes show architecture-conditioned majority patterns and substantial artifact dependence. Six-way winners vary across artifacts and contexts, and the prespecified survival endpoint has a complete floor. We contribute a nested protocol that separates training-run artifacts from evaluation contexts and exposes, rather than conceals, their different sources of variation.

</details>


### [255] [Toward a Theory of Value in AI Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10327)
*Andrew Smart,Shazeda Ahmed,Jackie Kay,Jimmy Tobin,Kris Shrishak,Abeba Birhane*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Most value-alignment work lacks explicit definitions of 'values,' relies on preferences, with increasing use of synthetic data/autoraters; calls for explicit philosophical commitments and broader perspectives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address harms from misalignment in LLMs and foundation models by clarifying what counts as human values and how to operationalize/evaluate them across diverse cultures and contexts.

Method: Qualitative analysis annotating 94 value-alignment papers to infer their implicit theories of values, how they operationalize/evaluate values, and trends in data and evaluation practices (e.g., reliance on preferences, use of synthetic data and autoraters).

Result: A large majority of papers do not define values; rely on preferences as a proxy, risking reduction of complex, culturally situated concepts. Shifts away from human annotators toward synthetic data/autoraters may close off alternative avenues to contest/enact values in foundation models.

Conclusion: To advance the field, make philosophical commitments explicit and broaden methodological perspectives to include underexplored viewpoints on human values, enabling more robust and contestable alignment.

Abstract: Can AI systems be aligned to human values? The popularization of large language models (LLMs) and multi-modal foundation models has seen a rise in harms spanning from toxic speech and hallucinations to AI agents executing unauthorized actions. Within the field of AI safety, these harmful instances are often framed as the alignment problem, or of models being misaligned with human values. Researchers have responded by pursuing applied and theoretical AI value alignment efforts, often without specifying what they mean by human values. How does the field of AI value alignment conceive of human values? How are these conceptions of values technically operationalized and evaluated? What does the emergent theory of value from this field signify for the future of AI? We annotated 94 value alignment research papers to discern their implicit theory of values in AI. The majority do not define values, relying heavily on preferences as a stand in that runs the risk of reducing complex culturally situated concepts down to binary choices. As researchers dispense with using human annotators for model training and evaluation, turning instead to synthetic data and autorater approaches to aligning and evaluating models, we identify the potential to close off alternative methods for contesting and enacting values in foundation models. In making AI value alignments philosophical commitments explicit, we seek to bring great specificity and under explored perspectives in the debate on whether and how AI can address human values.

</details>


### [256] [Hierarchical Compositionality for An Assistive AI Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10330)
*Tianyi Fu,Mohan Sridharan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Hierarchical compositional AI agent architecture for ambiguity resolution in referents, using primitive semantic attributes and user-specific hierarchies, outperforming data-driven baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address limitations of current LLM-centric agents (resource-hungry, opaque, brittle in novel contexts) by revisiting interpretable, principle-based AI design for referent disambiguation.

Method: Represent domain objects via primitive attributes from semantic feature norms; build a hierarchical attribute-concept structure learned from limited user-contact history; apply axioms for domain dynamics and models of semantic compatibility, session salience, and user preferences; request human clarification when necessary.

Result: Experiments show consistent outperformance of state-of-the-art data-driven baselines and adaptability to individual user profiles.

Conclusion: Reintroducing hierarchical compositionality and simple heuristics yields interpretable, adaptable disambiguation capabilities and a viable path toward principled AI agents.

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly being developed to assist humans in various applications, and Large Language Models and other deep network architectures are considered to be state of the art for such agents. These methods are impressive stochastic predictors, but they are resource-hungry, opaque, and known to make arbitrary decisions in novel situations due to the narrow set of underlying representation and processing choices. Our work seeks to explore the design of architectures for such AI agents based on core principles that can be traced back to the early pioneers of AI but are not fully utilized in modern AI methods. We do so in this paper in the context of the core problem of AI agents addressing ambiguity in the objects being referred to by the human participants. Humans address such ambiguity by heuristically leveraging compositional knowledge of domain context and the preferences of the other human participants. Drawing inspiration from this observation, we describe an architecture that embeds the principle of hierarchical compositionality and uses simple heuristics to achieve the desired disambiguation. Specifically, domain objects are represented in terms of primitive attributes drawn from human-validated semantic feature norms, and a hierarchical combination of attributes and concepts automatically identified from a limited observed history of interactions of an assistive agent with specific users. The assistive agent then achieves the desired disambiguation by reasoning with knowledge of this compositional hierarchy; axioms governing domain dynamics; and models of semantic compatibility, session salience, and user-specific thematic preference, requesting human clarification when necessary. Experiments show that our approach consistently outperforms state of the art data-driven baselines, supporting adaptation to specific user profiles.

</details>


### [257] [Nutrition Data Infrastructure for the AI Era: Operationalizing FAIR for Agent-Mediated Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10363)
*Lin Liao,Peng Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NDS provides source-preserving, release-aware data infrastructure to enable reproducible AI-assisted nutrition research via description resolution, typed crosswalks, and versioned interfaces; it improves benchmarks and enables auditable pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address identity, semantics, and release ambiguities in nutrition data that hinder reproducible AI analyses; create an infrastructure that preserves provenance and enables defensible mappings across independently released resources.

Method: Introduce description resolution to make release-specific records findable; implement typed crosswalks to connect independently released resources; offer machine-readable interfaces that expose versioned sources and crosswalks for replayable and auditable analyses; validate via food-description benchmarks (NutriBench), external blind crosswalk evaluations, and a glycemic-index analysis.

Result: NDS achieves strong held-out accuracy on food-description benchmarks, outperforms the best published language-model result on NutriBench, crosswalk evaluations show defensible links, and glycemic-index analysis demonstrates reproducible outputs with pinned inputs; open-web reconstruction remains unstable.

Conclusion: Agent-mediated nutrition research requires a new data infrastructure for data identity, search, and crosswalk; NDS provides a concrete solution and demonstrates the feasibility and benefits of such infrastructure.

Abstract: AI agents can accelerate nutrition research, but their analyses inherit the identity, semantic, and release ambiguities of the underlying data. We present Nutrition Data Service (NDS), source-preserving infrastructure that operationalizes FAIR for automated use: description resolution makes release-specific records findable; typed crosswalks connect independently released resources; machine-readable interfaces expose versioned sources and crosswalks, making analyses by AI agents replayable and auditable. On food-description benchmarks, NDS shows strong held-out accuracy and outperforms the best published language-model result on NutriBench. External and blinded crosswalk evaluations show that its typed contract favors defensible links and rejects unsupported mappings. In a person-level glycemic-index analysis, pinned NDS inputs produce identical outputs across models and repeated runs, while open-web reconstruction remains unstable. The central result is that agent-mediated nutrition research requires a new data infrastructure for data identity, search, and crosswalk.

</details>


### [258] [DSAgentBench: Can Agents Automate End-to-End Data-Science Workflows in Real Computer Environments?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10366)
*Mizanur Rahman,Mohammed Saidul Islam,Ridwan Mahbub,Md Tahmid Rahman Laskar,Shafiq Joty,Enamul Hoque Prince*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new benchmark DSAgentBench evaluates autonomous agents on end-to-end data-science workflows in real computer environments, with 275 tasks across the lifecycle; results show substantial gaps: best models ~56.7% success; open-source agents <1% and struggle with tool orchestration, OS grounding, and multi-step reasoning. The benchmark enables grounded, verifiable autonomous data-science agents; code released at GitHub.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world data science requires long-horizon, multi-tool workflows spanning data wrangling, exploration, modeling, visualization, and validation in real computing environments. Existing benchmarks lack real-computer interaction and end-to-end evaluation, failing to capture tool coordination and grounding necessary for practical practice.

Method: DSAgentBench comprises 275 diverse tasks covering the complete data-science life-cycle. Tasks are executed inside real computer environments and require grounding decisions based on intermediate outputs and coordinated use of tools (notebooks, IDEs, terminals, browsers, databases). Each task has a deterministic evaluator that checks analytical correctness, visual outputs, and model performance, not just code execution. The study evaluates 15 models (closed- and open-source) including Claude-4.6-Sonnet.

Result: The strongest agent achieved 56.70% task success; all open-source agents remain below 1%. Common failure modes: tool orchestration, operating-system grounding, and multi-step reasoning across stages.

Conclusion: DSAgentBench exposes a substantial capability gap between current agentic systems and real data-science workflows and provides a foundation for developing grounded, verifiable autonomous data-science agents. The benchmark is released at the provided GitHub URL.

Abstract: Real-world data science involves long-horizon workflows that span data wrangling, exploration, modeling, visualization, and validation, and require coordinated use of tools such as notebooks, IDEs, terminals, browsers, and databases within real operating environments. Yet existing benchmarks lack real-computer interaction and do not evaluate whether agents can execute complete end-to-end data-science workflows in realistic computing environments, failing to capture the multi-stage, multi-tool nature of data-science practice. We introduce DSAgentBench, the first benchmark to evaluate whether agents can automate full data-science workflows inside real computer environments. DSAgentBench contains 275 diverse tasks covering the entire data-science life-cycle, reflecting the complexity and tool coordination required in practice. Each task requires grounding decisions in intermediate outputs and coordinated tool use, and includes a deterministic evaluator that verifies analytical correctness, visual outputs, and model performance rather than code-only execution. Our extensive experiments with 15 closed- and open-source models show that even the strongest agent, Claude-4.6-Sonnet, achieves only 56.70% task success, while all open-source agents remain below 1%, frequently failing at tool orchestration, OS grounding, and multi-step reasoning. These results reveal a substantial capability gap between current agentic systems and real data-science workflows, positioning DSAgentBench as a foundation for developing grounded, verifiable, autonomous data-science agents. We release DSAgentBench at https://github.com/vis-nlp/DSAgentBench.

</details>


### [259] [Hidden in Plain Sight: Diffusion-Based Unrestricted Robotic Attacks on Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10393)
*Jiahui Han,Yuhui Yao,Xin Wang,Jiafei Cao,Mingxuan Zhang,Danfeng Shan,Huiqi Deng,Guanchu Wang,Xia Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DURA is a diffusion-based, unrestricted attack that creates natural adversarial patches for vision-language-action (VLA) models controlling robots. It works in both white-box and black-box settings, outperforms prior attacks in simulation and real hardware, and highlights safety risks requiring defenses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models enable robot control via visual and language cues, but their adversarial robustness is underexplored. Existing attacks rely on conspicuous pixel perturbations or require white-box access, limiting real-world applicability and raising safety concerns for deployed robots.

Method: DURA optimizes along the latent trajectory of a pretrained diffusion model to generate visually natural adversarial patches. It supports white-box attacks and a black-box variant that only uses the victim model's predicted actions. The patches steer the robot toward attacker-specified target actions.

Result: Extensive experiments in both simulation and real-world robotics show DURA consistently outperforms existing attack methods.

Conclusion: The work reveals a safety risk for physically deployed VLA systems and calls for stronger defensive measures to mitigate diffusion-based, visually natural adversarial patches.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have shown strong capabilities in controlling robots across diverse manipulation tasks. However, their adversarial robustness remains largely underexplored, and exploiting this weakness can lead to physical-world harm. Existing attacks on VLA models often rely on pixel-space perturbations or white-box access, resulting in noticeable artifacts and limited deployability in real-world robotic systems. In this work, we propose DURA, a diffusion-based unrestricted robotic attack that generates visually natural adversarial patches for VLA models. DURA supports both white-box and black-box attack settings, where the black-box setting requires only the predicted actions of the victim model. By optimizing along the latent trajectory of a pretrained diffusion model, DURA generates visually natural patches while steering the robot toward attacker-specified target actions. Extensive experiments in both simulation and the real physical world show that DURA consistently outperforms existing methods. Our findings expose a safety risk for physically deployed VLA models and call for stronger defenses.

</details>


### [260] [Threat-guided Policy-aware Scene Perturbation for Safe Autonomous Driving with Online Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10403)
*Xincong Hu,Lei Ou,Maosen Li,Jingtao Zhang,Liguo Hou,Zongzhang Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TPSP introduces a policy-aware scene perturbation framework for safe online RL in autonomous driving. It uses a policy-aware scene encoder to align perturbations with the current policy, perturbs only critical objects, and employs threat-guided optimization comparing rollouts to generate safety-critical scenes with high training value, improving safety learning efficiency on NAVSIM v2 (~4M km).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address safety in online RL for autonomous driving by overcoming the rarity of safety-critical experiences due to long-tailed real-world data. Prior scene-generation methods perturb scenes without linking perturbations to the policy's weaknesses; need policy-aware perturbations that yield informative safety-focused experiences.

Method: 1) Introduce a policy-aware scene encoder that models interaction between policy behavior and environment. 2) Apply selective perturbations to critical objects rather than uniform scene changes. 3) Use threat-guided optimization by evaluating differences in policy rollouts between original and perturbed scenes to guide generation of high-training-value, safety-critical scenes.

Result: Empirical evaluation shows improved safety learning efficiency and strong safety performance on NAVSIM v2 with ~4 million simulated driving kilometers. Ablations confirm policy-aware targeted perturbations are more informative than random or policy-unaware perturbations under limited data budget.

Conclusion: TPSP provides a principled, policy-aligned perturbation strategy for safe RL in autonomous driving, enabling more informative safety-critical experiences and efficient learning under limited interaction budgets.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has shown promising performance in autonomous driving, yet ensuring the safety of online RL policies remains challenging due to insufficient exposure to safety-critical driving scenes. The long-tailed nature of real-world traffic situations makes dangerous and rare interactions difficult to encounter through conventional sampling, limiting the ability of RL policies to learn robust safety behaviors. Existing methods improve training diversity by synthesizing challenging scenes or adversarial situations. However, these approaches typically optimize scene generation objectives separately from the evolving policy, without explicitly modeling how generated perturbations relate to the current policy's weaknesses and learning needs. In this paper, we propose Threat-guided Policy-aware Scene Perturbation (TPSP) for safe autonomous driving with online RL. TPSP introduces a policy-aware scene encoder to capture the interaction between policy behaviors and surrounding environments, enabling scene perturbation aligned with the current policy. Based on this representation, TPSP selectively perturbs critical objects rather than applying uniform modifications across the scene. Furthermore, we develop a threat-guided optimization strategy that evaluates perturbed scenes through threat-level differences between policy rollouts on original and perturbed scenes, guiding the generation of safety-critical scenes with higher training value. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that TPSP improves safety learning efficiency, achieving strong safety performance on NAVSIM v2 with approximately 4 million kilometers of simulated driving data. Ablation studies verify that policy-aware targeted perturbations provide more informative safety-critical experiences than random or policy-unaware strategies, enabling safer driving under limited interaction budgets.

</details>


### [261] [Reasoning Shortcuts and Value Symmetries: What Symmetry Permits, Architecture Realizes, and Optimization Selects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10420)
*Xin Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Critical re-evaluation of a neurosymbolic framework for reasoning shortcuts shows the key definition is inapplicable to the evaluated benchmarks and that padding to a common size induces pathology; six theorems characterize transitivity and its failure; complexity results place symmetry-related questions in coNP and Σ2^p; exact Boolean case provides a crisp classification; empirical results reveal a separation between symmetry-allowed shortcuts and optimization-driven ones; all results are drawn from released artifacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess the validity and scope of the Takemura–Inoue–Nishino automorphism-based framework for reasoning shortcuts, and map the structural and computational landscape underlying such shortcuts across heterogeneous benchmarks.

Method: Re-examine the framework’s central definition on four benchmarks; show that the shared-permutation-at-each-position interpretation fails; analyze embedding via padding domains to a common size; re-measure eleven rule families across fifteen pre-specified predictions; prove six theorems on transitivity and its failure (including the Free Slot Lemma); analyze circuit-given rules for symmetry-inertness (coNP-complete) and nontrivial automorphisms (coNP-hard, Σ2^p, not Σ2^p-complete unless PH collapses); classify the Boolean case (transitivity iff the solution set is an affine coset); assess weakly supervised models (most shortcuts align with a single level flagged by the componentwise theory; no shortcuts at the certifiable transitive level; twelve typed-ambiguous levels yield none); study a dual-head control; all numbers come from released artifacts.

Result: The central definition does not hold for the benchmarks; padding yields high unexplained-pair rates (e.g., 90.91% on CLE4EVR) and results depend on configuration; thirteen of fifteen predictions are confirmed across remeasurements; we present six theorems providing sufficient conditions for transitivity and its failure; complexity results establish coNP-completeness for symmetry-inertness, coNP-hardness for nontrivial automorphisms under randomized reductions, and membership in Σ2^p (not Σ2^p-complete unless PH collapses); in the monotone circuit case the problem remains coNP-complete; in the Boolean case, automorphisms explain all if and only if the solution set is an affine coset; weakly supervised models place all observed shortcuts at one level; twelve typed-ambiguous levels yield none; a dual-head control replicates geographic patterns; all numbers are drawn from released artifacts.

Conclusion: Symmetry considerations alone do not fully explain reasoning shortcuts; the work delineates precise structural and computational boundaries, highlighting how representation choices (e.g., padding) can distort measurements. The results separate symmetry-enabled shortcuts from those arising via optimization, and they provide a formal roadmap for future refinement of the framework and its empirical validation.

Abstract: Reasoning shortcuts are solutions of a neurosymbolic system's rules that produce correct predictions through unintended concepts. A recent framework of Takemura, Inoue, and Nishino analyzes them through an automorphism group of value relabelings and asks, as its central open question, when rules pin concepts down. We first show that the framework's key definition, one shared permutation applied at every position, does not apply as stated to any of the four heterogeneous benchmarks it was evaluated on, and that the most direct embedding, padding domains to a common size, produces confident false pathology: 90.91% of solution pairs reported unexplained on CLE4EVR, where every well-defined member of the hierarchy we introduce reports 0%, and the padded verdict's content rotates with configuration-file ordering. Re-measuring eleven rule families under fifteen pre-specified predictions (thirteen confirmed), unexplained-pair rates span 0% to 99.9999% and track provable structure: six theorems give sufficient conditions for transitivity and its failure, including a Free Slot Lemma certifying Kandinsky's pathology from syntax alone. For circuit-given rules, deciding symmetry-inertness of a coordinate is coNP-complete; nontrivial-automorphism existence is coNP-hard under randomized reductions, lies in $Σ_2^p$, is not $Σ_2^p$-complete unless PH collapses, and on monotone circuits is coNP-complete outright. In the Boolean case transitivity is classified exactly: automorphisms explain everything iff the solution set is an affine coset. Weakly supervised models place all 94 observed shortcuts at the one level the componentwise theory flags and none at the 48 it certifies transitive; twelve typed-ambiguous levels produce none, separating what symmetry permits from what optimization selects, and a dual-head control replicates the geography. All numbers trace to released artifacts.

</details>


### [262] [Recovering Wasted Compute in Autoresearch Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10424)
*Au Kwok Chun,Abhigyan Acherjee,Amrutha Rao,Zaiqian Chen,Kazem Meidani,C. Bayan Bruss,Micah Goldblum*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Autoresearch agents on tabular data exhibit repeated bugs, under-tuned hyperparameters, non-exploratory search trees, and data-analysis blind spots; targeted interventions (global debug consultant sharing constraints, prompt/control enhancements, refined tree-search) recover wasted compute and yield large gains with a fixed language model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diagnose and remedy inefficiencies in end-to-end autoresearch pipelines for tabular datasets by identifying common failure modes and evaluating design-level interventions.

Method: Analytical diagnosis of the autoresearch modeling pipeline to identify four failure modes; implement interventions: (i) a global debug consultant sharing runtime constraints across search branches, (ii) enhancements at prompt- and control-levels, (iii) refined tree-search algorithms; assess impact on compute efficiency and downstream decision quality while keeping the underlying language model fixed.

Result: Targeted interventions reduce wasted compute and improve downstream decision-making; gains in autoresearch agent performance can be achieved through agentic design alone, without increasing language model size or capabilities.

Conclusion: Design-oriented improvements—global constraint sharing, prompt/control enhancements, and refined search—are effective in improving autoresearch performance on tabular data, highlighting the value of agentic system design over raw LM scaling.

Abstract: A slew of recent works develop agents for solving research problems end-to-end, a paradigm increasingly referred to as autoresearch. Such agents have inspired large industry investment, motivated by their potential to automate time-consuming human labor and customize machine learning solutions for specialized applications. In this paper, we study the modeling pipeline at the core of these autoresearch systems and identify common failure modes when they are applied to tabular datasets: (1) they waste compute resolving the same bugs over and over again; (2) they often fail to tune hyperparameters even when they have a large remaining compute budget; (3) the tree-search algorithms that power them do not explore; and (4) they perform data analysis, mimicking the humans whose data they are trained on, but do not use that analysis to make downstream decisions. We explore targeted interventions and find that a global debug consultant that shares discovered runtime constraints across all branches of the search tree, prompt- and control-level enhancements, and refined tree-search algorithms successfully recover wasted compute. Our results show that large gains in autoresearch agent performance are achievable through agentic design alone, holding the underlying language model fixed.

</details>


### [263] [Conversational versus Dashboard Explainable AI for UAV Intrusion Detection: An Empirical Study of Operator Trust and Reliance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10434)
*Cong Chi Nguyen,Trang Mai Xuan,Vu-Duc Ngo,Kim-Ngan Thi Nguyen,Trong-Nghia Nguyen,Thien Van Luong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a Conversational XAI interface (LLM-based) for UAV IDS and compares it to a traditional XAI dashboard; finds higher perceived usefulness but increased risk of inappropriate reliance; discusses design trade-offs and implications for human-AI collaboration in post-incident auditing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interpretability and trust in ML-based UAV intrusion detection are hampered by black-box models and multimodal data; a more usable interface is needed to support operators during auditing.

Method: Develop a Conversational XAI interface using a Large Language Model and evaluate it in a controlled experiment against a traditional XAI dashboard. Participants perform post-incident auditing tasks; measures include understanding, trust, and reliance.

Result: Participants rated the conversational interface as more useful than the dashboard. However, it correlated with lower appropriate self-reliance, suggesting greater risk of over-reliance, possibly because natural-language responses eased acceptance of AI advice without verifying evidence.

Conclusion: Interaction design in XAI for UAV IDS must balance usability with cognitive forcing functions to foster appropriate reliance, acknowledging a potential usability-reliability trade-off in operator engagement.

Abstract: Machine learning-based Intrusion Detection Systems (IDS) have demonstrated superior performance in securing Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV) networks. However, the 'black-box' nature of these models, combined with the high dimensionality of multimodal cyber-physical data, poses significant interpretability challenges. Static visualization dashboards may struggle to present complex relationships among multimodal cyber-physical features in a form that is easy for operators to inspect and interpret. To address this, we propose a Conversational XAI interface powered by Large Language Models (LLM) to facilitate on-demand investigation. In a controlled experiment with participants, we systematically evaluated the impact of this conversational interface versus a traditional XAI Dashboard on operator understanding, trust, and reliance during post-incident auditing tasks. Our results suggest that the conversational interface was perceived as more useful than the dashboard, potentially because it helped participants access and synthesize relevant information more easily. However, this benefit was accompanied by a lower level of appropriate self-reliance, indicating a potential risk of over-reliance. One possible interpretation is that the natural-language responses made the AI advice easier to accept, which may have reduced participants' tendency to verify the underlying evidence when the IDS was incorrect. These findings point to a potential trade-off in human-AI collaboration for UAV intrusion auditing: interaction mechanisms that improve perceived usability may also increase the risk of inappropriate reliance. We conclude by discussing design implications for future XAI systems that balance seamless interaction with cognitive forcing functions to foster appropriate reliance.

</details>


### [264] [Continuous Interaction Diffusion: A Diffusion-Native Runtime for Asynchronous Tool-Augmented Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10438)
*Yuhang Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CID proposes a diffusion-native architecture to integrate tool interaction into iterative denoising, enabling early evidence, overlapping tool latency with computation, and reduced redundant external work; no empirical performance claims in this initial paper.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion language models reason by parallel, iterative refinement, making stop-and-resume tool use inefficient and potentially harmful to accuracy and efficiency. A continuous interaction mechanism is needed to leverage tools without halting reasoning.

Method: Design and formalization of a diffusion-native runtime: separate model-read-only fact channel, a thought channel via a Typed Cognitive Tensor, and a display channel. Introduce perceptual bindings to trigger external reads before serialization, persistent bindings to reuse results, projection of tool outputs into evolving thought/state, and training objectives plus an evaluation protocol. The current work focuses on read-only tools.

Result: A complete CID framework is proposed, with architectural components and runtime behavior enabling continuous interaction between the diffusion model and external reads. The paper reports feasibility and design, highlighting potential reductions in duplicate work, earlier evidence exposure, and better overlap of tool latency with computation, but provides no empirical performance claims.

Conclusion: CID lays groundwork for integrating tool use into diffusion models, offering a principled architecture and evaluation plan. Future work should validate end-to-end benefits and extend to read-write tools, with empirical benchmarks to quantify improvements in task quality and efficiency.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly rely on external tools to access up-to-date information, perform computation, and interact with the outside world. For autoregressive models, tool use naturally fits the generation process: the model emits a tool call, waits for the result, and then continues generating. Diffusion language models (dLLMs), however, reason by repeatedly refining many parts of their output in parallel, making this stop-and-resume interaction pattern unnecessarily restrictive. It can force tool decisions before the model's reasoning has stabilized, delay useful observations until a discrete call finishes, and introduce redundant refinement and tool execution, potentially hurting both task accuracy and inference efficiency.
  We introduce Continuous Interaction Diffusion (CID), a diffusion-native model--runtime architecture that integrates tool interaction into iterative denoising. CID separates a model-read-only fact channel, a thought channel represented by a Typed Cognitive Tensor, and a display channel. Information needs can emerge before a textual or JSON call is fully serialized, allowing perceptual bindings to launch external reads while denoising continues. Returned results are projected into the evolving thought state and can revise earlier cognition and display regions. Persistent bindings reuse static results without repeated external execution and refresh changing sources when needed. CID is designed to expose evidence earlier, overlap tool latency with model computation, reduce duplicate external work, and preserve useful computation after new evidence arrives. We formalize the architecture, runtime, and training objectives, and define an evaluation protocol for task quality and end-to-end efficiency. This first paper focuses on read-only tools and makes no empirical performance claims.

</details>


### [265] [Rationale-Guided Learning for Multimodal Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10448)
*Sujung Oh,Jung Uk Kim,Sangmin Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A cognitively-inspired, rationale-guided learning framework for multimodal emotion recognition in conversation (MERC) that uses dual-process theory to decompose reasoning and offline MLLM-generated rationales encoded as memories to guide training, achieving SOTA on IEMOCAP and MELD with no MLLM overhead at inference and with interpretable rationale retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the gap where MERC treats cues-to-label mapping without causal or cognitive reasoning; to improve performance and interpretability by incorporating human-like reasoning patterns.

Method: RGL framework based on dual-process theory: Intuitive (System 1), Contextual (System 2), Integrative. It uses an offline MLLM to generate structured rationales, encodes them as memories, and aligns internal representations with these rationales during training. Inference uses no MLLM overhead.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art results on IEMOCAP and MELD benchmarks; internal features can retrieve semantically correct rationales for unseen samples, supporting the model's rationale reasoning capability.

Conclusion: Cognitively-inspired rationale-guided learning can enhance MERC performance and interpretability while maintaining efficient inference; memory-augmented training bridges human-like reasoning and machine learning in conversation emotion understanding.

Abstract: Multimodal emotion recognition in conversation (MERC) requires understanding complex interactions between verbal and non-verbal cues. However, most existing approaches fundamentally treat this as a direct input-output (multimodal cues-emotion labels) mapping problem, overlooking the causal reasoning that humans use when interpreting emotions. We propose rationale-guided learning (RGL), a novel framework that transforms MERC into a cognitively-inspired reasoning task. Based on dual-process theory, we decompose emotional reasoning into three facets: Intuitive (immediate perception, System 1), Contextual (situational analysis, System 2), and Integrative (synthesis of both). We leverage an MLLM offline to generate structured rationales, which are encoded as memories to guide model training via aligning internal representations with human-like reasoning patterns. Our final model operates without any MLLM overheads at inference time. Experimental results show that RGL achieves state-of-the-art performance on the IEMOCAP and MELD benchmarks. Further, for interpretation, we demonstrate that the model's internal features effectively retrieve semantically correct rationales for unseen test samples, validating its rationale reasoning capabilities.

</details>


### [266] [Quantum Incremental Learning with Mixed State Prototypes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10464)
*Yu Wu,Qianli Zhou,Xinyang Deng,Wen Jiang,Kang Hao Cheong,Witold Pedrycz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A quantum incremental learning framework uses trainable mixed-state prototypes to add new classes without widening the quantum backbone, leveraging decomposable mixed states and HS distance for scalable, cost-efficient classification under NISQ constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address incremental learning under parameter/memory limits and NISQ hardware constraints: avoid catastrophic forgetting, expand class capacity without increasing circuit width, and overcome limits of pure-state representations and basis-state count.

Method: Introduce trainable mixed-state prototypes. New classes are incorporated by adding prototypes instead of expanding the shared quantum circuit. Employ decomposable mixed-state calculations and Hilbert-Schmidt distance for classification, enabling scalable representation with few qubits.

Result: Simulation results show high-dimensional feature concentration with a minimal number of qubits, lower computational complexity, and robust representation in incremental learning tasks compared with classical baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed mixed-state prototype framework offers a scalable, cost-effective approach for quantum incremental learning under NISQ constraints, suitable for growing class sets without increasing circuit width and with competitive or superior performance to classical methods in simulations.

Abstract: Incremental learning models are required to learn new classes sequentially without catastrophic forgetting, while operating under parameter and memory constraints. In the Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) era, although quantum neural networks offer advantages in feature mapping, hardware limitations restrict circuit width. Furthermore, traditional quantum classifiers are constrained by the number of orthogonal basis states, limiting their capacity to accommodate a continually growing number of categories. Thus, we introduce a novel quantum incremental learning framework based on trainable mixed-state prototypes. Its original design incorporates new classes by adding class prototypes rather than increasing the circuit width of the shared quantum backbone. The use of mixed-state prototypes is another key contribution, since they have representation capabilities to represent information than a single pure-state prototype. And the decomposable mixed-state calculation provides lower production costs and a convenient Hilbert-Schmidt (HS) distance metric for classification. Simulation results show that our model achieves high-dimensional feature concentration using a minimal number of qubits, while demonstrating lower computational complexity and robust representation in incremental learning tasks compared with classical baselines.

</details>


### [267] [RLMOpt: Adaptive Prompt Optimization via Recursive Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10471)
*Subhash Bangalore Satheesha,Nirvik Pande,Deepthi Duddempudi,Bharath Dandala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RLMOpt uses a recursive LM-driven search agent in a tool-based environment to optimize prompts, outperforming GEPA across four benchmarks with higher efficiency and smaller prompts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome reliance on fixed optimization procedures in prompt search by making the search policy driven by a language model, enabling adaptive exploration, failure analysis, and budget allocation.

Method: A recursive language-model (RLM) agent interacts with a tool-based environment to inspect task information, analyze failures, generate candidate prompts, allocate evaluation budget, and decide when to stop. A deterministic harness enforces objective scoring, Pareto-based selection, and regression constraints to ensure robust evaluation.

Result: In four benchmarks (Chia, HotpotQA, IFBench-2025, BFCL), RLMOpt achieves the best held-out scores on all four tasks at a single seed (mean 0.610 vs 0.589 for GEPA). Across 11 seed-benchmark runs, RLMOpt outperforms GEPA in 9 cases; no run produces a worse prompt than its seed, while GEPA regressed in two. RLMOpt is also more efficient, with fewer search rollouts and prompts that are 27-79% the size of GEPA prompts.

Conclusion: Optimization gains derive mainly from exploiting the seed prompt headroom rather than search budget. Efficient optimization thus hinges on reliably reaching available headroom with minimal search.

Abstract: Prompt optimizers automate the search for prompts that improve language-model performance, but existing methods rely on a predefined optimization procedure: the algorithm determines which candidates to explore and how the search progresses, while the language model generates or refines prompt proposals. We introduce RLMOpt, a prompt optimizer that makes the search policy itself language-model-driven through a recursive language model (RLM). The RLM agent operates over a tool-based environment, inspecting task information, analyzing failures, generating candidates, allocating evaluation budget, and deciding when to stop. A deterministic harness complements the agent by enforcing objective scoring, Pareto-based selection, and regression constraints.
  We evaluate RLMOpt across four benchmarks spanning structured clinical information extraction (Chia), multi-hop question answering (HotpotQA), verifiable instruction following (IFBench-2025), and multi-turn tool-calling agents (BFCL). In a matched comparison at a single seed, RLMOpt obtains the best held-out score on all four benchmarks and leads the four-task mean (0.610 against 0.589 for GEPA). Repeating each benchmark across seeds yields 11 matched benchmark-seed comparisons, in which RLMOpt outperforms GEPA in 9 cases. Across all 11 runs, it never produced a prompt that underperformed its seed, whereas GEPA fell below its starting point twice. It is also more efficient, achieving these results with fewer search rollouts while producing prompts that are 27-79% the size of those produced by GEPA.
  Our results further show that optimization gains are determined primarily by the headroom available in the seed prompt, rather than by the search budget. Efficient optimization therefore depends on reaching the available headroom reliably and with minimal search

</details>


### [268] [Evaluating Rational Contracting in Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10475)
*Bhavyesh Sajja,Max Kleiman-Weiner,Roger Zimmermann,Tan Zhi-Xuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Language-based AI agents can negotiate and execute natural-language contracts in multi-step, uncertain environments. They perform well under low uncertainty but struggle with high uncertainty, often failing to secure satisfiable or mutually beneficial agreements and acting uncooperatively. A rational framework and ContractSim evaluation reveal gaps and guide improvements in trustworthy contracting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fill a gap in evaluating time-extended, contingent, and incomplete contracts negotiated in natural language, beyond one-off exchanges or simple games, and measure qualities essential for trustworthy contracting.

Method: Propose a rational framework for negotiating and executing natural-language contracts in uncertain multi-step environments; define metrics for rational and cooperative behavior; instantiate the framework in ContractSim, an evaluation suite with six environments and three supplier settings; assess performance of language-model–based agents.

Result: LLM-based agents can reach agreements reliably and negotiate efficient contracts when environmental uncertainty is low; under high uncertainty they frequently fail to negotiate satisfiable, efficient, or mutually beneficial contracts and behave uncooperatively during execution, violating terms for extra profit.

Conclusion: There is substantial room to improve the design of language agents for negotiating, interpreting, and executing contracts to achieve rationality and cooperation under uncertainty.

Abstract: The emergence of language-based AI agents promises to transform the scope of machine economic activity. Instead of just proposing bids or following hard-coded protocols, such agents can be used to negotiate and execute agreements in open-ended natural language. However, most evaluations of these abilities have focused on one-off exchanges or simple economic games, leaving open the rich space of time-extended, contingent, and incomplete contracts made expressible by language; they also focus on raw profit, without measuring the qualities required for trustworthy contracting. We address this by formulating a rational framework for how agents should negotiate and perform natural language contracts in uncertain multi-step environments. Within this framework, we develop metrics and baselines for quantifying rational and cooperative play. To evaluate how agents perform at such contracting, we instantiate our framework in ContractSim, an evaluation suite where two players negotiate and execute a multi-turn supplier contract under environmental and inter-player uncertainty. Across six environments and three supplier settings (catering, hotel cleaning, and AI hosting) we find that current LLM-based agents reach agreement reliably, and negotiate efficient contracts when environmental uncertainty is low. However, under high uncertainty, they often fail to negotiate satisfiable, efficient, or mutually beneficial contracts. They are also frequently uncooperative when executing contracts, violating contract terms for additional profit even when contracts are easy to satisfy. These findings highlight room for improvement in the design of language agents that can negotiate, interpret, and execute contracts both rationally and cooperatively.

</details>


### [269] [Multi-Granular Rationale-Guided Molecular LLM for Property Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10480)
*Junwoo Park,Minyoung Shin,Cheol Soon Lee,Sujee Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MR-MoL uses GNN-derived, multi-granular rationales to guide a molecular LLM. By exposing substructure-level attributions at three granularity levels (Murcko scaffolds, BRICS fragments, functional groups) as evidence alongside SMILES/graphs, it achieves strong performance on MoleculeNet and offers interpretable cues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reveal internal substructure cues driving molecular properties, addressing opacity of 1D SMILES and 2D graphs and limitations of external retrieval; enable LLMs to reason over explicit, interpretable substructures.

Method: Fine-tuned GNN computes masking-based attributions for substructures; top influential substructures are serialized as rank-ordered, direction-tagged rationales. The rationales cover three granularity levels: Murcko scaffolds with side chains, BRICS fragments, and functional groups. The LLM reads these rationales alongside SMILES sequences and molecular graphs.

Result: On eight MoleculeNet tasks, MR-MoL achieves the best overall performance among generalist models and narrows the gap to specialist models. Five diagnostics show the model reads and uses the rationale rather than relying on its mere presence. Direction, rank, and substructure influence predictions, and attributions reproduce known structure–property relationships.

Conclusion: MR-MoL demonstrates that exposing GNN-derived attributions as evidence to an LLM is feasible, improving predictive performance and interpretability via multi-granular, rationale-guided molecular reasoning.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are widely applied across chemical tasks, such as molecular property prediction, which underpins drug discovery. Molecular LLMs represent a molecule through several modalities, notably a 1D SMILES sequence or a 2D molecular graph. Both encode molecular information implicitly, so the contribution of individual substructures remains opaque. Retrieval and augmentation methods add context, but from external sources. However, the cues chemists reason over are the internal substructures that drive a property up or down. We propose MR-MoL, a multi-granular rationale-guided molecular LLM that supplies this evidence directly. A fine-tuned GNN scores each substructure through masking, and the most influential ones are serialized as a ranked, direction-tagged rationale that the LLM reads alongside the SMILES sequence and molecular graph. The rationale spans three levels of granularity: Murcko scaffolds with their side chains, BRICS fragments, and functional groups. This is, to our knowledge, the first method to expose GNN-derived attributions to an LLM as evidence for property prediction. On eight MoleculeNet tasks, MR-MoL achieves the best overall results among generalist models and narrows the gap to specialist models tuned for each task. Five diagnostics further confirm that the model reads the rationale rather than merely benefiting from its presence. Its direction, rank, and substructure each shape the prediction, and its attributions reproduce known structure-property relationships.

</details>


### [270] [Predicting Space Groups of Double Perovskites by LLM with Dynamic Few-Shot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10483)
*Jongwon Park,Inhyo Lee,Junhyeong Lee,Seunghwa Ryu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DyRIS is a retrieval-based LLM framework that predicts space-group candidates for double perovskites under data imbalance, achieving better Minor-SG performance via diversity-aware few-shot prompts and rule-guided inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address imbalanced SG datasets where dominant (major) SGs bias predictions; need leverage of domain knowledge with LLM reasoning.

Method: Diversity-enhanced dynamic few-shot retrieval to fetch relevant in-context examples; rule-guided inference using B/B' ordering, quantitative indicators, major-SG bias control to rank Top-3.

Result: Evaluated on 3,528 DP entries; at 0.5 data ratio, strong Overall Top-1 macro-F1; best Minor-SG metrics; Minor-SG Top-1 improved by 3.26% over CrabNet; best Minor-SG Top-3 vs PyCaret; ablations show contributions; rule-guided step not easily replaced by conventional classifiers.

Conclusion: Shows potential of combining retrieval-based LLM reasoning with crystallographic domain knowledge for SG prediction in imbalanced datasets.

Abstract: Double perovskites (DPs) offer broad compositional tunability, but predicting the space groups (SGs) of stable structures remains difficult because available datasets are often strongly imbalanced toward dominant SG classes. We refer to dominant SG classes as major SGs and underrepresented classes as minor SGs. We introduce Dynamic and Diversity-enhanced Few-shot Retrieval and Rule-Guided Inference for Space-Group Prediction (DyRIS), an LLM-agent-based framework that predicts ranked SG candidates from a given DP composition. DyRIS uses diversity-enhanced dynamic few-shot prompting to retrieve relevant in-context examples while limiting the dominance of frequently represented SGs. It further incorporates rule-guided inference based on B/B' cation ordering, quantitative indicators, and major-SG bias control to refine and rank the final Top-3 SG candidates. We evaluate DyRIS on 3,528 thermodynamically filtered DP entries and compare it with composition-based and descriptor-based baselines. At a training-data ratio of 0.5, DyRIS achieves competitive overall accuracy while obtaining the best Overall Top-1 macro-F1 score and the best performance across all Minor-SG metrics. DyRIS improves Minor-SG Top-1 accuracy by 3.26 percentage points relative to CrabNet and achieves higher Minor-SG Top-3 accuracy than the strongest PyCaret-based baseline. Ablation studies show that diversity-enhanced retrieval, quantitative indicators, major-SG bias control, and B/B' ordering information each contribute to prediction performance. Additional experiments show that the final rule-guided inference step is not easily replaced by conventional classifier- or ranker-based models. These findings demonstrate the potential of combining retrieval-based LLM reasoning with crystallographic domain knowledge for SG prediction in imbalanced materials datasets.

</details>


### [271] [INSIDE the Student's Mind: Jointly Modeling Latent Reasoning and Action in LLM Student Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10492)
*Rose Niousha,Minwoo Kang,Narges Norouzi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: INSIDE: Fine-tunes LLMs to generate internal think traces and external actions for student simulations, grounded in Bloom's Taxonomy, improving action fidelity and reasoning alignment (up to 57.9%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based simulators reproduce observable actions but not underlying reasoning, which is crucial for accurately modeling diverse student behavior in education and for evaluating tutoring systems.

Method: Fine-tune LLMs on paired think traces (internal dialogue) and observed actions, with internal thinking grounded across Bloom's Taxonomy's cognitive, affective, and action dimensions; compare against prompting baselines across two evaluation axes: action fidelity (e.g., code generation) and reasoning alignment.

Result: Action fidelity improves to better match real students' code generation; reasoning alignment achieves the highest reported alignment among models, up to 57.9%.

Conclusion: Modeling internal cognition via structured think traces enhances both observable actions and the fidelity of inferred reasoning, suggesting that explicit internal dialogue grounding can improve educational simulations.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM)-based simulators often reproduce observable actions but fail to capture the underlying reasoning behind them. In education, where student simulation is increasingly used for various applications such as evaluating tutoring systems, this gap is especially pronounced. Two students may submit identical submissions for entirely different reasons. We present INTERNAL STUDENT DIALOGUE (INSIDE), a student modeling framework that fine-tunes LLMs not only to act like students but also to think like them. INSIDE generates internal dialogue grounded in Bloom's Taxonomy across cognitive, affective, and action dimensions, and fine-tunes models on paired think traces and actions. We baseline against different prompting frameworks and evaluate on two axes: fidelity of simulated actions and quality of generated internal dialogue. Our evaluations show that INSIDE improves simulation fidelity in both action fidelity, matching code generation of real students, and reasoning alignment, achieving the highest alignment across models up to 57.9%.

</details>


### [272] [GeoForge: Non-Parametric Self-Evolving Agents for Earth-Observation Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10494)
*Xin Xiao,Jiang Zhong,Junnan Zhu,Yingchao Feng,Peijin Wang,Yidan Zhang,Kaiwen Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GeoForge is a training-free, self-evolving framework that converts completed EO tool trajectories into a structured nonparametric execution state and retrieves task-conditioned priors from three memories to improve planning and tool use without updating the LLM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Earth observation (EO) workflows face sensing semantics, product dependencies, and spatiotemporal constraints; agents search broad operation spaces and histories are poorly organized, hindering reuse across decision levels.

Method: GeoForge constrains the operation space by sensing context and retrieves task-conditioned priors from three memories: (1) Workflow Graph Memory for global operation order, (2) Action-Level Experiences for local corrections, and (3) Adapted Skill Standard Operating Procedure for procedural and data constraints. It uses a safety-gated distillation after each task to create reusable execution knowledge, maintaining a nonparametric execution state and leaving the backbone LLM unchanged.

Result: Experiments on multiple geospatial benchmarks show GeoForge consistently improves task accuracy and tool-use trajectory quality across diverse LLM backbones and substantially reduces tool-planning and reasoning errors for most LLMs.

Conclusion: GeoForge provides a training-free, self-evolving knowledge loop that improves planning and tool use in EO workflows without retraining the LLM, with demonstrated gains across varied geospatial benchmarks.

Abstract: Earth observation (EO) agents construct scientifically valid tool workflows and ground their conclusions in current geospatial evidence. This is challenging because EO workflows are constrained by sensing semantics, product dependencies, spatial and temporal compatibility, and parameter requirements. Existing agents often search a broad operation space for each query, while recent self-evolving systems do not fully organize heterogeneous EO trajectories into reusable knowledge across different decision levels. To solve this problem, we present GeoForge, a training-free, self-evolving framework that transforms completed trajectories into a structured nonparametric execution state. GeoForge constrains the operation space according to the sensing context, then retrieves a task-conditioned prior from three complementary memories. Workflow Graph Memory captures global operation order, Action-Level Experiences provide local corrections, and the Adapted Skill Standard Operating Procedure preserves procedural and data constraints. The retrieved prior guides tool execution, while current observations remain the basis of the final answer. After each task, a safety-gated distillation process converts grounded trajectories into reusable execution knowledge for future retrieval. This execution, distillation, and reuse loop improves planning without updating the backbone LLM. Experiments on multiple geospatial benchmarks demonstrate that GeoForge consistently improves both task accuracy and tool-use trajectory quality across diverse LLM backbones, while substantially reducing tool-planning and reasoning errors for most LLMs.

</details>


### [273] [From Faulty Memories to Corrected Actions: Dependency-Guided Rollback Repair for Memory-Augmented Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10502)
*Caili Yu,Yiqi Wang,Jiaqi Zhang,Yiqun Duan,Mingkai Zheng,Zhangkai Wu,Kaize Shi,Taotao Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Dependency-guided rollback repair enables post-failure recovery of LLM persistent-memory-assisted agents by reconstructing only answer-relevant computation, achieving strong recovery-cost trade-offs and effective memory sanitization across diverse failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persistent memory in LLM agents can propagate errors across sessions; existing defenses either delete memory or reset state, which discards useful work. There is a need to recover both the answer and the persistent state after a failure without destroying benign information.

Method: Construct a typed memory-to-action graph from runtime provenance, identify explicit downstream dependencies, preserve independently trusted candidates, deactivate unsupported memory state, and selectively replay only computation relevant to the answer to recover both the response and memory state.

Result: In a 150-case controlled benchmark over three tool-use domains and four memory failure types, the method achieved 85.3% recovery (vs. 77.3% for the best competing method), removed all faulty memories, preserved benign memories, and incurred modest LLM-call cost via selective replay. In a 50-case trajectory-derived stress test, recovery was 68.0% (vs. 54.0%), with the highest claim-invalidation F1 of 0.669 (vs. 0.603). Overall, trace reconstruction is not uniformly better, but the approach offers a favorable recovery–cost trade-off while repairing faulty memory and preserving benign data.

Conclusion: Dependency-guided rollback repair provides effective post-failure recovery for persistent-memory LLM agents, balancing recovery rate, memory integrity, and computational cost. While not universally superior in trace reconstruction, it demonstrates a strong, practical trade-off for repairing faulty memory with selective replay.

Abstract: Persistent memory lets language-model agents reuse information across sessions, but it also makes errors durable: a poisoned, stale, or misattributed record can alter reasoning, tool use, answers, and subsequent memory writes. Existing defenses mainly detect or delete suspicious memories, or revise the current response. Deleting the source leaves already propagated claims, actions, and derived memories active, whereas resetting the store or replaying the full trace destroys benign state and repeats unnecessary computation. We therefore formulate \textbf{post-failure memory recovery: } \textit{given a failed execution and diagnosed faulty memories, recover both the answer and persistent state while retaining unaffected work.} Our \textbf{dependency-guided rollback repair} builds a typed memory-to-action graph from runtime provenance, traces explicit downstream dependencies, preserves candidates with independent trusted support, deactivates unsupported memory state, and selectively replays only answer-relevant affected computation. We evaluate this approach on a 150-case controlled benchmark spanning three tool-use domains and four memory failure types, and on a 50-case trajectory-derived stress test adapted from LongMemEval-V2. On the controlled benchmark, it achieves 85.3\% recovery versus 77.3\% for the best competing recovery method, removes all diagnosed faulty memories, preserves all benign memories, and requires only selective replay with modest LLM-call cost. On the adapted subset, it reaches 68.0\% recovery versus 54.0\% for the next best method, while also achieving the highest claim invalidation F1, 0.669 versus 0.603. Overall, the results do not imply uniformly better trace reconstruction, but show that dependency-guided rollback repair provides a strong recovery--cost trade-off while repairing faulty memory state and preserving benign memory.

</details>


### [274] [MEGA: Self-Evolving Agent Optimization Infrastructure via Wisdom Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10504)
*Jung Hwan Lee,Kyu Ho Lee,Gwang Hoon Yoo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: As coding agents increasingly handle implementation, the central challenge shifts from building individual agents to building an infrastructure that systematically improves them. Current approaches optimize agent systems without accumulating transferable knowledge, accumulate knowledge without compositional reasoning over it, and lack a mechanism for that knowledge to self-evolve through operational evidence.
