MEGA (Meta Evaluation-Grounded Adaptation) addresses these gaps as a self-evolving infrastructure: each optimization cycle produces durable assets, compositional reasoning over those assets guides subsequent optimization, and operational evidence refines both the accumulated wisdom and the reasoning that governs it. Layer 1 distills reusable wisdom from agent sessions through behavioral-pattern clustering and empirical A/B validation, transforming each process into a durable asset. Layer 2 decomposes these assets into atomic PCR (Primary-Context-Resultant) units within a typed Wisdom Graph and performs deductive, abductive, and inductive reasoning to expand implicit relations; it then assembles context-specific execution plans through compositional retrieval that surfaces bridging knowledge unreachable by embedding similarity alone. Layer 3 performs multi-agent collaborative optimization over heterogeneous agent workflows (code nodes, LLM calls, and tool-using agents), attributing improvement effects to specific strategy changes through controlled evaluation that eliminates data variance. Evidence fed back from Layer 3 drives the self-evolution of both the curation strategies that govern wisdom composition and the optimization trajectories accumulated across runs. The result is an infrastructure in which optimizing an agent system and evolving the knowledge that guides optimization are one and the same process.

</details>


### [275] [RadFusion: Towards Threshold-Controllable Radiology Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10505)
*Ying Jin,Noel C. F. Codella,John Corring,Mu Wei,Dinei Florencio,Eric Horvitz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RadFusion enables threshold-controllable radiology report generation by fusing a per-disease classifier with a VQA-based generator and an LLM rewriter, yielding ROC-consistent, context-adaptive, and more accurate reports.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the lack of control over sensitivity-specificity in automated radiology reports and to align with ROC-based regulatory validation. Clinical settings require different operating points (high sensitivity for emergencies, high specificity for confirmatory diagnoses).

Method: Integrate a multi-label classifier that outputs per-disease confidence with a VQA-based report generator; use an LLM to rewrite the report so diagnoses follow the classifier’s decisions at a selected threshold while grounding in the generator’s findings; validate on MIMIC-CXR by sweeping the threshold, mapping reports to class labels, and reproducing the classifier’s ROC curve.

Result: RadFusion’s reports conform to the classifier’s ROC curve across thresholds; conducting threshold sweeps reproduces ROC performance and enables operating-point selection aligned with clinical context; diagnostic accuracy improves with 6.9% sensitivity gain at matched specificity and 20.7% specificity gain at matched sensitivity.

Conclusion: RadFusion provides clinically adaptable, quantitatively verifiable, and more diagnostically reliable radiology report generation, supporting regulatory clearance through ROC-consistent evaluation and context-aware operating points.

Abstract: Automated radiology report generation is advancing rapidly in response to the shortage of radiologists, yet unlike a perception model, existing generation models offer no control over the sensitivity-specificity trade-off of their diagnostic content. Such control is essential because clinical scenarios diverge: emergency triage prioritizes sensitivity to reduce missed findings, whereas confirmatory interpretation emphasizes specificity to limit unnecessary interventions. A single fixed report can neither adapt to these scenarios nor support the ROC-based validation widely expected for regulatory clearance. We introduce RadFusion, a framework that equips report generation with threshold controllability. Our method fuses a multi-label classifier, which provides per-disease confidence scores, with a VQA-based report generator, which describes medical findings in detail; an LLM then rewrites the report so that its stated diagnoses follow the classifier's decisions at the selected threshold while staying grounded in the generator's descriptions. On MIMIC-CXR, the performance of RadFusion conforms to the classifier's ROC curve: sweeping the threshold and mapping the reports back to class labels reproduces the classifier's validated ROC performance. This conformance makes generated reports quantitatively evaluable through ROC analysis, strengthening the case for regulatory clearance, and enables operating-point selection that matches report behavior to clinical context. Moreover, combining the two model types improves diagnostic accuracy over uncontrolled generation: sensitivity increases by 6.9% at matched specificity, and specificity by 20.7% at matched sensitivity. These results show that RadFusion makes report generation clinically adaptable, quantitatively verifiable, and diagnostically more reliable.

</details>


### [276] [MAP-Graph: Provenance-Aware Shared Memory for Multi-Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10509)
*Yiqi Wang,Zihao Yan,Jiaqi Zhang,Zhangkai Wu,Mingkai Zheng,Zequn Sun,Yanming Zhu,Taotao Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAP-Graph presents a provenance-aware memory layer for language-model agents that enforces strict authorization, leverages graded trust, and gates actions using a typed execution graph; it outperforms baselines on synthetic tasks and maintains audit-friendly lineage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To mitigate the risk that restricted, private, poisoned, or revoked sources are inadvertently used by LMs during long reasoning workflows, and to move provenance from post-hoc audit to real-time control by explicitly separating hard authorization from graded trust.

Method: MAP-Graph constructs a typed execution graph capturing agents, sources, memories, claims, and actions. It traces ancestry, applies permission filtering to exclude ineligible records, reorders memories by semantic similarity and multiplicative path trust, and enforces a risk-sensitive gate before action execution while preserving lineage for auditing.

Result: On a controlled benchmark of 2,700 synthetic tasks across three domains, MAP-Graph achieves 94.96% overall task success, 72.70% exact decision accuracy, and 90.22% in the clean setting. Ablations confirm the roles of permission filtering, path trust, and action gating; transfer tests with two additional backbones retain exact-decision and access-control advantages.

Conclusion: Provenance can serve as an operational control signal, enabling safer, auditable LM actions beyond post-hoc analysis, as demonstrated in the evaluated setting.

Abstract: Shared memory helps language-model agents reuse information across long workflows, yet relevant evidence may not be admissible for a particular agent or action. Because restrictions propagate through derivations, summaries can conceal private, poisoned, untrusted, or revoked sources, enabling unauthorized reads or unsafe actions. Existing approaches provide semantic retrieval, scoped access, or lineage tracking, but do not clearly separate hard authorization from graded trust or adapt evidence requirements to action risk. We introduce MAP-Graph, a provenance-aware memory layer that represents agents, sources, memories, claims, and actions in a typed execution graph. It traces ancestry, excludes permission-ineligible records, reranks eligible memories by semantic similarity and multiplicative path trust, and applies a risk-sensitive gate before action execution while retaining affected lineage for audit. On a controlled benchmark of 2,700 synthetic tasks per method across three domains, MAP-Graph achieves 94.96\% overall task success, 72.70\% exact decision accuracy, and 90.22\% in the clean setting, where success requires a correct \textsc{Allow} rather than a safe intervention. Ablations isolate the roles of permission filtering, path trust, and action gating, while transfer tests with two additional backbones preserve the exact-decision and access-control advantages. These results support provenance as an operational control signal, rather than only post-hoc audit metadata, within the evaluated setting.

</details>


### [277] [Measuring Semantic Abstractness of SAE Features via Nonlocality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10537)
*Chuqiao Lin,Shivaji Sondhi,Xiao-Liang Qi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Feature Nonlocality (FNL): entropy-based measure of per-position influence on an SAE feature to quantify abstraction level; shows FNL correlates with semantic abstractness proxies, distinguishes context-dependent from token-driven features, and enables downstream applications (jailbreak mitigation audit and feature steering to improve MATH-500).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address difficulty in assessing whether SAE-derived explanations reflect genuinely high-level, abstract features or surface lexical cues. Existing autointerp and causal steering do not reliably resolve abstraction level; need a label-free, model-agnostic metric.

Method: Define FNL as the entropy of the normalized per-position influence on an SAE feature's activation. Empirically evaluate FNL: (1) correlate with proxy metrics of semantic abstractness; (2) pairwise feature discrimination (contextual vs token-level) across random pairs; (3) audit jailbreak-mitigation features; (4) implement steering using DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B and evaluate on MATH-500.

Result: FNL correlates with existing LLM-based proxy metrics of semantic abstractness; it distinguishes context-dependent reasoning features from token-driven ones, correctly assigning higher FNL to contextual features in 73–84% of random pairs. Jailbreak features tend to be low-FNL (often positional). Steering high-FNL features yields a 4.6-point improvement on MATH-500 over unsteered model, outperforming steering low-FNL features in a model-specific manner.

Conclusion: FNL provides an LLM-independent, label-free, correlational witness of a SAE feature's abstraction level, enabling evaluation of mechanistic explanations and informed feature selection for downstream interventions.

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) have helped uncover mechanistic explanations for LLM behaviours such as reasoning, jailbreaking etc., via understanding the corresponding task-relevant and causally effective features. To evaluate such mechanistic explanations, downstream studies must distinguish surface lexical features from genuinely high-level ones. However, neither an autointerp-based semantic description nor causal steering utility fully resolves the abstraction level of a feature. To this end, we introduce \emph{Feature Nonlocality} (FNL), defined as the entropy of the normalized per-position influence on an SAE feature's activation. We report that FNL correlates with existing LLM-based proxy metrics of feature semantic abstractness, and successfully distinguishes context-dependent reasoning features from token-driven ones, correctly assigning the higher FNL to the contextual feature in $73$--$84\%$ of randomly drawn pairs that consist of one contextual and one token-level feature.
  We demonstrate two downstream applications. We audit SAE-based features used for jailbreak mitigation and find surprisingly that most effective features are positional features with low FNL rather than genuinely recognizing harmful intents.
  We report that steering high-FNL features in DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B improves MATH-500 accuracy by $4.6$ points over the unsteered model and outperforms steering low-FNL features, though the gains are model-specific. We conclude that FNL provides an LLM-independent, label-free, correlational witness of the abstraction level of an SAE feature, with applications in evaluating mechanistic explanations as well as selecting features for downstream interventions.

</details>


### [278] [SKILLER: Language-Level Reinforcement Learning for Reusable Skill Extraction in Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10538)
*Chenhao Dang,Siyuan Xiong,Conghui He,Weijia Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SKILLER: a natural-language-driven reinforcement learning framework to automatically generate executor-specific skills for small models, using a strong model as both actor and critic; treats the small-model system as the environment and propagates RL signals entirely through natural language; shows significant performance gains on 9B and 4B Qwen models across five benchmarks, approaching closed-source performance on some tasks; code released on GitHub.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce inference costs and enable effective task execution with open-source, consumer-grade hardware by endowing small models with learned skills that constrain and guide their behavior, addressing the difficulty of crafting such skills for compact models.

Method: A reinforcement learning framework (SKILLER) in which a strong, high-capacity model serves as both the actor and critic. The environment is the small-model agent system, and all RL feedback is communicated in natural language rather than numeric rewards. The approach automatically generates executor-specific skills tailored to the small model.

Result: SKILLER outperforms three open-source and one closed-source skill generation/evolution baselines across five benchmarks using Qwen3.5-9B and Qwen3.5-4B, with absolute gains of 4.3–20.4 percentage points (9B) and 1.8–13.3 points (4B). It even matches the performance of strong closed-source models on single-skill tasks in SkillsBench.

Conclusion: Natural-language-driven RL can effectively generate useful skills for small models, enabling competitive performance relative to strong closed-source models while reducing computational costs. The approach is released at the project repository.

Abstract: Agent skills represent a standardized format for packaging procedural knowledge and domain expertise, serving within agent harness systems as an essential mechanism to continually constrain a language model's behavior space for repeatable, high-quality task execution. However, because strong closed-source models entail high inference costs, current popular agent harnesses, such as Codex and OpenClaw, remain prohibitively expensive when deploying these skills to accomplish real-world tasks. The rapid capability enhancement of open-source models deployable on consumer-grade GPUs presents a compelling opportunity to drastically reduce these costs by leveraging skill-based behavioral constraints. Nevertheless, automatically generating effective skills tailored specifically for such compact models remains a significant practical challenge. To address this, we propose SKILLER, a natural-language-driven reinforcement learning framework designed to automatically generate executor-specific skills for small models, which employs a strong model as the actor and critic, treats the small-model agent system as the environment, and propagates all reinforcement learning signals entirely via natural language. Extensive experimental evaluations across five relevant benchmarks using Qwen3.5-9B and Qwen3.5-4B demonstrate that SKILLER outperforms three open-source and one closed-source skill generation or evolution methods, achieving absolute gains ranging from 4.3 to 20.4 percentage points for the 9B model and 1.8 to 13.3 points for the 4B model, while remarkably matching the performance of strong closed-source models on single-skill tasks in SkillsBench. The project is available at https://github.com/DANG-ai/SKILLER.

</details>


### [279] [Reinforcement Learning-Based Laser Cutting Machine Parameter Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10549)
*Khanh Quan Pham,Majid Kundroo,Geunwoo Ban,Seongho Bae,Taehong Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RL2C is a Q-learning based reinforcement learning approach with epsilon-greedy policy and dynamic environment adaptability for laser-cutting parameter optimization, achieving faster convergence and reduced film wastage compared to other RL methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional trial-and-error parameter tuning for laser cutting is slow, inaccurate, and requires manual intervention. An adaptive, data-driven method is needed to tailor cutting parameters to different film types and processes.

Method: RL2C employs Q-learning with an epsilon-greedy policy to dynamically optimize laser cutting parameters (e.g., focal length and laser power) for various film types. It introduces a dynamic environment space adaptability mechanism to handle new states across multiple experiment batches, enabling robust learning and transfer.

Result: Compared with existing RL-based optimization methods, RL2C reduces optimization steps by up to 12.5% and processing time by up to 81.8%, while achieving high cutting accuracy with reduced taper and film wastage.

Conclusion: RL methods can effectively improve industrial laser-cutting processes, delivering better cut quality, lower processing time, less material waste, and reduced need for manual parameter tuning.

Abstract: Achieving high accuracy in laser-based cutting of optical films requires careful tuning of parameters such as focal length and laser power beam, adjusted according to the specific properties of each film type. Trial-and-error based traditional methods are used to find the most suitable cutting parameters for various films, but they are slow and inaccurate. To address this issue, this paper presents the Reinforcement Learning for Laser Cutting (RL$^{2}$C) algorithm, which uses Q-learning with an epsilon-greedy policy to dynamically optimize cutting parameters, significantly reducing taper size and film wastage. Additionally, RL$^{2}$C incorporates a dynamic environment space adaptability mechanism to allow it to adapt to new states encountered during the learning process over multiple batches of experiments. Experimental results demonstrate that RL$^{2}$C requires fewer steps and less time to find optimal cutting parameters compared to various RL-based optimization methods. Specifically, RL$^{2}$C reduces the number of optimization steps by up to 12.5\% and processing time by up to 81.8\% compared to existing methods. This study demonstrates the potential of RL in industrial laser-cutting processes by improving cut quality, reducing time and film wastage, and minimizing manual interventions.

</details>


### [280] [DashArena: Benchmarking LLMs on Interactive Analytic Dashboard Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10567)
*Xiaotong Wang,Dazhen Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DashArena benchmarks open-ended, task-grounded generation of interactive analytics dashboards by requiring a replayable interaction trajectory and end-to-end evidence-based judging, revealing interaction-aware failures not captured by static or execution-only checks; a distilled judge (DashJudge-8B) aligns with human judgments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating the usefulness of generated dashboards requires end-to-end evidence of analytical workflow, not just static visuals or isolated executions. The paper argues for a reproducible interaction trajectory to quantify interaction quality and analytical support.

Method: System generates both a dashboard and a replayable interaction trajectory. A browser executor replays the trajectory to produce reproducible visual and execution evidence. A Vision-Language Model (VLM) judge uses this evidence to compare candidates; Bradley–Terry aggregation computes the leaderboard. The judge is distilled into the open-weight DashJudge-8B. Human evaluations validate the approach; experiments with frontier models reveal rendering, analytical, and interaction failures.

Result: Interaction-aware evaluation aligns with human judgments better than static/execution-only checks. DashArena reveals persistent failures in rendering, analytics, and interaction for frontier models, underscoring the difficulty of realistic dashboard generation.

Conclusion: DashArena is the first benchmark for open-ended, task-grounded dashboard generation, highlighting the importance of interaction evidence in evaluation. DashJudge-8B provides a practical, open-weight judge, but significant challenges remain in producing realistic, usable dashboards.

Abstract: Analytic dashboards combine coordinated views and interactions for data exploration and decision-making. Recent models can generate them from data and natural-language goals, but evaluating their usefulness remains difficult. Dashboard generation is open-ended, and neither static appearance nor successful execution alone captures analytical support and interaction quality. We introduce DashArena, to our knowledge the first benchmark for open-ended, task-grounded generation of interactive analytic dashboards. Its key innovation is to require each system to generate both a dashboard and a replayable interaction trajectory. A browser executor replays the trajectory and turns the system's intended analytical workflow into reproducible visual and execution evidence. A VLM judge compares candidates using this evidence, and Bradley--Terry aggregation produces the leaderboard. We further distill the judge into the open-weight DashJudge-8B. Human evaluations show that DashJudge-8B effectively reproduces human judgments and ablations show that interaction evidence improves judge agreement. Experiments with frontier models reveal persistent rendering, analytical, and interaction failures. Together, these results show that realistic dashboard generation remains challenging and that interaction-aware evaluation captures failures missed by static or execution-only checks.

</details>


### [281] [Agentic Instruction Data Selection: Let DataMaster Interpret Your Intent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10579)
*Fanqi Zhou,Qiaosheng Chen,Zixian Huang,Gong Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Automated orchestration of instruction data selection via DataMaster to interpret user intent and compose optimal data selection strategies; outperforms baselines and often full-pool training; code available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: No single metric generalizes across real-world datasets; manual data curation/heuristics are tedious and error-prone; automation can reduce burden and improve selection across domains.

Method: Introduce DataMaster, an Instruction Data Selection Agent that takes natural language user descriptions, interprets intent, and autonomously composes data selection strategies; evaluated across math, medical, and code domains; demonstrates generalization beyond static baselines.

Result: DataMaster outperforms static baselines in most settings and surpasses full-pool training in a substantial number of cases.

Conclusion: Automated data selection reduces manual effort, enables cross-domain generalization, and achieves competitive or superior performance; implementation and reproducibility artifacts are publicly available.

Abstract: Although existing instruction data selection methods have introduced various metrics, the inherent complexity of real-world datasets makes it impractical for any single metric to generalize across all scenarios. Developers are thus often forced to manually inspect data and craft heuristic rules for each new application---a tedious and error-prone process. In this paper, we propose a paradigm shift from manual configuration to automated orchestration via the Instruction Data Selection Agent (DataMaster), which interprets user intent and autonomously composes optimal selection strategies. By allowing users to specify data needs through natural language descriptions, DataMaster simplifies data curation and removes the burden of manual strategy design. Extensive experiments across the math, medical, and code domains show that DataMaster outperforms static baselines in most settings and surpasses full-pool training in a substantial number of cases. The implementation of DataMaster and the scripts needed to reproduce the reported pipeline are publicly available at https://github.com/nju-websoft/DataMaster.

</details>


### [282] [HexEval: An Evidence-Driven Hexagonal Framework for Multidimensional Scholar Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10584)
*Xiaokang Qu,Yiting Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HexEval is an evidence-driven, two-layer framework for multidimensional scholar assessment that preserves intermediate evidence and rationales to enable auditable, interpretable outputs; it shows dimension-wise alignment with humans and references and highlights data coverage limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome reliance on traditional bibliometrics and single-paper evaluation by treating scholar assessment as evidence-driven reasoning across intrinsic quality and external scholarly behavior, enabling interpretable, auditable profiles.

Method: Propose HexEval with two complementary evidence layers: an intrinsic layer evaluating anonymized representative works on research rigor, methodological innovation, and scientific contribution; and an external layer characterizing scholars via knowledge translation, research coherence, and academic impact using heterogeneous evidence from GitHub, Lens, OpenAlex, and other verifiable sources. Outputs retain intermediate evidence, dimension-specific rationales, and verification signals instead of opaque aggregate scores.

Result: Experiments across six dimensions show dimension-dependent agreement with human/external criteria; structured calibration improves absolute agreement for intrinsic quality, while external modules recover broad trajectory and ordinal impact signals; results support evidence-driven reasoning over heterogeneous scholarly evidence and reveal coverage/attribution limitations of public data.

Conclusion: Evidence-driven reasoning over heterogeneous scholarly evidence is a promising paradigm for auditable AI-assisted scholar assessment, with practical benefits and caveats related to data coverage and attribution.

Abstract: Scholar assessment plays a fundamental role in faculty recruitment, funding allocation, academic promotion, and talent discovery. Existing scholar assessment methods predominantly rely on bibliometric indicators and reputation proxies, while recent large language model (LLM)-based approaches mainly focus on evaluating individual research papers rather than comprehensively assessing scholars. We argue that scholar assessment should be formulated as an evidence-driven reasoning problem that jointly considers intrinsic research quality and externally verifiable scholarly behavior. To this end, we propose HexEval, an evidence-driven hexagonal framework for multidimensional scholar assessment. HexEval explicitly organizes scholar assessment into two complementary evidence layers. The intrinsic layer evaluates anonymized representative works along three dimensions, namely research rigor, methodological innovation, and scientific contribution, whereas the external layer characterizes scholars through knowledge translation, research coherence, and academic impact using heterogeneous evidence collected from GitHub, Lens, OpenAlex, and other publicly verifiable sources. Instead of producing opaque aggregate scores, HexEval preserves intermediate evidence, dimension-specific rationales, and verification signals throughout the evaluation process, enabling interpretable and auditable scholar profiles. Experiments across all six dimensions show dimension-dependent agreement with human or external reference criteria: structured calibration improves absolute agreement for intrinsic quality, while the external modules recover broad trajectory and ordinal impact signals. These results support evidence-driven reasoning over heterogeneous scholarly evidence as a promising paradigm for auditable AI-assisted scholar assessment, while exposing the coverage and attribution limitations of public scholarly data.

</details>


### [283] [Curate Before You Connect: Identity and Ontology Tagging in a Production Knowledge Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10644)
*Vaibhav Dangaich,Kevin Lewis,Kundeshwar Pundalik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A large-scale ingestion and ontology-tagging pipeline builds a government document-derived knowledge graph. It relies on a record-identity ladder and a coarse canonical-name key to merge records, accepting automated de-duplication without identity benchmarks. The approach highlights ontology tagging challenges, evidence asymmetry, and a growing curation backlog, arguing for candidate-only identity decisions to avoid irreversible merges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To transform a validated extraction stream into a scalable, queryable knowledge graph from 98k government documents, while addressing identity management, deduplication, and ontology tagging at scale.

Method: Ingestion and ontology-tagging layer converts extractions into a KG. A record-identity ladder determines sameness using identifier columns, name columns, display names, and type-scoped position, separate from name similarity. The graph write uses a coarser canonical-name key that merges records with exact equality. Multi-class ontology tagging is performed, with evidence-based role assignments, anchored evidence to reduce misclassifications, and a curation queue for human review (48,403 pending proposals vs 775 decisions).

Result: A KG with 537,157 entities and 2,198,567 relationships derived from 98,795 documents. The system flags identity candidates rather than committing to identity, but a merge policy allows automated canonical-name-based merges. Notable findings include: (i) two surface forms of a name merge incident affecting other data; (ii) evidence asymmetry causes classifications when matching name fragments; (iii) anchored evidence reduces role assignments from 36 to 4; (iv) conformance debt quantified; (v) secondary classifications compensate for mis-parented primary classes; (vi) sizable curation backlog (~48k proposals).

Conclusion: The paper favors treating identity as a cautious, candidate-driven process to avoid irreversible mistakes, but demonstrates significant risks from de-duplication policies and ontology tagging misclassifications. It highlights the need for governance, provenance, and benchmarking in large-scale KG ingestion.

Abstract: Extraction produces candidate entities and relationships; writing them into a graph is where identity is decided, and identity decisions are destructive in a way extraction errors are not. A wrong type can be corrected later, but two records merged under one identity cannot be separated once their properties have been combined, and the merge leaves no error behind. This paper describes the ingestion and ontology-tagging layer that turns a validated extraction stream into a knowledge graph of 537,157 entities and 2,198,567 relationships drawn from 98,795 government documents. We describe a record-identity ladder that decides sameness from identifier columns, name columns, display names and type-scoped position rather than from name similarity. The ladder governs de-duplication within parsed tables, while the graph write applies a coarser canonical-name key, so records sharing a canonical name merge automatically on exact equality. We argue rather than demonstrate that this is where the automation line belongs: no identity benchmark is reported, and the over-merges the key permits are undetectable by construction. That policy, under which entity resolution only ever flags candidates, followed an incident in which two surface forms of one name were merged, corrupting a correct record and deleting eight entities from an unrelated document. We then describe multi-class ontology tagging and an evidence asymmetry we did not anticipate: an entity name is an instance label rather than a type assertion, so matching name fragments against a class index invents classifications. Requiring anchored evidence cut role assignments on an enriched sample from 36 to 4, all confirmed correct. We quantify the graph's conformance debt, show secondary classifications compensating for a mis-parented primary class, and describe a curation queue grown to 48,403 pending proposals against 775 human decisions.

</details>


### [284] [Decision-Aware Approximation of Belief Functions for Evidential Combinatorial Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10650)
*Sohaib Afifi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A decision-aware mass-function compressor preserves decision quality rather than distribution closeness, using a dynamic-programming framework to minimize decision regret; it outperforms representation-aware methods in experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mass-function compression for decision tasks; traditional distance-based approximations aim at closeness, but in decision contexts the quality of the resulting decision is what matters. Propose a decision-aware objective.

Method: Define a decision-aware approximation that minimizes regret under the true mass function; derive a one-point bound localizing regret at the true optimum; formulate exact DP for scalar case; extend to online version with pruning; compare to distance-based methods; experiments with linear and non-linear readouts.

Result: The decision-aware compressor flips the decision less often than a representation-aware compressor for both linear criterion and non-linear proxy read-out; provides theoretical bound and online algorithm.

Conclusion: Decision-aware compression yields better decision stability in evidential-cost optimization; introduces practical algorithms and analysis, with broader applicability to decision-making under uncertainty with evidence theory.

Abstract: Reducing the number of focal elements of a mass function is classically driven by an intrinsic distance, such as Jaccard or Jousselme, that keeps the approximation close to the original as a body of evidence. We consider instead the case where the mass function feeds a linear combinatorial optimisation problem with evidential costs. What should then be preserved is not the closeness of the two mass functions, but the quality of the decision they induce. We introduce a decision-aware approximation that targets the regret of the decision: one decides with the cheaper approximation and is evaluated under the true mass function. On a minimal shortest path, the distance-optimal approximation flips the decision while a decision-aware merge preserves it, and this occurs on a non-negligible fraction of random instances. We prove a one-point bound that localises the regret at the true optimum, turn it into an exact dynamic program for the scalar case, and extend it to an online version that prunes focal elements before the final cost is known. In experiments the decision-aware compressor flips the decision less often than representation-aware compression, for both the linear criterion and a non-linear proxy read-out.

</details>


### [285] [Operationalising Relative Causal Knowledge: Backbone Identifiability from Private Reports on a Shared Outcome](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10664)
*Fabrizio Russo,Mark Somers*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Local causal marginals in a two-agent common-effect setting do not uniquely identify the shared backbone; infinitely many joint intervention kernels can be consistent with private reports. Identification requires additional structure (additive separability) and, critically, communication of causally identified response functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate when a shared backbone for exchanging causal knowledge (RCK) is determined by agents’ private causal knowledge and what conditions enable identification of the backbone and transport mechanism.

Method: Theoretical analysis under standard assumptions (compatibility, non-degeneracy, local overlap); construction of families of joint intervention kernels yielding identical private reports; examination of additive separability; assessment of observational residuals; illustration via an education value-added example to highlight communication as a prerequisite for policy-assembly.

Result: Under the stated assumptions, local causal marginals do not identify a unique backbone; there exist infinitely many joint intervention kernels that induce the same private reports but differ on joint interventions. A conditional recovery result is obtained; additive separability reduces but does not eliminate hidden-interaction degrees of freedom. Identification becomes possible only when agents share causally identified response functions.

Conclusion: The core challenge is communicative: determining the backbone is not solely a matter of private causal knowledge but depends on translating that knowledge through communication. The education value-added example shows the necessity of causal communication before policy design and composition.

Abstract: The Relativity of Causal Knowledge (RCK) explains how a network of agents with different structural causal models can exchange causal knowledge through a shared interventionally consistent abstraction, or backbone. We ask the prior identification question that this transport mechanism presupposes: when is that backbone determined by the agents' private causal knowledge? In the basic two-agent common-effect case, two private causes influence one shared outcome and each agent identifies only the single-cause causal marginal relevant to its own perspective. We show that, under standard compatibility, non-degeneracy, and local overlap assumptions, those local causal marginals do not identify a unique backbone. Infinitely many joint intervention kernels can induce exactly the same private reports while disagreeing on joint interventions. We then give a conditional recovery result. Additive separability removes the hidden interaction degree of freedom, but observational residual summaries remain insufficient. Identification becomes possible when agents communicate causally identified response functions. An education value-added example illustrates why this is first a communication problem, and only then a policy-composition problem.

</details>


### [286] [VERDICT: Training-Free Step-Wise Verification of Multimodal Reasoning via Disagreement-Aware Consensus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10665)
*Rohit Sinha,Kunal Tilaganji,Tanuja Ganu,Nagarajan Natarajan,Amit Sharma,Vineeth Balasubramanian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A training-free, cross-modal verifier VERDICT leverages disagreement among frozen verifiers to produce a closed-form, thresholded, step-wise verification of multimodal reasoning; achieves improvements and competitive performance without supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current verification methods rely on labeled supervision or simple score aggregation, which perform poorly cross-task. The disagreement among verifiers contains information about step validity; a training-free, domain-agnostic approach can exploit this to verify reasoning.

Method: Model verification as a coupled scoring game among frozen verifiers (a coordination game) with a unique closed-form equilibrium. Disagreement signals instability while agreement indicates validity. Compute consensus scores in closed form to enable disagreement-aware filtering and stability-conscious ranking of reasoning steps; no task-specific training required.

Result: Evaluated on six benchmarks; base-model performance improves by up to 5.95 percentage points. The approach is competitive with domain-specific, supervised critics, demonstrating robustness of cross-modal agreement as a verification signal without task-specific adaptation and training.

Conclusion: VERDICT is the first training-free verifier that makes cross-modal disagreement explicit and actionable. It provides consensus scores via a closed-form solution, enabling both filtering and ranking of steps with disagreement-aware and stability-conscious signals, generalizable across domains without task-specific adaptation.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models often generate reasoning chains containing subtle errors that lead to incorrect answers. Current verification approaches have notable limitations. Existing approaches either require expensive labelled supervision with inconsistent cross-task performance or aggregate scores from multiple sources by simple aggregations, missing a key insight: when these scores disagree, that disagreement itself carries important information about whether a reasoning step is truly valid or not. We formalise this as a coupled scoring problem among disparate, frozen verifiers, interpretable as a coordination game with a unique closed-form equilibrium where agreement signals valid steps while disagreement reveals instability. Towards this end, we propose a training-free domain-agnostic step-wise verification approach we call VERDICT: VERification via Disagreement-Informed Coupled Thresholding. To our knowledge, VERDICT is the first training-free verifier that makes the structure of cross-modal disagreement explicit and actionable. It computes consensus scores through a closed-form solution, enabling both disagreement-aware filtering and stability-conscious ranking of reasoning steps. Evaluated across six benchmarks, \method consistently improves over the base model by up to +5.95%, and performs competitively with domain-specific critics that demand extensive supervision, demonstrating that cross-modal agreement provides robust verification signals without task-specific adaptation and Training-Free Verification

</details>


### [287] [FITTER: Vocabulary-Agnostic Cross-Domain Inference on Temporal Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10668)
*Jiaxin Pan,Mojtaba Nayyeri,Osama Mohammed,Daniel Hernandez,Rongchuan Zhang,Cheng Cheng,Steffen Staab*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A fully-inductive, vocabulary-agnostic temporal KG link predictor (FITTER) enabling cross-domain transfer by using time-relative encodings and message passing to learn predicate interaction patterns without retraining on unseen entities, relations, or timestamps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing temporal KG completion methods assume known entities, relation names, and timestamps at training time, limiting scalability across heterogeneous domains and vocabularies common in the Semantic Web.

Method: FITTER encodes predicates via interaction patterns with others and time using relative, not absolute, encodings. It uses message passing to fuse local and global temporal context, producing vocabulary-agnostic embeddings with time-shift invariant temporal encoding.

Result: Empirical evaluation on six diverse temporal-KG benchmarks shows FITTER consistently surpasses inductive baselines in cross-domain and cross-graph transfer without retraining.

Conclusion: Vocabulary-agnostic structural learning with time-relative encodings provides a viable foundation for inductive inference over heterogeneous temporal knowledge graphs.

Abstract: Temporal knowledge graphs are central to many uses of the Semantic Web, but existing completion methods assume the entities, relation names, and timestamps to be reasoned about are already known at training time, restricting each model to a single graph and vocabulary. We propose FITTER, the first fully-inductive structural model for temporal knowledge graph link prediction that supports cross-domain transfer: the inference graph may contain entirely unseen entities, relation names, and timestamps drawn from a different domain. FITTER represents each predicate by its interaction patterns with others and time through encodings of relative rather than absolute ordering; message-passing fuses local and global temporal context to produce vocabulary-agnostic embeddings. We prove the temporal encoding is time-shift invariant and evaluate FITTER on cross-domain, cross-graph transfer over six temporal knowledge graph benchmarks of diverse domains, granularities, and time spans. FITTER consistently outperforms inductive baselines without retraining, indicating that vocabulary-agnostic structural learning is a viable foundation for inference over the heterogeneous knowledge graphs of the Semantic Web.

</details>


### [288] [REDAgentBench: Executable Red Teaming and Faithful Measurement of LLM Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10669)
*Zixing Chen,Xingyuan Liu,Jie Zhu,Huaixia Dou,Shuo Jiang,Junhui Li,Lifan Guo,Feng Chen,Chi Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework (REDAgentBench) for executable, red-teaming evaluation of LLM agents to measure safety across attacker-executor interactions. It reveals that safety violations are frequent and context-dependent, and that simple ASR measures undervalue risk; training-free prompts can reduce violations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safety evaluations collapse exposure, execution, observation, and adjudication into a single attack-success-rate, potentially masking actual violations and how context affects behavior. There is a need for an executable, faithful measurement framework that can derive attacks from explicit safety constraints and validate harms via service-side evidence.

Method: Create REDAgentBench: an executable framework with isolated service sandboxes, attacks derived from explicit safety constraints and known vulnerabilities, verification via service receipts and final-state changes. Evaluated across 1,661 cases, five service surfaces, six models, and three agent harnesses.

Result: Macro-average attack-success-rate (ASR) 65.69%. Reported ASR depends on harness and evidence view; disclosure of evaluation context changes execution. Identified a Recognition–Execution Gap where violations occur after the agent states a constraint. A training-free policy reminder reduced confirmed violations by >70 percentage points in matched replay.

Conclusion: Executable evaluation improves safety measurement and identifies actionable intervention points (e.g., policy reminders). The framework enables systematic red-teaming and faithful assessment of LLM agent safety across surfaces and harnesses.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents combine language-based reasoning with external tools to perform complex tasks. Adversarial inputs can exploit interactions between the agent and its environment, causing the agent to violate safety policies during execution. Yet existing evaluations often reduce agent safety to a single attack success rate (ASR), collapsing exposure, execution, observation, and adjudication and potentially conflating actual violations with evidence visibility. We introduce REDAgentBench, an executable framework for autonomous red-teaming and faithful measurement. It derives attacks from explicit safety constraints and associated agent-system vulnerabilities, runs them in isolated service sandboxes, and verifies harmful effects from service receipts and final-state changes. The benchmark contains 1,661 cases across five service surfaces. Across six models and three agent harnesses, macro-average ASR is 65.69%; reported ASR varies with harness and evidence view, while evaluation-context disclosure changes execution behavior. In a state-grounded diagnostic cohort, almost one in five confirmed violations with resolved action anchors occurs after the agent states the relevant constraint or risk, revealing a Recognition--Execution Gap. Finally, a training-free policy reminder reduces confirmed violations by more than 70 percentage points in matched replay. These findings show that executable evaluation can improve safety measurement and identify actionable intervention points.

</details>


### [289] [Self-Correcting Long-Horizon Search Agents via Tree-Structured Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10676)
*Aijun Yang,Qianxue Guo,Ziyi Huang,Yuxuan Chen,Shiyou Qian,Jian Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReTree introduces a self-correcting, tree-structured memory for LLM-based search agents that bounds per-step context, preserves source-linked evidence, and enables selective revision when conflicting evidence arises, outperforming Full-Trajectory ReAct on QA/search benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs performing long-horizon search risk unbounded context growth and noisy, inaccurate trajectories. Existing compression either loses important details or fails to repair downstream reasoning after corrections. A bounded, provenance-backed memory could enable reliable self-correction without exploding context.

Method: Construct a rooted evidence tree where each node stores bounded summaries, evidence, and revision histories. Evidence provenance links nodes to source passages. When new evidence contradicts earlier claims, ReTree traces to the claim's introduction, replaces outdated evidence, regenerates affected summaries, prunes affected branches, and resumes search with updated reasoning. The framework keeps per-step context bounded and maintains traceable provenance to retrieved passages.

Result: Empirical evaluation on four public QA/search benchmarks shows ReTree consistently outperforms Full-Trajectory ReAct, with up to 25.6 percentage points higher answer accuracy. The average maximum per-step reasoning context of Full-Trajectory ReAct is 1.27–1.51× that of ReTree.

Conclusion: ReTree provides an effective self-correcting memory abstraction for long-horizon search, achieving higher accuracy while controlling context growth and preserving source-grounded provenance.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based search agents answer questions through multi-step interactions with external environments. However, providing complete execution trajectories to the LLM causes unbounded context growth and introduces noise. Existing compression methods reduce context at the cost of important details and often replace erroneous facts without repairing downstream reasoning derived from them. To address this problem, we propose ReTree, a self-correcting tree-structured memory mechanism for search agents. ReTree constructs a bounded per-step reasoning context while preserving source-linked evidence. It models search as an evidence tree whose nodes store bounded summaries, evidence, and revision histories. When newly retrieved evidence contradicts an earlier claim, ReTree traces back to the node where the claim was introduced, replaces outdated evidence, regenerates summaries, prunes affected branches, and resumes search. Source-grounded evidence provenance supports reliable conflict localization and keeps final claims traceable to retrieved passages. Experiments on four public question-answering and search benchmarks show that ReTree consistently outperforms Full-Trajectory ReAct, improving answer accuracy by up to 25.6 percentage points (pp); the average maximum per-step reasoning context of Full-Trajectory ReAct is $1.27$--$1.51\times$ that of ReTree. These results establish ReTree as an effective self-correcting memory abstraction for long-horizon search.

</details>


### [290] [Ex-Omni-2D: Expressive Omni-Modal Dialogue Models with Native Visual Presence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10720)
*Haoyu Zhang,Zhipeng Li,Xiaoying Tang,Tianshu Yu,Yiwen Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Ex-Omni-2D is an omni-modal dialogue framework that jointly generates text, personalized speech, and reference-conditioned video via a Visual Thought Plan (VTP), learning from a full-sequence Video Generator as teacher and distilling to a Streaming Student for efficient streaming generation with four-step inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To produce coherent, synchronized responses across text, speech, and video (avatar) without requiring large-scale, multimodal supervision across all modalities; enable incremental, real-time generation and alignment of audio-visual outputs.

Method: Predict a structured Visual Thought Plan (scene, emotion, motion) from a multimodal query, reference image, and reference audio; generate response text and native multi-codebook speech units; decode into speech and align with video frames via a shared acoustic-temporal interface; use a full-sequence Video Generator as Teacher; distill into a Streaming Student with Prefix Streaming for incremental, chunked generation; four-step inference on a four-GPU pipeline.

Result: Achieves end-to-end real-time factor of 1.293 at 400×720 / 720×400 resolutions on a four-GPU setup, demonstrating practical quality–efficiency trade-offs and the feasibility of coordinated text-speech-video responses without large cross-modal supervision.

Conclusion: The proposed Ex-Omni-2D framework enables synchronized multi-codebook speech, text, and video generation guided by a Visual Thought Plan, with efficient streaming inference via a Streaming Student, achieving practical RTF and avoiding the need for extensive cross-modal supervisory data.

Abstract: Omni-modal dialogue models can understand multimodal inputs and synthesize spoken replies, yet their responses remain visually disembodied. We introduce \textbf{Ex-Omni-2D}, an omni-modal dialogue framework that generates a coordinated response comprising text, personalized speech, and reference-conditioned video. Given a multimodal query, reference image, and reference audio, the model predicts a structured \textit{Visual Thought Plan} (VTP) describing scene, emotion, and motion, followed by response text and native multi-codebook speech units. These units form a shared acoustic-temporal interface: they are decoded into speech and aligned online with video frames. This interface enables the response and avatar pathways to be learned from heterogeneous speech, dialogue, and avatar-video data, avoiding the need for large-scale query--text--speech--video supervision. A full-sequence Video Generator serves as the primary Teacher. For efficient incremental generation, we further distill it into a few-step block-causal \emph{Streaming Student} whose Prefix Streaming mechanism carries a clean latent across consecutive chunks to reduce cumulative late-chunk degradation. With four-step inference, the complete four-GPU pipeline achieves an end-to-end RTF of 1.293 at $400\times720$/$720\times400$, providing a practical quality--efficiency operating point.

</details>


### [291] [Tree-of-Ideas: Automated Research Ideation via Cross-Trajectory Reasoning over Scholarly Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10740)
*Xun Li,Yiying Yang,Pengtao Li,Xiao Yao,Suyu Liu,Xiaoyang Ye,Ziyu Lu,Yuan Yao,Yangning Li,Yinghui Li,Wenhao Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ToI presents a two-stage framework to mine evolving research trajectories from citations and generate grounded, novel ideas across trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the insufficiency of treating papers as unstructured context or modeling scholarly evolution as isolated chains; enable cross-path reasoning to uncover convergent problems and complementary solutions.

Method: Stage 1 EvoTrace reconstructs branching scholarly trajectories from citations, tracking evolving methods, resolved problems, and gaps. Stage 2 EvoAgent reasons across trajectories to identify convergent problems and complementary solutions, generating grounded research ideas.

Result: Applied to six AI topics; ToI achieves the highest automatic-method score (6.27) vs strongest baseline (5.36) on a 10-point scale, with Novelty 6.36 and Groundedness 7.00; its score nears human-referenced papers (6.29).

Conclusion: Cross-path evolutionary reasoning via ToI can produce grounded, novel research ideas that approach human references, highlighting the value of modeling scholarly evolution as interconnected trajectories.

Abstract: Effective research ideation requires moving beyond a static understanding of prior work to trace how research problems and solutions evolve across the literature. Existing methods either treat papers as unstructured context or model scholarly evolution as isolated citation chains, overlooking interactions among research trajectories. We propose Tree-of-Ideas (ToI), a two-stage framework. EvoTrace reconstructs branching scholarly trajectories from citations, tracking evolving methods, resolved problems, and gaps. EvoAgent then reasons across trajectories to identify convergent problems and complementary solutions, generating grounded research ideas. Across six AI research topics, ToI achieves the highest score among automatic methods (6.27 vs. 5.36 for the strongest baseline on a 10-point scale), with strong Novelty (6.36) and Groundedness (7.00). Also, its score approaches that of human-paper references (6.29), demonstrating the value of cross-path evolutionary reasoning.

</details>


### [292] [Compositional Benchmark Synthesis for Hierarchical Human Action Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10765)
*Farnaz Soleimani,Abdelghani Chibani,Yacine Amirat,Ghazaleh Khodabandelou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A four-level hierarchical-intention benchmark is synthesized from a flat action corpus, enabling cross-level behavior understanding with real action features. The framework addresses circular-supervision risk and subject usage imbalance, yielding 15k episodes and releasing the generator for reproducibility. Four baselines reveal a persistent held-out macro-F1 gap (~0.13–0.17), suggesting structural challenges beyond model families.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable recognition of human behavior across semantic hierarchy levels (actions, activities, LLIs, HLIs) by constructing a scalable, synthetic benchmark from flat data while preserving real action features and ensuring valid evaluation separate from generation rules.

Method: A transition-model assembles episodes with a subject-consistency constraint and a coverage-aware sampler to reduce subject-usage inequality. Four baselines (from different model families) characterize difficulty rather than perform recognition. The framework enforces disjoint first-order-logic evaluation rules to avoid circular-supervision. The instantiation yields 15,002 episodes; an ontology, transition model, and generator are released for reproducibility.

Result: Computed macro-F1 gaps of 0.13–0.17 on held-out evaluation across all baselines, even with a graph-aware model; a logic-free baseline violates semantic rules; order-destroying controls affect macro-F1, validating generator-consistency checks. The Gini-like subject-usage metric improves from 0.566 to 0.248 with the coverage-aware sampler; 15,002 episodes generated.

Conclusion: The framework provides a reproducible, scalable benchmark for hierarchical intentions, highlighting intrinsic structural challenges in cross-level generalization rather than model-specific limitations. The authors’ releases enable regeneration and extension of the benchmark, supporting future research in semantic-hierarchy understanding.

Abstract: Recognizing human behavior across levels of abstraction, from atomic actions to long-horizon intentions, requires data annotated along a semantic hierarchy. Large corpora provide isolated, atomically labeled clips without temporal composition, whereas recorded composite-activity corpora offer shallow, domain-narrow, fixedhierarchies. A benchmark-generation and evaluation frameworkis proposed that synthesizes a four-level hierarchical-intention benchmark, spanning actions, activities, low-level intentions (LLIs), and high-level intentions (HLIs), from a flat single-label action corpus while retaining real pre-extracted features at the action level. Episodes are assembled by a transition model under a subject-consistency constraint, and a coverage-aware sampler reduces the subject usage Gini from 0.566 to 0.248. Synthesizing such a benchmark raises a circular-supervision risk that recorded datasets avoid: if the rules generating the episodes also govern the evaluation, models can succeed by recovering the generator rather than through genuine reasoning. Validity is addressed by design, holding sequence-generation rules disjoint from the first-order-logic rules used at evaluation. The instantiation yields 15,002 episodes. Four reference baselines from different model families characterize difficulty, not as recognition methods. A compositional held-out gap of 0.13 to 0.17 macro-F1 appears across all baselines, including a graph-aware model that recognizes best yet does not close the gap, indicating a structural property of the benchmark rather than a model artifact. A logic-free baseline still violates the held-out semantic rules above their intrinsic data rate, and the order-destroying control changes macro-F1 within seed variation, serving as a generator-consistency check. Theontology, transition model, and generator are released so the benchmark can beregenerated and extended.

</details>


### [293] [Rule of Thumb: Explaining Artificial Intelligence Systems using Partial Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10766)
*Kaivalya Rawal,Daria Onitiu,Brent Mittelstadt,Sandra Wachter,Chris Russell*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RoT explanations identify the most relevant features for predicting an AI system's behavior on a specific datapoint, enabling concise, datapoint-specific XAI. It is well-suited for zero-shot classification with LLMs, auditing opaque AI without model access, and aiding scientific discovery; model-agnostic, regulation-friendly, visualizable, and faster than alternatives. Code available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a need for interpretable, scalable, and regulation-aligned explanations that work across diverse AI tasks, including zero-shot classification, auditing without access to model internals, and scientific discovery. Traditional XAI methods can be limited by access constraints, computational cost, or regulatory requirements.

Method: Introduce Rule of Thumb (RoT) explanations, a partial-information formulation that identifies the most relevant features driving a datapoint's predicted behaviour, producing succinct, human-understandable explanations.

Result: RoT is presented as suitable for (a) zero-shot classification with large language models, (b) auditing opaque AI systems without direct model access, and (c) use in scientific discovery; it provides familiar interfaces and visualizations, is model-agnostic, and offers faster performance than competing methods.

Conclusion: RoT fulfills regulatory considerations and delivers practical, efficient XAI explanations with an accompanying open-source implementation.

Abstract: Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) seeks to explain how an Artificial Intelligence (AI) system arrived at a particular decision. We propose ''Rule of Thumb'' (RoT) explanations, a new approach to XAI based upon a novel formulation that identifies the most relevant features for predicting the behaviour of an AI system, for a particular datapoint. We show how RoT is well-suited to enable XAI in: (a) zero-shot classification using large language models (LLMs), (b) auditing of opaque AI systems without model access, and (c) the use of AI in scientific discovery. Additionally, RoT meets specific requirements from leading AI regulations, provides a familiar interface and visualisations for XAI practitioners, is model-agnostic, and is substantially faster than alternatives.
  Code available at: https://github.com/KaiRawal/Rule-of-Thumb-Explaining-Artificial-Intelligence-Systems-using-Partial-Information

</details>


### [294] [SkillLens: Visual Skill Cards for Retrieval-Augmented GUI Action Prediction and On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10775)
*Zhou Liu,Ligang Huang,Zeli Su,Zewei Pan,Zhaoyang Han,Xing Chen,Yuanfeng Song,Wentao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Visual Skill Cards (VSCs) give a state-conditioned memory that binds reusable GUI procedures with applicability cues, visual evidence, and verification signals. SkillLens constructs and queries VSCs to enable grounded GUI action prediction; CardDistill distills VSC evidence into a student model that operates without runtime card retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents using rich software interfaces lack a compact, visual-aware procedural memory. Raw interaction traces are long and noisy; text-only skills miss visual state that indicates procedure applicability. A memory representation that ties procedures to visual evidence and verification signals can improve reliable GUI task execution.

Method: SkillLens builds VSCs via Trace-to-Visual-Skill-Card from heterogeneous interaction traces. At inference, it retrieves relevant cards and expands only the evidence needed by a fixed visual-language executor to predict grounded GUI actions. CardDistill uses VSC evidence as privileged teacher context to train a student model that operates without runtime card retrieval. Evaluation is conducted on two benchmarks (Multimodal-Mind2Web and WebLINX-BrowserGym) using a GPT-5.4-mini executor (Step SR and Overall metrics) and a student model (Qwen3-VL-2B).

Result: SkillLens improves the GPT-5.4-mini executor by +11.6 points in Step SR and +2.9 points in Overall. CardDistill further improves the student-only Qwen3-VL-2B metrics by +12.0 points (Step SR) and +3.2 points (Overall).

Conclusion: The VSC representation provides effective, compact, and transferable state-conditioned memory for grounded GUI action prediction, achieving notable gains across benchmarks and enabling teacher-student distillation to enable card-free execution.

Abstract: Computer-using agents can perceive rich software interfaces, yet their decisions often lack visual procedural memory: they may recognize individual controls without identifying which familiar workflow is active, which control matters next, or what evidence would confirm progress. Raw interaction traces preserve such information but are long and noisy to condition on, whereas text-only skills often omit the visual state that makes a procedure applicable. We introduce Visual Skill Cards (VSCs), a state-conditioned memory representation that binds reusable procedures with applicability cues, visual evidence, and verification signals. SkillLens constructs VSCs from heterogeneous interaction experience through Trace-to-Visual-Skill-Card and, at inference time, retrieves relevant cards and selectively expands only the evidence needed by a fixed visual-language model executor for grounded GUI action prediction. The same representation also supports CardDistill, which uses VSC evidence as privileged teacher context to train a student that acts without runtime card retrieval. Across Multimodal-Mind2Web and WebLINX-BrowserGym, SkillLens improves the frozen GPT-5.4-mini executor by +11.6 points in Step SR and +2.9 points in Overall, respectively; CardDistill further improves the corresponding student-only Qwen3-VL-2B metrics by +12.0 and +3.2 points.

</details>


### [295] [ChemWorld: Programmable Chemical Worlds for Controlled and Replayable Agent Experimentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10792)
*Jiangjie Qiu,Yijun Li,Xiaonan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ChemWorld is a programmable, replayable chemical environment that separates public experimental contracts from private laws, enabling exact replay, failure recovery, and systematic comparison across varied chemical worlds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between costly, hard-to-repeat physical labs and fixed digital environments by providing a configurable, auditable substrate for chemical experimentation that can vary world rules while keeping public tasks constant.

Method: Design and implement ChemWorld as a modular, compiled executable world with reusable process/observation components, transactional execution logs, and a clear separation between evaluator-owned chemical laws and the public experimental interface; validate with full-census registry, extensive test suites, deterministic cases, and world forks.

Result: Demonstrated exact replay and reliable failure recovery across eight deterministic experiments; showed isolation of private-law effects via six parent–child world forks; achieved full lifecycle execution in both a reference and non-reference world; compiled 52 compositions and validated interfaces, modules, and invalid actions.

Conclusion: ChemWorld serves as a controlled, replayable substrate for systematic study of experimentation across varied chemical worlds, complementing physical lab evidence and calibration.

Abstract: Autonomous chemistry increasingly depends on environments in which agents can repeatedly act, observe, and adapt.Physical laboratories provide essential real-material evidence but are costly to repeat and difficult to use for tightly matched interventions, whereas most digital environments keep the underlying experimental world largely fixed. We introduce ChemWorld, a programmable chemical environment in which reusable process and observation components are compiled into executable worlds. ChemWorld separates the public experimental contract available to an agent from evaluator-owned chemical and material laws. Researchers can therefore vary world composition and operating conditions, or change a single hidden law while holding the public task and interaction conditions fixed. Transactional execution records operations, failures, resource changes, and state transitions, allowing complete environment-action trajectories to be replayed exactly and audited. Full-census qualification covered the reference registry, 52 generated compositions, and module, interface, compilation, and invalid-action tests. Eight deterministic experimental cases demonstrated shared lifecycle semantics, failure recovery, and exact replay, while six parent-child world-fork pairs isolated the effects of single private-law interventions under matched public conditions. An independent agent also completed a full lifecycle in a non-reference world through the same public interface. Within the declared component and model domain, ChemWorld provides a controlled and replayable substrate for studying experimentation across systematically varied chemical worlds, complementary to physical-laboratory evidence and calibration.

</details>


### [296] [EvoMem: Memory-Augmented Evolution for Code Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10795)
*Viktor Volkov,Valentin Khrulkov,Andrey V. Galichin,Danil Sivtsov,Nikita Glazkov,Olga Volkova,Konstantin Pchelin,Iaroslav Bespalov,Dmitry V. Dylov,Petr Anokhin,Ivan Oseledets*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvoMem provides persistent memory for LLM-based evolutionary program search to reuse mutation knowledge across runs, improving efficiency and performance with task-aware instructions; results show improvements but with variability across tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-driven evolutionary frameworks discard cross-run knowledge, causing repeated rediscovery and inefficiency; there is a need for persistent, transferable memory of successful mutations.

Method: A two-phase approach: after each run, extract and store promising mutation ideas with provenance; in subsequent runs, retrieve a small, task- and context-aware set of instructions to guide mutation. The memory is persistent and designed to be task-aware.

Result: Across benchmarks in geometric optimization, multi-hop QA, GPU kernel optimization, and related tasks, EvoMem shows positive average improvements in target metrics or search speed for most settings, with variability across tasks.

Conclusion: Persistent memory can reduce redundant exploration and improve reuse/adaptation of successful mutation strategies in LLM-driven evolutionary search; EvoMem demonstrates cross-run knowledge reuse benefits.

Abstract: Successful mutation strategies in evolutionary code search may contain reusable knowledge that is useful beyond a single run, and in some cases may transfer across related tasks and domains. However, existing LLM-driven evolutionary frameworks largely discard such knowledge, repeatedly rediscovering similar ideas and limiting opportunities for cross-run and cross-task learning. We introduce EvoMem, a persistent memory architecture for LLM-based evolutionary program search that captures and reuses candidate mutation knowledge. EvoMem converts successful mutation events into structured, task-aware advice for future runs. It operates in two phases: after each run, it extracts and stores promising ideas with provenance, and during subsequent evolution, it retrieves a small set of relevant instructions based on the current task and program context to guide mutation. Across geometric optimization, multi-hop question answering, GPU kernel optimization, and related benchmarks, our experiments show positive average improvements in target metrics or search speed for most evaluated settings, while also revealing variability across tasks. Overall, EvoMem provides evidence that persistent memory can reduce some redundant exploration and improve the reuse and adaptation of successful strategies in LLM-driven evolutionary search.

</details>


### [297] [Hypothesis Frontier: Verifier Guided LLM and Symbolic Search for First-Order Induction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10843)
*Serafim Batzoglou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Verifier-guided neurosymbolic framework (Hypothesis Frontier) for first-order concept synthesis that iteratively refines LLM-generated hypotheses with symbolic verification to solve more problems and compress formulas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the intractable search space of first-order formulas by leveraging LLM-promising hypotheses and direct verification on training objects, with symbolic repairs and simplification to ensure correctness and efficiency.

Method: Iterative loop: generate candidate LLM formulas; verify on each training object; retain strongest verified hypothesis; use remaining errors to guide next generation; apply symbolic repairs anchored to LLM hypothesis; simplify train-valid formulas without changing predictions; final exact simplification to compress.

Result: Outperforms repeated original-prompt generation under matched models, problem sets, and LLM budgets; exact simplification reduces formula size while preserving training predictions.

Conclusion: Exact symbolic reasoning enhances both induction problem solving and post-hoc compression; neurosymbolic verification effectively harnesses LLMs for first-order concept synthesis.

Abstract: First-order concept synthesis asks a system to infer one formula that classifies labeled objects consistently across several finite relational structures. Every candidate can be evaluated exactly, but quantified first-order formulas form a vast search space, and LLM outputs are often semantically promising without being fully correct. We introduce Hypothesis Frontier, a verifier-guided neurosymbolic framework that evaluates each LLM formula on every training object, retains the strongest verified hypothesis across rounds, and uses its remaining errors to guide subsequent generation. Symbolic processing repairs invalid formulas while remaining anchored to the LLM-generated hypothesis, and simplifies train-valid formulas without changing any training prediction. Under matched models, problem sets, and LLM-round budgets, Hypothesis Frontier solves substantially more problems than repeated original-prompt generation. After the final formulas are selected, exact simplification shortens many train-valid formulas while preserving every training prediction. Exact symbolic reasoning therefore helps both to solve more induction problems and to compress many of the resulting formulas.

</details>


### [298] [Enhanced Filtering Algorithms for the Euclidean Traveling Salesperson Problem and its variants in Constraint Logic Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10881)
*Alessandro Bertagnon,Marco Gavanelli*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces geometry-aware filtering in CLP for Euclidean TSP and EGTSP, yielding stronger propagation and faster solving compared to distance-matrix CP approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exploit coordinates to prune search space beyond full-distance matrices; address gaps in standard CP formulations for Euclidean TSP.

Method: Develop new constraint filtering algorithms that use geometric information; implement in constraint logic programming; extend to Euclidean Generalized TSP.

Result: Empirical results show computational advantages: faster solve times and better pruning due to stronger propagation.

Conclusion: Geometry-based filtering in CLP effectively improves Euclidean TSP/EGTSP solving; potential applicability to other Euclidean TSP variants and routing problems.

Abstract: The Traveling Salesperson Problem (TSP) is one of the best-known problems in computer science and arises in many engineering applications, such as smart vehicles and intelligent transportation systems. In the "Euclidean" case, each node is defined by its coordinates in the plane and distances are computed using the Euclidean metric. In the Constraint Programming (CP) literature, the Euclidean TSP is typically addressed by computing the full distance matrix and treating it as a general case; however this approach ignores the geometric information carried by the points' coordinates. In this work, we propose new filtering algorithms, implemented in Constraint Logic Programming (CLP), that exploit such geometric information to achieve stronger constraint propagation than existing approaches. Moreover, we show how this methodology can be extended to other Euclidean variants of the TSP, including the Euclidean Generalized Traveling Salesperson Problem (EGTSP), which is relevant in practical routing and logistics applications. Experimental results demonstrate the computational advantages of the proposed approach.

</details>


### [299] [ComBodied Agents: a New Paradigm of Human-Centric Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10915)
*Qianggang Ding,Xingyao Wang,Rui Feng,Zhibin Wang,Feixiang Wang,Kelong Mao,Hao Sun,Zhiyao Luo,Jiankai Tang,Lei Li,Jiadong Guo,Minheng Ni,Weicong Lin,Chenxi Yang,Hongxiang Gao,Zhenghua Chen,Yang Bai,Min Wu,Jun Cheng,Huazhu Fu,Dacheng Tao,Bang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Combodied Agents offer a human-centered, closed-loop framework for modeling and supporting evolving human states, using multimodal perception, memory, personal world models, and an admissible intervention policy to shift AI from task completion to sustained human benefit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identify and address a structural gap in Agentic AI: current software- and embodied-agents primarily act on states but do not model or influence the person’s evolving state and agency; a unified, user-centered approach is needed to sense, model, predict, and support individuals over time with consent, safety, reversibility, and governance.

Method: Propose Combodied Agents architecture: event-based multimodal perception; longitudinal memory; Personal World Models; admissible intervention policy; use sensors, wearables, robots, and human services as action channels; closed loop with feedback; purpose-bounded, uncertainty-aware representations; organize design space; scenario-centered evaluation; governance directions.
